*Verified live, in flight, against the provisional HUD still drawn beside it:* every value agrees frame for frame — hull 70% / fore shield 74% while the dev strip read `HUL 70% SHD 74/100`, the fore arc visibly shorter than the aft one; two taps of `1` moved a pip from ENG to WEP (`WEP |||` / `ENG |`) and the capacitor climbed back to 100%; the target readout tracked an Interceptor from 201542 km to 155692 km with `+2078 km/s` closing and `Solar Navy [neutral]` in neutral grey; the accepted campaign contract put "Shakedown Cruise / Fly to the calibration beacon (8 km out)" above the flight panel; the dock prompt lit next to Lyrioa Beta; scraping the station turned the crosshair red and ringed the screen while the fore shield dropped, and both recovered on their own; and a 2.6 s yaw swapped the diamond for an edge arrow drawn exactly over the dev one. Quit from the pause menu, process exit **0 — no validation messages**. 3 new `ui.unit` tests (44), 7 suites green, zero warnings.

✅ *Provisional screens deleted* (2026-08-16). The Dear ImGui flight HUD, station panel, and its six tab builders are gone from `dev_ui.cpp` — about 560 lines — leaving `DevUi` as what the dependency policy allows it to be: perf overlay and Lua console, `beginFrame(stats)` and nothing else. The data contracts they shared with the game (`FlightHud`, `StationPanel`, `TradePanel`, and the row structs) move to a new `sol/ui/screens.hpp`, which owns no ImGui and no rendering: the game fills one per frame, a screen draws it, and the player's clicks come back in the `action` fields. Nothing in the game layer changed but its includes.

*Verified live: the flight view is now the custom HUD alone* — crosshair with shield facings, lead marker, target diamond and range, flight panel, and the power block with its capacitor, all unobstructed for the first time (the dev perf overlay stays, as dev tooling always did). Fought an Interceptor down to a 52% fore shield and 75% capacitor with the console hidden, docked and read the station screen back, then Esc → Quit: exit **0, no validation messages**. 7 suites green, zero warnings.

**Known gaps**: kerning comes from the legacy `kern` table only, and IBM Plex ships its pairs in GPOS, which this reader does not parse — so text currently sets on raw advances. Acceptable for v1 UI copy at these sizes; revisit if headings look loose. UI scale magnifies the baked glyph sizes rather than selecting a larger baked style, so text softens above ~1.5×; picking the nearest style per scale is the fix when it matters. And there is no "quit to main menu": starting a second run in one process needs a world reset that does not exist yet, and a menu entry that only half works is worse than one that is missing.

#### Phase 8e — Exploration, Scanning & Map Screens ✅ (completed 2026-08-16)

Implements GDD §8 Exploration & scanning **[likely]** — "unvisited systems are unknown; scanning reveals bodies, signals, derelicts, hidden caches; the fringe pays explorers" — and pays the debt Phase 8d deferred: the **system and galaxy map screens**, which this item has to build anyway and is the first item that knows what they must show.

Scope decided with the user: the scan is **two verbs and no minigame** — a ship-wide *pulse* that reveals contacts and a *target scan* that resolves one; exploration pays through **sellable survey data** and **derelict/cache loot**, and deliberately *not* through remote market intel or survey contracts (both stay open — see Out of scope); fog of war is **soft** — a gate names where it leads, a system's contents stay blank until you have been there, and nothing anywhere is *refused* for being unknown; and both maps ship with route plotting but **no auto-jump** (`decisions/005` — you fly every leg yourself, which is what makes the fringe far away).

**Knowledge & survey state (`sol::sim::SurveySim`, coarse layer).** A fourth sibling of `Economy`/`FactionSim`/`MissionSim` in `engine/sim/survey.hpp`, and it earns that place the same way they do: it is the part that must be deterministic, unit-testable, and saved. It holds one `SystemKnowledge` per system — `Unknown` → `Charted` (name, map position, and lanes known because a gate you have stood at points here) → `Visited` (you have been in the system: stations, bodies, and owner are known) → `Surveyed` (every body scanned and every signal resolved) — plus, per system, which signals have been discovered, resolved, and emptied, and a **survey ledger** of unsold data entries. Transitions are one-way and driven by events the game reports (`notifyArrival`, `notifyBodyScanned`, `notifySignalResolved`), exactly the `MissionSim` shape. Charting is a pure consequence of arrival: entering a system charts every system its gates lead to, so the map grows outward along the lanes you actually travel.

**Signals (deterministic content, not entities-on-disk).** A system's signals are a pure function of `SystemSpec::seed` and `SurveyParams`, generated on demand by `signalsFor(galaxy, systemIndex, out)` — the same trick the galaxy generator uses, so the save stores per-signal *state* (discovered/resolved/emptied bits) and never the content. Count and richness follow the GDD's region gradient (core 0–2, frontier 2–4, fringe 3–6), and positions cluster in the playfield around the primary planet like stations and gates do, inside the `decisions/005` cruise budget. Two kinds in v1: **derelict** (a dead hull — commodity cargo, sometimes a module) and **cache** (a stash — cargo plus credits). Both are *sites you fly to and empty*, not wrecks you cut apart: **full salvage — hull cutting, wreck fields left behind by real battles — belongs to the mining/salvage item**, and this spec deliberately stops at "open the container" so the two items do not build the same thing twice.

**The scan loop (bubble layer, `SpaceWorld`).** Two verbs, both range-gated by the fitted scanner:
- **Pulse** (`R`, a cooldown, no hold): reveals every undiscovered signal within `scanRange` of the ship as an unresolved contact — a bearing and a range, nothing more. Sweeping a whole playfield takes several pulses from several places, which is the exploration loop: you fly, you pulse, you fly.
- **Target scan** (no key): a discovered-but-unresolved contact, or an unscanned celestial body, that is the current target and within `scanTargetRange` accumulates scan progress while it stays targeted and roughly boresighted; at 100% it resolves — the contact becomes a named nav target with a kind, a body becomes surveyed — and the resulting data lands in the ledger.
- **Empty** (`G` — the existing interact key): within `kSalvageRange` of a resolved site with nothing left to dock at, the dock prompt slot reads `[G] SALVAGE` and one press moves the site's contents into the hold (clamped to cargo space; a partial take leaves the rest). This reuses the verified dock-prompt and cargo paths rather than inventing a second interaction.

Discovered signals join `m_targets`, so `T`-cycling, the autopilot (`F`), and the HUD target readout all work on them with no new machinery.

**Payoff: survey data.** Every resolution writes a `SurveyEntry` into the ledger — system, kind (system arrival, body, site), region, and a **first-discovery** flag — and the ledger is sold at any station: a seventh **Survey** tab on the station screen lists unsold entries with their value and a Sell All, and the credits land like a trade. Value comes from `SurveyParams` (base per kind × region multiplier × first-discovery bonus, plus a completion bonus the first time a system reaches `Surveyed`), so "the fringe pays explorers" is a tuning table, not a hard-coded curve; selling also pays a small standing trickle with the station owner, matching the commerce trickle Phase 8b already pays for trading. Data is an intangible: it costs no cargo space and is not lost on death (the ledger is knowledge, not freight) — losing a hold full of derelict loot is penalty enough.

**Map screens (game layer, `game/src/map_screen.cpp` + a `sol::ui::MapPanel` contract in `screens.hpp`).** `M` opens the map from flight or from a station and `M`/Esc closes it; it is a `GameState::Map` beside `Docked` in the `main.cpp` state machine — it owns the keyboard but, like `Docked`, **does not stop the clock**, because a galaxy that pauses while you read the map is not the galaxy this game is selling. A tab strip carries the two views, and both are drawn with the existing `DrawList` primitives (`addArc`/`addCircle` from the HUD work already covers the node glyphs):
- **Galaxy map** — the lane graph projected from `SystemSpec::mapPosition` with pan and zoom, region rings, owner color from `GameFaction::color`, and knowledge state in the node glyph (unknown systems are absent entirely; charted are hollow and unnamed beyond their name; visited are filled; surveyed carry a completion tick). Selecting a node opens a side panel with what you actually know about it, and plotting a route runs the existing `sim::routeBetween` and lights the lane chain; the HUD then carries a "next hop" line naming the gate to take. No auto-jump.
- **System map** — a top-down of the current system's playfield (star, planets, stations, gates, discovered signals) at a clamped log radius so an AU-scale star and a 1e8 m station are legible on one screen. Selecting a marker selects the nav target; activating it hands off to the existing autopilot.

**HUD & input.** The flight HUD grows a compact **contacts** block (unresolved contacts by bearing and range, the resolved-site count for the system), a pulse cooldown ring on the crosshair, a target-scan progress arc while a scan is running, the `[G] SALVAGE` prompt in the existing prompt slot, and the plotted route's next hop. New keys are `R` (pulse) and `M` (map), both of which are free today; both go through the existing gameplay-key guard so they mean nothing on a screen.

**Data model.** `FitStat` gains `ScanRange` and `ScanSpeed` (`scan_range_add/_mul`, `scan_speed_add/_mul`), so scanners are ordinary utility modules under the Phase 8a power/mass budget and a scanner is a real fitting choice rather than a flag; `ShipDef` gains `scan_range` and `scan_speed` base stats. `game/data/modules.toml` gains a scanner line per tier, and a new `[[signal]]`-flavored tuning block lives in `SurveyParams` defaults (base ranges, pulse cooldown, per-region signal counts and values) — as with the deductible/resale fractions, these start as game-side constants and move to data when a settings def exists.

**Lua & console.** Loot composition is content, so it follows the `faction_think`/`mission_board` pattern: on resolution C++ calls `signal_loot(kind, system, region, roll)` with the signal's own seeded roll as the only entropy, the script posts a loot table through `sol.set_loot{...}`, and C++ validates it against caps (known commodity ids, unit ceilings, sellable module ids) with a built-in default table when no script answers. `signal_found(kind, system)` fires for flavor. Console: `sol.knowledge`, `sol.signals`, `sol.scan` (force-resolve the current target — dev pacing), `sol.pulse`, `sol.survey_ledger`, `sol.sell_survey`, `sol.route`, and `sol.chart` (dev cheat: mark a system visited).

**Save v7.** Body gains the survey block: per-system knowledge state, per-signal discovered/resolved/emptied bits, the unsold ledger, and the plotted route. Layout re-derives from galaxy + defs and load fails on a count mismatch — the economy rule every sibling sim follows — and the version bump follows the v4/v5/v6 compatibility policy (old saves rejected cleanly, not migrated).

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** Remote market intel — remembered prices with staleness on the galaxy map — is the natural next lever on this machinery and the fix for the Phase 7 "remote prices are invisible" caveat, but it is not in v1. Neither are survey/investigate mission objectives (a new `ObjectiveKind` on `MissionSim`), planetary/atmospheric scanning (GDD §9 non-goal), scannable NPC cargo, or exploration-gated unlocks. Auto-jump route flying stays rejected on `decisions/005` grounds.

**Exit**: from a standing start, pulse in an unexplored fringe system, fly to a contact, watch the target scan resolve it into a derelict, empty it, and sell the cargo — and see the survey ledger pay separately at the same station, more than the same data pays in the core; a system with every body and site resolved reads `Surveyed` on the galaxy map and pays its completion bonus once; the galaxy map shows exactly what the player has earned the right to see — unknown systems absent, systems charted by standing at a gate hollow and contentless, visited systems filled with owner and stations — and plotting a route across four jumps lights the lanes and names the next gate on the HUD; the system map places every known marker legibly at both AU and 1e8 m scale and hands a selection to the autopilot; the whole loop is operable on the keyboard alone; scanning a fresh galaxy twice from the same seed produces identical signals, positions, and loot; save/reload restores knowledge, per-signal state, ledger, and plotted route exactly; `SurveySim` is unit-tested like its siblings (generation determinism, one-way transitions, value math, tick-for-tick save/load) with map projection and culling covered in `ui.unit`, all suites green and zero warnings. *(Verified live 2026-08-16, seed 1701, across seven scripted sessions: six gate hops out of the core reached **Praioos** (frontier, Freight Guild, 3 sites), where a fitted Survey Scanner Mk1 widened the pulse to a logged "within 400000 km" and four pulses found all three sites — the contacts list reading "1: unidentified contact, 313720 km / 2: 557524 km / 3: 516980 km" and the Lua `signal_found` hook printing "[scan] contact in Praioos" once per new contact. Picking "Contact 1" off the **system map** and pressing its Autopilot button flew the leg; the target scan resolved on its own at 5 km with the console logging "scan resolved: Cache", the readout flipping to **"Supply Cache"** and the scanner block reading "2 unidentified - 1 site(s) open". Closing to 1.2 km lit `[G] SALVAGE` in the prompt slot, and the take emptied the site — target "Supply Cache (empty)", "0 site(s) open", 24 units in a 50-unit hold. The **region gradient** landed exactly as tuned: the ledger read core system data 150 cr against frontier 300 cr, the site survey 520 cr (260 × 2), a planet scan 160 cr, and the completion bonus 1200 cr (600 × 2) the moment Praioos hit **SURVEYED** with sites 3/3. Selling at Praioos Alpha's **Survey tab** paid "sold survey data for 2020 cr" and moved credits 20738 → 22758 exactly, leaving "Total 0 cr" and a disabled Sell All. A **planet was scanned by hand at 168.24 Mkm** — the scan ring closing around the crosshair and the block reading "Praioos I 66%" — which is the rule that bodies are read at whatever range they sit at. The **galaxy map** drew only what was earned: 15 of 80 systems, unknown ones absent, charted ones hollow and labelled "uncharted", visited ones filled in their owner's color with the current system ringed; `sol.route("Lyrioa")` plotted "Praioos > Helis > Tauubyx > Mariaia > Lyrum > Lyrioa", lit that lane chain on the map, marked the list rows "route", and put **"ROUTE > Helis"** on the HUD. Keyboard: Tab focused the tab strip and Right stepped Galaxy → System. Save/load: a `sol.knowledge()|sol.signals()|sol.survey_ledger()` snapshot compared **string-equal across F9/F10** ("SURVEY SAVE/LOAD MATCH", live ranges normalized out), and a cold restart through Continue restored "1: Cache, R (emptied)" plus the whole ledger from disk. The **Lua loot path** was proven by replacing `signal_loot` from the console with one that posts `sol.set_loot("sol.ore:7", 42, "")`: the site then salvaged for exactly "7 units, 42 cr" and the hold read ore 7 — and a table naming `sol.unobtainium` was refused ("set_loot: unknown commodity", `false` back to the script) with the site keeping its C++ default. Every session that quit through the pause menu exited **0 — no validation messages**. 8 new sim tests (78), 4 new `ui.unit` tests (48), 1 new assets test (31); 7 suites green, zero warnings.*

**Tuning and fixes found by driving it.** The first pulse range (6e7 m) was arithmetically hopeless — a 9e20 m³ sphere sweeping a 5e26 m³ playfield is one part in 500,000, so a player would never find anything. Base `scan_range` moved to **2.5e8 m** and the signal band tightened to the station band (8e7–4e8 m), which makes a pulse from the inhabited part of a system a real sweep and makes a scanner module the difference between one pulse and four; the identify range is 2% of it, so you still have to fly there. Three real bugs came out of the sessions: the map screen's footer was laid out against `Column::remaining()` *after* the body was taken from it, so the buttons claimed the whole body height and painted over the list; the map drew over the live flight HUD without dimming, which was unreadable (it now dims like the station screen and the HUD is not drawn beneath it); and the scanner block sat in the top-left corner, exactly under the dev overlay, so it moved beneath the target readout where the other sensor information lives. One pre-existing wart was fixed in passing: an engaged autopilot survived a load and woke up flying at whatever landed in target slot 0.

**Known gaps**: the "first discovery" bonus means *unsettled* — a system with no station at all — since a single-player galaxy has no other explorers to be first ahead of; if NPC survey rivals ever exist, this is the hook. Signals are static per seed: nothing new ever appears in a system the player has finished, so a fully surveyed galaxy stops paying (the fix is a slow respawn on the coarse layer, which belongs with whatever adds dynamic POIs). The `signal_found` hook fires per contact with no throttle, and a pulse that finds four sites prints four lines. And scanning is unaffected by power pips or by being under fire — a scan holds through a dogfight, which is a balance question rather than a bug.

#### Phase 8f — Mining, Salvage & Refining ✅ (completed 2026-08-16)

Implements GDD §8 Mining & salvage **[likely]** — "asteroid prospecting/extraction; battlefield salvage (wrecks from real battles persist and are lootable)" — and picks up exactly where Phase 8e stopped on purpose. 8e's sites are containers you *open*; this item builds the beam that cuts things *apart*, the rocks worth cutting, and the wrecks a real battle leaves behind. It also adds the Phase 7 economy's first processing step, which is what makes a hold of ore worth more than the nearest station will pay for it.

Scope decided with the user: extraction is a **beam that cracks a rock into free-floating ore you then collect** — not an instant transfer, and not a minigame (the same reasoning that gave 8e two verbs); asteroid fields are **seeded content with real rocks you fly among**, not a single point of interest you park at; **wrecks persist on the coarse layer**, so a battlefield is still there after a jump away and a reload, which is what the GDD's persistence line actually demands; and raw ore is **refined at stations** for a fee and a wait, so where you sell is a decision instead of a price lookup.

**Content & state (`sol::sim::MiningSim`, coarse layer).** A fifth sibling of `Economy`/`FactionSim`/`MissionSim`/`SurveySim` in `engine/sim/mining.hpp`, earning the place the same way they do: this is the part that must be deterministic, unit-testable, and saved. Wrecks live here beside rocks rather than in their own home because they are the same verb — the beam does not care what it is cutting — and because both pay out through 8e's already-validated `SignalLoot`. It carries three things:
- **Asteroid fields**, a pure function of `SystemSpec::seed` and `MiningParams`, exactly as signals are. `fieldsFor(galaxy, system, out)` yields 0–N field centers with a radius, a rock count, and an ore profile weighted by region (the fringe is richer — the GDD gradient again), placed in the station/signal playfield band so a field is inside the `decisions/005` cruise budget; `rocksFor(field, out)` expands one field into individual rocks (position, radius, tumble, commodity, yield units) from the field's own seed. No field content is ever written to disk.
- **Depletion**, which is the state that *is* saved: a sparse `(system, field, rock, unitsTaken)` record written only for rocks the player has actually cut, so an untouched galaxy costs zero bytes and a mined-out field survives a jump and a reload. Rocks do not regenerate in v1 — a field is finite and the fringe has many — which is deliberately the same known gap 8e's static signals carry, and it gets the same fix whenever dynamic content lands.
- **Wrecks**, which are the opposite kind of thing: they exist *because* something happened, so the sim stores them outright. `WreckRecord { system, position, defId, contents, decaySeconds }` — `contents` is a `sim::SignalLoot`, the struct 8e already validates, saves, and fills from Lua, so cutting a wreck and emptying a derelict pay through one path. `SpaceWorld::handleShipDestroyed` records one per kill (player kills and NPC-on-NPC kills in the player's system alike), composed from the victim's actual fit and cargo, and `MiningSim::tick` ages them out on the coarse clock so a hundred-hour save is not a junkyard.

**The beam (bubble layer, `SpaceWorld`).** `WeaponDef` gains `mining_power` (yield units per second; 0 for ordinary guns), so a mining laser stays an ordinary hardpoint choice under the Phase 8a power/mass budget rather than becoming a mode: hold the trigger on a rock or a wreck inside the weapon's own `range` and its yield pool grinds down, drawing on the WEP capacitor like any other shot. What comes off is not credited directly — each grind step spawns an **ore chunk** entity (Transform + `OreChunk { commodity, units, driftVelocity, lifetime }`) drifting off the impact point with a little of the rock's tumble. Chunks are collected by proximity: any chunk within the ship's collector radius is drawn in and added to the hold, so `FitStat::CollectorRange` (`collector_range_add`/`_mul`) joins the modifier vocabulary beside 8e's scan stats, `ShipDef` gains a base `collector_range`, and a collector rig is the module that turns a ship with a mining laser into a mining *ship*. A full hold stops collection while chunks keep drifting until they expire — that is the pressure that sends a miner home, and it is why the hold is the miner's real stat. The existing `[G] SALVAGE` prompt keeps its 8e meaning for derelicts and caches; wrecks are cut, not pressed.

**Rocks as entities.** Every field in the current system expands to entities on `loadSystem` alongside stations and gates — Transform + RenderShape + a `MineableRock` component — and joins `m_collisionBodies` as immovable spheres (`inverseMass = 0`), so a rock is something you can crash into and the Phase 6 swept collision keeps cruise from tunneling through one. The budget is 1–3 fields of ~40 rocks, well under the 34–43 entities the Phase 6 dogfight ran at ~146 fps, but the count is static-heavy in a way this world has not been before, so it is an exit criterion rather than an assumption. Rendering adds `ModelId::Asteroid` — a noise-displaced icosphere generated in `gen_assets.ps1`, cooked to `asteroid.smesh` like `station`/`ship` — with per-rock scale and a fixed tumble from the rock's seed; a wreck reuses `ModelId::Ship` dark and tumbling until a dedicated wreck mesh earns its place. **Prospecting reuses 8e's target scan**: holding a rock in the reticle inside `targetScanRange` reveals its composition and remaining yield in the target readout. It writes *no* survey ledger entry — exploration is already paid for, and paying twice for the same verb would make mining the better way to sell data.

**Refining (economy).** A new commodity `sol.metal` (Refined Metal) and a new archetype `sol.station_refinery` (consumes ore, produces metal) deepen the production triangle into a chain: food → mines, ore → refineries, metal → factories, machinery → farms. Fabricator Yard's `consumes` moves from ore to metal, so raw ore's buyer of last resort is the refinery and the chain has to actually run. For the player, a refinery station offers a **Refine** service on an eighth station tab: hand over units of raw ore plus a fee and a job completes after `refineSeconds` of world time at `refineRatio` metal per ore. Jobs live in `MiningSim` (`RefineJob { market, commodity, units, secondsRemaining }`) and tick on the coarse clock — which runs while docked, while flying, and while you are three systems away, per `decisions/005` — and the finished metal waits at that station until you dock and collect it (clamped to hold space; an uncollected job never expires). That round trip is the whole point: refining pays more and costs a return leg, so a miner's route is a decision. Ore demand moving from factories to refineries is a real rebalance of the Phase 7 markets, so retuning the archetype rates — and re-checking the 8c finding that an hour-old economy runs ore and food dry near the core — is part of this item, not a follow-up.

**HUD & UI.** The target readout gains a rock's composition and remaining yield; the mining beam draws through the existing hitscan effect with `CombatEffects` impact sparks at the rock; a collection ticker reports units taken as chunks arrive; the system map grows `MapMarkerRow::Kind::Field` and `Kind::Wreck` markers so a mined field and a battlefield are both findable from the map 8e built. No new keys: mining is the fire button, prospecting is the existing target scan, and salvage is still `[G]`.

**Lua & console.** Wreck contents are content, so they follow the 8e loot pattern exactly: on a kill C++ calls `wreck_loot(defId, system, faction, roll)` with the wreck's own seeded roll, the script answers through the same validated `sol.set_loot{...}`, and a scriptless default composes the victim's fitted weapon at salvage odds, part of its cargo, and scrap as ore. `rock_mined(commodity, units)` fires for flavor. Console: `sol.fields`, `sol.rocks`, `sol.wrecks`, `sol.mine` (dev: empty the targeted rock), `sol.refine`, `sol.refine_jobs`, `sol.collect`.

**Save v8.** Body gains the mining block: sparse rock depletion, the wreck store, and outstanding refine jobs. Layout re-derives from galaxy + defs and load fails on a count mismatch — the rule every sibling sim follows — and the version bump follows the v4–v7 policy (old saves rejected cleanly, not migrated).

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** No rare-mineral tier beyond the commodity set, no player-owned refineries or station building, no mining drones or turrets, no asteroid respawn, no wreck-field mission objectives, no locational damage deciding what a wreck holds, and no tractor beam as a weapon. NPC miners as economy agents are the natural follow-up — mining outposts still produce ore from nothing — but they are not in v1.

**Tuning and fixes found by driving it.** Three numbers were wrong in the spec and only flying it showed why. Fields were authored at a 20–60 km radius, which scattered forty rocks nine kilometres apart — a "field" you could sit inside and see nothing; **2–6 km** puts several rocks in front of you the moment you arrive. The collector rig reached 750 m against a beam that reaches 800, so ore was vacuumed the instant it broke off and the gathering step the user picked never happened; base `collector_range` dropped to **120 m** (rig ×2.5) so an unfitted ship has to fly in and scoop. And chunks left the rock in a *random* direction, which meant most of what you cut simply drifted away — a 3 s burst put 0.8 of 11 units in the hold. They now leave **toward whoever is cutting** with a spread (`kChunkSpread`), and the same burst lands all 10.8. One real bug came out of the first launch: the HUD's prospect readout calls `Registry::storage<T>() const`, which asserts the pool exists, so a system with no rock, no wreck, and no loose ore crashed on boot — the mining pools are now created up front (`ensureMiningPools`, also after a snapshot load). Two dev levers were added for pacing that are useful in their own right: `sol.target(namePart)` selects any nav target by name, and `sol.warp_rock()` parks the ship off the nearest rock with the nose on it.

**Known gaps**: rocks do not regenerate, so a picked-over field stays picked over — the same shape as 8e's static signals, and it wants the same fix (slow respawn on the coarse layer). Refining pays its advertised premium against *base* prices, but a refinery's own dock is exactly where ore is scarcest and dearest, so selling ore there can match refining it — the margin is real when the metal is hauled to a factory system, which is the intended lesson but is not signposted anywhere in the UI. Rocks join the per-tick collision pass as static bodies, and `resolveCollisions` is O(n²) over all bodies; ~28 rocks measured fine (300–490 fps), but a maximum-size system (3 fields × 48) has not been measured and this is the first content that scales that pass. Wrecks reuse the ship mesh at 1.4× rather than having a broken-hull model of their own, and the wreck's own weapon is never what you recover — the scriptless default rolls a module off the whole catalog instead.

**Exit**: fly to a field in a fringe system, target a rock and read its composition off the target readout, hold the mining laser and watch chunks break off, drift, and get collected until the hold is full; jump away, come back, and find that rock still depleted — and the same after a full save/reload; kill a fighter, cut its wreck open for its fit and cargo, jump two systems away, reload, and find the wreck where it fell with the same contents; dock at a refinery, start a job, fly a round trip, collect the metal, and sell it for meaningfully more than the ore would have paid, with the ore → metal → machinery chain visible in station prices; the same seed produces identical fields, rocks, and yields twice; a fully instantiated field holds frame time inside the Phase 6 dogfight budget; `MiningSim` is unit-tested like its siblings (generation determinism, depletion accounting, wreck decay, refine-job timing, tick-for-tick save/load); all suites green, zero warnings, validation clean. *(Verified live 2026-08-16, seed 1701, across nine scripted sessions. **The field**: two gate hops out of the core reached **Queis**, whose Asteroid Field 1 joined the nav list on arrival and drew the autopilot in over a 489,000 km cruise leg — arriving among a couple of dozen rocks, tumbling, each 45–230 m across, at 300–490 fps with 39–63 entities live. `sol.rocks(1)` listed them per rock ("23: sol.ore 61.1/61.1, r 105 m, 6 km") and the field as "2224 units". **The beam**: with a Mining Laser mounted, holding the trigger on a rock 479 m off cut it down through the HUD's prospect readout — "Raw Ore / 49 of 50 units left" — at exactly the tuned rate (a 3 s burst took 10.8 units against `mining_power` 4.0/s), each bite breaking off as drifting ore that the collector gathered and the ticker reported ("+10.8 Raw Ore"); the hold read back **"HOLD 10.799998283386 ore"**. **Persistence**: the field stood at 2213 units, a jump out to Lyrioa and back read **"AFTER JUMP 2213 -> 2213"**, and a cold reload of a save taken at the field restored the same depletion. **Wrecks**: `sol.wrecks()` read "(no wrecks)", a spawned Shuttle killed with the beam became "#1 Shuttle in Queis: 24 units, 255 cr, 3595 s left" with the prospect readout flipping to "Shuttle / 24 units aboard"; cutting the hull drew its cargo out as chunks (24 → 9 units aboard, ore into the hold) and F9/F10 brought it back as "AFTER RELOAD #1 Shuttle in Queis: 22 units, 255 cr, 3590 s left". **Refining**: a walk of stations refused the service with "refine: no refinery here" until Lyrum Gamma took the order — "refining 6 units for 12 cr; ready in 72 s" — the job ran on the world clock while docked, `sol.refine_jobs()` read "market 81: 5 sol.ore -> 3 sol.metal, ready", and `sol.collect()` handed over the metal ("collected 3 units"; hold metal 2.75 from 5 ore at the 0.55 ratio). The **Refinery tab** showed the same facts on the station screen (rate, ore aboard, "68 s", 1/10/100 order buttons, a disabled Collect), and the **system map** carried both new marker kinds — "Asteroid Field 1 … 2224 units" and "Wreck: Shuttle … units aboard". The ore → metal → machinery chain is visible in prices (Raw Ore 15.00, Refined Metal 40–47, Machinery 54.83–79.32 across the systems visited). Quitting through the pause menu exited **0 — no validation messages**. 7 new sim tests (86), 1 new assets test, 1 new loadout test (33); 7 suites green, zero warnings.)*

#### Phase 8g — Economy Coherence: Production Chains, NPC Miners & Market Intel ✅ (completed 2026-08-16)

Closes three deferrals that turn out to be one defect seen from three sides: the 8c finding that an hour-old economy runs ore and food dry near the core, the 8f deferral that mining outposts still produce ore from nothing, and the Phase 7 caveat (renewed as an 8e deferral) that remote prices are invisible so scouting is manual. Stated together: **the chain does not actually run, supply cannot reach demand, and the player cannot see either fact.** Fixing one without the others just moves the incoherence somewhere less visible, which is why they are one item.

Scope decided with the user: **economy tuning + NPC miners + remote market intel**, taken as a single coherence pass over the Phase 7 economy.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).** The spec is written against four concrete findings rather than an impression, because "the economy feels off" is not something you can verify an exit criterion against:

- **Production is unconditional.** `Economy::step` moves each commodity by `(production − consumption) · dt`, clamped to `[0, capacity]`, with no reference to whether the inputs were there. A refinery produces 1.4 metal/s holding zero ore; a factory produces 1.5 machinery/s holding zero metal. 8f deepened the *story* into a chain but the sim never became one. A mining outpost inventing ore is not a special case — it is the first link of this.
- **Demand outruns the trader fleet by roughly an order of magnitude.** One Fabricator Yard consumes metal at 1.6/s ≈ 96 units/min. One trader moves `traderCargo` 50 units per round trip of `traderLegSeconds·2 + hops·jumpSeconds` ≈ 180–240 s ⇒ ~0.25 units/s delivered. Feeding a *single* factory takes ~6 dedicated traders; `traderCount` is 40 for a galaxy of ~158 stations. Consumers therefore drain to zero and stay there. That is the 8c finding restated as arithmetic.
- **The galaxy-wide flows do not balance.** Weighting the `stations.toml` rates by placement weight: food ≈ +9.0 vs −8.9 (balanced), ore +10.0 vs −7.2 (surplus), metal +4.2 vs −5.3 (**structural deficit**), machinery +4.95 vs −1.5 (large surplus). Machinery pins at capacity galaxy-wide while metal can never be satisfied even with perfect logistics.
- **There is no bid/ask spread**, and `buy`/`sell` price the whole batch at the pre-trade marginal price — so a block trade clears at a price that only moves afterward, and buying a station's entire stock and selling it straight back is exactly break-even rather than a loss.

**Input-gated production (`Economy`, the chain becomes a chain).** A station's inputs split into two kinds, which is what keeps gating from deadlocking a cyclic production graph:
- **Feedstock** — the thing the station transforms. Production is throttled by it: the *satisfaction ratio* is the minimum over feedstock commodities of `min(1, available / desired)` for the tick, and **both** actual production and actual feedstock consumption scale by that ratio. A refinery with no ore consumes no ore and produces no metal; a half-fed factory runs at half rate.
- **Upkeep** — what the station's people and machines burn regardless. Consumed at full rate, clamped at zero, and it does **not** gate production. A hungry station is a hook for a later unrest/decline mechanic, not a production stall today.

`[[station]]` gains `feedstock = ["sol.ore:2.4"]` beside the existing `consumes` (now unambiguously upkeep); an archetype with no feedstock always runs at full rate. This split is load-bearing: today's graph is a *cycle* — food → ore → metal → machinery → food — and gating on every input would let any single empty link seize the whole galaxy permanently. Under the split, Agricultural Stations (feedstock: none) and Mining Outposts (feedstock: rock, see below) are unconditional sources and the chain flows outward from them. The payoff is the mechanism GDD §6 has always claimed and never had: a raid on an ore system now starves a refinery two jumps out, then a factory beyond it, and machinery prices rise in a fourth system that was never touched.

**NPC miners (the outpost's feedstock is real rock).** Mining Outposts stop conjuring ore. `Economy::tick` gains an optional **feedstock source** — an abstract `struct FeedstockSource { virtual float draw(std::uint32_t market, std::uint32_t commodity, float units) = 0; }` — from which a market may draw feedstock that no other station supplies. A null source (the default) leaves production ungated, which keeps every existing `Economy` unit test valid and, more importantly, keeps `sim::Economy` free of any dependency on `sim::MiningSim`; the two stay siblings and the coupling lives in the one implementation the game installs. That implementation depletes the asteroid fields in the outpost's **own system** through the same `MiningSim::mineRock` path the player's beam uses.

Two consequences are the point of the design rather than side effects. A mining outpost in a system with **no** fields produces nothing at all and its ore price pins at 2× — the galaxy finally answers "where does ore actually come from", and the answer is the fringe, where `MiningParams` puts 1–3 fields of 30–48 rocks at a ×1.8 yield multiplier against the core's 0–1. And the player mining a field is now **competing with the local outpost for the same rock**, which is the first time the sandbox and the economy have touched the same finite resource.

**Field regeneration (required here, not deferred again).** A field measured live in 8f holds ~2,200 units; an outpost drawing at its current 2.5 ore/s would strip one in ~15 minutes and the galaxy inside an hour. So NPC draw runs at its own much slower rate (`npcMineRate`, order 0.05–0.15 units/s per outpost) **and** rocks regrow: `MiningSim::tick` decays depletion records back toward zero at `rockRegenPerSecond` on the coarse clock, dropping a record entirely once it reaches zero so the sparse store stays sparse. This closes the 8f known gap ("a picked-over field stays picked over") with exactly the fix that gap named, and it is not optional here — without it NPC mining is a countdown to a dead galaxy.

**Throughput and price mechanics (`Economy`).** The order-of-magnitude gap gets closed from both ends and measured, not guessed:
- `traderCount` rises (order 40 → 200) and `traderCargo` with it (order 50 → 120); consumption rates come down so one station's demand is within a few traders' reach; the metal deficit and the machinery surplus are corrected in `stations.toml` so the steady state is reachable at all.
- `traderThink` is O(markets × commodities) per idle trader per tick and calls `price()` — which recomputes the capacity lookup — inside the inner loop. Before raising the fleet size, `step` precomputes a flat per-market price vector once per tick (markets × commodities ≈ 632 floats) and `traderThink` reads that. This is a straight win independently of the tuning, and it is what makes 200 traders affordable.
- `marketFor` is a linear scan over every market, called from HUD and UI paths every frame; it gets a precomputed `system → first market index` offset table.
- A **bid/ask spread** (order 5%) separates `buyPrice` and `sellPrice` around the existing curve, for agents and player alike. Without it, thin margins are indistinguishable from good ones and a round trip at one station is free; with it, a route has to actually be worth flying. This changes the Phase 7 exit run's exact numbers (its criterion — buy low, jump, sell high — still holds) and the expected values in existing economy tests, which is called out here so the churn is not a surprise.

Raising the fleet size spends time in the galaxy tick, and performance hardening is a **separate unpicked Phase 8 item** whose budget this must not quietly consume: the coarse tick cost is measured before and after, and the result is recorded in the verification note.

**Market intel (the player can see it).** The counterpart to 8e's survey ledger: there the player *sells* what they found, here they *buy* what someone else found.
- **Market memory** — docking at a station records that market's full price list with the sim time it was taken. Saved. It lives in `sim::SurveySim` as a second ledger beside the knowledge ladder, not in `Economy`, because `Economy` currently holds **zero** player state (`buy`/`sell` are pure market operations) and that property is worth keeping; `SurveySim` is already the home of "what the player knows about the galaxy".
- **Intel is bought, not free** — a station's Trade tab sells a **market data** package: for a fee, the current price lists of markets within a jump radius, written into memory as fresh entries. A credit sink, and the reason to come back.
- **Memory goes stale** — entries carry their timestamp; the UI shows age ("2m ago", "41m ago") and greys entries past a staleness threshold. Prices have moved since you looked, and saying so honestly is what keeps bought intel worth re-buying instead of being a one-time unlock.
- **Surfacing** — the **galaxy map** (8e) gains a Trade overlay: pick a commodity and every system with remembered data colors by its best known price, age shown, route plotting already there. The **station Trade tab** gains a "best known price elsewhere" column per commodity. That column is the smallest change that closes the 8f known gap "*the margin is real when the metal is hauled to a factory system, which is the intended lesson but is not signposted anywhere in the UI*" — standing on a refinery pad you will see metal worth more three jumps away, and the round trip stops being folklore.

**Save v9.** Body gains market memory (per remembered market: price vector + timestamp). Field regen needs no new state — the existing sparse depletion values simply shrink. Feedstock gating is stateless. The trader array changes size, so old saves are rejected cleanly rather than migrated, per the v4–v8 policy.

**UI & console.** Console: `sol.market_memory`, `sol.buy_intel`, `sol.best_price(commodity)`, `sol.feedstock` (a station's current satisfaction ratio and what is limiting it), `sol.field_stock(system)` (NPC draw and regen against a system's fields), and `sol.economy_report` — galaxy-wide per-commodity production/consumption/stock totals. That report is the diagnostic that made the four findings above measurable, and it stays in as a live tool because the exit criteria are stated against it.

**Known gaps.** The steady state is verified at seed 1701 and against the shipped station weights; a very differently shaped galaxy could land outside the band, and nothing re-checks the balance per seed at runtime. The economy tuning test runs a whole galaxy for four sim hours in a debug build and costs about a minute, which is now most of the sim suite's runtime. The trader fleet is still uniform — every hauler ranks routes the same way, so they do herd somewhat, and the inbound-cargo reservation is what keeps that survivable rather than any diversity between agents (commodity specialisation stays out of scope). Market intel is bought per station visit with no subscription or standing arrangement, `sol.field_stock` needs an explicit system index because the binding has no default argument, and the intel radius (3 jumps) is a constant in `space_world.cpp` rather than data. The coarse tick cost was **not** separately profiled before and after the fleet change as the spec asked: the fleet ended up *smaller* than the number the spec proposed and the measured frame rate stayed at 340–550 fps throughout, so the question the measurement was meant to answer went away, but it is not a measurement that was taken.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** Player-owned stations, mining claims, or supply contracts. NPC miners as *visible* ships in the bubble — promoting coarse agents to bubble ships remains the untouched Phase 7 caveat, and it covers traders and miners alike. Commodity specialization or personality for trader agents. Smuggling, contraband, tariffs, or a black market. Reputation price modifiers (still deferred from 8b). Any stock market, futures, or speculation layer. Rare-mineral tiers beyond the current commodity set (unchanged from 8f).

**What driving it changed about the plan.** The exit criterion as written — "an hour of unattended sim" checked by hand — is unrunnable (an hour of wall clock per tuning iteration) and too weak besides, because every arrangement of these numbers looks healthy for the first hour and the broken ones only show it over the next several. It became a **sim test** instead: a real generated galaxy at seed 1701, the rates `game/data` actually ships, four hours of sim in a few seconds, asserting both the stock band and that the total quantity of goods holds steady. Every number in this item came out of that test, and most of them contradicted what the spec assumed.

**Three bugs it found that the shipped game already had.** Traders **jettisoned** any cargo the destination could not absorb — the old comment called that "cheap and rare", but at any real fleet size haulers converge on the same starved market, the first few fill it, and the rest destroy their entire hold on arrival; it was a galaxy-wide leak that drained every market given a few hours. A station at capacity went on **consuming feedstock**, and a mining outpost went on tearing rock out of the ground, to make output the clamp then threw away. And hoisting prices into a per-tick snapshot — a performance fix from this same item — removed the feedback that stopped the whole fleet piling onto one route together, which had been harmless at 40 traders and was not at 260.

**Three things about the balance that were simply not what the spec expected.** **Warehouse size, not fleet size, was the binding constraint**: a buffer smaller than the gap between hauler visits leaves consumers dry and producers backed up, which throttles production below consumption and ratchets the whole chain down; `stock_capacity` 1000 → 2500 is what finally held it. **The fleet has an upper bound as well as a lower one** — at 260×200 the haulers permanently held a fifth of every good in the galaxy in their holds, because a surplus trader does not idle, it goes on buying things nobody needs; 120×150 moves about twice what the galaxy consumes and ties up under a tenth of it. And ranking hauls by **absolute profit means nobody ever carries anything cheap**, however badly it is needed, since a hold of machinery ties up ten times the capital of a hold of ore — ranking by *return on capital* is both what a firm with finite working capital does and what finally got ore moving. Two further changes followed: production runs about a quarter ahead of consumption throughout (an economy balanced to the last decimal cannot recover from one late delivery), and output **tapers** across the top of the warehouse rather than stopping dead at full, which is what removes the ratchet entirely. Refineries moved out of the core to sit near the rock, because hauling bulk ore to the customers was never going to work.

**Exit**: `sol.economy_report` shows every commodity holding inside a 15–85% stock band galaxy-wide with nothing pinned at zero or capacity, and the total quantity of goods flat rather than ratcheting — the 8c finding closed against a measurement rather than an impression; a Mining Outpost's ore output tracks its own system's fields, and a system with none supports no mine at all; a field the player mined out recovers and its depletion record is dropped once whole; buying market data and opening the galaxy map's Trade overlay for a commodity yields a readable route; standing at a station the Trade tab shows each good's best known price elsewhere with its age; save/reload restores market memory and its timestamps exactly; the same seed still produces an identical economy twice; `Economy`, `MiningSim`, and `SurveySim` are unit-tested on gating, feedstock draw, regen, the spread, intel staleness, and the shipped rates' steady state; all suites green, zero warnings. *(Verified live 2026-08-16, seed 1701. **The report**: at boot `sol.economy_report` read every commodity 50.0% full with production and consumption exactly as designed — food 5.72/4.46, ore 9.24/7.31, metal 4.73/3.90, machinery 3.12/2.42, the intended ~25% surplus throughout — and after 251 s of unattended running all four sat at 45.6–45.7% with **0 empty, 0 full** and one station feedstock-throttled, across 124 markets. Prices verify against the curve to the decimal: at 1249/2500 stock the 1.25× scale put food at 10.00, ore 15.00, metal 40.00, machinery 65.00. **NPC mining**: `sol.field_stock(-1)` in the core system Lyrioa read "(no rock here) — Lyrioa: 0 field(s), **159 rock(s) being worked**", which is the whole design in one line: outposts draw from real asteroid fields, and a system with none supports nothing. `sol.feedstock()` read "Lyrioa Alpha: running at 100% of nominal". **Market intel**: docking recorded the local market for free (`sol.market_memory()` → "Lyrioa (market 0), 5 s ago: sol.food 10.00, sol.ore 15.00…"); `sol.buy_intel()` bought "market data on 35 markets within 3 jumps for 750 cr"; `sol.best_price("sol.metal")` tracked real divergence over the session, 40.05 → 56.70 at Galubyx. The **Trade tab's "Best elsewhere" column** showed, from the pad at Lyrioa Alpha, metal at 40.65 locally against 56.70 at Galubyx and machinery 64.16 against 91.28, both green — which is exactly the 8f known gap ("the margin is real when the metal is hauled to a factory system … not signposted anywhere in the UI") closed. The **galaxy map's trade overlay** coloured the lane graph by remembered price — Lyrioa 40.01, Saria 40.60, Queis 40.60 green against Galubyx 56.70 and Lyrum 54.26 red — with the footer legend "36 markets known: 39.56 - 56.70 cr/unit". F9/F10 returned "MEMORY SURVIVED: true" and the reloaded `best_price` kept ageing (13 s). 8 new sim tests (94 total), all 7 suites green, zero warnings. One UI bug found by looking at it: five overlay choices across the map's narrow list column drew their labels on top of each other, so the picker became a single cycling "Colour: …" button.)*

#### Phase 8h — Situational Awareness & Navigation ✅ (completed 2026-08-17)

The first item on this roadmap whose scope came from **playing the game rather than reading it**. On 2026-08-17 the user flew a session and reported five things, and they are one complaint seen from five sides: **the player cannot see what is around them, cannot separate where-I-am-going from what-is-shooting-me, cannot look closer at the map, cannot write down a place worth returning to, and cannot read their own ship without docking.** Every one of these is an *awareness* gap rather than a missing mechanic — the simulation already knows all of it and simply never says so.

Scope decided with the user: **contact radar + target-class split + system-map zoom/pan + bookmarks + ship information screen**, taken as one item because all five are the same seam (`screens.hpp` fill → `*_screen.cpp` draw → action back) and because bookmarks have to surface on both the radar and the map — split across two items, that surface gets built twice.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **The HUD carries exactly one contact.** `FlightHud` (`sol/ui/screens.hpp:17`) has `targetName`/`targetDirectionCamera`/`targetDistanceMeters` and a boresight prospect. Stations, planets, gates, signals, fields, wrecks and every live ship are invisible unless one of them happens to be the current target. `SpaceWorld` holds all of it already in `m_targets` and `m_spawnedShips`; nothing surfaces it.
- **One key walks two lists that are already separate.** `cycleTarget()` (`space_world.cpp:2730`) is one modulo over `m_targets.size() + m_spawnedShips.size()`. In a system with a station, five planets, a star, three signals, two fields and a wreck, reaching the fighter shooting at you takes thirteen presses. The two classes are already distinct in the data model — the conflation is entirely in that one function and the one `T` binding, which is why this is the cheapest of the five.
- **The map projection is fixed.** `fitGalaxyMap` fits every visible system; the system view's two tiers take their extent from the marker set (`orbitExtent`, `playfieldSpan`). Nothing lets the player change either. `MapProjection` already carries `origin`/`scale`/`center`, and `InputState::scrollDelta` is already fed from `Window::wheelDelta()` — so the inputs exist and only the state and the control are missing. The crowding the playtest hit is specifically in the **playfield tier**, where a station, gates, signals and fields share one bubble.
- **Nothing records a position.** Nav targets are generated (`rebuildDynamicTargets` appends in discovery order) and the route plotter holds a *system* path. "The good rock in the third field" cannot be written down anywhere.
- **Ship state is station-side.** Hull and shield fractions reach the HUD, but the fitted module list, the aggregated `FitStat` totals, and the cargo manifest are readable only from Outfitting, while docked — which is precisely when they cannot be checked against what the ship is doing.

**A. Contact radar (top-down disc, log range).** `FlightHud` gains `std::span<const RadarContact> radarContacts`, keeping `buildFlightUi` pure geometry over a filled struct. A contact carries its offset in **ship-local** space, a kind (the `NavKind` vocabulary plus `Ship`), an attitude tag (already computed for `targetFaction`/`targetAttitude` in 8b), and flags for *is the current target* and *is a bookmark*.

The projection goes in a new `sol/ui/radar_projection.hpp` — its own header rather than an add-on to `map_projection.hpp`, because the disc is not a map and because that split is what makes it headlessly testable, the same arrangement that gave `map_projection_tests.cpp`. Bearing is exact and taken about the ship's forward axis, so **the disc rotates with the ship** and a contact's dot answers "which way do I turn". Radius runs on a log curve with its own decade constant: the playfield spans a fighter at 800 m to a star at 1e11 m, and a linear disc shows either combat or the system, never both. The outer ring is labelled with the distance it stands for and anything past it clamps to the rim **drawn hollow**, so "far away" is never confused with "at the edge of the disc". A vertical stalk from the disc plane to each dot, signed by local Y, carries above/below — the one piece of a flat disc that has to be right or the whole thing lies about a 3D space.

Contacts are capped (order 32, nearest first) and **individual rocks are not contacts** — a field of 48 is one marker, consistent with 8f's ruling that rock is read off the boresight rather than through the target cycle. Placement is decided by looking at the running HUD, against the constraint that it must not overlap the flight readout, the pips, the mission line or the contextual prompts; the dev overlay's flight strip (client y ≳ 653) is a screenshot artifact and not a layout constraint.

**B. Target-class split.** `cycleTarget()` becomes `cycleNavTarget()` (wraps within `[0, m_targets.size())`) and `cycleContact()` (wraps within the ship range). Each class remembers its own slot, so switching classes does not lose the other selection — but there remains exactly **one** current target, because weapons lead, autopilot, the HUD readout and the map's Set Target all read `m_targetIndex`, and forking that into two simultaneous selections is a much larger change than the complaint asks for (named out of scope below). Switching class re-points the single selection at that class's remembered slot.

`cycleContact()` orders by **threat, then distance** — hostiles nearest-first, then everything else — so the first press selects the thing shooting at you, which is the actual complaint. It is called *contacts* and not *hostiles* because with nothing hostile present it still cycles neutrals, and a key that silently does nothing is worse than one that is honestly named. `T` keeps nav/POI: it preserves the existing habit, the docs, and every drive script. Contacts go on **`C`** (free, mnemonic; `B` and `I` below are likewise free — the bound set today is A D E F G J M Q R S T V W X, arrows, Tab, Space, LeftControl, LeftShift, Num1–4, Escape, F3/F5/F9/F10).

**C. Map zoom and pan.** Applied as a **screen-space transform after the tier projections**, not as a change to either curve: one `Vec2 pan` + `float zoom` (clamped 1×–8×) over the drawn panel, wheel zooming about the cursor so magnification goes where you point, LMB-drag to pan, pan clamped so the disc cannot be dragged out of the panel, and a `Reset view` footer button. The screen-space choice is deliberate — it magnifies exactly what the player is looking at, including the crowded playfield bubble, and it leaves the two-tier design and its extent maths untouched.

The consequence that makes it worth more than magnification: **label text stays at fixed size and `LabelPlacer` re-runs at the transformed positions**, so zooming in spreads the markers apart and labels the placer had been suppressing come back. Zooming does not just enlarge the crowded region, it un-crowds it — which is the actual playtest complaint ("things get a bit tight when concentrated around the planets"). Keyboard zoom is left out: arrows are already contended on the map screen (they step tabs when a tab is focused, and move widget focus otherwise).

**D. Bookmarks — and the text-field widget they need.** Two parts, and the first is engine plumbing this item cannot route around.

*Part one, the text field.* `Window` gains `[[nodiscard]] std::string_view textInput() const`: UTF-8 characters accumulated from `WM_CHAR` since the last `pumpEvents` and cleared each pump — the same lifetime contract `mouseDelta()` and `wheelDelta()` already set. `Key` gains `Backspace`, `Delete`, `Home`, `End` (Left/Right exist). **The risk to watch is the dev UI**: ImGui already consumes `WM_CHAR` through `setMessageHook`, whose contract is *return true to consume*, so accumulation must respect what the hook ate or the F1 console and a focused text field will both receive every keystroke. `UiContext` then gains `textField(const Rect&, std::string& value, std::size_t maxLength)` → true on change: caret with blink, insertion at the caret, Backspace/Delete, Left/Right/Home/End, click-to-place-caret, horizontal scroll once the text outruns the box, a focus ring matching the other widgets, and **it swallows nav keys while focused** — otherwise typing "s" also steps a list. Caret arithmetic and clipping are exactly the kind of thing that is wrong the first time, so both are asserted in the ui suite rather than eyeballed.

*Part two, the feature.* The ledger lives in `sim::SurveySim`, beside the knowledge ladder and 8g's market memory, on 8g's own precedent that `SurveySim` is the home of what the player knows about the galaxy — which keeps `SpaceWorld` the flying-and-drawing layer and reuses a save path that already exists. A bookmark is `{ system, DVec3 position, name, label, createdWorldSeconds }`. Dropping one is **`B`** in flight: a small overlay opens with the new text field **prefilled with a context-derived default** ("4.1 Mm from Ceres", "Ore Field 2"), Enter accepts, Esc cancels. Auto-naming therefore still carries the common case and typing is the override, which is the better half of both options considered.

Surfacing, all of it reusing machinery that exists: bookmarks appear as dots on the radar; as markers in the system map's playfield tier with their own glyph; in a `Bookmarks` list on the map screen with Set Target and Delete; and as nav targets in the `T` cycle for the current system. That last one carries an invariant that must be honoured rather than discovered — `rebuildDynamicTargets` is **append-only in discovery order** precisely because a live target index and an in-flight scan point into that tail, so `NavKind::Bookmark` joins the same slot discipline and compacts only on delete. Because a bookmark is an ordinary nav target, **Autopilot flies back to it for free**. A bookmark in another system shows on the galaxy map and plots a route there; arriving makes it local. No auto-jump, per 8e's standing rule. Capped per system (order 16) so neither the nav cycle nor the disc drowns.

**E. Ship information screen.** `GameState::ShipInfo` on **`I`**, reachable from flight *and* docked — checking the ship while its numbers are doing something is the entire point — with `game/src/ship_screen.cpp` drawing a new `ShipInfoPanel` and `ship_ui.cpp` filling it, matching the seam six screens already use, and following the map screen's convention on whether the world keeps running underneath. Content is all already computable: hull class and integrity, shield arcs and recharge, the fitted module list per slot, the aggregated `FitStat` totals (thrust, mass, turn rates, cargo capacity, scan range, collector range, mining power, weapon DPS), the cargo manifest with units and value against capacity, credits, and what the current pip allocation is doing to those numbers. **Read-only** — refitting stays a station activity, which is a design statement rather than a shortfall. Incidentally it is the first place the outfitting numbers can be read *while their consequences are happening*.

**Save v10.** Body gains the bookmark ledger (system, position, name, label, timestamp per entry). Old saves are rejected cleanly rather than migrated, per the v4–v9 policy.

**Console.** `sol.radar` (the contact list the HUD is being handed, nearest-first with bearings), `sol.contacts` (the ship cycle in threat order), `sol.bookmark(name)` / `sol.bookmarks()` / `sol.bookmark_delete(i)` / `sol.warp_bookmark(i)` (dev teleport, the shape 8f's `sol.warp_rock` established), and `sol.ship_info` (the panel's numbers as text — which is how the screen gets verified without reading pixels).

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** Rebindable keys: `C`, `B` and `I` are hardcoded like every other binding today, and a settings-driven binding table is its own item. The radar as a **fittable module** with range as a `FitStat` — the scan-range precedent makes it tempting, but gating basic orientation behind a purchase is hostile; the disc is free and always on, and a range-extending module can come later. Simultaneous separate nav and combat targets. Bookmark folders, sharing, or expiry (EVE's mechanics). Text field: no clipboard, no IME, no selection ranges, no multi-line — insertion and deletion only. Galaxy-map zoom does **not** re-lay-out the lane graph, it magnifies it. Promoting coarse traders to bubble ships (still the untouched Phase 7 caveat) — so the radar shows what is really there, and what is really there is still not NPC haulers.

**Exit**: in a system holding a station, planets, a gate, a field and two spawned ships, the radar disc shows each at its true bearing with the stalk sign matching above/below, contacts past the ring clamped hollow to the rim, and every dot mirroring when the ship turns 180° — the projection asserted headlessly in the ui suite, the drawing verified by screenshot; `T` reaches every nav point in that system and never a ship, `C` reaches every ship and never a nav point with the first press landing on the nearest hostile, each class remembers its slot across switches, and weapons, autopilot and the HUD all follow the single selection; the system map wheel-zooms about the cursor through 1×–8× with drag-pan, fixed-size labels, **more** labels visible zoomed in than out, `Reset view` returning to fit, and nothing drawn outside the panel; `B` opens a naming field prefilled from context, typing replaces it, and the bookmark then appears on radar, system map, `T` cycle and the map's Bookmarks list, with Autopilot flying back to it and a cross-system bookmark plotting a route; deleting a bookmark compacts the tail without disturbing a live target or an in-flight scan; save/reload restores bookmark names, positions and timestamps exactly; the ui suite covers caret movement, insertion at the caret, backspace at both ends, clipping past the box width, and a focused field swallowing nav keys; `I` in flight and docked shows the fit, the aggregated stats agreeing with `sol.ship_info`, and the cargo manifest, with the numbers moving when a pip allocation or a module changes; all suites green, zero warnings, and the frame rate recorded with the radar live.

*(Verified live 2026-08-17, seed 1701. **Target split**: in Lyrioa with five contacts, four `T` presses gave `Lyrioa Beta`, `Lyrioa Gamma`, `Gate: Saria`, `Gate: Queis` — no ships — and four `C` presses gave `Interceptor`, `Freighter`, `Interceptor (Solar Navy)`, `Interceptor (Solar Navy)` — no nav points; a fifth `T` returned to `Gate: Queis`, the slot the nav cycle had left off at. `sol.contacts` printed the threat order with the **unaffiliated freighter at 2 km ahead of three neutral Navy interceptors at 1 km**, which is tier beating distance as designed. **Radar**: disc drawn bottom-centre with a labelled 1.00 Mkm rim; station, gates, planets, ships and bookmarks each at their own colour, altitude stalks in both directions, out-of-range contacts hollow on the rim, and the whole pattern rotating with the ship (every lower-half contact swapped to the upper half over a turn). 7 headless projection tests. **Map zoom**: wheel-zoom verified *numerically* — the star at client (789,386) landed at (837,355) against a predicted (832,365) for 2.49× about the cursor at (760,400) — drag panned by exactly the drag delta (−145,−81 for −140,−80), `Reset View` returned pixel-identical to 1× and re-greyed itself, and each tab kept its own framing. The label consequence is real: at 1× six of the playfield bubble's labels were stacked with leader lines, at 2.5× all of them sat cleanly beside their own dots. **Bookmarks**: `B` opened the prompt prefilled `At Lyrioa Alpha` with the caret at the end; typing produced exactly `Rich Rock`; a second accepted from its suggestion unchanged; `sol.bookmarks` listed both, F9/F10 returned `BOOKMARKS SURVIVED: true`, and they drew as gold diamonds on the system map, gold dots on the radar, and `* `-prefixed rows in the `T` cycle. Autopilot to one **closed from 71 km to 1 km**. The slot invariant was checked the way it can actually fail: with the selection parked on `Far Mark`, deleting the *earlier* bookmark left `selection held: true (* Far Mark)` and the survivor kept id 2 rather than being renumbered. A bookmark in Lyrioa showed on the galaxy map as a gold diamond over that node while the ship was in Saria, whose footer read `1 bookmark(s)`. **Ship screen**: `I` from flight and from a pad; the console `sol.ship_info` agreed with the panel line for line, and the pip-derived numbers moved with the allocation — capacitor `x0.90 from WEP pips` at two pips against `x1.25` at three. 3 new map-view tests, 5 text-field tests, 7 radar tests, 2 bookmark sim tests: ui 49→64, sim 94→97, all 7 suites green, zero warnings. **Frame rate 140–480 fps** across every drive with the radar live, so the disc costs nothing measurable at 32 contacts.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** The spec named the WM_CHAR/ImGui interaction as the risk in the text field and it turned out to be handled already — the dev-UI hook runs ahead of the platform switch and `WM_CHAR` falls inside `WM_KEYFIRST..WM_KEYLAST`, so the existing "swallow keyboard while ImGui wants it" rule covered it without a new one. The real bugs were elsewhere, and **two of them were older than this item and would have broken any text field ever added**:

- **Every typed character arrived twice.** `pumpEvents` calls `TranslateMessage`, and `WM_KEYDOWN` then fell through to `DefWindowProc`, which translated it a second time. `WM_KEYDOWN` is consumed now; `WM_SYSKEYDOWN` still falls through so Alt+F4 keeps working.
- **A key whose release ImGui swallowed stayed latched down forever**, because key state was recorded *after* the message hook. ImGui owns Tab for its own focus navigation, so **Tab stuck** — which reads as "navigate next" every single frame and quietly dragged focus out of the text field onto the Save button, which is how it was found. Whether a key is down is a physical fact and is recorded before the hook now; whether the game *acts* on it stays gated by `wantsMouseCapture`. This had been latent since the dev console existed.
- **A text field over the flight view got no keyboard at all**, because `uiHasKeys` was `!inFlight` — and typing a name flew the ship, since the input mapper reads the window directly. An open field now owns the keyboard on the same terms a menu does, and the mapper is skipped exactly as it is for a menu.
- **Bookmark auto-naming was circular**: it picked the nearest nav target including other bookmarks, so the second one came out `At * Rich Rock`, display prefix and all. Bookmarks are skipped when looking for a landmark.

Three things only looking at it could have caught. Radar blips are drawn at the end of their altitude stalk, so a rim contact with a tall stalk **escaped the disc** — contacts now plot on a disc inset by the stalk's reach, which keeps every bearing exact where clamping the blip back afterwards would have put a high far contact where a lower nearer one belongs. A 3 px circle at ten segments is a lumpy polygon rather than a ring. And on the ship screen, sizing the detail column per row let rows *without* a detail push their value out to the panel edge, so the numbers did not line up in the column the whole layout exists to produce, and `4.0 s delay` was clipped.

One thing the spec got right by accident: the log-curve **decade constant matters more than the curve**. At the 1 km the spec implied, every dogfight range piles into the innermost few percent of the disc and the radar is useless in the one situation it is most needed; at 10 m, 100 m–3 km spreads across 13–31% of the radius while a station at 1e8 m still sits at 87%.

**Known gaps.** The threat ranking's top tier — a pilot *currently attacking the player* — was never exercised live: the spawned hostiles never entered Attack state during the drives, so tiers 1 and 2 are what the ordering was verified against. Keys are hardcoded (`C`, `B`, `I`) as every binding is. The radar's range is a constant in the header rather than data, there is no range control, and the contact cap is 32 nearest-first — a busier system silently shows only the nearest 32. The bookmark prompt withholds text input while `B` is held or has just come up, which is an **empirical** guard: the character does not reliably land on the same frame as the key event that generated it, and that was not chased down further. `Bookmark::label` is saved and surfaced nowhere — always 0, since the fixed-label-list option was not taken — and `SurveySim::renameBookmark` exists with no UI calling it. Text field: no clipboard, no IME, no selection ranges, no multi-line, and `maxLength` is a byte budget rather than a character count. Map zoom is screen-space magnification, so the galaxy lane graph does not re-lay-out, and there is no keyboard zoom. The ship screen is read-only with no fit comparison, and `ShipDef` has no hull-class field, so its subtitle carries hull price and total ship value instead of a class name.

#### Phase 8i — Mission Objectives & Threat Selection ✅ (completed 2026-08-17)

The second item taken from a playtest rather than the roadmap, and the first that starts from a **defect**. On 2026-08-17 the user activated the main campaign mission, was told to reach a nav beacon 8 km out, and had no way whatsoever to find it. That is not a missing feature: the game knows exactly where the beacon is and never says. `sim::MissionObjective` has carried `core::DVec3 position` and `double radius` since Phase 8c, and they are read in exactly one place — `m_missions.notifyPosition(...)` (`space_world.cpp:3746`), which only *tests* arrival. Nothing surfaces them: not a nav target, not a map marker, not a radar contact. The HUD carries `missionObjective` as **text only**, and that text is the mission author's prose. **The authored campaign spine is therefore unplayable as it stands**, which is what makes this item the one to do next.

Alongside it, the cheapest of the three playtest notes: a key that always jumps to the **nearest hostile**. `C` walks contacts in threat-then-distance order, so its *first* press already lands there — but only from a standing start; mid-cycle it keeps walking, and the player wanted a way back. Both notes are the same shape as 8h's work (a selection and the surfaces that draw it) and share the same seam, which is why they are one item.

Scope decided with the user: **objective surfacing + a nearest-hostile key**. The third playtest note, **click-to-select in space and on the radar, is deliberately deferred** — but the input-model question it raised was settled now rather than left open: **fire moves to middle mouse** when that item is built, freeing left-click for selection. Recorded here so the decision is not re-litigated later.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **The objective's position reaches nothing that draws.** `rebuildDynamicTargets` (`space_world.cpp:548`) builds the nav tail from signals, fields, wrecks and bookmarks. There is no mission source. `NavKind` has eight members and `RadarKind`/`MapMarkerRow::Kind` mirror them, so the surfaces are all there and none of them has anything to show.
- **The HUD line names the objective but never its destination.** `main.cpp:900` prints `objective.text` plus a kill/unit tally. For a `Dock` or `Deliver` it never says *which station*, and for anything in another system it never says *which system* — so even the objectives that point at an already-targetable place are only findable by reading the mission prose and guessing.
- **The threat ranking is already computed and already thrown away.** `contactOrder` (`space_world.cpp:2870`) sorts by tier then distance every time it is called; `cycleContact` uses the ordering and discards the tiers. Nothing else can ask "is this one hostile" without recomputing it.

**A. `NavKind::Objective` — the tracked mission's destination as an ordinary nav target.** A ninth slot, appended by `rebuildDynamicTargets` from the tracked mission's *current* objective whenever that objective is a `FlyTo` **in the current system**. This is the whole fix, and it is deliberately the same move bookmarks made in 8h: once the objective is a nav target, it inherits the target cycle, the radar dot, the system-map marker, the HUD's distance readout and **Autopilot** without any of those learning what a mission is.

The slot honours `rebuildDynamicTargets`' append-only discipline, which exists because a live `m_targetIndex` and an in-flight scan both point into that tail. There is at most one objective slot, so it carries `index = 0` and its liveness test is "the tracked mission still has a current `FlyTo` here". The consequence is chosen rather than incidental: when one `FlyTo` completes and the next is also in this system, the slot survives and its **position moves to the new waypoint**, so a selection and an engaged autopilot carry over to the next leg instead of dropping. When there is no such objective, the slot dies and compacts like a decayed wreck, carrying the selection with it.

Only `FlyTo` gets a slot. `Dock` and `Deliver` name a station that is already a nav target, and inventing a second target on top of it would put two markers on one place; `Kill` has no position at all. Those cases are answered by **B** instead.

The marker reads `> <objective text>`, the way a bookmark reads `* <name>` — one glyph of prefix so the nav cycle says what a slot *is* without a legend.

**B. The HUD line says where.** The tracked-mission line gains a destination clause built from the objective's own fields: the station and system for `Dock`/`Deliver`, the system for a `FlyTo` elsewhere, and the victim faction and system for `Kill`. Cross-system objectives read `— Kepler Station, Vega` so the map's route plotter has something to aim at. This is prose assembly over data the mission already holds, and it closes the half of the complaint that a nav target cannot ("the beacon is not in this system" is not a marker problem).

**C. Surfaces.** `RadarKind::Objective` and `MapMarkerRow::Kind::Objective` join their enums with their own glyph and colour, distinct from a bookmark's gold so "somewhere I chose" and "somewhere I was sent" never read alike. `MapSystemRow` gains `bool hasObjective`, exactly the shape `bookmarkCount` took in 8h, so the **galaxy map marks the system the tracked objective is in** — which is what makes an off-system objective findable at all, and the `Plot Route` that follows is already built.

**D. `O` selects the objective; `H` selects the nearest hostile.** Both are free keys after 8h took `C`/`B`/`I`. `O` selects the objective nav slot outright rather than cycling to it — the point of the item is that the player should never have to hunt, and hunting through twenty nav slots to find the thing you were sent to is the same complaint one level down. `H` reads `contactOrder`'s existing ranking and selects `order[0]` when its tier is hostile, so it is genuinely free of new policy: the ordering that decides it is the same one `C` and the radar already use. Both report honestly when there is nothing to select rather than silently doing nothing — `sol.` log lines, matching `T`/`C`.

`contactOrder` gains a tier-out overload so "is the head hostile" is answered from the sort that already ran, rather than by a second, separately-drifting definition of hostile.

**Console.** `sol.objective` (the tracked objective: kind, destination, system, distance, and whether it currently holds a nav slot — which is how the slot logic gets verified without reading pixels) and `sol.target_hostile` (the `H` path).

**No save-format change.** Missions are already saved; the nav slot is derived state rebuilt per system, like every other dynamic target. The save stays **v10**.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** Click-to-select in space and on the radar, with fire on middle mouse — the next playtest item, decided but not built here. A slot per *active* mission rather than the tracked one (four markers for four contracts is the crowding 8h just fixed; tracking is the existing selector for "the one I care about"). Objective markers for missions in systems the player has never visited beyond the galaxy-map system flag. `Kill` objectives do not point at a victim — the sim does not place the kill, and a marker that says "somewhere in this system" is worse than the text. Rebindable keys: `O` and `H` are hardcoded like every binding today, and that item's case gets stronger with every phase.
