**Exit**: with the campaign mission active and its `FlyTo` in the current system, the objective appears as a nav target in the `T` cycle, a dot on the radar at its true bearing, and a marker on the system map; `O` selects it from any other selection and `F` then flies to it and arrives, completing the objective; when the next objective is a `FlyTo` in the same system the marker moves to it with the selection intact; when it is not, the slot disappears and neither a live selection nor an in-flight scan is disturbed; a `Dock`/`Deliver`/cross-system objective names its destination station and system on the HUD and flags its system on the galaxy map, from which `Plot Route` reaches it; `H` selects the nearest hostile from a nav target, from another ship and from the hostile itself, and says so plainly when nothing is hostile; `sol.objective` agrees with what is drawn; all suites green, zero warnings.

*(Verified live 2026-08-17, seed 1701, on the actual campaign mission the playtest got stuck on — `act1.m1` "Shakedown Cruise", whose first objective is a `FlyTo` at (0, 1500, −8000) with a 1.2 km radius. **The slot**: accepting it and undocking put `nav slot 9: > Objective, 8.1 km away, radius 1.2 km` in the tail, drawn as a magenta ring-and-dot on the radar and a magenta halo marker on the system map beside `Lyrioa Alpha`, listed as `> Objective` in the marker list with the mission's own sentence in the detail column. **`O`** logged `Objective: > Objective` from a selection two `T` presses away, and the target readout and the in-world diamond both moved to it at 8062 m. **`F` then flew it and it completed**: `Autopilot: flying to '> Objective' (arrive 0.6 km out)` → `[missions] 'Shakedown Cruise': Return and dock at the station`, after which the HUD line read **`Return and dock at the station - Lyrioa Alpha`**, `sol.objective` read `no nav slot (not a FlyTo in this system)`, and the marker was gone from the radar, the map and the list. **Cross-system**: a posted campaign offer with a `FlyTo` one gate out gave `Far Errand / Fly to the far beacon - Saria` on the HUD, `O` answered **`No objective marker here - objective is at Saria`** rather than doing nothing, and the galaxy map put the magenta ring on **Saria** — a system still `uncharted` — while Lyrioa kept its gold current-ring, footer `- MISSION OBJECTIVE`. **`H`**: with nothing hostile it said `Nothing hostile in this system`; with an unaffiliated freighter at 0 km among four neutral Solar Navy interceptors, the sequence `O` → `H` → `C` → `H` logged `Objective: > Objective` / `Nearest hostile: Freighter [unaffiliated]` / `Contact: Interceptor (Solar Navy) [neutral]` / `Nearest hostile: Freighter [unaffiliated]` — straight back from mid-cycle, which is the whole ask. **F9/F10** rebuilt the slot from the loaded mission state, as derived state should. All 7 suites green, zero warnings, **frame rate 144–521 fps** across every drive.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** Three things the spec did not see, all found by flying it rather than reading it:

- **Autopilot parked *outside* the objective.** The general arrival standoff is 1.5 km and a campaign `FlyTo` radius is 1.2 km, so "autopilot for free" delivered the ship to a stop 300 m short of the sphere it had to enter, and the objective never ticked over. Arrival range is now clamped to half the objective's radius for that one target — and since the engage message was advertising the unclamped number, both the message and the steering now read it from one `autopilotArrivalRange`, so what it announces is what it does.
- **A destination that ceases to exist made autopilot fly somewhere else.** When the `FlyTo` completed, its slot was compacted out and the selection slid onto the next slot — a neutral interceptor four kilometres away — which autopilot, still engaged, then dutifully flew to. The compaction now disengages autopilot when the slot being removed *is* the current selection. This was never objective-specific: deleting the bookmark you were flying to would have done the same since 8h.
- **Mission prose is not a target name.** The slot first carried the objective's own text, and "Fly to the calibration beacon (8 km out)" overran the map's name column into the detail beside it (`ui.selectable` has never clipped), collided with its neighbour's label on the map itself, and was truncated in the HUD readout. The name is now `> Objective` — one glyph of prefix like a bookmark's `*` — and the sentence rides in the detail, placed *before* the distance because that column is right-aligned and clips from the left, so the row still shows the distance every other row shows.

**Known gaps.** Only a `FlyTo` gets a marker: `Dock`/`Deliver` lean on the station already being a nav target and `Kill` has no position, so both are answered by the HUD line alone. Only the **tracked** mission is surfaced — three other active contracts say nothing. `MapSystemRow::hasObjective` is a bool, so two objectives in one system would be one ring (impossible today, since only one mission is tracked). `ui.selectable` still does not clip its label, so a long *bookmark* name will overrun the marker list exactly as the objective text did — worked around here, not fixed. The objective's completion radius is not drawn anywhere; the player sees a point, not the sphere. `O` and `H` are hardcoded, as every binding is. The threat ranking's top tier (*currently attacking the player*) was exercised only incidentally — a spawned unaffiliated fighter did attack and kill the player during a drive, but `H` was not re-tested against it, so the 8h gap stands. And the map's wide footer detail cell was never captured reading out the full objective line, because the objective completed mid-drive and took its row with it.

#### Phase 8j — Click-to-Select ✅ (completed 2026-08-17)

The last note standing from the playtests, and the third item in a row whose scope came from playing rather than from this roadmap. The complaint is one sentence: **the player can see the thing they want and cannot point at it.** Every selector in the game is a cycle — `T` walks nav targets, `C` walks contacts, `O` and `H` jump to one distinguished slot each — so "target that station" costs up to twenty presses in a busy system, and the radar disc that 8h added to answer *what is around me* is, by construction, a picture you cannot touch. The only non-keyboard selector that exists at all is the system map's `Set Target`, which is a different screen with the flight view paused behind it.

The input-model question this raises was settled when 8i was scoped rather than left open: **fire moves to middle mouse**, freeing left-click for selection. That is a decision, not a proposal.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **The forward projection exists twice and neither copy is reachable.** `project()` is a static function in `game_ui.cpp`'s anonymous namespace (`game_ui.cpp:180`), the focal length is derived inline at the draw site (`game_ui.cpp:786`), and the disc's geometry — `kRadarRadius`, `kRadarStalkLimit`, `kRadarPlotRadius`, and the centre computed from `screenSize` inside `drawRadar` — are all anonymous-namespace constants beside it. Nothing outside the draw code can ask *where on screen is this thing*, which is the entire question a pick has to answer.
- **The radar fill throws identity away.** `fillRadarContacts` (`main.cpp:206`) pushes nav targets then contacts, then **sorts nearest-first and truncates to 32**. `RadarContact` carries `isTarget` as a bool decided at fill time and nothing else, so after the sort there is no way back from a dot to the target index it came from. A blip is un-clickable in principle today, not merely un-clicked.
- **The seam it lands on is already built and already shared.** `SpaceWorld::selectTarget(index)` (`space_world.cpp:2956`) is exactly the operation a click performs, it already updates the per-class remembered slot so a later `T`/`C` resumes from what the player chose, and the map's `Set Target` and the console bindings already go through it. A pick is a new way to compute an index, not a new kind of selection.
- **Left mouse is free the moment fire moves.** `input.trigger` is `MouseButton::Left` (`main.cpp:719`) and the UI's own click (`main.cpp:1092`) is read on the same button — but the flight view has no widgets, so the two never contend. `MouseButton::Middle` is fully plumbed in the Win32 layer (`window_win32.cpp:205`), so the move costs one line.

**A. Fire moves to middle mouse.** `input.trigger` reads `MouseButton::Middle`. **Mining moves with it**, because 8f deliberately made mining the fire button and that ruling stands. Nothing else changes about weapons.

**B. `sol/ui/pick.hpp` — hit-test by projecting forwards, not by inverting.** The obvious reading of this item is "write the inverse of `radar_projection.hpp`", and that is the wrong shape: the radar projection **is not invertible where it matters**. Contacts past the outer ring clamp to the rim, so many distances map to one radius; the dot is drawn at the end of a stalk whose length is a *second* function of the same offset; and the disc discards `y` into that stalk rather than into position. Instead, every candidate is **projected forwards to the exact point it was drawn at** and the pick is the nearest one within a grab radius. That is exact, it costs a linear pass over at most a few dozen candidates once per click, and — the reason it is the right call rather than merely the easy one — it makes the point the player clicks the same point they were shown, *by construction*, for the disc and the flight view alike.

The new header holds the maths and nothing else, the way `map_projection.hpp` and `radar_projection.hpp` do: `focalLength(screenHeight, tanHalfFovY)`, `screenPoint(directionCamera, center, focal)` — which is `game_ui.cpp`'s `project()` **moved**, not copied, so the marker and the pick cannot drift — and `pickNearest(candidates, cursor, …)` returning the winning index or none. `radar_projection.hpp` gains `radarCenter(screenSize)` and takes ownership of `kRadarRadius`/`kRadarStalkLimit`/`kRadarPlotRadius` plus a `radarDot(offset, …)` that composes `radarPoint` with `radarStalk`, so the draw loop and the hit test read one definition of where a blip is. Headless `ui.unit` coverage: a candidate under the cursor, one just outside the grab radius, two overlapping (nearest-to-cursor wins, then nearer-in-world), one behind the camera (never picked), a rim-clamped radar contact, a stalked one, and the round trip that a contact drawn at *p* is picked at *p*.

**C. What is a candidate, and how big is it on screen.** Candidates are exactly the things `selectTarget` accepts — nav targets `[0, navTargets)` then contacts — carrying their camera-space direction, their range, and their **screen radius**, `surfaceRadius / distance × focal`. A station or a planet is a large object and the player will click its middle, not its centre point, so the grab radius for each candidate is `max(kGrabPixels, screenRadius)`; the base grab is generous (~28 virtual px) because a fighter at four kilometres is three pixels and the complaint being fixed is that precision selection is expensive. Ties go to the candidate nearest the cursor, then to the nearer one in world — so clicking a fighter silhouetted against a planet selects the fighter. Everything is in **virtual UI pixels**: the cursor is divided by `uiScale` exactly as `uiInput.mousePosition` already is, so the UI-scale setting moves the drawn marker and its hit box together.

**D. Where the click lands, and what happens while the cursor is captured.** One click routes in one place: if the cursor is inside the radar disc, it is a **radar pick**; otherwise it is a **space pick**. While the cursor is locked for mouse-look (RMB held for steering), `mousePosition()` is meaningless by contract — so a click in that state picks **at the boresight**, using the same code with the cursor at screen centre. That is not a special case bolted on, it is the same pick asked a different question, and it is the one the player wants mid-dogfight: *target what I am pointing the ship at*. A miss selects nothing and **leaves the current selection alone** — losing your target to a stray click is worse than the click doing nothing. A hit logs the same one-line `Target: <name>` that `T`/`C`/`O`/`H` already print, because a selection that changed silently is the complaint 8i fixed one level up.

**E. `SpaceWorld` learns how it is being looked at.** The pick needs the camera frame, the virtual screen size and the field of view, all of which live in `main.cpp` and none of which `SpaceWorld` has ever held. A `ViewFrame` (camera position and orientation, `uiSize`, `tanHalfFovY`) is pushed into the world once per frame beside the existing per-frame fills, and `SpaceWorld::pickTarget(Vec2 virtualPixels)` does the candidate build, the routing from **D**, and the `selectTarget` call. The click path in `main.cpp` is then two lines, and — the reason it is worth a seam at all — **the console can perform the identical pick**, so the routing and the maths are verifiable without pixel-perfect mouse work in a drive script.

**F. Surfaces.** `RadarContact` gains `std::uint32_t selection`, the target index it came from, set by the fill and carried through the sort and the truncation — which is also what lets the disc stop recomputing `isTarget` from a comparison the fill does not own. Console: `sol.pick(x, y)` (a click at virtual-screen coordinates, reporting what it hit, what kind of pick it routed to, or that it missed) and `sol.pick_boresight()` (the captured-cursor path).

**No save-format change.** Nothing here is state. The save stays **v10**.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** Drag-select, box-select, or any multi-selection — there is one selection and everything reads `m_targetIndex`. Click-to-select on the *system map*, which already has `Set Target` on a row. Hover highlighting or a mouse-over readout in the flight view (a cursor that lights things up as it passes is a different item, and 8h's lesson about drawing before you can aim applies). Individual asteroids, which 8f ruled are read off the boresight rather than the target list, so they are not candidates. Right-click context actions (dock, hail, scan). Cursor-driven flight or a "mouse flight" mode. And **rebindable keys**, again: `T`/`C`/`O`/`H`/`B`/`I` stay hardcoded and fire is now hardcoded to a *different* button, which strengthens that item's case rather than settling it.

**Exit**: left-clicking a ship in the flight view selects it, and the diamond and the HUD readout follow to it; clicking a station's body anywhere on its disc selects the station, not the fighter beside it; clicking empty space keeps the current target; left-clicking a blip on the radar selects the thing that blip stands for, including one clamped to the rim and one on a tall stalk, and the target ring on the disc lands on the blip that was clicked; while RMB steering, a left click takes whatever is at the boresight; **middle mouse fires**, left mouse no longer does, and middle mouse also cuts rock; `sol.pick` agrees with what the mouse does at the same coordinates; the pick honours a non-1.0 UI scale; all suites green, zero warnings.

*(Verified live 2026-08-17, seed 1701, across five drives. **Real clicks in space**: with an inert freighter spawned dead ahead and the selection cycled away to `Lyrioa Gamma` with `T`, a real left click at client (640,300) logged `ship click: Freighter` and the HUD readout, the target diamond and the disc's target ring all moved to it; a click at (680,270) — **40 px off the station's centre point, on the hull the player can see** — logged `station click: Lyrioa Alpha`, which is the pick radius doing exactly the job it was added for; a click on empty sky at (60,700) left `Lyrioa Gamma` selected, because a stray click must not cost the player their target. **Real click on the radar**: a click at client (632,586) logged `radar click: Freighter` and the ring landed on that blip. **The console pick agrees with the mouse**: a Lua sweep of the whole view (`sol.pick` on a 30 px grid) reported `space: Freighter n=1 at 640,300`, `space: Lyrioa Alpha n=1 at 640,270` and the routing prefix `radar:` for every sample inside the disc; a 4 px sweep of the disc itself found ten contacts at 12–25 samples each — including `radar: Lyrioa n=14 at 692,610`, the **star at ~1e11 m, clamped hollow to the rim**, and blips drawn at the end of tall stalks — which is the round trip the item rests on: every blip the disc draws is a blip the pick can hit. **Fire moved**: a 2 s LEFT hold on a freighter left it at 100/100/100 with the capacitor untouched, and a 2 s MIDDLE hold took its aft shield to **40%**. **Mining moved with it**: after a gate walk to Queis and `sol.warp_rock()`, a 2.5 s LEFT hold left the prospected rock at **67 of 67 units**, and a 2.5 s MIDDLE hold took it to **58 of 67** (with the ore chunks visible as a jump from 38 to 60 entities). **UI scale 1.25**: `sol.pick(640,360)` answered `nothing at (640, 360) of 1024x576` — the raw client coordinate is off the virtual screen entirely — while `sol.pick(512,288)` and `sol.pick_boresight()` both answered `Lyrioa Alpha`, and a **real** click at client (640,360) selected it, the cursor having been divided by the same scale the layout uses. **Captured cursor**: with RMB held for mouse-look, a left click moved the selection from `Lyrioa Gamma` to `Lyrioa Alpha` — the boresight object — which is the one case where the cursor position is meaningless by contract. 10 new `ui.unit` tests (64 → 74), all 7 suites green, zero warnings, 144–559 fps across every drive.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** The spec's own exit criterion caught the one thing it had got wrong. **A station's click box was the 28 px floor**, because `NavTarget::surfaceRadius` is documented as "0 for point targets (station)" — so the promise that you can click the hull filling your view was false for exactly the objects that fill it most. The candidate's radius now comes from a `pickRadiusOf` that answers per nav kind — a station is 130 m, a gate 70 m, a planet and a star carry their own — rather than from `surfaceRadius`, which could not be changed without moving the HUD's surface-relative distance readout and autopilot's standoff with it. The station's number was already in the code as a bare `130.0` literal for the NPC avoidance sphere and is now `kStationRadiusMeters`, used by both.

Two smaller things only flying it could have said. **In chase camera the ship's boresight is not the screen centre**: a ship spawned dead ahead projects about 60 px above it, so "pick at the boresight" means *pick where the crosshair is drawn*, which is the centre — the pick agrees with the reticle rather than with the hull, and that is the right answer for the same reason the whole item is built on forward projection. And **the drive script's own defocus click now selects something**, since left mouse no longer fires: the 8f lesson that parking the cursor over the 3D view quietly mines the rock in front of you is dead, replaced by a click that quietly re-targets. Both are the same fact from two sides — left mouse means something everywhere in the flight view now.

**Known gaps.** A **ship's** click box is the 28 px floor, because `TargetInfo` carries no radius for a spawned ship; at knife-fighting range a freighter is drawn wider than its own grab box. Individual asteroids are not candidates (the 8f ruling that rock is read off the boresight), so a field is clickable and the rocks in it are not. A click inside the radar disc that hits no blip selects nothing rather than falling through to the world behind the disc — deliberate, but it means the disc is a dead 156 px circle for world picks. There is no hover feedback of any kind: nothing lights up under the cursor, so a miss and a hit look identical until the readout changes. The grab radii (28 px in space, 11 px on the disc) are header constants with no setting. The pick is answered against the view frame published *this* frame before the HUD is built, so it is never stale — but that frame is published unconditionally from whichever camera is live, so `sol.pick` will happily answer from behind a menu or in freecam, where a *click* is correctly ignored (`inFlight && !typing`); only the console can reach that state, and it was left reachable because that is what makes the pick verifiable at all. Rebindable keys, again: the fire button is now hardcoded to a *different* mouse button, which is the strongest form the argument for that item has taken yet.

#### Phase 8k — Rebindable Controls & the Settings That Do Nothing ✅ (completed 2026-08-17)

The first Phase 8 item since 8g whose scope did **not** come from a playtest — the backlog those three phases were built from is empty for the first time since it was started, and this is the roadmap item with the strongest case standing. That case was built by the phases themselves: 8h hardcoded three new keys (`C`, `B`, `I`), 8i added two more (`O`, `H`), and 8j **moved the fire button to a different mouse button** with nothing in the game saying so. A player who has not read the plan learns that by holding left mouse over a rock and watching nothing happen. Every control in this game is a literal in a source file, and there is no screen that will even *tell* the player what the controls are, let alone change them.

Riding with it, a defect found while reading that code: **the Settings screen already has three controls that are wired to nothing.** `mouse_sensitivity` and `invert_pitch` are parsed, clamped, saved and drawn as a slider and a checkbox (`menu_screens.cpp:73-74`, `211-213`) and then read by **nobody** — `ShipInputMapper` uses a hardcoded `kStickSensitivity = 0.0035f` and has no inversion path at all. `vsync` is the same shape: the checkbox writes a bool that no one reads, because `createInfo.presentMode` is the literal `VK_PRESENT_MODE_FIFO_KHR` (`swapchain.cpp:89`). This is the class of defect 8i started from — a control that exists, looks live, and does nothing — and it is squarely inside an item about input configuration. The user's ruling was to fix **all three**, V-Sync included.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **Every binding is a literal at its use site, and every edge is hand-rolled beside it.** `main.cpp` holds **34 `bool previousX` members** (`main.cpp:329-374`) — one per key that needs press-edge rather than held state — each paired with a `const bool xDown = gameplayKey(Key::X); if (xDown && !previousX) {...} previousX = xDown;` block. The pattern is correct and it is copied twenty times. A binding table with no edge helper would leave that pile intact and merely change what each literal reads, which is half the item.
- **The flight axes are a second, differently-shaped copy.** `ShipInputMapper::update` (`main.cpp:117-131`) reads thirteen keys directly off the window as *held* state, inside a class that also owns the cursor lock and the virtual stick. It is skipped wholesale rather than fed neutral input when the UI owns the keyboard (`main.cpp:636-644`, an 8h lesson), so it cannot simply be handed a table and left alone.
- **Two mouse buttons are gameplay and one is a mode.** `input.trigger` is `MouseButton::Middle` (`main.cpp:653`), the pick is `MouseButton::Left` (`main.cpp:699`), and steering-look is `MouseButton::Right` inside the mapper (`main.cpp:102`). A binding type that only holds keys cannot express the very change that made this item urgent, which is why the chord is *key or button* from the start.
- **The HUD prints its prompts as string literals.** `"[J] JUMP"`, `"[G] DOCK"`, `"[G] SALVAGE"` (`game_ui.cpp:471-473`) and `"[R] SCAN"` (`game_ui.cpp:648`). A rebind that does not reach these leaves the HUD confidently telling the player to press a key that no longer does anything — strictly worse than the hardcoded state it replaced.
- **Nothing needs new platform plumbing.** `Window` already exposes `isKeyDown(Key)` over a `Key` enum with a `Count` sentinel and `isMouseButtonDown(MouseButton)` with the same. Capture — *"press the key you want"* — is a scan of `[0, Key::Count)` and `[0, MouseButton::Count)` for a chord that went down this frame, about 70 comparisons on the frames where a capture is armed. The 8h ruling that key state is recorded **before** the dev-UI message hook is what makes that scan trustworthy.
- **The settings file cannot hold a table yet, but the parser can.** `Settings::save` is a single `snprintf` into a fixed `char[512]` (`menu_screens.cpp:81`), which ~34 bindings overflow. `sol::core::TomlValue` already parses `[table]` sections and exposes `members()` as key/value pairs, so the reader is the easy half.

**A. `sol/platform/input_bindings.hpp` — the chord, and a table that knows nothing about this game.** An `InputChord` is a tagged union of "unbound", "a `Key`", or "a `MouseButton`" — one input, no modifier combinations (nothing in the game uses one, and `LeftShift` is *boost*, a binding in its own right). It carries a stable serialisation name (`"F"`, `"Space"`, `"Left Ctrl"`, `"Middle Mouse"`) and a `chordFromName` inverse, because the name in the settings file must survive a `Key` enum that gains members — an ordinal would silently reassign every binding the next time a key is inserted mid-enum. The `BindingTable` is keyed by an opaque `std::uint32_t` action id and holds the chord per action plus the three operations that are easy to get wrong: `find(chord)` (which action holds this, if any), `assign(action, chord)` implementing **steal** (see **E**), and `pressed(action, window, previousState)` — the edge helper that retires the pile of `previousX` bools into one bitset the table owns. No game action names appear in this header; it is a generic table, in `platform` beside the `Key` enum that it must not outlive.

**A new `platform.unit` suite.** There is no platform test target today (`engine/test` has core, ecs, sim, scripting, assets, ui, bench), and this is the first platform-layer logic that is pure, headless and genuinely easy to get subtly wrong. Coverage: every chord name round-trips through `chordName`/`chordFromName`; an unknown name yields *unbound* rather than a garbage key; `assign` over an occupied chord leaves the previous holder unbound and reports it; assigning an action its own existing chord is a no-op and does **not** unbind it (the obvious off-by-one in a steal implementation); `find` on an unbound chord answers nothing; and the edge helper reports exactly one press for a held key and re-arms on release.

**B. `game/src/input_actions.{hpp,cpp}` — what this game can be asked to do.** The `Action` enum and, beside it, the stable string id (`"cycle_nav_target"`) and human label (`"Cycle Nav Target"`) that the settings file and the Controls screen respectively need. **34 actions in four groups**, which is also the order the screen lists them:

- **Flight** (13, held): pitch up/down, yaw left/right, roll left/right, thrust forward/reverse, strafe left/right/up/down, boost.
- **Systems** (6): assist toggle, cruise toggle, and the four power-pip commands.
- **Targeting & Navigation** (9): cycle nav target, cycle contact, select objective, nearest hostile, autopilot, jump, dock/salvage, scan pulse, bookmark.
- **Views & Mouse** (6): camera cycle, map, ship info, plus fire, select, and steering-look.

Defaults are exactly today's layout, so a player who never opens the screen notices nothing. The enum is game-layer because the engine has no business knowing what "autopilot" is; the table underneath it is engine-layer because chord parsing and conflict resolution are not game logic. That split is the same one `pick.hpp` (maths in the engine) and `target_pick.cpp` (what a candidate *is*, in the game) already draw.

**C. Contexts, and the chords that may not be taken.** Bindings do not remove the context rules; they inherit them. `gameplayKey`'s `inFlight && !typing` gate still applies to every Flight/Systems/Targeting action, `G` keeps its `inFlight || docked` exception for undocking, and `M`/`I` keep theirs. What changes is that the gate reads a table instead of a literal. **Menu and text-editing keys are not actions and are not rebindable** — `Escape`, `Enter`, `Backspace`, `Delete`, `Home`, `End` and the arrow keys' menu-navigation role stay welded, because they are how the player reaches and leaves the very screen that does the rebinding. So are the dev keys (`F1` console, `F3` debug draw, `F5` shader reload, `F9`/`F10` save/load), which are tooling, not controls. Those chords are **reserved**: a capture that lands on one is refused with a one-line reason rather than silently accepted, and refusing is the one place the conflict policy in **E** does not apply. Locking the player out of their own pause menu is not a configuration the game should let them build.

**D. One place that reads bindings, and one that draws them.** The `previousX` pile collapses into `BindingTable::pressed(Action, ...)` calls, so each command in the frame loop becomes a single `if (bindings.pressed(Action::Jump, window))`. `ShipInputMapper` takes a `const BindingTable&` and asks it for held state per axis, keeping its existing wholesale skip when the UI owns the keyboard. The HUD prompts become `chordName(...)` lookups — `"[J] JUMP"` is built, not written — which is the half of this item that makes a rebind *visible* rather than merely possible.

**E. Conflicts steal, and the victim is shown.** Assigning a chord another action already holds gives it to the new action and leaves the old one **unbound**, with its row highlighted in the list and a line naming what just happened. The alternative — refusing the assignment — turns swapping two keys into a three-step dance through a temporary third key, and a player rebinding `T` and `C` onto each other should not have to discover that. An unbound action is a legal, visible state (`—` in its row), never a crash and never a silent no-op: the screen shows it as needing attention, and a **Reset to Defaults** button is one click away.

**F. The Controls screen.** A new `GameState::Controls`, reached from a **Controls** button on the Settings screen rather than crammed into it — 34 rows do not fit a 540 px panel with four sliders, and the existing `beginScroll`/`endScroll` and `selectable` widgets already do everything this needs. Each row is *label · current chord · [Rebind]*. Clicking a row arms capture: the row reads **"press a key or mouse button…"**, the next chord that goes down is assigned per **E**, and `Escape` cancels the capture without binding (which is exactly why `Escape` is reserved). Captures are armed one at a time. Group headers separate the four sections from **B**.

**G. The three dead settings, made live.** `mouseSensitivity` multiplies `kStickSensitivity` in the mapper, so the slider that has been sitting in the menu since 8d finally moves the stick. `invertPitch` flips the sign of the mouse's pitch contribution **only** — not the keyboard's pitch axis, which the player can invert by swapping two bindings now that bindings exist. `vsync` becomes a `presentMode` argument threaded through `Swapchain::create`/`recreate`: **FIFO** when on, **MAILBOX** when off with **IMMEDIATE** as the fallback and FIFO as the floor, chosen against `vkGetPhysicalDeviceSurfacePresentModesKHR` rather than assumed — the current code's one honest comment is that FIFO "is always available", and the others are not. A toggle sets the frame loop's existing `needRecreate` flag (`main.cpp:1250-1281`), so the swapchain rebuild path is the one that is already exercised by every window resize rather than a second one written for this.

**Persistence.** Settings gain a `[bindings]` table of `action_id = "Chord Name"`, and `Settings::save` moves from the fixed `char[512]` to a `std::string` builder. Unknown action ids and unparseable chord names are **skipped with a warning, not fatal** — the same rule the existing readers follow, and the one that matters most here because a stale settings file must never be the reason the game will not start. Any action absent from the file keeps its default, so a settings file written by an older build stays valid when new actions are added. **This is not the save file: the save stays v10.**

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** Modifier chords (`Shift+F`), chord sequences, and multiple bindings per action — one input, one action. Gamepad or joystick input of any kind: this is a rebinding item, not a device item, and `Window` has no gamepad surface to build on. Rebinding the menu, text-editing and dev keys (**C**). Per-context bindings, where the same key means different things in flight and while docked — the contexts exist but one key means one thing. Binding *profiles* or presets beyond Reset to Defaults. Remapping the mouse **axes** (invert yaw, separate look sensitivity per axis) beyond the single invert-pitch flag being fixed here. And a controls *reference* card in-flight — the HUD prompts will be correct, but there is no "press ? for help" overlay.

**Exit**: open Controls from Settings and see all 34 actions with their current chords; rebind `Jump` from `J` to `K`, and `K` jumps while `J` does nothing; the HUD prompt reads `[K] JUMP` without a restart; rebind `Fire` off middle mouse onto a key and shoot with it, and the mining beam moves with it; assign `T`'s key to another action and watch `Cycle Nav Target` become unbound and highlighted rather than the assignment being refused; try to bind `Escape` and be told no; Reset to Defaults restores every row; quit and restart and every binding survives; mouse sensitivity visibly changes turn rate and invert pitch visibly inverts it; V-Sync off measurably lifts the frame rate off the refresh cap and back on when re-enabled; a settings file with a garbage binding name still boots; all suites green including the new `platform.unit`, zero warnings.

*(Verified live 2026-08-17, seed 1701, across seven drives. **Defaults survive the rewrite**: a cold boot with no settings file reports `jump = J / fire = Middle Mouse / select = Left Mouse`. **A rebind moves a real keypress**: with `cycle_nav_target` on `K`, a real `K` walked the target from `Lyrioa Alpha` to `Lyrioa Beta` and the HUD readout followed, while a real `T` — the key it used to be — left it exactly where it was. **The steal, through the screen rather than the console**: arming `Pitch Up` and pressing `W` set it to `W`, turned `Thrust Forward`'s cell to an accent **"needs a key"**, and printed *"Pitch Up took W from Thrust Forward, which now needs a key."*; `Reset to Defaults` put both back and the file written on the way out read `pitch_up = "Up"` / `thrust_forward = "W"`. **Refusals are refusals**: `Escape` answered *"is reserved by the menus and cannot be bound"*, an unreadable chord *"no such key or button 'Nonsense'"*, an unknown action *"no such action"*. **Esc cancels the capture and not the screen** — the row disarmed, the notice read "Rebind cancelled.", and the list stayed up. **The HUD stopped lying**: with the interact action on `K` the chip over the station read **`[K] DOCK`**, unbinding it read **`DOCK (unbound)`**, and with the scan action on `L` a real `L` fired the pulse and the scanner chip read **`[L] SCAN`** over a draining charge bar. **Fire is a binding now**: with it moved to `K`, a 2.2 s hold of **middle mouse** left the target freighter at 100/100/100 with the capacitor untouched, and a 2.2 s hold of **K** took its aft shield to **32%** and the capacitor to **71%**. **Persistence across a cold restart**: `settings.toml` carried `jump = "K"` and `cycle_nav_target = ""`, and a fresh process reported `jump = K / cycle_nav_target = (unbound)` — the unbound action is written as an empty string rather than omitted, precisely so the reader does not treat it as "use the default". **The three dead settings are alive.** Invert pitch: the same injected mouse push moved the station **33 px up** with it off and **33 px down** with it on — mirrored exactly. Mouse sensitivity, measured below the stick's ±1 clamp so neither case saturates: the same 40 px push moved the station **1 px at 1.0 and 56 px at 4.0**. V-Sync: unticking it logged `V-Sync off` then `Swapchain 1280x720, present mode MAILBOX`, and the frame rate went from **144.2 fps (8.33 ms, FIFO on a 144 Hz panel) to 815.8 fps**. **A hand-mangled settings file still boots**: one file carrying a bad chord name, an action this build has never heard of, a binding of the wrong type, one chord claimed by two actions, and a deliberately empty one produced a running game with `jump = J` and `scan_pulse = R` (defaults kept), `autopilot = N` (the one valid override applied), `bookmark = (unbound)` (the empty one honoured), and the duplicate repaired in enum order — `cycle_nav_target = T`, `cycle_contact = (unbound)`. 19 new `platform.unit` tests in a new suite, all **8** suites green, zero warnings, 144–816 fps.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** Two things, both found by looking at the screen rather than by a test. **The capture prompt overran its cell**: at 220 px "press a key or mouse button…" ran under the Rebind button, because `ui.label` does not clip — the same fact 8i recorded about `ui.selectable`, met again from the other side. The value column is now 260 px and the prompt is "press any key or button", which is what the measurement said would fit. And **the dev overlay's control crib was the last hardcoded lie**: it still read "RMB steer, WASD thrust, Tab cruise…" as if it were current. `sol::ui::DevUi` is engine-side and has no route to the game's binding table, so rather than plumb one it now says **"defaults:"** and points at Settings — the honest fix for a hint that cannot know.

**Known gaps.** One chord per action, one action per chord: no modifiers, no chord sequences, no secondary binding. No gamepad or joystick — `Window` has no device surface to build on. Bindings are global rather than per-context, so an action that would mean different things docked and in flight cannot; the contexts exist in `main.cpp` but the table does not see them. The reserved list (`kReservedKeys` in `input_actions.cpp`) is a literal array, and the dev keys in it still carry their own `previousX` latches in `main.cpp` rather than going through the table. Nothing warns the player that an action is unbound *outside* the Controls screen — the HUD chips say "(unbound)" for the three they draw, but a player who unbinds Autopilot finds out by pressing nothing. `ui.label` still does not clip, so the 260 px value cell is a measured fit rather than a guarantee. There is no keyboard route through the rebind list (the rows are reached by mouse; nav focus reaches the buttons but the wheel is the only scroll). Mouse **axes** are still fixed apart from the invert-pitch flag: no per-axis sensitivity, no invert yaw. And the V-Sync toggle chooses MAILBOX over IMMEDIATE without asking, so a player who wants an uncapped tearing present mode specifically cannot have it.

#### Phase 8l — Mission Flow: Kill Credit & a Board That Keeps Up ✅ (completed 2026-08-17)

From the fourth playtest, and like 8i it starts from a **defect that breaks an accepted contract**. The user took a bounty for two raiders, killed one, and watched **local security interceptors kill the second**. The objective never advanced. Both raiders were dead — the thing the contract paid for had happened — and the mission sat in the journal permanently unfinishable. The second note from the same session is smaller and shares the seam: **the mission board does not refresh while docked**, so continuing a quest chain or seeing a new contract means undocking and redocking.

Neither of these is a missing feature. In both cases the machinery exists and is being denied the event that would drive it.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **Mission kill credit is gated behind a rule written for reputation.** `space_world.cpp:4016` opens `if (attackerIndex == playerEntityIndex())` under the comment *"only player kills move standings"* — which is correct, and which `recordShipKill` should keep. But `m_missions.notifyKill(...)` sits inside the same block, and this is its **only call site in the entire codebase**. A raider killed by anyone other than the player therefore reports nothing to `MissionSim`, whose own `notifyKill` (`missions.cpp:372`) is perfectly correct and simply never hears about it. The bug is one gate too wide, in the game layer, not in the sim.
- **A bounty has no deadline, so its dead end is permanent.** `init.lua:184` calls `sol.mission_deadline(480 + 420 * jumps)` for every haul contract. The bounty branch (`init.lua:216-221`) calls `mission_begin` → `mission_obj_kill` → `mission_post` and **never calls `mission_deadline`**, leaving `Mission::deadline = 0` — which `tick` reads as "no expiry". A haul that becomes impossible eventually fails and clears itself; a bounty that becomes impossible occupies one of `MissionParams::maxActive = 4` slots until the player pays standing to abandon it. The user's stuck contract could not have timed out no matter how long they waited.
- **The board's staleness is entirely a question of *when*, not of caching.** `station_ui.cpp:239` rebuilds its offer rows from `missions.offers()` every frame, so the screen shows whatever the sim holds the moment it is drawn. What is stale is the sim: `runMissionBoard()` is called from `content.cpp:2042` only on a dock event or when `tickBoard` says a refresh is due, and `tickBoard` (`missions.cpp:61`) is a plain accumulator against `MissionParams::boardRefreshInterval = 180.0` seconds. Completing a chain leg while docked changes nothing the player can see for up to three minutes. Undocking and redocking works because it is a dock event, which is exactly the workaround the user found.
- **There is no record of who hurt a ship before the shot that killed it.** `handleShipDestroyed` receives a killing-blow `attackerIndex` and nothing else: a ram credits no one by design (`space_world.cpp:3635`), a projectile credits `projectile.shooterIndex` (`:3693`), a beam credits the firing `entityIndex` (`:3832`). An assist needs damage history, which does not exist. It does not need much of one, though — **all three damage sites already funnel through `noteDamage`** (`:3950`), and that function already contains the precedent for the exact mechanism required: `m_playerDamageTimer = kDamageFlashSeconds`, a short countdown stamped when damage lands on the player. This item wants the mirror of it, stamped on the victim.

**A. The assist window.** `noteDamage` gains an `attackerIndex` parameter, supplied at the two sites that know one (the projectile's `shooterIndex`, the beam's firing entity) and left absent at the ram, which credits no one now and should keep crediting no one. When the attacker is the player, it stamps a countdown on the victim's `ShipDefense` — the component that already *is* that ship's combat state, and which is not serialised for NPC ships (`m_spawnedShips` is cleared and repopulated rather than saved), so **the save stays v10**. The window is `kAssistSeconds`, a game-side constant starting at **10 seconds**: long enough that the raider you have been dogfighting for a minute still counts when a patrol lands the last shot, short enough that a ship you clipped once and abandoned across the system does not.

**B. Kill credit leaves the reputation gate.** `notifyKill` moves out of the `attackerIndex == playerEntityIndex()` block and is called when the player killed the victim **or** the victim died inside the assist window. `recordShipKill` and the standing log stay exactly where they are, under the unchanged player-only rule — a security patrol's kill must not move the player's standing with the victim's faction, and this item is not a licence to change what reputation means. The two rules are now written separately because they *are* separate rules; the defect was that one line of code was serving both.

**C. Bounties get a deadline.** The bounty branch in `init.lua` gains `sol.mission_deadline(...)` on the same shape as hauls, scaled by kill count and distance rather than volume. This is the honest fix for the general dead end, and the reason it is the right one is that **detecting "no qualifying targets will ever spawn again" is not answerable** — raiders spawn from ongoing faction raid intensity, so a system that is quiet now may not be in five minutes. A deadline does not need to answer it: an impossible bounty simply expires, frees its slot, and costs the player the standing penalty that expiry already carries. Kill credit through **A**/**B** is what stops the *common* case from arising at all; **C** is what stops the uncommon one from being permanent.

**D. The board re-composes on the events that change it.** `runMissionBoard()` is additionally called when, while docked, a mission event lands that could alter what the board should offer — completion, failure, abandonment — not merely on the 180 s tick. `content.cpp` already collects those events into `m_missionEvents` a few lines below the board block (`:2048-2049`), so the ordering question is real and is settled by moving the board decision **after** the event drain, so a leg completed this frame is visible to the composition that follows it rather than one frame or three minutes later. The refresh interval stays as the backstop for offers that should rotate on their own. `openBoard` clears and re-posts, so a re-composition mid-dock is already a supported operation — this only asks for it at the right moments.

**E. What the player is told.** A stolen kill that counts should say so, because the alternative is a number that changes for no visible reason: the existing kill log line gains an assist form, and the mission journal's objective row is what the player checks. No new UI surface — 8i established that the journal and HUD mission line already carry objective prose, and this item adds no new kind of thing to look at.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** Assist *credit* in any other sense — reputation, payouts and the survey/economy trickles stay player-only, and there is no partial reward for a partial kill. Damage-weighted attribution (whoever did 51% gets it): the window is a boolean, because a bounty asks whether you were fighting it, not how much of it you personally removed. Kill credit for a ship destroyed by the player's *wingmen* or turrets — there are none. Mission *reissue* or a "target lost" replacement objective; **C** expires the contract rather than repairing it. Any change to `MissionParams::maxActive`, to the offer composition policy in `init.lua` beyond adding the deadline, or to how bounty rewards are priced. And a board that updates while **undocked** — the board belongs to a station you are standing in.

**Exit**: take a bounty, damage a raider, let a security patrol land the killing blow, and watch the objective advance and the journal count fall; do the same to a raider you have never touched and watch it *not* count; kill one yourself and confirm standing still moves exactly as it did before, while a patrol's kill moves no standing; confirm a bounty now carries a visible deadline and that letting it run out fails the mission and frees the slot; complete a campaign leg while docked and see the next leg appear on the board without undocking; accept a contract, complete it, and see the board re-compose in place; all suites green including new `sim.unit` coverage for kill credit and expiry, zero warnings.

*(Verified live 2026-08-17, seed 1701, across nine drives, against a bounty posted with `sol.mission_obj_kill` for Norea Reavers. **The assist counts, and it counts only what it should.** Damaging a clan freighter and letting Lyrioa's own security land the killing blow printed `assist vs Norea Reavers: someone else finished it, bounty credited`, took the journal from `(3 left)` to `(2 left)`, and left standing at **−20.0, unchanged** — no `kill vs` line, because reputation is still strictly the player's own kill. A clan interceptor spawned and **never fired on** was destroyed by the same security and the journal stayed at `(4 left)`, with no assist line at all. And the player's own kill still behaves exactly as Phase 8b built it: `kill vs Norea Reavers: standing now −30.0 (neutral)`, standing **−20.0 → −30.0**, journal `(4 left)` → `(3 left)`. **The board keeps up, proven by A/B rather than assertion.** The same scripted run — same seed, same route, the only difference being `content.cpp` stashed — completes `Shakedown Cruise` by docking, bumps the campaign to `stage now 1.0`, and then: **before the reorder the board reads only `1: Bounty: Ironstar Hegemony in Lyrum`, with `Teeth in the Dark` absent**; after it, `1: Teeth in the Dark (Solar Navy, 900 cr) [campaign]` is already there, in the same dock, with no undock. That is the user's report reproduced and then fixed. **Bounties carry a clock**: the board line now reads `Bounty: Ironstar Hegemony in Lyrum (Solar Navy, 1318 cr, 1500s)` — 600 + 420×1 jump + 240×2 kills, exactly the new formula. Expiry itself is covered by the new sim test rather than 25 minutes of wall clock. 1 new `sim.unit` test (97 → 98), all **8** suites green, zero warnings, 144–530 fps.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** Nothing in the design, but two things worth recording about *reaching* the evidence. **Local security is fast and it steals kills constantly** — a clan fighter spawned near Lyrioa Alpha was dead in one to two seconds, before a console round trip could even pull the trigger, and it took a tanky freighter to hold together long enough to be shot at all. Three separate attempts to land a *player* kill near the station were beaten to it by patrols, which is incidental confirmation of how ordinary the user's situation is: this is not an edge case, it is what happens when you fight in someone else's space. **`pilot_hull` is the wrong probe for "did I hit it"**, because shields absorb first and it reads 1.0 through an entire shield bar; the HUD's own SH-F/SH-A/HULL readout is the honest one. And `sol.objective()` is `describeObjective` — it *reports* the objective and does not select it, so an autopilot issued after it flies to whatever was already targeted.

**Known gaps.** The assist window is a boolean over 10 s, not damage-weighted: one glancing shot inside the window counts the same as a minute of dogfighting, and a kill 11 s after the player's last hit counts for nothing at all — the boundary is sharp and nothing in the UI shows it. `kAssistSeconds` is a constant in `space_world.hpp`, not data. The window rides on `ShipDefense`, so anything that never gets that component cannot be assisted on. A **ram still credits nobody**, which now means a raider the player softened and then rammed to death counts (assist) while one they only rammed does not — defensible, but not a rule stated anywhere. Assist credit is mission-only by design: no reputation, no payout share, no survey or commerce trickle. There is still no *reissue* or "target lost" path — an impossible bounty expires rather than repairing itself, and the standing penalty for expiry applies even when the contract became impossible through no fault of the player, which is the one place this item leaves a rough edge. The 180 s `boardRefreshInterval` is untouched and still a `MissionParams` constant. And the board re-composes only for the three terminal events; an `ObjectiveComplete` mid-chain does not, which is right today only because no offer's availability depends on another mission's *progress*.

#### Phase 8m — The Cockpit, and a HUD That Lives In It ✅ (completed 2026-08-17)

From the fourth playtest, in the user's own words: *"a cockpit view with an integrated HUD."* It is the one genuine **feature** of that session's three notes — the other two were defects and shipped as 8l — and the largest item since 8d built the UI stack. Scope was settled with the user before writing this: a **modelled** cockpit rather than a painted frame, the cockpit as the **default** view on a new game, and **free-look in scope** as a held key.

Unlike 8i and 8l this does not start from a broken contract. It starts from a promise the game has been making since the first slice and has never kept.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **The cockpit already exists as a camera and as nothing else.** `CameraMode::FirstPerson` has been in `ship_camera.hpp` since the first vertical slice: it puts the eye at `kCockpitOffset = {0, 0.8, -5}` in ship space (`ship_camera.hpp:46`), and `main.cpp:693` hides the ship mesh for it (`includeShip = cameraMode != FirstPerson`). The HUD's mode line prints **`"COCKPIT"`** (`main.cpp:797`). So the game already tells the player they are sitting in a cockpit and then draws a bare view from a point in space with the hull deleted. This item is not "add a camera" — the camera has been there since Phase 2 — it is *give it a cockpit and mount the instruments in it*.
- **Every HUD panel is anchored to a screen corner, and nothing else.** `drawFlightPanel` sits `kMargin` from the bottom left (`game_ui.cpp:276`), `drawPowerPanel` bottom right (`:343`), `drawTargetReadout` top right (`:410`), `drawScannerPanel` under it, `drawMissionLine` above the flight panel, and the radar at `radarCenter(screenSize, kMargin)`. `buildFlightUi` takes exactly one geometric input — `screenSize` — so there is no surface for a cockpit to integrate *with*. The layout is in the **virtual** screen (pixels ÷ UI scale), so anything built here has to work in that space or 8d's UI-scale setting breaks the moment it is touched.
- **The projection this needs already exists and is already the single definition.** `sol::ui::screenPoint` (`pick.hpp:44`) maps a camera-space direction onto the virtual screen, and it is what the target diamond, the lead marker and the click-pick all agree through. A dash anchor is a fixed point in *cockpit* space; run it through that same function and a panel sits on the dash at any FOV, any window size and any UI scale — and follows the frame when the head turns. That is 8j's anti-drift ruling (one definition for "where a thing is drawn") applied to the cockpit, and it is the reason this item does not need a single new piece of maths.
- **The mesh path takes everything a cockpit needs and needs nothing new.** `MeshRenderer::draw` takes an arbitrary MVP and model matrix plus a per-draw `emissive` whose comment already reads *"engine housings, windows"* (`mesh_renderer.hpp:58`), and `mesh.frag` computes `albedo * (ambient + sun * diffuse + emissive)`. Instances are already drawn camera-relative (`scene_renderer.cpp:231`), so a ship-attached cockpit is an ordinary `RenderInstance` at the ship's transform. The cooker cooks **every** `.gltf` under `assets/meshes` with no manifest to edit (`tools/cooker/src/main.cpp:196`), and `tools/scripts/gen_assets.ps1` already carries `Add-Box`, `Add-Torus`, `Add-FlatTriangle` and `Write-Gltf` — the generator that authored the station, the ship and the asteroid.
- **Lighting is the reason this is geometry and not paint.** `kAmbient = 0.012f` (`scene_renderer.cpp:31`): an interior lit by one directional sun and 1.2% ambient is **black** wherever the star is not, which is what `emissive` exists to answer. It is also the answer to the obvious objection — a cockpit rigidly locked to the *camera* projects to identical pixels every frame and could just as well be painted into the UI layer. This one is locked to the **ship**, which is what makes free-look swing the view *inside* it and what puts the moving sun on the dash.
- **Nothing in the frame loop distinguishes "where the ship points" from "where the camera points", because in cockpit view they have always been the same vector.** `hud.targetDirectionCamera` and `hud.leadDirectionCamera` are already built against `camera.orientation` (`main.cpp:806`, `:916`), so those survive free-look untouched. The **reticle does not**: `buildFlightUi` draws it at the centre of the virtual screen (`game_ui.cpp:795-798`) on the assumption that the centre *is* the boresight. 8j recorded the same fact from the other side — *"in chase camera the ship's boresight is not the screen centre"* — with the difference that in chase, the crosshair being the aim point is a harmless convention, and under free-look a crosshair that stays centred is telling the player they are aiming somewhere they are not.
- **All three mouse buttons are spoken for**: left selects, middle fires, right steers (`input_actions.cpp:86-91`). Free-look has to be a key, and it has to be a key that Windows does not want.

**A. The cockpit is a ship-attached mesh, not a camera-locked one.** `assets/meshes/cockpit.gltf`, generated by an extended `gen_assets.ps1` out of the same box and flat-triangle helpers that built the station, authored in **ship space around the existing eye point** so the seat stays exactly where `kCockpitOffset` already puts it and the geometry is built around the camera rather than the camera moved to suit the geometry. A new `ModelId::Cockpit`; in cockpit view `main.cpp` appends one `RenderInstance` at the ship's interpolated transform after `buildRenderInstances`, and the model switch in `recordCommands` gives it the hull texture and a non-zero `emissive` so the interior still reads with the star behind the ship. One extra draw call.

Attaching it to the **ship** rather than the camera is the load-bearing choice in this whole item: it is what lets free-look look *around* the frame instead of dragging it along, and it is what makes the sunlight sweep. A camera-locked cockpit would be a decal.

**The hull stays hidden.** The eye is 5 m forward of the ship origin on a 12 m faceted wedge with no interior, so drawing `ship.gltf` around it would park the camera inside a solid shell. Anything of the ship that should be visible from the seat — a nose section, a wing root through the glass — is authored into `cockpit.gltf`, which is the only mesh that knows where the seat is.

**B. `sol/ui/cockpit_frame.hpp` — the anchors, and the one projection they all go through.** A header-only frame in the shape `map_projection.hpp` and `radar_projection.hpp` already have, so `ui.unit` can assert on it headlessly:

- a small fixed table of **anchor points in cockpit space** — left console, right console, centre console, upper glass — that must match the mesh, generated and consumed from the same numbers so a moved dash moves its instruments;
- `cockpitAnchors(shipToCamera, center, focal)` → those anchors as virtual-screen points, through `screenPoint` and nothing else;
- the **aperture**: the axis-aligned rect of glass currently visible, which is what world-referenced HUD elements clip to and what the off-screen target arrow rides the rim of. Axis-aligned because `DrawList::pushClip` takes a `Rect`, and an inscribed rect is an honest approximation of an opening that is not one — stated here rather than discovered later.

`FlightHud` gains a `HudFrame` (the anchor rects plus the aperture) and `buildFlightUi` lays every panel against the frame instead of against `screenSize`. **The external cameras supply a frame whose anchors are exactly today's corners and whose aperture is the whole screen**, so chase and freecam come out pixel-identical to what shipped and there is one layout path rather than two — which is also how the item gets A/B tested.

Panels keep their **pixel** sizes and take only their **anchor** from the projection. A panel sized by the projection would grow and shrink with the FOV and take its text with it, and the text is the reason the HUD exists at all.

**C. The dash carries the panels, the glass carries the world.** One rule decides where everything goes. Anything that describes *the ship and its systems* is mounted on a surface and moves with the frame: flight panel, power block, target readout, scanner block, mission line, radar disc. Anything that *points at something outside* stays in the glass and is clipped to the aperture: reticle and its shield arcs, lead marker, target diamond, contextual prompts, scan ring. The off-screen target arrow rides the **aperture rim** instead of a ring at `0.38 × min(screen)`, because the rim is where the view actually stops.

A click **outside the aperture selects nothing**, rather than picking the world behind the dash — the same ruling 8j made for a click inside the radar disc that hits no blip, for the same reason: the player cannot see through it, so it must not act as though they could.

**D. The radar keeps its one definition.** `radarCenter(screenSize, margin)` lives in `radar_projection.hpp` because 8j made blips clickable and the draw and the hit test must agree. It takes the frame's centre-console anchor instead of the screen size; the pick keeps agreeing because it keeps reading the same function. Nothing about the disc's own geometry changes.

**E. Free-look: hold to look around.** A new `Action::FreeLook` in the Views & Mouse group ("Free Look"), default **`Z`** — not `LeftAlt`, which Windows routes through `WM_SYSKEYDOWN` and the window menu (the 8h lesson about `WM_SYSKEYDOWN` falling through to `DefWindowProc` is exactly this hazard), and not a mouse button, because all three are spoken for. While held, the cursor locks the way steering already locks it and the mouse yaws and pitches the **eye** within limits — about ±120° yaw and ±60° pitch, enough to check your six and not enough to turn your head through the seat. On release the eye eases back to the boresight on the same exponential shape `ShipCamera::thirdPerson` already uses for the chase. The offset is a camera-space rotation applied on top of the ship orientation inside `ShipCamera::firstPerson`, so **it moves the camera and nothing else**: flight input, weapons and autopilot never see it.

Two consequences that are the actual work:

- **The crosshair follows the nose.** With the head turned the boresight is no longer the screen centre, so the reticle, its shield arcs and the scan ring are drawn at the projection of the ship's forward direction in camera space and clipped to the aperture — looking sideways *loses* the crosshair rather than lying about where it points. The boresight pick (`pickBoresight`, used while the cursor is locked) keeps asking about the ship's nose, which is what a player holding the fire button means by "that one".
- **The panels swing with the dash**, so far enough off-axis the flight panel leaves the screen entirely. That is correct: it is what "integrated" costs, and looking back is what it costs.

Free-look applies in cockpit view only. In chase it does nothing, and in freecam the mouse is already owned by the debug camera.

**F. Cockpit is the default view.** `cameraMode` starts at `CameraMode::Cockpit` instead of `ThirdPerson`, and the enum member is renamed `FirstPerson` → `Cockpit`, because the HUD has been printing "COCKPIT" for it since 8d and the name should match the thing it names. The cycle order is unchanged: Cockpit → Chase → Freecam → Cockpit. The mode is **not persisted** — no settings key, and **the save stays v10**.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** HUD elements rendered as world-space geometry on the instrument surfaces: the UI draw list runs in the present pass after tonemap, and moving text into the HDR pass is a render-path item, not a cockpit item — so the panels are screen-space rects anchored to projected points, and on a surface angled hard away from the eye they will read as flat. Per-hull cockpits: one `cockpit.gltf` for every ship in the shipyard, and `ShipDef` gains no cockpit field. A visible pilot, hands, or switches that move. Head bob, camera shake, damage-driven view roll, cracking glass — atmosphere, not integration. Head-tracking hardware, VR, or a second seat position; free-look is one held key and one mouse. A cockpit for anything that is not flight: docked is a screen and stays one, and the map and station screens still dim the world and draw over everything. Changing `kCameraVerticalFov`, which the scene, the sky and the HUD projection all share. And re-laying-out the HUD in **chase** view, which stays exactly as it shipped — that is what makes the A/B possible.

**Exit**: start a new game and be sitting in a cockpit — frame, dash, world through the glass; the sun visibly crosses the dash as the ship turns, and the interior stays readable with the star behind you; flight panel, power block, target readout, scanner block, mission line and radar are all mounted on the cockpit rather than floating at the screen edges, and they stay mounted at a different window size and a different UI scale; a target marker does not draw over the dash, and its off-screen arrow rides the aperture rim; hold Free Look and the view swings inside the frame while the crosshair stays on the nose, release and it eases back; fire while looking sideways and the shots go where the crosshair is, not where the screen centre is; a click still selects what is under it, a click on the dash selects nothing, and a click on a radar blip still selects the blip that was drawn; press the camera key and chase view is pixel-identical to what it was before this phase; rebind Free Look and it moves; all suites green including new `ui.unit` coverage for the anchor projection and the aperture, zero warnings.

*(Verified live 2026-08-17, seed 1701, across seven drives. **A new game boots into a cockpit**: canopy pillars, brow, instrument shelf and the ship's own nose ahead of the glass, with the flight panel on the left of the dash, the contact disc on the centre console and the power block on the right — the mode line reading `COCKPIT` over a view that now has one in it. **The crosshair follows the nose, and the guns follow the crosshair.** With the head turned 24° right the reticle sits at x≈418 rather than at the screen centre of 640 — the projection predicted 410 — and the whole dash swings with it, disc and panels together. Firing from that pose at an inert freighter **dead ahead** took its aft shield from **100% to 0%** while the camera was pointed 24° away, which is the claim stated as sharply as it can be: the head moves the view and nothing else. **The dash is opaque to the mouse**: `sol.pick(640,690)` on the instrument shelf answers `nothing`, `sol.pick(60,300)` on the pillar answers `nothing`, and `sol.pick(640,300)` through the glass answers `space: Lyrioa Alpha`; `sol.pick_boresight()` names the station down the nose. **Chase view is pixel-identical, proven by A/B rather than asserted** — the same scripted run against a build of `dev` and a build of this branch, differenced over the flight panel, power block, target readout and radar disc: **0 differing pixels out of 175,700, max channel delta 0.** 9 new `ui.unit` tests (74 → 83), all **8** suites green, zero warnings, 490–580 fps with the cockpit drawn.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** The design held; the *numbers* did not, and both corrections came from looking at the screen rather than from a test. **The first cockpit read as a warehouse.** Wearing `hull.png` — an exterior grey authored to be seen from hundreds of metres — the interior came out brighter than the starfield it framed, and one 256 px panel stretched across a 2 m dash gave it the scale of a wall. The fix is a dedicated dark `cockpit.png` whose seams are *lighter* than its panels (a dark surface reads its detail off highlights, not shadows), UV tiling by physical size (`Add-Box`/`Add-Beam` gained a repeats-per-metre argument that **defaults to the old 0..1 stretch**, so every existing mesh regenerated byte-identical), a nose trimmed from a runway to a hint, and cabin light dropped from 0.16 to 0.10.

**The layout defect the spec did not see coming is worth stating on its own: the dash is measured in ANGLES and the panels in PIXELS.** Everything here projects through a focal length taken from the screen's *height*, so shortening the virtual screen — which a **1.25 UI scale on a 720p window already does** — shrinks the distance between the two console mount points while the flight panel stays 320 px wide, until it laps over the disc between them; at 1600×620 all three collided and the disc's range label ran off the bottom edge. Three changes, in the three places that own the relevant fact. The centre-console anchor moved from −0.48 to **−0.40** so the disc plus its label fits down to a virtual height of ~500 px. `game_ui.cpp` — which is where the panel widths live, and the frame header deliberately does not know them — pushes the two side panels outward until they clear the disc, **expressed against the disc rather than against the screen**, which is what lets the whole row still swing together when free-look turns the head; a screen-relative clamp would have glued the panels to the edge as the dash slid out from under them. And `cockpit_frame.hpp` carries **one** honest screen-relative clamp, a floor under the disc for the 2.0 UI scale, where a 360 px virtual screen simply has nowhere to put it.

**Known gaps.** The panels are screen-space rects pinned to projected points, so on a dash surface angled hard away from the eye they read as flat — the glass-cockpit option was ruled out of scope and stays out. **Free-look costs the player every instrument**: turn far enough and the consoles leave the screen and the aperture collapses, so hull and shields go with them; that is what "integrated" buys and there is no reduced fallback readout. The aperture is an axis-aligned rect approximating an opening that is not one, and it is computed from four independently-projected edge points, so at large head angles it is looser than the frame it stands for. The anti-collision push is verified by the drive and not by a headless test, because the panel sizes live in the game layer and `ui.unit` cannot see them. On a very wide, short window the `[G] DOCK` prompt lands on the disc's rim and is drawn under it. There is **one** cockpit for every hull in the shipyard — `ShipDef` has no cockpit field — and the eye is still `kCockpitOffset`, a constant shared by hand between `ship_camera.hpp` and `gen_assets.ps1` rather than by any mechanism. The camera mode is not persisted, so a player who prefers chase re-presses `V` every launch. Free-look has no invert-yaw and shares the one `mouse_sensitivity` slider with steering; its limits (±120°/±60°) and ease-back rate are header constants. Nothing warns that Free Look is unbound. And the cockpit is drawn with a per-*model* emissive rather than a per-instance one, so it is the only lit-from-inside object the renderer can express.

#### Phase 8n — Performance Hardening: Measure First, Then Cut ✅ (completed 2026-08-17)

The first Phase 8 item since 8k that does not come from a playtest, and the first ever whose scope was *narrowed* by looking at the code before writing the spec. Scope was settled with the user against three findings, below; two of them contradict the roadmap line this item was picked from.

The roadmap has carried "performance hardening (incl. def cooking to binary packs, deferred from Phases 5/7)" since Phase 8 was sketched. **Def cooking is not in this item**, and §4's Phase 8+ line is amended to say so. The reasoning is recorded here rather than in a commit message because it is the third time this sub-item has moved and it should not move a fourth under a heading that does not fit it.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **There is no profiler, so nothing in this project has ever been measured per-subsystem.** `OverlayStats` (`dev_ui.hpp:17`) carries eight fields — fps, frame ms, camera position and speed, draw calls, sim ticks, entity count, sim alpha — and not one of them attributes time to anything. The only clock in the engine outside the test suites is `QueryPerformanceCounter` in `system_win32.cpp:20-25`, which produces the frame delta and nothing else. Every performance claim in this document is therefore either an end-to-end fps number or arithmetic. **This is already a recorded cost**: Phase 8g's spec promised that *"the coarse tick cost is measured before and after, and the result is recorded in the verification note"* (§4, Phase 8g), and 8g's verification note records instead that the measurement was never taken — not from oversight, but because there was no instrument to take it with. That is the item's first deliverable and the precondition for the rest of it.
- **The frame already has clean seams to instrument, except where it matters most.** `main.cpp` runs `pumpEvents` (`:388`), input and screen state, the fixed-loop block that calls `world.tick` and `content.tick` up to eight times (`:654-658`), the camera and HUD build, `buildFlightUi` (`:1179`), `devUi.beginFrame` (`:1307`) and `renderer.drawFrame` (`:1333`). Those are free. **`SpaceWorld::tick` is not**: it is one function of 568 lines (`space_world.cpp:3384-3952`) holding orbital motion, flight integration, the collision pass, the projectile sweep, hitscan, mining and ore collection. It has no internal function boundaries to borrow, so the sim's zones have to be placed by hand — which is the bulk of the instrumentation work and the reason this is an item rather than an afternoon.
- **The collision pass's stated contract was broken by Phase 8f and nobody noticed.** `collision.hpp:38` says in as many words: *"O(n^2) pairs - fine at encounter scale (1-20 ships + a handful of statics)."* That was true when Phase 6 wrote it. Then 8f added asteroids. `instantiateMiningEntities` (`space_world.cpp:1202`) spawns **every rock of every field in the system** on entry, with no distance culling and no cap beyond `kMaxRocksPerField = 1024` (`mining.cpp:19`); a fringe system rolls 1–3 fields of 30–48 rocks each (`mining.hpp:102-103`). Rocks carry `RenderShape` and no `FlightBody`, so the second body-building loop (`space_world.cpp:3572-3588`) sweeps every one of them into `m_collisionBodies` as an immovable. A worst-case fringe system is therefore ~144 rocks plus ships, station, gates, star and planets — call it ~175 bodies, ~15,300 pairs, every tick, at 60 Hz.
- **The great majority of those pairs are geometrically impossible, not merely unlikely.** Fields are placed at least `fieldMinDistance = 8.0e7` m apart (`mining.hpp:106`), so a rock in one field is tested against a rock in another 80,000 km away, forever, at 60 Hz. Worse, they are *all statics*: `resolvePair` returns at its first branch when `inverseMassSum <= 0.0` (`collision.cpp:16-19`), so a static-vs-static pair costs two body loads and a compare to conclude what the body list already knew. With ~144 statics out of ~175 bodies, roughly **two thirds of all pairs are static-vs-static** and provably resolve to nothing.
- **The same over-long body list is rescanned per projectile.** The projectile loop (`space_world.cpp:3664-3672`) walks all of `m_collisionBodies` for every live bolt every tick, and the hitscan path does the same at `:3804`. So the rocks are not one cost but a multiplier on a second one, and a busy dogfight is when both peak together.
- **Def cooking to binary packs is not a performance problem at this project's payload size.** The entire def set is **34 KB of TOML** across seven files in `game/data`; all of `assets/` is 538 KB. Phase 5 deferred cooking to "the Phase 7 pack/manifest work"; Phase 7 deferred it again to "Phase 8 performance hardening", and stated the real blocker in its own verification note — *"since no shipping layout exists yet"*. That is still true, and it is a packaging problem, not a speed one. Cooking 34 KB would save an unmeasurable fraction of startup while costing the data hot-reload that §3's Phase 5 line calls *"a force multiplier, not a luxury"*, or else costing a second code path to keep both. It is removed from this item and belongs to a future distribution/packaging item, alongside pack files and the asset manifest, when there is a shipping layout to serve.

**A. `sol::core` gets a frame profiler, because measurement is the deliverable.** A named-zone timer in the core layer — below everything, dependency-free, testable in `core.unit` like the rest of `sol::core`. Zones are opened and closed by a scoped RAII guard, nest, and accumulate per frame into a fixed table keyed by a string literal, so a zone costs a `QueryPerformanceCounter` pair and an add. Zone names are compile-time literals and the table is fixed-capacity: no allocation in the frame path, because a profiler that allocates is measuring itself.

The profiler keeps a **ring of recent frames**, not just the current one, and reports per zone: last, mean, and max over the window. Mean is what tells you where the frame goes; **max is what tells you about the hitch**, and a hitch is exactly the thing an fps counter averages away. That distinction is the reason this is a ring and not a running total.

Zones nest but do **not** subtract: a parent reports inclusive time and the profiler reports parent and children side by side, with the child indented under it. Exclusive time is a display convenience that invites double-counting bugs, and the tree already answers the question.

**B. The zones, placed where the seams are.** At frame level: input and event pump, sim (the whole fixed-loop block), HUD and UI build, dev overlay, render submit, present. Inside the sim: one zone per `world.tick` phase — orbital motion, flight integration, **collision body build**, **collision resolve**, **projectiles**, hitscan, mining and collection — plus `content.tick` split into the Lua hooks (`on_tick`, `pilot_think`, `faction_think`) and the coarse sims. The sim block also records **how many ticks ran this frame**, because the fixed loop runs up to eight and a sim zone that quietly represents eight ticks rather than one is a measurement trap, not a measurement.

The collision zones additionally record **body count and pair count**, so the arithmetic in the diagnosis above stops being arithmetic. A profiler that reports only time tells you a pass is slow; one that reports the size of the input tells you *why*, and that is the difference between this item finding the real cost and confirming my guess.

**C. It is visible in the overlay and readable from the console.** `OverlayStats` gains the profiler's report and `DevUi` draws the zone tree with its three columns. More importantly the report is queryable from Lua — `sol.perf` printing the tree, `sol.perf_zone(name)` returning one zone's numbers — so a **drive script can assert on a time budget** rather than a human reading it off a screenshot. That is what makes any fix in section E provable by the same A/B standard 8l and 8m set, and it is what stops this item's own gains from silently regressing later.

**D. Then measure, and only then cut.** A drive that establishes the real numbers before anything is optimized: a fringe system carrying three asteroid fields, the player in a dogfight with spawned hostiles firing, at the default window and UI scale. That single measurement decides the rest of this item. The suspects are ranked by the arithmetic above — collision resolve first, projectile sweep second — and the spec deliberately does **not** commit to a fix in advance, because committing to one is how you optimize the thing you already suspected instead of the thing that is actually costing you.

**E. The candidate fixes, cheapest first; measurement picks the rung.** Named here so the work is bounded and so a later reader can see what was considered, not to pre-authorize all of it:
