1. **Do not spawn rocks that are astronomically far away.** Distance-culled instantiation in `instantiateMiningEntities` — the field you are at exists, the field 80,000 km away does not until you approach it. This is a game-layer fix that shrinks the collision list, the projectile scan, the render instance build and the ECS iteration *at once*, and it is the only candidate that fixes all four. It costs rock pop-in at the cull radius and needs the radius chosen so it is never visible.
2. **Stop testing static against static.** The pair is provably inert; the body list already builds movers first and statics after (`space_world.cpp:3548-3549` says so in a comment). Making that ordering a documented precondition of `resolveCollisions` and running the outer loop over movers only removes ~two thirds of the pairs with no behavioral change whatsoever — the cheapest correct change available, and unit-testable in `sim.unit` against the existing collision tests.
3. **A broad phase**, if 1 and 2 leave the pass indicted. Sweep-and-prune on one axis before the narrow phase. Deliberately last: it is the most code, the most risk to a pass that currently has no known correctness bugs, and it may well be unnecessary once the input set is honest.

The same ranking applies to the projectile sweep, which fix 1 improves for free.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** **Def cooking to binary packs, pack files and the asset manifest** — moved out of this item entirely, per the diagnosis; §4's Phase 8+ line is amended to point at a future packaging item. Multithreading the sim: §2.5's "fixed order first" still stands, and a job-graph parallel sim is a much larger item that should be argued on its own evidence and not slipped in under "hardening". GPU profiling and Vulkan timestamp queries — this item measures the CPU frame; if `renderer.drawFrame` turns out to be the cost, that is a finding and a *next* item, because GPU timing needs query pools, per-frame resolve and a different set of tests. A release-build performance pass or compiler-flag tuning. Any change to the collision *response* maths. Sim-LOD changes beyond the rock culling in E1. Persisting or configuring the profiler, and shipping it to players: it is dev tooling on the ImGui side of §2.9's line, exactly as the console and overlay are. And a profiler UI beyond the zone tree — no flame graph, no history plot, no capture-to-file.

**Exit**: the dev overlay shows where the frame actually goes, per subsystem, with mean and max over a window, and the sim's zones report how many ticks they covered; `sol.perf` prints the same tree to the console and `sol.perf_zone` returns one zone's numbers, so a drive can assert a budget instead of a human reading a screenshot; the collision zones report body and pair counts, and those counts confirm or refute the ~175-body/~15,300-pair worst case stated above from a real fringe system in a real dogfight; a stated frame budget is met in that worst case with the fix that measurement indicted, proved by the same drive run against `dev` and against this branch; whatever is *not* fixed is recorded as a known gap with its measured cost, so the next reader inherits numbers rather than suspicions; new headless coverage in `core.unit` for the zone accumulation and ring statistics, plus `sim.unit` coverage for any change to `resolveCollisions`; all suites green, zero warnings.

*(Verified live 2026-08-17, seed 1701, across five drives. **The measurement overruled this spec's own diagnosis, which is what it was built to be able to do.** The frame budget is **6.94 ms**: the swapchain logs `present mode FIFO (vsync)` and the display is 144 Hz. Inside it, the game does **~0.7 ms of CPU work and waits ~6.2 ms**. `render.submit` is that wait, not work, proved by holding the scene fixed and sweeping the window across a 13× pixel range — 464×321 → **6.194 ms**, 1272×720 → **6.196 ms**, 1904×1041 → **6.230 ms**, a 0.6% spread where rasterisation cost would have moved 13×.*

*The collision pass — the suspect this item was written around — measured **0.034 ms mean / 0.18 ms max** at a real two-field asteroid system (73 bodies, 2,628 pairs, 354 fps). It reaches 0.49 ms only at 138 bodies with ~60 movers, a state produced by spawning sixty ships from the console and not by play. **So §E was not built**, and the ladder's own ranking was wrong as well as unnecessary: a scaling sweep (73→103→133 bodies) shows cost tracks **mover count**, not pair count — 3.3× the pairs cost 13× the time — because static-vs-static pairs already return at `resolvePair`'s first branch. Fix E2 would have optimised the pairs that were already free.*

*What measurement did indict was **`sim.coarse.economy`: 3.31 ms max against a 6.94 ms budget**, mean 0.03 — a once-a-second burst and the largest CPU cost in the game. Zoning inside it put 99% in `economy.produce` and 99% of that in `MiningSim::drawFromSystem`. Two redundancies removed, no economics touched: `rocksFor` regenerated every rock from the system seed on every call (memoised by (system, field), read by reference); and `drawFromSystem` called `mineRock` per rock, so each draw cost O(rocks × records) of sorted-insert memmove (collected and applied in one linear merge). **3.311 → 2.479 ms max on the identical drive, and `sim.unit` fell 56.48 → 41.72 s** because 8g's four-sim-hour steady-state test runs this path — two independent measurements, both ~25%. The depletion table, its key order and the save are unchanged (still **v10**).*

*17 new `core.unit` tests (66 → 83) for zone accumulation, nesting, the ring statistics and the overflow paths, driven through a clock seam so they assert stated times rather than racing real ones; `sim.unit` 98 → 99. All **8** suites green, zero warnings.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** Everything. The spec's diagnosis was arithmetic — pair counts multiplied by tick rates — and every load-bearing number in it was wrong in the same direction: it assumed the CPU was the constraint. It is not, and no amount of reading the code would have said so, because the cost that dominates the frame is a *wait* and waits are invisible to inspection.

Two smaller corrections came out of the first drive rather than the design. **A counter has to carry forward across frames its zone did not run in**: the sim ticks at 60 Hz under a render loop several times faster, so most frames run no tick, and the first drive dutifully reported `BODIES=0` in a system full of rocks. Time is a property of the frame and still honestly reports zero; input size is a property of the zone. And **the overlay's fps counter is systematically optimistic on a bimodal frame** — it EMAs the *rate*, `1/deltaSeconds`, so a mix of 0.8 ms and 28 ms frames reads near 490 fps when the true mean is 144. That is the precise blindness the mean/max split was added to remove, and it turned up in the first thing the profiler was pointed at.

**Known gaps.** The GPU is still unmeasured: `render.submit` is known to be a wait rather than work, but what the GPU does underneath it needs timestamp queries, which stay out of scope — that is the natural next perf item and it now has a reason rather than a suspicion. The remaining **~2.1 ms** of the economy burst is structural, not wasteful: the even-spread draw has to look at every rock in the system, and that policy is what lets regrowth hold an equilibrium, so it was left rather than traded away. The economy still bursts once a second instead of amortising across the interval; staggering extractors across ticks would flatten it further and was not needed to get under budget. The rock memo is unbounded — one entry per (system, field) ever asked for, ~276 KB at the extractor set's size, dropped only on `initialize`. Collision keeps its O(n²) pass and its stated contract in `collision.hpp` is still the pre-8f one, now known to be wrong at 138 bodies but not wrong enough to matter. The profiler is single-threaded by construction, has no GPU zones, no flame graph and no capture-to-file, its 64-zone/16-deep table is a compile-time constant, and `SpaceWorld::tick`'s zones are placed by hand because that function is 568 lines with no seams to borrow. Nothing measures the *first* frame after a system load, where the rock memo is cold.

#### Phase 8o — GPU Timing: What the Wait Is Waiting For ✅ (completed 2026-08-17)

Picked with the user as 8n's declared successor. 8n ended with one measured fact and one open question: the frame budget is **6.94 ms**, the game does **~0.7 ms of CPU work**, and the remaining **~6.2 ms sits inside `render.submit` as a wait rather than as work** — proved by holding the scene fixed and sweeping the window across a 13× pixel range for a 0.6% spread. What 8n could not say is *what that wait is waiting for*, because a CPU profiler cannot see across the submit boundary. This item makes the other side of the boundary visible.

**This item is diagnostic by design and may correctly ship no optimisation at all.** 8n's spec named two suspects from arithmetic and measurement refuted both; the lesson recorded there — *a perf spec's suspects must be measured before they are believed* — applies to this spec too, and is why §D below is a measurement with a decision attached rather than a fix with a measurement attached. With 6.2 ms of idle headroom against a 144 Hz display, the honest prior is that **there is nothing to cut**, and the deliverable is the instrument plus the numbers it reads.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **`render.submit` is one zone over four different things, and three of them can block.** `SceneRenderer::drawFrame` (`scene_renderer.cpp:344-407`) runs, in order: `vkWaitForFences(..., UINT64_MAX)` on this frame slot's fence (`:352`); `acquireNextImage` (`:355`); `recordCommands`, which is genuine CPU work — the instance loop, the UI batches, ImGui (`:366`); then `vkQueueSubmit2` and `present` (`:391`, `:394`). 8n proved the zone's 6.2 ms is a *wait*, but the zone cannot say **which** wait, and the three candidates mean different things. A fence block means the CPU is gated behind the GPU or behind present pacing two frames back. An acquire block means the presentation engine is holding images. **This split is pure CPU-side instrumentation, needs no Vulkan feature and no query pool, and it is the precondition for interpreting any GPU number** — a GPU pass time of 2 ms means one thing if the CPU is blocked 6 ms on a fence and something else entirely if it is blocked on acquire. It is the cheapest deliverable in the item and it goes first.
- **Nothing in the engine has ever asked the device whether it can timestamp.** `Context` exposes instance, surface, physical device, device, queue family and queue (`context.hpp:31-39`) and no limits. `context.cpp` calls `vkGetPhysicalDeviceProperties` twice — at `:209` to filter candidates on `apiVersion`, and at `:240` to log the device name — and discards the `VkPhysicalDeviceLimits` both times. GPU timing needs two numbers from there that nothing currently reads: **`limits.timestampPeriod`**, the nanoseconds per tick that converts a raw counter delta into time, and the graphics family's **`VkQueueFamilyProperties::timestampValidBits`**, which says how many low bits of the counter are meaningful. **Both have legitimate failure values.** `timestampValidBits == 0` means the queue cannot timestamp at all — a real case, not a theoretical one — and any code that divides by the period or masks by the bit count without checking will either crash or silently report garbage. The instrument must degrade to "unavailable" and say so, on a device it cannot measure.
- **The command buffer already has exactly the pass boundaries this wants, and they are already named.** `recordCommands` (`scene_renderer.cpp:233-339`) is two brackets: the **HDR scene pass** — `beginHdrScenePass` → the mesh instance loop → planet impostors → the sky → the star → particles → debug lines → `endHdrScenePass` — and the **present pass** — `beginPresentPass` → tonemap → the game UI batches → the dev overlay → `endPresentPass`. `scene_pass.hpp` owns those four entry points. So the zones do not have to be invented; they are read off the structure that is there, which is the opposite of 8n's sim instrumentation, where `SpaceWorld::tick` was 568 lines with no seams and every zone had to be placed by hand.
- **The frame ring is 2, so a GPU number is always about a frame that has already ended.** `kFramesInFlight = 2` (`scene_renderer.hpp:146`) and the pool is reset wholesale per frame (`vkResetCommandPool`, `:365`). Reading a query pool's results for frame N *during* frame N means `vkGetQueryPoolResults` with `WAIT`, which blocks the CPU on the GPU — it would manufacture exactly the stall this item exists to measure, and the reported number would include the instrument. The results for frame N must therefore be read at the top of frame N+2, **after** the `vkWaitForFences` at `:352` has already guaranteed that frame slot's completion, with `VK_QUERY_RESULT_64_BIT` and no wait flag. **This is the load-bearing design constraint of the item**, and it is the same shape as 8n's counter-carry-forward lesson: the number is honest but it is about the past, and pretending otherwise is how a profiler lies.
- **The query pool must be reset on the device timeline, not the host one.** `vkResetQueryPool` (host-side) requires the `hostQueryReset` feature, and `context.cpp:256-260` enables exactly three: `dynamicRendering`, `synchronization2`, `shaderDemoteToHelperInvocation`. Recording `vkCmdResetQueryPool` at the top of the command buffer costs nothing, requires no feature change, and keeps the reset ordered against the writes that follow it. No device-creation change is needed for this item, which is a deliberate constraint: touching `hasRequiredFeatures` would change which GPUs the game runs on, for a dev tool.
- **`vkCmdWriteTimestamp2` measures a pipeline stage reaching a point, not a bracket.** Timestamps are written when prior commands reach the given stage; a "zone" is two of them and its meaning depends entirely on the stage chosen at each end. `TOP_OF_PIPE` at the open and `BOTTOM_OF_PIPE`/`ALL_COMMANDS` at the close is the honest pairing for "how long did this pass take on the device", and **GPU passes overlap**, so the children of a frame will not sum to the frame and must never be presented as if they should. This is the GPU analogue of the inclusive-time rule 8n already settled — and the reason that rule was written as "report the tree, do not subtract" rather than as an implementation detail.

**A. Split `render.submit` first, and let that number stand on its own.** Four CPU zones inside `drawFrame` — `render.waitFence`, `render.acquire`, `render.record`, `render.present` — replacing the single blob. This lands before any Vulkan work and is independently verifiable: the four must sum to what `render.submit` reports today, on the same drive, within noise. If this alone explains the 6.2 ms, the item says so plainly in its verification note rather than continuing to build instrument for the sake of the spec.

**B. `Context` answers what the device can do.** `timestampPeriod()` and `timestampValidBits()` on `rhi::Context`, captured during `initialize` from the properties it already fetches at `:240` and from `vkGetPhysicalDeviceQueueFamilyProperties` for the chosen family. Logged once at startup beside the existing `GPU:` line, because a capability the log does not mention is one nobody checks before disbelieving a number. `timestampValidBits == 0` is reported as unavailable and is not an error: the game runs, the GPU rows read `n/a`.

**C. `sol::rhi::GpuProfiler` — query pools are a device resource, so they live in the RHI layer.** A sibling to `descriptors.hpp` and `resources.hpp`, holding one query pool per frame in flight, a fixed zone table sized at construction, and the two-frame resolve described above. It is handed a command buffer and writes timestamps; it does not know what a scene pass is. `SceneRenderer` places the zones, because the game layer is what knows where the passes are.

**The arithmetic comes out into a dependency-free header so it can be tested.** There is no `rhi.unit` suite and this item does not add one — a suite that needs a Vulkan device is not a headless test, and building the harness for one is a bigger item than this. So the part that can be wrong quietly — masking the counter to `timestampValidBits`, converting a tick delta to milliseconds through `timestampPeriod`, handling wraparound when the end value is numerically below the start, and rejecting a result the pool reported as not-ready — goes in a small plain-integer helper tested in **`core.unit`**, and the Vulkan plumbing around it is verified live by drive. That is the same move `radar_projection.hpp` and `pick.hpp` already make in this repo: the maths is extracted to where it can be asserted, and only the plumbing needs a running game.

**D. The zones, and then the measurement that decides whether anything else happens.** `gpu.frame` end to end, with `gpu.scene` and `gpu.present` inside it from the four `scene_pass.hpp` brackets, and inside the scene pass `gpu.meshes`, `gpu.impostors`, `gpu.sky` and `gpu.particles` — the sky is a full-screen ray-marched pass and is the most plausible fill-rate cost in the frame, which makes it the one zone worth naming in advance. The drive is 8n's resize sweep re-run with GPU rows live: the same scene held fixed across a ~13× pixel range. **That sweep is the whole experiment.** CPU cost did not move with pixels; if `gpu.frame` does move with pixels while the frame budget does not, the GPU is doing real work inside a wait that hides it, and the headroom is quantified for the first time. If `gpu.frame` is a small fraction of 6.94 ms at the largest window, then the display is the only constraint in the game, that is the finding, and **no optimisation follows**.

**E. It reads out where 8n's numbers already read out.** GPU zones report into the same ring the CPU zones use, so `sol.perf` prints one tree, the overlay draws one table, and a drive asserts a GPU budget with the `sol.perf_zone` binding it already has. The zones are prefixed `gpu.` and the report marks them, because **their `last` value is two frames stale by construction** (§ diagnosis) and a reader who does not know that will chase a phantom. Mean and max over the window are unaffected — the same samples, shifted — and that is stated in the header rather than left to be rediscovered.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** Any change to device creation or the feature set (`hostQueryReset` explicitly not enabled — see the diagnosis). Pipeline statistics queries: a different query type answering a different question, and this item is about time. Multi-queue or async-compute timing — there is one queue. A GPU-side flame graph, capture-to-file, or any profiler UI beyond the rows added to the existing table. Shipping the profiler to players: it stays on §2.9's ImGui side of the line with the console and overlay. **Any renderer optimisation**: if §D indicts a pass, that is a finding and a *next* item with its own spec, for the same reason 8n refused to pre-authorise its own §E ladder — and here the prior is stronger, because 6.2 ms of measured idle says the frame is not short of time. Changing the present mode, the frame-in-flight count, or anything else about pacing: the vsync toggle 8k shipped is the existing lever, and re-pacing the game is a design decision, not a profiling one.

**Exit**: `render.submit` is four zones and their sum reconciles with the old single number on the same drive; `Context` reports `timestampPeriod` and `timestampValidBits`, logs them at startup, and a device that cannot timestamp degrades to `n/a` rather than to a crash or a wrong number; `sol.perf` prints GPU rows in the same tree as the CPU rows, marked as two frames stale, and `sol.perf_zone` returns them so a drive can assert a GPU budget; 8n's resize sweep re-run across a ~13× pixel range shows what `gpu.frame` does as pixels scale, and that number is recorded against the 6.94 ms budget whether or not it indicts anything; new headless coverage in `core.unit` for the valid-bits mask, the tick→millisecond conversion, counter wraparound and the not-ready result; all suites green, zero warnings.

*(Verified live 2026-08-17, seed 1701, across two drives — a resize sweep and a console-hidden tree capture at both ends of it. **The item shipped no optimisation, which is the outcome its own spec called the honest prior.** The GPU is not the constraint either, and now that is measured rather than assumed.*

***What the wait was waiting for: the fence, not the acquire, and not the GPU.** Splitting `render.submit` answered §A on the first drive and the split reconciles to four decimal places — at 1904×1041 the children are `waitFence` 5.468 + `acquire` 0.017 + `record` 0.586 + `present` 0.115 = **6.186** against a parent of **6.190**, and the same 0.003–0.004 ms of unattributed overhead appears at all three window sizes. So **93% of the frame is one `vkWaitForFences` call.** `acquire` returns in 0.01 ms, which looks at first like an alibi for the presentation engine and is not one: `acquireNextImage` hands back an index immediately and defers the actual wait into the `imageAvailable` **semaphore**, which the GPU then blocks on before it can start. The cost is real, it is just not billed where a reader would look for it — and that is exactly the kind of thing only a split zone can show.*

***The GPU does scale with pixels, and it does not matter.** Console hidden, scene held static, 624×441 → 1904×1041 (**7.2× the pixels**): `gpu.frame` 0.35 → **0.49 ms** (1.4×), `gpu.scene` 0.10 → 0.22 (2.2×), `gpu.sky` — the full-screen ray-march this spec named in advance as the most plausible fill cost — 0.03 → **0.10 ms** (3.3×), `gpu.meshes` 0.04 → 0.07. That the scene pass responds in the right direction and roughly the right magnitude is the instrument validating itself; that it tops out at **0.49 ms against a 6.94 ms budget — 7% — is the finding.** Saturating the budget at this scaling would take on the order of 14× more GPU work than the game does. `gpu.present` barely moves (0.24 → 0.26) because it is dominated by the fixed-size dev overlay rather than by fill.*

***So both sides of the submit boundary are now measured and both are idle.** 8n: ~0.7 ms of CPU work. 8o: ~0.5 ms of GPU work. Budget 6.94 ms. The remaining ~5.7 ms is the display, and there is nothing in this game to optimise for speed until something changes that.*

*Corroboration worth keeping: with the console hidden the overlay's fps counter reads **147.5 fps / 6.22 ms** at 1080p and 145.3 / 7.52 at 624×441 — i.e. honest, and near the 144 Hz refresh. 8n recorded that counter as systematically optimistic; this run shows the qualifier that note needed, which is that it only lies on a **bimodal** frame. Typing into the dev console is what makes the frame bimodal, and an untouched one reads true.*

*9 new `core.unit` tests (83 → 92) for the valid-bits mask, the full-width case that cannot go through a shift, counter wraparound at both a byte boundary and a 36-bit one, the tick→millisecond conversion, and the difference between an unmeasured result and a measured zero. All **8** suites green, zero warnings, `ctest --preset dev` ~40 s.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** Less than 8n, because this spec was written after 8n taught the lesson — it committed to no fix in advance and its §D was a measurement with a decision attached rather than the reverse. Two things did change shape.

**The readback perturbs what it reads, and the spec did not say so.** The probes are typed into the dev console, which draws ImGui, which lands in `gpu.present`; the ring is 120 frames — about 0.8 s at 144 Hz — so the typing is still inside the window being averaged when Enter lands. The first sweep's `gpu.frame` means bounced between 0.248 and 0.589 across probes at the *same* window size, which is noise from the instrument's own operator rather than signal. The fix is the second drive: hide the console with F1, let the ring refill on an untouched frame, screenshot the overlay's tree. **The console is fine for a CPU zone that ImGui does not touch and wrong for a GPU zone in the present pass** — and `gpu.scene`, which ImGui never enters, was stable across probes throughout, which is what identified the mechanism.

**The sweep could not reach 8n's small end.** 8n swept down to 464×321; this drive floors at 624×441, because the probes have to be *typed*, and the console's input row sits at client y 322 — below the client area of anything shorter. 7.2× instead of 13×, stated rather than quietly narrowed. It does not weaken the conclusion: a cost that reaches 7% of budget after a 7.2× increase is not one the remaining 1.8× was going to indict.

**Known gaps.** The instrument perturbs its subject: both ends of every zone are written at `ALL_COMMANDS` rather than the cheaper `TOP_OF_PIPE`/`BOTTOM_OF_PIPE` pairing, which brackets the zone's own work honestly but costs some overlap between adjacent passes — it over-reports rather than under-reports, which is the right direction to be wrong in, but it is not free. GPU zones are published all-or-nothing per frame, so one unavailable query drops the whole frame's rows rather than part of them. The GPU zone table is 16 zones / 32 queries as a compile-time constant, with no counter equivalent to the CPU side's input-size column — there is nothing yet that reports triangles or fragments against a `gpu.*` row, so a slow pass still cannot say *why*. `gpu.present` mixes the game UI with the dev overlay, so it cannot report what the shipping game's present pass would cost. Nothing is measured on a device without timestamps, because this machine's has them: the degrade path is written and logged but never exercised. The two-frame lag is documented and marked rather than corrected, so a `gpu.*` row's `last` column is about an older frame than the CPU row beside it. And the deeper question 8n opened stays open by choice: **the game leaves ~5.7 ms idle every frame and nothing yet spends it** — that is a design decision about what the game should do with the headroom, not a profiling one, and it should be argued on its own.

#### Phase 8p — A Toggle for the Dev Overlay ✅ (completed 2026-08-17)

**From playtest session 5**, the first note of three: *"there needs to be a toggle added to hide the performance overlay as it takes up a lot of space now."* The word carrying the item is **now** — the overlay did not used to take up a lot of space. **8n added the zone tree and 8o added the `gpu.*` rows**, so this is a legibility regression introduced by the two immediately preceding phases, reported by the first human to play the game since they shipped. It is the smallest item on the board by a wide margin and it goes first for that reason.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands).**

- **The console has a visibility flag and the overlay has none.** `DevUi` holds `m_showConsole` (`dev_ui.hpp:71`), which `F1` flips at `dev_ui.cpp:243-245` and which gates the console's `ImGui::Begin`/`End` pair at `:215-240`. The overlay's own `Begin("perf##overlay")`/`End()` at `:160`/`:213` is **unconditional** — there has never been a way to turn it off, because until 8n it was five short lines and nobody wanted to.
- **What it grew into.** The window is now, in order: an fps/frame-time line, a camera position line, a speed/draws line, a sim line, **three `TextDisabled` crib lines** (`:172-174`), and then the profiler zone tree (`:176-211`) — which is a row per zone across mean/max columns, plus the `gpu.*` rows and their two-frames-stale footnote. The crib and the tree are the bulk, and the fps line is the part a player glancing at it actually wants.
- **A sixth dev key is free.** The fixed dev keys are `F1` console, `F3` debug draw, `F5` shader reload, `F9` save, `F10` load (`main.cpp:798,836,843,852` and `dev_ui.cpp:243`), all five listed in `kReservedKeys` (`input_actions.cpp:114-117`) so the Controls screen cannot bind them. **`F2` is unused and adjacent to the console toggle**, which is the whole argument for it.
- **This is a UI item, not a performance one.** 8o closed performance as a topic — ~0.7 ms CPU + ~0.5 ms GPU against a 6.94 ms budget. Nothing here is about making the game faster, and the overlay's cost is not the complaint; the space it occupies is.

**A. Three states, not two, and this is the one real design decision in the item.** The note asks for a way to hide it, but the *reason* given is space — and a plain on/off toggle answers "space" by also throwing away the fps counter, which is the cheapest and most-wanted line in the window. So `DevUi` gains an `OverlayMode` cycled by `F2`: **Full** (exactly what is drawn today), **Compact** (the fps/frame-time line alone — no camera, no sim, no crib, no tree), **Hidden** (no window at all). `F2` cycles Full → Compact → Hidden → Full. **Default is Full**, so nothing about a cold boot changes and the 8n/8o drives that screenshot the tree keep working untouched.

**B. It lives beside the console flag, engine-side, and is not a game binding.** `m_overlayMode` sits next to `m_showConsole` and is flipped by `ImGui::IsKeyPressed(ImGuiKey_F2)` in the same place `F1` is handled. It deliberately does **not** go through the game's `BindingTable`: `dev_ui.cpp:168-172` already records why the crib says "defaults:" rather than reading the live table — `sol::ui::DevUi` is engine-side and cannot reach the game layer. A dev key that pretends to be a player binding would be the same lie in the other direction. `F2` is added to `kReservedKeys` so the Controls screen cannot hand it out.

**C. The crib line names the new key, because a toggle nobody can discover is not a toggle.** `dev_ui.cpp:174`'s fixed-key line gains `F2 overlay` — and it is the one line that must survive into Compact mode's... no: Compact shows the fps line only, so the crib is gone there by construction. That is the trade, and it is why **Full is the default**: the only state that advertises `F2` is the state you start in.

**D. `ImGui::Begin` and `ImGui::End` must stay paired.** Hidden mode wraps *both* calls, not just the body. This is the one way to get this item wrong quietly — gating only the `Begin` leaves an unmatched `End` and ImGui asserts inside a frame that otherwise looks fine.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** Persisting the mode across runs: it is dev state, `settings.toml` is player settings, and `imgui.ini` already owns window geometry. Any change to what the tree *contains*, to the profiler, or to `sol.perf` — the console readout is the other way to read these numbers and it is untouched. Moving, resizing or restyling the overlay window. Making the overlay player-facing in any form: §2.9's line stands, this is dev tooling. A settings-screen control for it, for the same reason.

**Exit**: `F2` cycles the overlay Full → Compact → Hidden → Full in a running game; Full is byte-identical to what ships today and is what a cold boot shows; Compact leaves the fps/frame-time line and nothing else; Hidden draws no overlay window at all and leaves no unmatched ImGui state (verified by a drive that cycles all the way round twice and keeps rendering); the fixed-key crib names `F2`; `F2` is reserved from the Controls screen; the console (`F1`) and the zone tree's contents are unchanged; all suites green, zero warnings.

*(Verified live 2026-08-17, seed 1701, 1280×720 @ UI scale 1.0, across two drives — a cycle drive and a crib/reserved drive. Every exit criterion met, no surprises, and the change is 3 files.*

***The complaint was an understatement.** The cold-boot screenshot is the argument for the item: in Full the overlay runs from client y 40 to y 750 — **the full height of the window** — and covers the left half of the cockpit, burying the ship name, the hull and shield bars and the assist chip behind the zone tree. In Compact it is a single 150×26 box and the entire HUD is legible. That is the whole item in two images.*

***The cycle is repeatable, which the file sizes prove independently of reading them.** Seven screenshots across two full rounds cluster exactly by state — Full 347.7 KB ×3, Compact 372.4 KB ×2, Hidden 373.4 KB ×2 — and the ordering is the tell: **hiding the overlay makes the PNG bigger**, because what it was covering is starfield and cockpit detail that compresses worse than a flat dark panel. Round 2's Full shot is identical in layout to the cold-boot one with the clock advanced (sim tick 477 → 1107), so nothing latched.*

***No unmatched ImGui state:*** *after six `F2` presses the drive quit through the pause menu and the process returned **exit code 0**, which in this repo is the standing assertion that no Vulkan validation message and no ImGui assert fired. §D's risk — gating the `Begin` without the `End` — was the one way to get this wrong quietly, and it is the thing the round-trip drive exists to catch.*

***Reserved, with a negative control.*** `print(sol.bind("jump","F2"))` answers *"F2 is reserved by the menus and cannot be bound"*, and `sol.bind("jump","K")` immediately after answers *"Jump Through Gate = K"* — so the refusal is about `F2` specifically and not a bind path that stopped working. The crib line, read with the console hidden because the console window sits directly over it, is `F1 console, F2 overlay, F3 debug draw, F5 shaders, F9/F10 save/load (fixed)`. All **8** suites green, zero warnings.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** Nothing — the first item in a while where the spec survived contact unamended. Worth recording *why*, since the streak before it went the other way: the item is three files, touches no simulation and no player-facing state, and its one real design decision (three states rather than two) was settled with the user before any code rather than discovered in a drive. Small items with one decision in them behave; 8n and 8m did not, because their decisions were downstream of measurements nobody had taken yet.

**Known gaps.** The mode is not persisted, by choice (see out of scope), so every run starts in Full — a developer who wants Compact pays two keystrokes per launch, and `imgui.ini` is right there if that ever grates. Compact keeps the fps/frame-time line, which 8n and 8o both established is **the least trustworthy number in the overlay** on a bimodal frame — Compact is the mode most likely to be read at a glance and it shows the one figure that lies under load, with the mean/max columns that would correct it hidden behind two `F2` presses. Hidden hides the game's own numbers along with the dev ones: there is no state that keeps the zone tree while dropping the control crib, which is the split a profiling session would actually want. And the crib is only visible in Full, so a developer who leaves the overlay Hidden has no on-screen reminder that `F2` is what brings it back — the same discoverability trade `F1` has always had.

#### Phase 8q — Remote System Maps ✅ (completed 2026-08-18)

**From playtest session 5**, the second note of three: *view the system map of systems already visited, to plan routes and "other things".* Picked with the user as 8p's successor over the comms item, which is larger and splits in two. The complaint is exact: the galaxy map tells you a system exists, who owns it and how many stations it has, and then the System tab beside it can only ever show you the system you are standing in — so "should I go there?" is a question the map screen refuses to answer about anywhere you are not.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **The System tab is hardwired to *here* in six places, and they are all in the fill.** `fillMapPanel` reads the live nav list at `map_ui.cpp:240` (`world.navTargets()`) and `world.currentSystemIndex()` at `:235`, `:256`, `:258`, `:269` and `:274`. `map_screen.cpp` — the draw — reads no system index at all: it draws whatever markers it is handed, against `panel.hubPosition`. **So the whole of this item lands on one side of the fill/execute seam**, and the two-tier projection, the label de-collider, the zoom/pan transform and every glyph come along unchanged. That is the seam doing its job, and it is why this item is smaller than the backlog estimated.
- **⚑ Nothing needs regenerating from a system seed. The content is already resident, for every system, right now.** The backlog note assumed a remote system's bodies would have to be rebuilt the way `MiningSim::rocksFor` rebuilds rocks. They do not: `SystemSpec` (`universe.hpp:92-104`) carries `planets`, `stations` and `gates` **with positions in sim space**, plus `starRadius` and `primaryPlanet`, and `Galaxy` holds one per system in memory from generation onward. `SpaceWorld::rebuildSystemSideData` (`space_world.cpp:1977-2012`) is a pure read of that spec — the star is named for the system and sits at the origin (`:1979`), planets and stations copy straight across, a gate is `"Gate: " + destination name` (`:1987`). Everything else the map draws is already exposed per-system and knowledge-gated: `SurveySim::signalsFor(galaxy, system, out)`, `MiningSim::fieldsFor(galaxy, system, out)`, `MiningSim::wrecksIn(system, out)`, `SurveySim::bookmarksIn(system, out)`, and `MissionObjective::system`. **There is no new content path in this item and no new state of any kind.** The save stays **v10** and `settings.toml` is untouched.
- **The fog rule has a home already, and it is one function short.** `map_projection.hpp:76-81` declares `systemVisible` under the comment *"This is the fog rule in one place: everything that draws goes through it"*, with `laneVisible` beside it. Both are galaxy-tier. There is no marker-tier equivalent because until now the only system ever drawn was one the player was standing in, where the answer is always yes.
- **Two of the three markers that need per-object gating are already bitmasked.** `SurveySim` holds `signalsDiscovered`/`signalsResolved`/`signalsEmptied` per system (`survey.hpp:265-272`) and answers them for any index, so a remote system's signals can be filtered by exactly the bits the local map filters by. Fields need no gate at all — `space_world.cpp:652-653` records that an asteroid field is *"a visible thing in the playfield"* known on sight — and a `WreckRecord` only exists because the player watched something die there.
- **The detail column is distances from the ship, which is a meaningless number four jumps away.** `map_ui.cpp:253` measures every marker against `world.shipState().position`, and `:260` formats it. `MapMarkerRow::targeted` (`:309`) and the footer's Set Target / Autopilot / Delete are all statements about the *current* system's nav list, indexed by nav-target slot — `executeMapAction`'s `SelectMarker`, `Autopilot` and `DeleteBookmark` cases all pass `action.index` straight into `world.selectTarget()` or `world.navTargetBookmark()`. **A remote marker row is not a nav-target index and must never be handed to one of those.** This is the one way to get the item wrong destructively rather than cosmetically.
- **`signalTargetName` is in an anonymous namespace and would have to be written twice.** `space_world.cpp:533-545` turns (kind, resolved, emptied, slot) into `Contact 3` / `Derelict Hull` / `Supply Cache (empty)`. It reads nothing off `SignalInstance` except `kind`. A second copy in `map_ui.cpp` is how the local and remote maps come to call the same site two different things six months from now.

**A. The fog rule goes beside the other two, as a pure function, and gets tested.** `markerVisible(MapKnowledge tier, MapMarkerRow::Kind kind)` joins `systemVisible`/`laneVisible` in `map_projection.hpp`, under the same comment that already claims to hold the fog rule in one place. **Agreed with the user before any code: the tier gates the *categories*, and the per-object bits gate the *individuals*.**

| Rung | Star | Planets | Stations | Gates | Fields | Signals | Wrecks | Bookmarks |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Unknown | — | — | — | — | — | — | — | — |
| Charted | ✓ | — | — | — | — | — | — | ✓ |
| Visited | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | discovered only | ✓ | ✓ |
| Surveyed | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | all (by definition) | ✓ | ✓ |

Charted is the ladder's own words — *"named by a gate you have stood at; contents blank"* (`survey.hpp:37`) — so it gets the star, which is the system, and its name, and a footer line saying to go and look. Surveyed adds no new *category*: it is Visited with every signal bit already set, which is what makes the rule two columns rather than four. Bookmarks ignore the tier for the reason `map_ui.cpp:161-163` already gives about the galaxy map — a bookmark is the player's own knowledge and they were standing there when they made it. **A signal is filtered by `signalDiscovered`, not by the rung**, which is the half of the rule the table cannot show and the half that keeps a remote system honest: an unswept system shows no sites even at Surveyed-adjacent rungs, because it has none discovered.

**B. A second fill path, keyed on a system index, sharing every naming rule with the first.** `fillMapPanel` gains a remote branch: when the system being viewed is not the current one, markers come from `Galaxy` + the two content sims instead of from `navTargets()`, in the same order the target cycle uses (stations, gates, planets, star, then signals, fields, wrecks, bookmarks) so a player who knows one map can read the other. `signalTargetName` is **hoisted** to take `sim::SignalKind` and declared in `space_world.hpp` beside `NavTarget` — moved, not copied, which is the ruling 8j made when `project()` went into `pick.hpp`. The local path is not rewritten: it stays exactly as it is, because it has facts the remote path cannot have (live rock counts, wreck cargo, the target ring) and pretending otherwise would cost the current system information to buy the remote one consistency it does not need.

**C. The System tab follows the Galaxy tab's selection.** Also agreed with the user before any code. `MapScreenState` already keeps `selectedSystem` across frames; `MapPanel` gains a `viewSystem` **input** field, set from it in `main.cpp` exactly as `tradeCommodity` is at `main.cpp:1115`, and read by the fill. This is the existing precedent for view state that the fill needs, down to the one-frame lag, which is invisible because the selection cannot change on the same frame the tab does. No new widget: you find a system on the galaxy map, press System, and you are looking inside it. The header names the system being displayed and how far away it is — `routeBetween` already answers the jump count — and a **Show Current** footer button snaps back. Viewing an Unknown system is not reachable, because `drawSystemList` never lists one.

**D. Every action that means "here" is disabled when the view is remote, and the two that still mean something stay.** Set Target and Autopilot go grey: there is no nav-target index to hand them and `executeMapAction` must never receive a remote row (see the diagnosis). Delete stays live for a bookmark, because a bookmark is galaxy-wide state addressed by a never-reused id — but it can no longer come off `navTargetBookmark()`, so `MapMarkerRow` gains the bookmark id the way it gained `selection` for the radar in 8j, and `DeleteBookmark` carries the id rather than the row. **Plot Route joins the System tab's footer**, because "plan routes" is the note's own stated payoff and it is the one action that is *more* meaningful remotely than locally. `MapMarkerRow::targeted` is false throughout a remote view, and the ship glyph is not drawn — `MapPanel::hasShip` is exactly the flag for that and `map_screen.cpp:450` already honours it.

**E. The detail column answers a different question when the view is remote.** Distance-from-ship is replaced by distance from that system's own star, which is what the map is drawn against anyway, and the per-marker extras that need live state are dropped rather than guessed: no rock counts (that is a `rocksFor` walk per field for a number the player has not earned), no wreck cargo, no scan state on a body beyond what `bodyScanned` already says. A station's detail names its archetype instead. The footer's summary line carries the rung and the jump count, so the one thing a remote map must never do — imply it is as current as the local one — is said in words on screen.

**F. A console binding, so a drive can assert the fog rule without reading pixels.** `sol.system_map(index)` prints the marker list the fill would produce for that system, one row per marker with kind and name. This is how the Charted/Visited difference is verified against a system the player has genuinely only been told about, rather than by squinting at two screenshots — the same role `sol.contacts` and `sol.pick` play for 8h and 8j.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** Any save-format change: there is no new state, and a stale-snapshot fog (option C when this was put to the user) was declined precisely because it would need one. Remote **prices** beyond what 8g's market memory already colours the galaxy map with — the trade overlay is a galaxy-tier feature and stays there. Jumping, auto-jump, or any change to route execution: `decisions/005` stands and Plot Route still only plots. Making a remote marker targetable, autopilot-able, or bookmarkable from a distance. Regenerating **rocks** for a remote field, or any per-rock state (§E). A third map tier or any change to the two-tier projection, the zoom/pan transform, or the label de-collider. Showing NPC ships, traders or patrols in a remote system: the coarse layer knows where they are, but a live enemy count four jumps away is a scouting feature and a different item. Anything about comms — the third session-5 note is untouched and stays on the backlog.

**Exit**: selecting a Visited system on the galaxy map and switching to the System tab draws that system's star, planets, stations, gates and fields, with its bookmarks and only its discovered signals; a Charted system draws its star alone and says why; an Unknown system cannot be reached from the list; the current system is unchanged from what ships today, ship glyph and target ring included; Set Target and Autopilot are disabled on a remote view and Plot Route works from the System tab; Delete still removes the right bookmark after another bookmark elsewhere in the galaxy has been deleted; Show Current returns to where the player is; `sol.system_map(index)` prints a marker list that matches the screen at every rung; new headless coverage in `ui.unit` for `markerVisible` across all four rungs and all nine marker kinds; save format unchanged at v10; all suites green, zero warnings.

*(Verified live 2026-08-18, seed 1701, 1280×720 @ UI scale 1.0, across five drives — a fog-rule probe, a screen drive, a per-object-bits drive, a remote-delete drive and a fields drive. Every exit criterion met. 9 files, ui 83 → 84, save unchanged at **v10**.*

***The item was much smaller than the backlog estimated, and the diagnosis is why.*** *The note was written expecting a remote system's contents to be regenerated from its seed the way `MiningSim::rocksFor` regenerates rocks. They never needed to be: `SystemSpec` carries every system's planets, stations and gates **with positions**, resident from generation onward, and the two content sims already answer `signalsFor`/`fieldsFor`/`wrecksIn`/`bookmarksIn` for any index. The whole item landed on one side of the fill/execute seam — `map_screen.cpp` reads no system index at all — so the two-tier projection, the label de-collider and the zoom transform came along untouched. **Reading the code before believing the estimate is what turned a "medium item" into an afternoon.***

***The fog rule reads correctly at every rung, and the console probe is what proves it.** `sol.system_map(i)` runs the real fill rather than a parallel read, so a rung's marker list is asserted without reading a pixel. At a cold boot it classifies the galaxy in one probe: **`unknown=75 known=5 charted=8 visited=0`** — the starting system plus its four gate destinations, which is exactly what `notifyArrival` should have produced. Charted answers `Saria - charted, 1 jump(s) away` / `star Saria - charted only` / `1 marker(s)`; the same system after a visit answers 11 markers. **An Unknown system makes the probe refuse rather than substitute** — the screen falls back to the current system (correct, since such a system cannot be selected from the list) but a probe that did the same would report on system 3 when asked about system 42 and look exactly like a fog-rule bug.*

***The per-object signal bit was proven in both directions, and the first attempt failed to set up its own precondition.** A pulse fired 469,563 km from the hub reported `0 new contact(s) within 250000 km`, so the site stayed undiscovered and the remote map showed 11 markers with no signal row — the negative half, by accident. Warping into the playfield first (`sol.warp(i, 20000, 0, 0)` across four stations, pulsing at each) discovered it, and the same remote map then read **`signal Contact 1 - 105120 km - unscanned`**. Named `Contact 1` rather than `Derelict Hull`, because discovering a site is not identifying it — the remote map inherits that distinction from `signalTargetName` for free, which is the argument for having hoisted it rather than written it twice.*

***Delete on a remote bookmark deletes the right one — the defect the spec named as the only destructive way to get this wrong.** Two bookmarks, `mark-A` (id 1) in Lyrioa and `mark-B` (id 2) in Saria; standing in Saria, selecting `* mark-A` on Lyrioa's remote map and clicking Delete left **`2: mark-B` / `1 bookmark(s), 1 in Saria`**. The pre-8q path read the id off `navTargetBookmark(row)`, which is only answerable for the system the player is in, so row 9 of a remote system would have deleted whatever occupied slot 9 at home. Confirmed separately that ids survive a deletion elsewhere: deleting id 2 leaves `mark-A` still at id 1.*

***Plot Route from the System tab is the note's own stated payoff and it works***: clicking it while looking at Lyrioa lit the Saria→Lyrioa lane on the galaxy tab, tagged Lyrioa's row `route`, and enabled Clear Route. **Show Current** returns the header, the summary line and the two footer actions to the local set. A remote **asteroid field** was verified separately by gate-walking to Queis and looking back at it (`field Asteroid Field 1 - 250715 km`), because Lyrioa and Saria both rolled zero fields and the field branch would otherwise never have run. All **8** suites green, zero warnings.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** Three things, two of them found only by looking at the screen.

**Distances had to become two-tier, and the spec said "from that system's own star".** That is what shipped first, and it was wrong in a way only a screenshot shows: everything in the playfield orbits the primary planet, so at Lyrioa the three stations and four gates all read ~51 Mkm — the hub's orbital radius, reported seven times, with nothing distinguishable from anything else. Measuring each marker from the origin of **its own tier** — planets from the star, playfield things from the hub — is both more informative and the same split the map already draws: `MapMarkerRow::inPlayfield` was already the flag for which origin a marker belongs to. The gates then read exactly `600000 km`, which is `GalaxyParams::gateDistance`, and that is the instrument validating itself. The star's own distance is suppressed, because zero-by-construction is noise.

**A Charted system leaked the position of its primary planet.** The playfield bubble is pinned at `panel.hubPosition` and drawn unconditionally, so a system showing nothing but its star still drew a ring — around the exact spot where the inhabited part of a system the player has never visited happens to be. Fixed in both places it could be fixed: the fill sends a zero hub below Visited, and `drawSystemMap` skips the bubble when no marker is `inPlayfield`. **The fog rule is not only about which rows exist; it is about everything the picture implies.**

**Set Target and Autopilot are swapped out rather than greyed out.** The spec said grey. But the footer already carries five buttons and route planning is the whole point of the feature, so greying two and appending two would have made seven — at 118 px each that eats the detail cell at any window narrower than this one. Swapping keeps the count and the layout identical and gives the remote view the two actions that are *more* meaningful there than at home. The cost is that a player never sees Set Target greyed with a reason, which is a discoverability trade rather than a defect; the summary line says `visited, 1 jump(s) away` right above the list, which is the reason.

**Known gaps.** **Remote content is live, not remembered**: a station demolished or a wreck decayed while the player is away would still be drawn, because the map reads today's galaxy through yesterday's knowledge flags. That is a deliberate consequence of declining the snapshot fog (out of scope) and it is invisible today only because nothing in the sim removes a station. Rock counts and wreck cargo are dropped from a remote view rather than estimated, so a picked-over field looks identical to a full one until you fly there — the one number a route planner would most want is the one the item refuses to invent. **A station's row carries only its distance**: naming its archetype needs a def lookup and `SpaceWorld` exposes no `defs()` accessor, so the spec's §E line about archetypes was dropped rather than half-built. The **Wreck and Objective** marker kinds are never exercised on a remote map by any drive — they share the identical `add` path and visibility rule as the seven kinds that are, but "structurally identical" is an argument, not a measurement. Fields are shown at Visited with no discovery bit of their own, which matches the local nav list's "known on sight" rule but means a system you jumped straight through reveals its fields. The System tab has no selection independent of the galaxy tab, so comparing two systems means losing your place on the galaxy map. `viewSystem` is not persisted and resets to the current system on load. And the detail column still clips from the left at 96 px, so any detail longer than a distance survives in the row as a mid-word tail — 8i recorded this and 8q worked around it by keeping the star's detail to two words rather than by fixing the cell.

---

#### Phase 8r — Docking Clearance ✅ (completed 2026-08-18)

**From playtest session 5**, the third and last note — its **docking half**. The note asks for two things under one heading, *comms*: speak to other pilots for tips about where to go, and **request docking permission, then park at a designated port**. The backlog has said since it was written that these are really two items and that splitting them is the obvious scoping move; the user picked this half. **The pilot-info half stays on the backlog untouched.**

The complaint is exact. Docking today is one keypress from two kilometres out and then you are inside: no request, no answer, no approach, and no port. The one rule that does exist — a hostile owner refuses you — announces itself to the *console log*, where a player never looks.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **⚑ `isDocked()` is not the state machine, and the backlog's estimate is wrong the same way 8q's was.** `isDocked()` is `m_dockedStation != kNoIndex` (`space_world.hpp:388`) and every one of its ~65 call sites in `space_world.cpp` asks one question: *am I inside the station.* Trade, refit, hire, accept a mission, sell the survey ledger, start a refine job, the `sol.warp` refusal, and the "a docked ship takes no damage" safe-room rule all ask exactly that and all keep exactly today's answer. **A clearance only exists while the ship is NOT docked**, so it is a second, *disjoint* piece of state rather than a widening of the first. No call site needs auditing. What actually changes is `tryDockNearestStation` (one function, `:2116-2169`), the interact key at `main.cpp:589`, the HUD's `dockInRange` chip (`game_ui.cpp:524`), and the dev shortcut at `content.cpp:202`.
- **The station is a 100 m collision sphere inside a 130 m avoidance sphere, so a berth cannot sit on the hull.** `modelBaseRadius(ModelId::Station)` is `100.0` (`space_world.cpp:81`) and the player physically bounces off it; `loadSystem` pushes a `kStationRadiusMeters = 130.0` sphere per station into `m_obstacles` (`:2031`), which NPC steering and autopilot both dodge. The drawn station is a torus of major radius 90 and tube 12 (`gen_assets.ps1:376-388`), so its outer edge is at **102 m — inside both spheres**. A berth is therefore a marked volume *off* the ring, not a hole in it, and its capture sphere must not reach inside 130 m or autopilot would be steering away from the place it was told to go.
- **The current dock point is one point 250 m straight up, shared by every station in the galaxy.** `dockPoint` (`:2108-2114`) returns `station.position + {0, 250, 0}`, with a comment that says why: outside the collision sphere, close enough to read as parked. `undock` releases at +500 m (`:2180`). There is **no notion of a port, a pad, a berth or a bearing anywhere in the codebase**, and `StationSpec` (`universe.hpp:79-84`) carries only name, archetype and position.
- **Autopilot already carries the exact special case this needs, and 8i is what put it there.** `autopilotArrivalRange` (`:3315-3332`) stands off by `surfaceRadius + max(1500, surfaceRadius * 0.5)` and then clamps that for *"the one target whose whole point is arriving"* — a mission `FlyTo`, whose radius is smaller than the general standoff. **A cleared berth is the second instance of that shape**, and 8i's ruling applies verbatim: the engage message and the steering must read *one* number, or autopilot advertises a range it does not fly.
- **NPC ships never dock, so berth occupancy is not simulated anywhere.** The trader fleet is coarse-sim (the Phase 7 caveat still stands) and the only ships in the bubble are patrols and raiders; nothing in `space_world.cpp` ever sets `m_dockedStation` for anybody but the player. **"All berths full" would therefore be invented state**, which is why a queue is named out of scope below rather than half-built.
- **There is no comms surface of any kind.** `FlightHud` (`screens.hpp:105-200`) has prompts, chips and readouts but no message list, and no phase has ever needed one. The prompt chips (`game_ui.cpp:497-560`) are built from live bindings since 8k and sit under the crosshair.
- **The hook pattern this wants is already written four times.** `signal_loot`, `signal_found`, `wreck_loot` and `mission_board` all run *C++ enumerates → Lua composes → C++ validates*, each with a scriptless default already in place and a `m_xHookFailed` latch that disables the hook until scripts reload (`content.cpp:2276-2318`). A docking dispatcher is that shape exactly.

**A. A berth is a pure function of the station, and it lives in the engine so it can be tested.** New `engine/sim/include/sol/sim/docking.hpp`, a pure header in the shape `map_projection.hpp` took for the fog rule — the geometry in one place, with the reason beside it:

- `kBerthCount = 4`, evenly spaced around the station's equator.
- `kBerthRingRadius = 200.0` m — 70 m clear of the avoidance sphere, 100 m clear of the hull.
- `kBerthCaptureRadius = 60.0` m, so the capture sphere spans **140 m–260 m** from the station centre and **never reaches inside the 130 m avoidance sphere**. That margin is the whole reason these two numbers are what they are, and the test asserts it rather than the comment claiming it.
- `kBerthApproachSpeed = 50.0` m/s. The shuttle tops out at 220 and the freighter at 120, so this is a real gate: you arrive slow or you fly through.
- `berthPoint(stationPosition, berth)` and `inBerth(shipPosition, shipSpeed, berthPoint)`.

Berths are **derived, never stored** — the same ruling 8q made about remote map content. `StationSpec` is untouched and nothing new is serialised.

**B. The clearance is disjoint state, and that is the load-bearing decision.** `SpaceWorld` gains one member and a comment saying why it is not folded into `m_dockedStation`:

```
struct DockClearance {
    std::uint32_t station = kNoIndex;   // station index in the current system
    std::uint32_t berth   = kNoIndex;   // which berth was assigned
    double secondsLeft    = 0.0;        // kClearanceSeconds, ticked down
};
```

`isDocked()` is not touched, not widened, and not renamed. A clearance is dropped on jump (`loadSystem`, on the line that already drops autopilot for the same reason — the target list is about to change under it), on docking, on expiry, and when the owner faction goes hostile. `kClearanceSeconds = 180.0`, which is long enough to fly 2 km at any speed and short enough that a forgotten clearance does not follow you around the system all session.

**C. The request, on the key that already means "interact", with a precedence ladder that changes nothing that works today.** The interact binding (`Action::DockSalvage`) already carries dock and salvage, and 8e settled that a station in range beats a wreck. The ladder becomes, in order:

1. docked → undock (unchanged);
2. a station within `kDockRange` (2 km, unchanged) → dock or request (see §D);
3. salvage in range → salvage (unchanged);
4. **new:** a station within `kDockRequestRange = 20'000.0` m → request clearance.

Rung 4 is the only addition and it fires only where nothing happened before, so **no existing behaviour moves** — in particular a wreck 5 km from a station still salvages on the same key it always did. Calling ahead at 20 km is what makes "request first, then fly in" a sequence rather than a formality.

**D. Who answers, and in what words: `dock_request`, the fifth hook of that shape.** C++ enumerates (station name, owner faction name, the player's standing with it, `kBerthCount`, and one seeded roll — the only entropy the hook gets); Lua composes through `sol.grant_docking(berth, message)` / `sol.deny_docking(message)`; C++ validates the berth index against the count and keeps the message. The **scriptless default** denies a hostile owner in the words `tryDockNearestStation:2135-2143` already logs and otherwise grants `floor(roll * kBerthCount)`, so the feature works with no scripts at all. `game/data/scripts/init.lua` gets a modest authored implementation so a refusal sounds like a person; a hook that errors latches off until scripts reload, as the other four do.

**E. The berth is a nav target, which is how it gets a radar blip, a marker, and autopilot for free.** `NavKind::Berth` is appended as a tenth kind by `rebuildDynamicTargets` while a clearance is live — one slot, exactly the shape 8i gave `NavKind::Objective`, and for exactly 8i's reason: a slot buys the radar dot, the world marker, the target cycle, the map marker and Autopilot in one move rather than five. **Granting selects it**, since the player just asked for it and it is certainly the thing they want. When the clearance ends the slot is compacted out, and the disengage-on-removal rule 8i already wrote ("Autopilot: disengaged, the destination is gone") handles the ship that was flying to it — that path is reused, not rewritten. `autopilotArrivalRange` gains its second clamp: for a berth, `min(range, kBerthCaptureRadius * 0.5)` — 30 m, well outside the 130 m avoidance sphere at a berth 200 m out, so autopilot can actually reach its own goal. Message and steering read one number, per 8i.

**F. Arrival docks you; anything else refuses you in words.** Each tick, if a clearance is live and the ship is inside its berth's capture sphere below `kBerthApproachSpeed`, docking happens — the existing `tryDockNearestStation` body from `m_dockedStation = nearest` onward, unchanged, so `notifyDock`, `processMissionDeliveries`, `recordDockedMarket` and the `m_dockEventPending` flag all fire exactly as they do today. Entering a berth **without** clearance, or after a denial, does not dock and instead sends one comms line saying why, throttled so it is said once per approach rather than once per tick. This is the enforcement the user picked: **refused, with a reason, and no new punishment system** — no standing penalty and no station turrets. Undock releases at the berth rather than 500 m above the station.

**G. The comms line, which is the seam the pilot-info half will reuse.** `SpaceWorld` holds a short ring of `CommsMessage { from, text, secondsLeft }` (three lines, `kCommsMessageSeconds = 8.0`), exposed as a span; `FlightHud` gains it as `std::span<const CommsLine>` the way `radarContacts` arrived in 8h; `game_ui.cpp` draws it top-left, clipped to the aperture in the cockpit. **This is a message list, not a comms screen** — the pilot-info half is what turns it into a conversation, and the point of building it here is that the other half inherits a channel instead of starting from nothing. Its placement is verified at **1.25 UI scale on a 720p window**, which 8m established is the layout that breaks first, against 8m's rule that panels are pushed relative to the radar disc rather than the screen edge.

**H. Console, so a drive can assert the state machine without reading pixels.** `sol.request_dock()` performs the real request; `sol.clearance()` prints station, berth, distance and seconds left; `sol.berths(station)` prints the four berth points and their distance from the hull. **`sol.dock()` keeps its current meaning as the dev shortcut** — grant and park, bypassing the flight — because every drive script in [[solproject-drive-script]] uses it and breaking them to make a point is not worth it.

**Save v10, unchanged.** A clearance is transient and is dropped on load exactly as it is on a jump; `settings.toml` is untouched; no new binding (the interact key already exists), so the action count stays at 35.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** **Berth occupancy, queues, or "all berths are full"** — nothing in the sim docks an NPC, so any busy signal would be invented state, and inventing it is how a feature comes to lie. **NPC ships requesting clearance or using berths**, and promoting coarse traders to bubble ships (the Phase 7 caveat stands). **Station turrets or any new hostility path** — the user chose refusal-with-a-reason, and a station that shoots needs a hostility system stations do not have. **Any standing penalty for barging.** **A comms screen, hailing arbitrary ships, or dialogue** — that is the pilot-info half and it stays on the backlog. **Docking as a skill test**: no alignment gate, no orientation gate, no fuel, nothing beyond the one speed gate. **Berth count per archetype in `stations.toml`** — a count varies nothing until occupancy is real. **Persisting a clearance across save/load.** **The death-respawn re-dock**, which bypasses clearance on purpose and must keep doing so: dock is the safe room (`decisions/007`), and a player who died at a station whose dispatcher would now refuse them has no move left. **Landing on planets.**

**Exit**: hailing a station 15 km out returns a named berth in a comms line on the HUD; the berth appears on the radar, in the target cycle and on the system map, and is selected on grant; flying into it below 50 m/s docks you, with the mission board, market snapshot and dock event firing exactly as they do today; arriving fast flies you through it; entering a berth with no clearance refuses in words rather than silently; a hostile owner denies the request in words instead of writing to a log nobody reads; Autopilot engaged on the berth arrives *inside* it and the range it prints is the range it flies; the clearance expires after 180 s, on a jump, and when the owner turns hostile, and autopilot disengages with the destination gone rather than flying on to whatever slid into the slot; `sol.clearance()` tracks every one of those transitions; undock releases at the berth; the death respawn still re-docks unasked; new `sim.unit` coverage for berth geometry asserting the capture sphere clears the 130 m avoidance sphere; save format unchanged at v10; all 8 suites green, zero warnings.

*(Verified live 2026-08-18, seed 1701, 1280×720 @ UI scale 1.0, across four drives — a clearance drive, a refusal drive, a layout drive and a lifecycle drive. Every exit criterion met bar one, which is asserted headlessly instead and named below. 13 files, sim 99 → **103**, save unchanged at **v10**, no new binding (the interact key already existed), so the action count stays at 35.*

***The full sequence works and the console is what proves it.** `sol.request_dock()` from 3,000 m — outside the 2 km dock band, inside the 20 km hail range, i.e. rung 4 of the ladder and the only rung this item added — answers `You: Requesting docking clearance.` then `Lyrioa Alpha: Cleared for berth 1. Mind your approach.`, and `sol.clearance()` reads **`Lyrioa Alpha berth 1 - 2800 m away, 177 s left`**. The target readout says `Berth 1`, so the grant selected it. `sol.autopilot(1500)` then flies the approach and arriving docks: `Docking clamps engaged. Welcome aboard.`, the station screen opens on the Trade tab with its market report, and `sol.docked_at()` answers `Lyrioa Alpha` — the mission board, the market snapshot and the dock event all fired through `completeDock` exactly as they did before there was a berth.*

***The arrival clamp is visible in one line of log: `Autopilot: flying to 'Berth 1' (arrive 0.0 km out)`.** 30 m rounds to 0.0 km, which is 8i's rule holding — the message and the steering read one number — and it is what puts the ship *inside* the 60 m capture sphere instead of parking 1.5 km short of it. **The berth ring radius is validated by the instrument, not by the comment**: warping to the station-relative offset `(200, 0, 0)` lands exactly in berth 1 and triggers the uncleared refusal, and undocking from berth 1 leaves the ship at **`station 300 m`** — 200 m of ring plus the 100 m release push, to the metre.*

***Every refusal path says something, which is the whole complaint this item started from.** No clearance and sitting in a port: `Berth 1 is not yours. Hail us or stand off.` Hostile owner: `Clearance denied. Solar Navy wants you gone.` (authored) with `sol.clearance()` still `no clearance`. Cancelling with the interact key: the chip reads `[G] CANCEL (BERTH 1)` while cleared and reverts to `[G] REQUEST DOCKING` after. **Revocation was the best of the four, because it proved something inherited rather than written**: `sol.set_rep(1, -60)` mid-approach produced `Clearance revoked. Leave the approach lane.` *and* `Autopilot: disengaged, the destination is gone` — 8i's compaction rule catching the vanishing slot with no new code at all.*

***The safe room still bypasses the dispatcher.** Ramming the station at boost killed the player, who woke **docked at Lyrioa Alpha** with credits 1000 → 540 (the deductible), exactly as `decisions/007` requires — a respawn that could be refused would leave a dead player with no move. **A save/load drops the clearance**: cleared for berth 3 with 178 s left, saved, loaded, `no clearance`, comms panel gone, target readout back on the station.*

***The one criterion not verified live is the speed gate.*** *Arriving fast is asserted in `sim.unit` (`docking_in_berth_needs_both_distance_and_speed`, which checks both halves and the boundary in both directions) rather than in a drive, because there is no console lever that puts the ship inside a 60 m sphere at a chosen speed, and steering manually through one at cruise is not a repeatable measurement. Said plainly rather than counted as met. All **8** suites green on a clean rebuild, zero warnings.)*

**What driving it changed about the plan.** Two things, and the first is a layout rule this repo keeps re-learning.

**The comms panel clipped its own sentences, and the first fix made it worse.** At 440 px the message cell held ~35 characters and every line lost its tail — "Cleared for berth 1. Mind your appro". The reflex fix was to clamp the width against the opening (`panelArea.width() * 0.45`), which at 1280×720 produced **391 px, narrower than the value it replaced**. The right clamp is expressed against the **target readout it must not collide with** (`panelArea.width() - kTargetPanelWidth - 28`), which is 8m's ruling about pushing the side panels off the radar disc, one panel over: *the thing a panel must give way to is the other panel, not the screen.* Verified at 900×640 with a 1.25 UI scale — 8m's known-worst layout — where the panel shrinks and the sentence clips rather than overlapping. **The other half of the fix was in the strings**: every message named the station, which the sender column already says. Dropping the repetition is what made them fit.

**A clearance survived a save/load, and the reason is worth recording because the fix was already written.** `loadSave` does not go through `loadSystem`, so the reset added there never ran on a load — and the first lifecycle drive read a stale binary because the edit had not been rebuilt, which made a two-line omission look like a design flaw. **`m_dockedBerth` in particular has to be reset explicitly on load**: it is live state from the run being replaced, and left alone it would park a loaded ship in a berth it was never assigned, at a station it may never have visited.

**Known gaps.** **Berth occupancy does not exist**, so all four berths are always free and "all berths full" is a sentence the game cannot say — deliberate, since nothing in the sim docks an NPC and a busy signal would be invented state, but it does mean the dispatcher is choosing between four identical options. **A berth is not visible**: nothing is drawn at the ring, so the port is a radar blip and a HUD marker rather than a lit pad on the hull, and the station mesh has no bay to fly into. The **speed gate has no UI** — there is no "too fast" cue on approach, so a pilot who overshoots learns it by flying through the berth and being told nothing. The clearance is **not persisted**, by choice, so a save made on approach wakes up with the berth to re-request. **Only the player ever asks for clearance**; patrols and raiders ignore berths entirely. The refusal throttle is a flat 8 s rather than per-station, so drifting between two stations' berths says one thing and then goes quiet. A berth draws with the **station's own radar and map glyph**, which at map scale is the same pixel as the station itself — the name is the only thing separating them. And **`kBerthCount` is a compile-time 4 for every archetype**, so a mining outpost has as many ports as a trade hub; the count is data the moment occupancy makes it mean anything.

#### Phase 8s — Pilot Comms ✅ (completed 2026-08-18)

**From playtest session 5**, the third note's **pilot-info half** — *speak to other pilots to gain tips about locations worth visiting* — and the last item left on the backlog. 8r shipped the docking half and deliberately built its comms surface as a general channel rather than a docking one, so this item arrives at a seam that was left for it on purpose.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **The channel exists and is general by construction, so this item writes no new HUD surface.** `say(from, text)` (`space_world.cpp:2272-2279`) pushes onto a three-line ring of `CommsMessage { from, text, secondsLeft }` at `kCommsMessageSeconds = 8.0`; `FlightHud::comms` is a `std::span<const CommsLine>` (`screens.hpp:210-214`) and `game_ui.cpp` draws it top-left of the glass, clamped against the target panel. Nothing in any of it is docking-specific.
- **Addressing is already solved, and there is exactly one selection.** `C` cycles ship contacts, `targetIsContact()` (`space_world.hpp:790`) answers whether the current selection is a ship, and `contactInfo(slot)` (`space_world.cpp:3031-3056`) already returns that pilot's display name, faction name and the player's attitude toward their faction — **everything a hail needs to name its recipient is already computed every frame for the HUD readout.** No new list, no new cycle, no second definition of "who is selected".
- **A pilot already has a role, a faction, and a Lua think.** `ShipPilot` (`space_world.hpp:135-147`) carries `PilotRole` — Fighter / Trader / Patrol (`:118-123`) — plus `PilotState` and `factionIndex`, and `pilot_think(ship, role, state, attitude)` (`init.lua:79`) has been handing Lua exactly those strings since Phase 8b. **The role split this item's design rests on is a field that already exists**, not one it invents.
- **Both tip stores exist, and both are already wired to a screen the player reads.** `SurveySim::recordMarket(market, prices, now)` (`survey.hpp:222`) is what `sol.buy_intel` writes and what the station Trade tab's "elsewhere" column and the galaxy map's trade overlay read back through `bestRemembered` (`:230`). `SurveySim::addBookmark(system, position, name, label, now)` (`:235`) is what `B` writes, and a bookmark buys a nav slot, a radar blip, the target cycle, a map marker and Autopilot in one move — the argument 8i and 8r both used for a single slot. **A tip therefore needs no new store, no new screen, and no new save field.**
- **`Bookmark::label` has been serialised and never set since 8h.** It is declared as a "colour/category tier; 0 = plain" (`survey.hpp:132`) and every bookmark in the game today is `0`. A rumour is precisely the second category, so this item closes a recorded 8h gap by using the field for what it was declared for rather than adding one beside it.
- **`kIntelJumpRadius = 3` and a `hopsFrom` BFS are already written** (`space_world.cpp:43`, `:50-53`), with a comment explaining why a broker knows their neighbourhood and not the reachable galaxy. A pilot's horizon is the same question and gets the same answer rather than a second constant that can drift from it.
- **Spawned ships are not serialised**, so per-pilot state costs nothing in the save — the same reasoning 8l used to put its 10 s assist window on `ShipDefense` and keep the save at v10. `m_spawnedShips` is repopulated by `loadSystem` and no pilot survives a jump.
- **The hook pattern is written five times**: `signal_loot`, `signal_found`, `wreck_loot`, `mission_board` and 8r's `dock_request` all run *C++ enumerates → Lua composes → C++ validates*, each with a scriptless default and an `m_xHookFailed` latch that disables it until scripts reload. `content.cpp:2371-2405` is the drain to copy.
- **What does not exist is exactly four things**: a way to address a ship, any notion of a pilot knowing something, any per-pilot memory of having spoken to the player, and any tip content. That is the whole item.

**A. `Action::HailTarget`, the 36th action, default `Y`.** Not a fifth rung on the interact key: that key already means five things (undock / cancel clearance / dock / salvage / hail station), the ladder is strictly order-dependent, and *a ship selected while parked near a station* is exactly the case where the game would have to guess which the player meant. A hail is its own verb and gets its own key, rebindable since 8k. **`Y` because both mnemonic keys are gone** — `H` is Nearest Hostile and `C` is Cycle Contact, both already in the Targeting group — and `Y` is free and sits in the same physical cluster as `T` and `H`. One row in `kActions` (`input_actions.cpp:25-98`), which the `static_assert` on `kActionCount` enforces.
