- **Gates are drawn unrotated**, `addStatic(gate.position, {70,70,10}, ModelId::Cube)` with an identity `Transform`, so every gate in the galaxy currently presents the same arbitrary world-Z face regardless of where its lane goes. **An aperture rule needs a normal anyway, so orienting the mesh to it is the same fact applied twice** rather than extra work.
- **The parked gate follows directly from 8v suspending the sim.** The transition arms, `simRunning` goes false, and the ship stops dead — so the object the player just flew at stays nailed in the centre of the view for 2.7 s while the stars streak past it, which reads as a screen effect rather than as travel.
- **`loadSystem` zeroes the `FlightBody`** (`:2183`), so momentum does not survive a jump today. That stays true here: emerging at speed is a gameplay change, not a presentation fix.

**A. A gate stops being solid.** A marker component on gate entities, skipped in the static collision loop beside projectiles and ore chunks. Nothing else in the game collides with gates today — NPCs never use them, and gates are absent from `m_obstacles`, so steering is already unaffected.

**B. The trigger becomes an aperture crossing, and direction stops being a special case.** Replace 8v's swept sphere with a swept **plane** test: the segment from `previousPosition` to `position` must cross the gate's plane (point `gate.position`, normal `normalize(gate.position - hub)`), and the crossing point must lie within `kGateRadiusMeters` of the centre. **Accidental jumps become impossible by construction rather than unlikely by tuning** — flying past, alongside, or stopping short can never satisfy a plane crossing. A gate works in **both** directions, because the test does not care about the sign of the crossing. `kGateCaptureRadius` is deleted; the frame radius is the whole rule, which is what the note means by "right up on it".

**C. Autopilot must aim THROUGH the doorway, not at it.** 8v clamped a gate's arrival range to 0 so the ship would aim at the centre — but the centre lies **on** the plane, and arriving at a plane is not crossing it, so `steerTravel` decelerating to rest there would stop exactly one epsilon short of a jump forever. That is 8v's 78 m failure in a new costume, and it is named here so it is not rediscovered. For a gate target, autopilot's destination point becomes the centre offset along the axis to the **far side from the ship**, so flying to it means going through.

**D. The ship coasts through the transition.** While the tunnel runs, the player's transform advances on its existing velocity — transform only, no forces, no collision, no sim. The gate the player flew through recedes behind them, and the streaks read as motion. This is the whole of note 2, and it costs one integration line because the sim is suspended and nothing else is moving.

**E. Gates face their lane.** The gate mesh is oriented to the axis from §A's diagnosis, so a gate visibly presents its opening to the ship coming from the hub. This is cosmetic in itself and load-bearing for the aperture rule being *legible* — the player must be able to see which way through is.

**No save change — still v11.** No settings change, no new action (35 stays). **The tunnel's timing is untouched**, on the user's explicit verdict.

**Tests.** `sim.unit` gains the aperture rule as a pure function beside the jump timing: a segment straight through the centre crosses; a segment through the frame off-centre crosses; a segment crossing the plane **outside** the frame radius does not; a segment that approaches and stops on the near side does not; a segment running parallel to the plane inside the radius does not (the accidental-jump case stated as a test); and the reverse direction does. The 8v test asserting a 110 m capture clears a 78 m solid gate is **replaced**, not amended — the sphere it was about no longer exists.

**Exit**: flying at a gate from the hub side passes through the opening and jumps; flying past it at any distance, or stopping in front of it, does not; autopilot to a gate goes through rather than parking on the plane; the gate recedes behind the ship during the tunnel instead of sitting in frame; gates visibly face their lane; the timing is unchanged; all 9 suites green, zero warnings.

**Out of scope.** No momentum through a jump (`loadSystem` keeps zeroing the body). No gate mesh replacement — it is still the provisional squashed cube, and a real model belongs to the asset-tool item the user has deferred. No gate effects, no NPC gate traffic (that is the living-universe item), and **no change to the tunnel's duration or look**.

**What driving it changed about the plan.** Almost nothing, and that is the notable part — **the spec survived contact unamended**, because §C had already named the trap in advance. The one implementation surprise was a compiler error rather than a design fault (`Transform`'s designated initialisers must follow member order, so the orientation goes after `previousPosition`). **This is the first item this session whose plan was not corrected by flying it**, and the reason is that 8v had just paid for the two lessons it needed: that a gate is solid, and that autopilot stops at what it aims at.

**Verified live**, quitting through the pause menu at **exit code 0**:

- **⚑ The ship reaches 32 m from a gate, closing at 125 m/s** — a distance that was *physically impossible* before this item, when a 70 m collision sphere plus an 8 m hull stopped it dead at 78 m. The gate's face fills the screen and the ship goes through it. That is the note (*"you have to get right up on it"*) answered literally.
- **Autopilot goes through rather than stopping on the threshold**: `Autopilot: flying to 'Gate: Lyrioa' (arrive 0.0 km out)` → `jumping: Queis -> Lyrioa` → `Arrived in 'Lyrioa'`, with §C's predicted failure never occurring.
- **The gate leaves the frame during the tunnel and the ship coasts at 123 m/s** where 8v sat at 0.0 m/s with the gate parked in the middle of the view. Second note answered.
- **The profiler corroborates the collision change independently**: `sim.collision.build` **n=13 → 9** and `sim.collision.resolve` **n=78 → 36**, which is exactly Lyrioa's four gates leaving the set and the pair count following as `n(n-1)/2`. **A number nobody was looking at agreeing with a change made somewhere else is the cheapest confirmation there is.**
- All **9** suites green, zero warnings; `sim.unit` **129 → 131** (three aperture tests in, 8v's now-meaningless sphere-margin test out).

**Known gaps.** **The accidental-jump case is proved by test, not by drive** — `jump_transition_aperture_refuses_everything_that_did_not_go_through` states flying past the gate parallel to its plane as an assertion, but no live drive flies past a gate and confirms nothing happens, because scripting a near-miss at a chosen offset is fiddlier than the rule is subtle. **A gate is now non-solid for everything, not just the player** — nothing else in the game touches gates today, so this is invisible, but an NPC that ever uses one will fly through the frame rather than around it. **`ModelId::Cube` identifies a gate by elimination** in the static collision pass; it is exact today because bolts and ore are excluded a line above, and a future Cube-shaped static would silently become non-solid. **The gate is still a squashed cube** with a placeholder checker texture, so "the opening" is a face rather than a hole you can see through — the aperture is legible only because the frame now faces you. **Momentum still does not survive a jump**, so you arrive at rest having entered at speed. **A jump is still silent.**

#### Phase 8x — A Living Universe: Ships With Something To Lose ✅ (completed 2026-08-19)

**From playtest session 7, note 4**, chosen by the user over discovery-by-exploring:

> *"i think it would be good to start thinking about putting more ships into the universe so it feels more alive. traders, freighters, miners, etc should start populating the sectors so they can be raided/protected and fuel the growth or downfall of corporations and factions."*

**⚑ And it is §2's own architecture finally being built.** The Simulation-LOD bullet has said since the plan was written that *"the player's current system runs the full sim (bubble). Everything else runs a coarse agent simulation — ships as schedule entries moving between markets, **battles resolved statistically** — **promoted to full fidelity when the player arrives**. This is the EVE/X4 trick and it is an **architectural commitment**: every gameplay system (economy, AI, missions) must define both its full-fidelity and coarse representations."* The coarse half has existed since Phase 7; **the promotion has never been built for anything**, and "battles resolved statistically" has never happened at all. §A is the promotion. §C is the statistics. **The user asking for X4 is the plan asking for itself back** — and note the same reference turned up in session 7's other note about gates.

**This is also two long-standing debts turning out to be the same item.** The Phase 7 caveat has read *"promoting coarse traders to bubble ships still open"* since Phase 7. 8u's largest recorded gap reads *"the contest is a meter, not a battle — nothing simulates a war's attrition, so an NPC war changes nothing"*, and 8u deliberately counted **only the player's kills** toward a contest **because** crediting ambient dogfights would have been inventing state the sim does not have. **Ships that can actually be lost is the state the sim does not have.** Pay it once and both close.

Scope decided with the user from three questions, each framed against what the code makes cheap. **All three answers took the fuller option**, and the third took the fullest available:

1. **A bubble trader is a PUPPET of a coarse one.** The 120 `EconomyTrader`s stay the source of truth; a body appears when one is flying a leg in the player's system. **The economy cannot desync from what the player watched**, and 8g's fleet tuning keeps its meaning.
2. **Losses happen where the player is not.** Dangerous systems cost traders whether or not anyone is looking.
3. **Raiders hunt traders, AND the board posts protection contracts** — so a sector has events in it, and defending one is payable work.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **⚑ The coarse fleet is 120 traders and it is already economically load-bearing.** `EconomyParams::traderCount = 120`, `traderCargo = 150`, tuned in 8g against a four-sim-hour steady-state test — and 8g's comment records that **both directions break it**: too few and consumers drain to zero, too many and *"a surplus hauler does not sit idle, it goes on buying and carrying things nobody needs"* (at 260×200 the fleet permanently held a fifth of every good in the galaxy). **Anything this item does to trader lifetimes changes that equilibrium**, so `economy_shipped_rates_hold_a_steady_state` is the instrument that guards the whole phase, not an afterthought.
- **⚑ A coarse trader has no position and no origin.** `EconomyTrader` is `{market, phase, travelRemaining, commodity, cargo}` (`economy.hpp:126-133`) — a destination index and a countdown. It knows where it is going and when it arrives, and **nothing about where it came from**, so it cannot be placed in space today. All five fields are serialised (`economy.cpp:585-592`), so giving it a route is a **save format change**.
- **⚑ But the time model already decomposes a leg the exact way a puppet needs.** `traderLegSeconds = 90.0` is *"in-system travel per endpoint"* and `jumpSeconds = 20.0` is *per gate transit*, so a haul is already **depart leg → jumps → arrive leg**. A puppet is therefore not an invention: it is the in-system portion of a leg the economy is already simulating, drawn.
- **`Economy::raidSystem` already destroys in-transit cargo** (`economy.cpp:272-276`): any trader in transit *to* a raided system has `cargo = 0`. So the coarse layer has always modelled raid losses abstractly — **§C is that mechanism made continuous and given a cause, not a new concept.**
- **⚑ `PilotRole::Trader` has existed since Phase 6 and has never once been spawned by the game.** `spawnAmbientPilots` spawns only `Fighter` and `Patrol` (`space_world.cpp:2004-2009`); `Trader` is reachable only from `sol.spawn_pilot(id, "trader")`. The role, its Lua name, and its `pilot_think`/`pilot_hail` branches are all built and waiting. **8s's known gap says so explicitly** — *"ambient traders are coarse-sim and never enter the bubble, so the trader branch is the least likely to fire in a real session"* — which means this item also retires that gap and finally exercises 8s's trader tip.
- **`spawnAmbientPilots` is one lambda with a roster, a role, a count and an anchor**, already called three ways (owner patrols, resident raiders, 8u's contest wings). A trader puppet is a fourth call, not new spawning machinery.
- **Mining is already abstract on the station side.** 8g gave `Economy::tick` a `FeedstockSource` so an extractor station draws real rock from `MiningSim` without `Economy` depending on it, marked by `produces_from = "field"`. **So an NPC miner is the visible face of a draw the sim already performs** — the same puppet relationship as a trader, against a different coarse actor.
- **`ObjectiveKind` has five members** (`missions.hpp:34-44`), the fifth being 8u's `Hold`. An escort is the sixth, and 8u is the worked example of adding one: a member, a checker, a `MissionSim` report, a Lua builder, and a `mission_board` argument.
- **Cost is understood in advance.** 8n measured that collision tracks **mover count, not pair count**, at 0.034 ms for a real system and 0.49 ms at 138 bodies with ~60 movers, against a 6.94 ms budget with ~6 ms idle. **A dozen more movers per system is not a performance question**, and 8n's own rule applies: do not optimise without re-measuring.

**A. A puppet is a body for a coarse trader, and the coarse trader stays the truth.** `EconomyTrader` gains `origin` (the market it departed) and `legTotal` (the leg's full duration), which is what makes its progress and therefore its position derivable. A puppet exists only while the trader is flying an **in-system** portion of its leg — outbound from its origin station toward the gate, or inbound from the gate toward its destination station — and only in the system the player is standing in. Between those it is in the jump graph and has no body, which is the honest reading of `jumpSeconds`. **The entity is a view; the record is the trader.**

**B. What killing one does.** A puppet's death applies to its coarse trader exactly what `raidSystem` already applies: the cargo is gone. The trader is then returned to `Idle` at a market rather than deleted, so **`traderCount` stays fixed and 8g's tuning is not silently re-run** by attrition — losing a hauler costs the galaxy a *cargo* and a *round trip*, not a member of the fleet. Ore chunks and a wreck drop through 8f's existing machinery, so raiding pays the player in the currency the game already has.

**C. Attrition where nobody is watching.** Each coarse tick, an in-transit trader passing through a dangerous system risks the same loss. Danger is read from state that already exists — `FactionSim::raidIntensity` and 8u's contest pressure — so no new model is invented. **⚑ And a loss inside a contested system feeds that contest.** This is the sentence 8u could not write: attrition now exists, so NPC pressure is no longer a lie, and the meter can finally be moved by a war rather than only by the player. 8u's rule that *only the player's kills count* is **superseded here deliberately and its comment must be updated**, not left contradicting the code.

**D. Predation, so a sector has events and not just traffic.** Raider and hostile-faction fighters in the bubble take trader puppets as targets through the existing pilot AI. The player can arrive to find a fight already running and pick a side. A puppet killed by an NPC takes the same §B consequence, which is what stops predation being theatre.

**E. `ObjectiveKind::Escort`, the sixth member.** *Keep coarse trader T alive until it reaches system S.* **The objective tracks the COARSE TRADER INDEX, never the entity** — a puppet is destroyed and recreated every time the player follows it through a gate, so an entity handle would fail at exactly the moment the mission gets interesting. Completion is the trader arriving; failure is its loss. Failure carries **no standing penalty when the trader dies to someone else**, on 8u's `Lost` precedent, and the poster is the trader's faction.

**F. Freighters and miners.** *Freighter* is a puppet wearing a bigger hull — the roster comes from the faction's ship lists exactly as patrol and raider wings already do, so cargo capacity picks the def. *Miner* is the same puppet relationship against a different coarse actor: an extractor station's `FeedstockSource` draw gets a body working its field. **No new sim in either case** — both are existing coarse activity, drawn.

**Save v12.** `EconomyTrader` gains `origin` and `legTotal`; the mission block gains the `Escort` objective's fields. Old saves are **rejected cleanly, not migrated**, per the v4–v11 policy.

**Console.** `sol.traders()` (the coarse fleet: phase, route, cargo, and which have bodies), `sol.puppets()` (what is actually spawned here and for which trader), `sol.trader_kill(i)` (the dev lever for a loss — and **8u's rule applies: it must reach only states the sim can reach**, so it goes through the same path as a real death), `sol.danger(system)` (what §C reads), and `sol.escort_candidates()` beside the existing candidate probes.

**Tests.** `sim.unit` gains: leg decomposition (a trader's progress maps to depart/jump/arrive with the boundaries where `traderLegSeconds` and `jumpSeconds` put them); a lost trader returning to `Idle` with `traderCount` unchanged; cargo destroyed exactly once; attrition raising contest pressure only inside a contested system and only for the attacker; an `Escort` objective completing on arrival, failing on loss, and **charging no penalty on a third-party kill**; and **⚑ `economy_shipped_rates_hold_a_steady_state` re-run with attrition live** — if the galaxy cannot hold equilibrium while losing ships, the attrition rate is wrong and that test is where it must be discovered.

**H. Build order**, because this is the largest item the project has taken on and each stage must be verifiable alone:

1. **Route state + save v12 + tests.** No visuals: a trader knows where it is, and the suite proves it.
2. **Puppets (presence).** Bodies appear and fly their legs. At the end of this stage the sectors are populated and the item could pause here.
3. **Loss and attrition (§B, §C)**, including the contest feed and the steady-state re-run.
4. **Predation (§D).**
5. **Escort missions (§E).**
6. **Freighters and miners (§F).**

**Exit**: flying into a busy system shows traders running between its stations and gates; killing one destroys its cargo, drops loot, and is visible in the coarse fleet's books; a system left alone but dangerous loses traders anyway, and a contested system's pressure moves without the player firing a shot; raiders attack traders unprompted; an escort contract completes on arrival and fails without penalty when the trader is killed by a third party; the galaxy still holds economic equilibrium over four sim hours **with attrition live**; all 9 suites green, zero warnings.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** No player-owned ships or hired NPC crews flying routes for you. No boarding, disabling or cargo theft from a living ship — a kill drops loot, and taking cargo without destroying the hull is its own item. No docking animation or NPCs occupying berths (8r's *"berth occupancy does not exist"* stays true). No trader-versus-trader economics beyond what 8g already models, no player-set prices, and no corporate ownership of individual ships. **No faction fleets or capital ships** — this item populates trade, not war fleets. No change to gate travel, and **no change to 8v/8w's jump**.

**Shipped in six stages, each verified live before the next began** (`f91899d` spec, then `fd8152e` routes, `621645f` puppets, `5a3a7aa` loss and attrition, `9d6333f` predation, `6dc2bac` escort contracts, `03fb0cc` freighters and miners). **Save v11 → v13** (v12 for the trader's origin and leg, v13 for the escort objective's trader). `sim.unit` **131 → 161**. **Both long-standing debts are closed**: coarse traders are promoted to bubble ships, which retires the Phase 7 caveat that had stood for six phases, and NPC attrition now moves a contest, which supersedes 8u's *"the contest is a meter, not a battle"* — deliberately, with 8u's own comment rewritten rather than left contradicting the code.

**⚑ What building it changed about the plan. Every stage but the first was corrected by flying it, and in five different ways.**

- **The two clocks do not agree, and cannot** (stage 2). A leg is `traderLegSeconds` however far it is, while a hull crosses it at its own cruise speed: a freighter tops out at 3.0e6 m/s and needs ~260 s for the 6.0e8 m a leg quotes at 90. Flown freely a hauler is three times too slow, is mid-lane when its books say it arrived, and is deleted in deep space — which is why a system holding 45 bodies still showed an empty sky at every station. **The record paces the middle, where a hull is far below a pixel, and the ship flies both ends. That handover IS the Simulation-LOD seam** §2 committed to, and `kTraderApproachDistance` is not chosen: it is where `steerTravel`'s own profile drops the shipped freighter back into its normal envelope.
- **A convoy that spawns together annihilates itself** (stage 2, measured). The coarse fleet converges, so a dozen haulers leave one station on one tick for one destination and the record puts every one at the same point; `resolvePair` depenetrates a zero-distance pair along an arbitrary axis and the stack throws itself apart hard enough to do impact damage. Most of a 45-ship fleet died that way. `laneSlotOffset` gives each trader a sunflower slot **at both ends**, or the formation re-converges on the approach.
- **A trader can only be lost where it could have been seen** (stage 3). Attrition rolls on Depart and Arrive only — the windows a body exists in — so **the gate network is the safe part of a haul**, and the player's own system is sheltered because there a hauler dies by being shot, in the open, leaving a wreck. That is what makes an escort contract coherent rather than a dice roll behind the player's back.
- **Four constants were wrong at once and only flying found any of them** (stage 4). The hunter's reach was budgeted against the prey's approach window and answered with the nearest hauler *three times that budget away*, so nothing ever hunted; a raider that took the nearest hauler chased an outbound one and lost it at the gate, because inbound is both the laden leg and the one that ends somewhere it can be caught; a stern chase is the wrong shape entirely, so the hunter flies to where the prey is *going*; and **the record was winning every fight** — `travelRemaining` counted down through a firefight, so a hauler under fire arrived on schedule and was rebuilt elsewhere, watched twice at 2 km with the raider already shooting.
- **A bound written as "the last enum member" is a bug with a fuse in it** (stage 5). `readObjective` clamped the objective kind at `FlyTo`, which was the last member when it was written; from the moment 8u added `Hold`, **any save carrying one failed to load and took the world down with it**, and nothing noticed because a save only holds a `Hold` if a contest happens to be in reach of the board. Found by writing a round-trip test that writes *every* member, and A/B'd by reverting the one-line fix.
- **Shooting your own charge is a failure, not a loss** (stage 5). 8u's `Lost` carries no standing penalty on purpose, and applied unchanged that made betrayal the cheapest way to finish an escort. Who fired is known **only** in `handleShipDestroyed`, so a one-frame list bridges it to the coarse loss drain.
- **A miner that crossed its own field died every sixty seconds** (stage 6). Picking the next rock in generation order sent a freighter **5,377 m at 207 m/s** one sample before `'sol.freighter' destroyed`, with nothing hunting it: **rocks are solid statics and nothing in the game avoids them** — `m_obstacles` holds stations and planets only. `chooseWorkRock` takes the nearest rock whose straight line clears every other rock, and refuses the hop when there is none.

**Verified live across sixteen drives, every one quitting through the pause menu at exit code 0.** A cold boot reads `0/52/26/42` on the first tick a console can reach (the staggered fleet; stage 2's lockstep gone). 45 bodies in Lyrioa agreeing with the record, arriving at 6–7 km doing 0–90 m/s. `LOSSES 2 2 3 6 9 12 16 21 24` over four minutes with the player parked at a gate doing nothing, and `PRE p=0.44 → KILL=true → POST p=0.46`, which is `contestPerTraderLoss` exactly. In Saria, **with no console-spawned ships**, 8u's own contest wing hunted: `3 fighter(s) here, 3 hunting a hauler`, one closing 185,073 km → 1 km, `'sol.shuttle' destroyed`, `[attrition] trader 91 lost in Saria`, and the contest stepping **up** against its own decay. An escort offered at 2,495 cr, accepted, and flown the intended way — five hops ahead of the hauler, standing at the far pad as it came in — paying **+2,495 cr and +8 rep**; killed instead, for *"We lost the hauler. Stand down."* with Solar Navy standing unmoved. And a Freighter working Tauubyx Alpha's field at 817 m, killed for `output 0% [no miner]`, with a replacement on station and digging ninety seconds later; **151 body-samples in a busy system without one shuttle carrying more than it can hold.**

**Known gaps.** **A hunter avoids nothing while it cruises** (`steerTravel` skips avoidance above the envelope and `m_obstacles` holds only stations and planets), so it can ram its prey — or the player — at 10⁶ m/s; two raiders spawned at one point annihilated each other and the player. **A lost trader is silent**: a log line and a counter, no comms line, no map mark, and none of 8t's nine cues covers it. **Whose trader was lost is not asked** — a coarse trader has no faction, so a loss in a contested system presses the holder's ground whoever it was hauling for. **The player-kill half of an escort is proved by test, not by drive**, because a hauler is only catchable inside the 35 s window at either end of a leg. **An escort is still winnable by doing nothing** when the destination stays quiet, and **an offer is a snapshot**, so the board can sell a run already 94% of the way through its departing leg. **Traffic in the start system is thin** — three drives read one hauler at ~900,000 km — so the phase is least visible exactly where a new player starts. **A paced puppet reports the envelope speed it was handed**, not its real rate of travel. **A miner is a view with one consequence**: killing it stops its outpost's draw for a leg's worth of seconds, and that hold is transient game-side state that no save carries, so a reload forgives it. **A miner boxed in by rock quietly stops moving**, which is correct and indistinguishable from a bug without the console. **Nothing spawns a raider because a contract was taken** — that would be inventing state, and stage 4 already puts raiders where the danger is.

---

#### Phase 8y — See What You Hit: One List For What Blocks You ✅ (completed 2026-08-19)

**Not from a playtest — from 8x's own known-gaps list**, chosen by the user over discovery-by-exploring, campaign Act 2 and packaging. 8x recorded it in the plainest terms it could: *"a hunter avoids nothing while it cruises … so it can ram its prey — or the player — at 10⁶ m/s."*

**⚑ The framing that earned it the slot: one missing capability has now been worked around three times in a single phase, and never fixed.** Stage 2's convoy annihilated itself and was answered by `laneSlotOffset` — a *spawn-position* fix. Stage 6's miner flew into a rock and was answered by `chooseWorkRock` — a *routing* fix that refuses hops with no clear line. Stage 4's raider rams its prey and the player, and was answered by **writing it down**. Three symptoms, three different levels of the stack, and the actual hole is that **a ship at speed does not know what is in front of it.** Both existing workarounds are good rules and both stay; neither is load-bearing after this.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **⚑ The set of things you can HIT is a strict superset of the set anything AVOIDS, and the two are separate lists built in separate places.** `m_obstacles` (`space_world.cpp:2934-2941`) is built once per system load and holds **stations at `kStationRadiusMeters` = 130 m, the planets, and the star** — nothing else, ever. `m_collisionBodies` is rebuilt **every tick** and holds **every ship**, every static `RenderShape` except projectiles, ore chunks and gates, plus the star and planets. So rocks, wrecks and other ships are in one list and absent from the other. **8v already paid for this exact confusion once** — its diagnosis checked `m_obstacles`, correctly found gates absent, and read "nothing steers around it" as "nothing stops you". Avoidance and collision have been two different lists since Phase 6.
- **⚑ Avoidance is a lateral nudge, and at cruise it cannot work by construction.** `avoidObstacles` (`steering.cpp:165-194`) pushes the *desired velocity* sideways with a danger radius of `radius * 1.5 + 100`. `steerTravel` applies it **only when `!cruise`** (`steering.cpp:107-109`), and the comment says why: *"at cruise speeds a tick crosses them entirely."* The numbers: `cruiseSpeedScale` defaults to **25,000**, so the starter shuttle cruises at 220 × 25,000 = **5.5e6 m/s** and a freighter at **3.0e6 m/s**; one 145 Hz tick is **~38 km**. A station is 130 m across, a rock at most 240 m, a ship 8 m base radius (32 m on the 4× freighter). **A cruising ship crosses any of them hundreds of times over inside a single tick**, so there is no deflection to make and the `!cruise` guard is correct rather than lazy. The fix cannot be "avoid harder"; it has to be **speed**.
- **⚑ And the collision is swept, so the ram is reliable rather than unlucky** (`collision_fast_mover_does_not_tunnel`). A cruising ship pointed at something does not miss it.
- **⚑ Impact damage makes every one of these fatal, and that is not a tuning problem.** `kImpactDamageFactor = 0.01` against `v²` with a `kImpactDamageMinimum` of 1.0 (`space_world.cpp:34-35`). The starter shuttle carries hull 100 + armor 50 + shield 100, so **~158 m/s is already lethal** — and the *normal* flight envelope is `maxSpeed * 2` = **440 m/s**, worth 1,936 damage. Lowering the factor to make cruise survivable would make every ordinary bump harmless, so the answer is to not arrive at that speed.
- **A ram credits nobody**: `destroyedShips.push_back({.victim = entityIndex}); // rams credit no one`. That is deliberate and stays — but it is why stage 4's rammed haulers surfaced as *attrition with no attacker*, and why a rammed player dies with no killer named.
- **The steering seam is already shared.** `steerTravel` flies NPC `PilotState::Travel` **and** the player's autopilot (`autopilotInput` builds `m_autopilotObstacles` by filtering `m_obstacles`), so a fix inside it lands on both without a second code path. `PilotState::Attack` runs its own `avoidObstacles(..., 8.0)`.
- **The warning surface already exists and needs no new UI.** `say(from, text)` posts to 8r's comms panel — 3 lines, 8 s each — and **8s already rode that channel without adding any UI of its own**. `m_berthRefusalTimer` is the existing precedent for throttling a repeated warning so it says its piece once per approach rather than sixty times a second.

**Decisions taken with the user before any code.** (1) **Everyone is protected, but a manually cruising player is warned first** and keeps the drive for a grace moment before it cuts — NPCs and autopilot slow silently. (2) **The full sweep**: the cruise look-ahead, rocks and wrecks joining the avoidance set, and ships avoiding each other while travelling but *not* while fighting.

**A. One list, not two.** The avoidance set is rebuilt every tick from **the same pass and the same exclusions** as the collision set, so the two cannot drift apart — the property this phase exists to establish is structural rather than a promise to maintain. `AvoidanceSphere` gains the caller's own handle (the trick `PreyCandidate::index` already uses: an opaque index in, untouched out) so a query can exclude **itself** and, where §C says so, its target. `m_obstacles` as a load-time list goes away.

**B. A cruise look-ahead, because the deflection cannot.** A new pure function in `steering.hpp` answers *how far this ship can travel along its current heading before it hits something*, built on `segmentHitsSphere` — the same primitive 8x stage 6 used for a miner's hop and the mining laser uses for its beam. `steerTravel` then does two things with the answer: **refuses cruise while the path is blocked**, and **caps the desired speed with its own existing braking profile** (`sqrt(2 * brake * clearDistance)`) applied to the obstacle instead of to the destination. **The fix is the profile the function already computes, pointed at a second thing.**

**C. Ships avoid ships while travelling, never while fighting.** The mover half of §A is consulted from `Travel` and from the player's autopilot only. `Attack` and `Flee` keep the static set alone, so **ramming in a dogfight stays a legitimate move** and a hunter closing on prey slows to weapon range rather than through it. A hunter must also never treat its own target as an obstacle, which is what §A's handle is for.

**D. The player's manual cruise: a warning, then the drive cuts.** While the player holds cruise on a blocked path, `say` posts a proximity warning on the comms channel and a grace timer starts; when it expires the applied input's `cruise` flag is cleared and the flight model's existing interruptible-cruise braking takes over — no new flight rule, just the flag the player would otherwise have to clear themselves. Throttled like the berth refusal so a long approach warns once. **Cruise only exists in assist mode (`input.assist && input.cruise`), so this is the assist system doing its stated job**; flying with assist off is still raw Newtonian with no help at all.

**E. What this deliberately does NOT do.** **Sub-cruise manual flight stays unprotected** — you can still fly into a planet at 440 m/s and die, and the assist system caps your speed, not your judgement. **No pathfinding**: the only lateral behaviour remains `avoidObstacles`' nudge at sub-cruise speeds, and "blocked" is answered with *slow down*, never with *route around*. **No change to impact damage**, and **rams still credit nobody**.

**No save change, no settings change, no new action.** New constants only, and one new field on `AvoidanceSphere`.

**Tests.** `sim.unit` gains the look-ahead as a pure function: a sphere dead ahead blocks at its true distance; one beside the path does not; one behind does not; a ship already inside a sphere reports zero rather than a negative; the handle-exclusion drops exactly one sphere and no others; an empty set never blocks; and the speed cap equals the braking profile evaluated at the blocking distance, which is the property that keeps §B from being a second, subtly different deceleration curve. `steering_tests` gains the composed behaviour: `steerTravel` at cruise with a blocker on the line returns `cruise = false` and a speed it can brake from, and the identical call with the blocker moved aside returns cruise unchanged.

**Exit**: a raider hunting a hauler stops short of it instead of ramming it; two ships told to fly through each other do not destroy each other; a ship crossing an asteroid field steers around the rocks rather than relying on stage 6's hop refusal; a manually cruising player pointed at a station is warned and then dropped out of cruise, close enough to see it; a player with assist off can still fly into anything they like; the profiler shows no new zone worth naming; all 9 suites green, zero warnings.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** No pathfinding, waypoint graphs or formation flying. No ramming as a *mechanic* — no bonus damage, no attribution, no achievement for it. No change to `kImpactDamageFactor` or to the collision resolver. No avoidance for projectiles or ore (they are excluded from collision for a reason). No protection for manual sub-cruise flight, and none at all with assist off. No change to gates (still non-solid, still flown through), and **no revisiting of 8x's `chooseWorkRock` or `laneSlotOffset`** — both stay, both are correct, and both stop being the only thing between a ship and a rock.

**Shipped in two stages** (`303a75e` spec, `8d09e4a` the query, `220048d` the list and the guard). **No save change, no settings change, no new action.** `sim.unit` **161 → 163**.

**⚑ What building it changed about the plan.**

- **The cruise refusal is not a rule, it is arithmetic.** §B guessed it would need a flag; it does not. Cruise is already gated on the desired speed exceeding the normal envelope, so **capping the speed turns cruise off by itself** — and once sub-cruise, `avoidObstacles` finally gets the chance to steer that it never had at cruise. One number does both jobs and they cannot disagree.
- **⚑ The first test asserted the wrong property, and the game was right.** It expected a blocker on the lane to turn cruise *off*; it does not, because the cruise drive brakes at 12,000× normal, so at 30 km of clearance a shuttle can still legitimately do 119 km/s and stop in time. **The speed is the mechanism; the flag is a consequence.**
- **⚑ The second test could not see the bug it was written for.** A per-tick proximity sample missed a ram entirely: at cruise a tick covers ~38 km, so the ship jumps clean over a 130 m station between two samples. The test measures the **swept segment** now — which is exactly why the collision pass sweeps, and the same fact from the other side.
- **⚑ The guard blinded itself exactly as it succeeded, and only flying found it.** The look-ahead was four stopping distances, and stopping distance falls with the **square** of speed: at cruise it watched 28 km ahead, and by 220 m/s it was watching **40 cm**. So it cut the drive, stopped seeing anything, let the held throttle rebuild the speed, and oscillated its way into the station it had just saved the ship from. The look-ahead now has a **floor derived from the hull's own tuning** — the distance it needs to stop from its normal envelope — so the query always reaches far enough to be worth asking.
- **⚑ The warning is measured in distance, not in seconds, and that is a departure from the letter of the agreed design rather than from its intent.** At 5.5e6 m/s one second is 5,500 km, so a fixed grace second is meaningless at one speed and already fatal at another. What is constant across every hull and every speed is how much room the brakes need: the warning goes out with four of them left and the drive cuts with two, so the player is always told before anything is taken away.
- **A blocker that appears inside your braking distance is arithmetic, not an avoidance failure.** A drive spawned a hull 550 m in front of a ship doing 649 km/s and it died, correctly: it was 0.0008 s away. The rule protects against obstacles that exist ahead of time, which is every obstacle the game actually has.

**Verified live**, and the regression surface mattered as much as the feature: **8r's berth flow still docks** (hail → berth → fly in, station screen up), a hauler still arrives, the autopilot still cruises a 600,000 km leg, and **a ship crosses an asteroid field at 5.36e6 m/s and comes out at 100% hull** where rocks were invisible to steering before. The new pass costs **0.01 ms at 38 spheres**. The cruise guard flown end to end: `comms: Proximity: Obstruction ahead - cut the drive.` on the glass and in the log, the drive cut, and the ship **coasted to a dead stop 197 m off the station at 100% hull**.

**Known gaps.** **The ram-avoidance itself is proved by test, not by drive** — the unit test carries a negative control (told about nothing, the ship flies through the obstacle and out the far side; told about it, the same flight never touches it), but no live drive reproduces a raider ramming a hauler, because the geometry cannot be scripted deterministically and a kill by gunfire and a kill by ramming look identical from the console. 8w's accidental-jump case has the same shape. **Holding the throttle open THROUGH the warning still kills you**: cutting cruise leaves the ship at its normal top speed, and 220 m/s is worth 484 impact damage against a starter hull's ~250 — §E arriving exactly as written, but worth knowing before calling this "protection". **Sub-cruise manual flight is unprotected and assist-off flight entirely so.** **"Blocked" is still answered with speed alone**: there is no routing, so a ship in a genuinely enclosed space slows and waits rather than going around. **Movers are sampled once per tick**, so a ship reacts to where another ship was at the top of the frame. **Every ship shares one clearance margin** rather than using its own hull radius, which is generous for a shuttle and merely adequate for a freighter.

---

#### Phase 8z — Found, Not Given: A System That Does Not Introduce Itself ✅ (completed 2026-08-19)

**From playtest session 7's note 3**, the oldest open player-found item, picked by the user over the deferred asset-authoring tool and the three roadmap items. The note, in full: *"Points of interest should be DISCOVERED by scanning and flying, not handed over on arrival."*

**⚑ The framing that earned it the slot: the game already has the loop the note is asking for, and points it at the wrong things.** `kTargetScanRangeFraction = 0.02` carries a comment written in Phase 8e that states this note's own design intent word for word — *"a target scan works far closer than a pulse reaches: you find a contact from across the playfield, then fly to it to learn what it is"* (`space_world.hpp:858-860`). The starter ship pulses at **250 Mkm** and identifies at **5 Mkm**: a 50× ratio, tuned and shipped three years of phases ago. **Signals answer to it. Stations, gates, planets and the star do not** — they are handed over whole on the frame `loadSystem` runs. So this is not a new mechanic. It is the 8e loop finally applied to the objects a player actually flies to.

**Diagnosis (measured from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **⚑ Arrival hands the system over in TWO independent ways, and the backlog note names only one.** (1) `loadSystem` calls `m_survey.notifyArrival` (`space_world.cpp:3061`), which promotes the system to `Visited`, and `markerVisible` (`map_projection.hpp:100-113`) rules that Visited reveals every marker **kind** — that is the *remote* map, and it is the half the note describes. (2) **The system you are standing in has no fog at all**: `m_targets` is rebuilt from `SystemSpec` unconditionally (`space_world.cpp:2895-2901`), so every station, gate and planet becomes a nav target, a radar blip, a map marker and an autopilot destination the instant you arrive. **The second half is the bigger one and nothing had recorded it.**
- **⚑ THE TARGET LIST IS WORLD STATE THAT NPCs READ, SO FOG MUST NEVER SHORTEN IT.** `stationPosition()` is `m_targets[0].position` (`space_world.hpp:1227`) and Lua's patrol offsets anchor every ambient wing to it (`content.cpp:162`). The list's *layout* is load-bearing too: `m_signalTargetBase`, `m_planetTargetBase` and `m_starTargetIndex` are indices into it, and `targetSignalIndex()`/`targetBodyIndex()` decode a selection by range-checking them (`space_world.cpp:958-986`). Dropping an undiscovered station would shift all of them and **move NPC patrol anchors the moment the player scanned something** — the world reshaping itself around what the player knows. **The fog is therefore a predicate beside the list, never a filter on it**, and that is the one structural decision this phase turns on.
- **Per-object knowledge already exists for two of the six kinds, and for none of the ones in question.** `SystemSurvey` holds `bodiesScanned`, `signalsDiscovered`, `signalsResolved` and `signalsEmptied` as per-system bitmasks (`survey.hpp:266-273`), saved, tested and asserted against a ceiling at `initialize()`. There is nothing whatever for stations or gates. **The ladder to be extended is already written; it simply has no bits for the objects the player cares about.**
- **The dynamic tail is already knowledge-gated by construction, and says so.** Signals enter it only when discovered (`space_world.cpp:802-807`), while fields do not need finding — *"an asteroid field is a visible thing in the playfield"* (`space_world.cpp:808-809`). A wreck exists because something died in front of you, a bookmark because you wrote it, a berth because you were cleared for it, an objective because you took the contract. **So only the static head needs fog at all**, and the tail's existing comment is the precedent for which things are exempt.
- **The geometry says this is discovery rather than tedium.** Stations sit 1.0e8–4.0e8 m from the primary planet, sites 8.0e7–4.0e8, gates at 6.0e8 (`universe.hpp:65-73`); the starter pulse reaches 2.5e8 (`ships.toml:29`). **You arrive at a gate, 600 Mkm out from the hub, so a pulse fired on arrival does not reach the inhabited part of the system at all** — flying inward is already the motion the geometry wants, and two or three pulses from vantage points along the way cover a system.
- **The seams are few and named.** The target cycle (`space_world.cpp:4606`), the radar fill (`target_pick.cpp:144-148`), the click pick (`target_pick.cpp:110`), the local map markers (`map_ui.cpp:499-501`), the remote map markers (already behind `markerVisible`, needing the per-object bit applied on top exactly as signals get today), and three console/Lua readouts (`content.cpp:1257, 1685, 1711`). **Six seams, one predicate** — and the predicate lives on `SpaceWorld` so that no seam re-derives the rule from `SurveySim` and drifts from the others. This is 8y's "one list" lesson applied to knowledge instead of to collision.

**Decisions taken with the user before any code.** (1) **Arrival still gives the star and the planets and nothing else** — you cannot hide a planet, and landmarks are what keep an unexplored sky legible. Stations, gates and sites are all found. (2) **The two-step ladder is uniform**: a **pulse** finds an anonymous contact, a **target scan** identifies it. No passive proximity reveal — patience must not substitute for using the tool. (3) **Fog only**: no survey-ledger change, and discovery pays nothing new.

**A. Two bits per station and per gate, in the sim, beside the ones signals already have.** `SystemSurvey` gains `stationsDiscovered`/`stationsIdentified` and `gatesDiscovered`/`gatesIdentified`, with the accessor and notify pairs that mirror the signal ones exactly, and `initialize()` asserting the per-system ceiling as it already does for 5 bodies and 6 signals. The word is **identified** rather than 8e's *resolved* because a site resolves into loot while a station resolves into a name. **Save v13 → v14**; a v13 save is rejected cleanly, as every bump in this project has been, and no migration is invented.

**B. You always know what you are touching.** Arriving through a gate identifies **that gate**, and being docked at a station identifies **that station** — otherwise a new game starts the player inside an "Unknown contact" and the first jump strands them at an anonymous object they just flew through. `loadSystem` already receives `fromSystem`, so the arrival gate is the one whose `toSystem` matches it: **derived, never stored**, which is 8u's rule for faction home systems and 8w's for gate facing, a third time.

**C. The pulse finds; the scan names; and a station is approach-gated like a SITE, not read at range like a BODY.** `pulseScan()` (`space_world.cpp:1004`) walks `m_signals` and range-tests against `m_scanRange`; it gains the same walk over the current system's stations and gates. `updateScan` (`space_world.cpp:1059-1063`) gains a third scannable case beside `scannableSignal` and `scannableBody`. **⚑ The existing rule at `space_world.cpp:1071-1077` reads bodies at any range because they are AU-scale scenery — and if a station inherited that, the player would pulse once from the rim and identify an entire system without flying anywhere, which is precisely the complaint.** Identification costs the 5 Mkm approach.

**⚑ C-bis. A GATE IS FOUND AT ANY RANGE INSIDE THE SYSTEM, and this exception was added after the drive, because without it the game cannot be played.** A station is found inside the scanner's reach; a gate runs a lane beacon and is found anywhere. The measurement that forced it: gates sit at `6.0e8` m from the hub and stations at `1.0e8`–`4.0e8`, so with a `2.5e8` starter pulse **the nearest gate to any of Lyrioa's three stations was 278,512 km against a 250,000 km reach** — no gate is findable from anywhere a player would naturally be. Everywhere else that is survivable, because you arrive *through* a gate and §B identifies it, so you can always leave the way you came; **the start system is the one place with no arrival gate, and it is exactly where a new player is.** The exception costs the phase nothing: a beacon says *a gate is here*, not where it goes, so identifying one still takes the flight and the map still grows only along lanes actually looked at.

**D. An unidentified contact has a name that says so, and a gate's destination is the payload.** `signalTargetName(kind, resolved, emptied, slot)` is the precedent and its shape is copied: a discovered-but-unidentified station or gate reads as a numbered anonymous contact and takes its real name at identification. **⚑ For a gate, what identification buys is where it goes** — so `notifyArrival`'s "a gate names where it leads" charting of every neighbour (`survey.cpp:183-189`) moves from *arrival in the system* to *identification of the gate*. **The galaxy map then grows along the lanes the player has actually looked at rather than the ones they happened to fly past**, which is the sharpest single expression of the note and the one change here a future session is most likely to want to re-litigate.

**E. The fog is one predicate with three states, asked six times.** `SpaceWorld` answers `Hidden` / `Contact` / `Identified` for a nav index. **Hidden** is skipped by the cycle, the radar, the click pick and both maps — it is not selectable, so nothing downstream (autopilot, hail, dock request) needs its own guard. **Contact** shows anonymously and is scannable. **Identified** is today's behaviour exactly. The star, the planets and the whole dynamic tail answer `Identified` always, per §A's decision and the tail's existing rule.

**Tests.** `sim.unit` gains the ladder as sim state: a fresh galaxy has no station or gate discovered anywhere; discovery and identification are one-way and idempotent; identifying a gate charts its destination and discovering it does not; the bitmask ceiling holds at the generated maximum; and a v14 round trip carries all four masks for a system in every combination of the two bits, which is stage 5 of 8x's enum-bound lesson applied to a bitmask before it can bite. `ui.unit` gains `markerVisible` under the per-object bit, including the case the note is about — **a Visited system with an undiscovered station shows no station marker**, which is the assertion that would have failed before this phase and is the single best summary of it.

**Exit**: jump into a never-visited system and the map shows a star, its planets and the gate behind you; a pulse from the rim finds nothing because nothing is in reach; fly inward, pulse, and anonymous contacts appear; fly to one and hold a scan and it becomes a named station with an owner; the galaxy map charts that system's neighbours only as their gates are identified; the station you start docked at is known from the first frame; NPC patrols anchor to the same point before and after the player scans anything; all 9 suites green, zero warnings.

**Shipped in three commits** (`6daa230` spec, `07baed3` the fog and the ladder, `ca87aac` the gate beacon the drive forced). **Save v13 → v14.** `sim.unit` **163 → 166**. 10 files.

**⚑ What building it changed about the plan.**

- **⚑ THE ONE THING ONLY FLYING FOUND: no gate is within pulse range of anywhere a player would naturally be, and in the start system that means they cannot leave.** Measured from all three of Lyrioa's stations, the nearest gate was **278,512 km** and the furthest **907,304 km**, against a **250,000 km** pulse. It is structural rather than unlucky — `gateDistance` is `6.0e8` m and `stationMaxDistance` is `4.0e8`, so even a perfectly aligned bearing leaves `2.0e8` and a misaligned one leaves a megametre. Every other system survives it because §B identifies the gate you arrived through, so you can always go back; **the start system is the only one with no arrival gate, and it is exactly where a new player is.** Answered in §C-bis: a station is found inside the scanner's reach, a gate anywhere. **The spec was right about the ladder and wrong about the geometry, and no amount of reading would have caught it — the numbers were all correct and all in the plan.**
- **The fix cost the phase nothing, which is why it was the right one.** A beacon says *a gate is here*, not where it goes. Identification still takes the 5 Mkm flight, and the galaxy map still grows only along lanes the player has actually looked at — the property the whole item exists for.
- **⚑ The selection had to be carried off slot 0, and that leak was invisible from the sim.** `rebuildSystemSideData` sets `m_targetIndex = 0`, which used to be the first station and is now usually hidden — so arriving would have printed a hidden station's masked name in the HUD's target readout and handed it to Autopilot. `snapSelectionToVisible` runs wherever the list is rebuilt or the fog moves. **Six seams were named in the diagnosis and there was a seventh**: `selectTarget` itself, the choke point the map's Set Target, the click pick and `sol.target` all share, which now refuses a hidden slot outright.
- **A draw-kind was needed as well as a predicate.** Hiding a thing is only half of it: a *discovered* station still drew under the station glyph, which said what the name was withholding. `navTargetDrawKind` answers `Signal` for anything unidentified, so the shape and the label agree, and both maps read it from one place.
- **The pulse log lied and the drive caught it in words rather than in state** — *"5 new contact(s) within 250000 km"* for a sweep whose gates were 813,507 km away. Two counts now. **8s's rule for the fourth time: the console proved every rule and the only falsehood was a sentence on a screen.**
- **⚑ And a drive lesson worth more than the phase: this game does not accept synthetic input.** `PostMessage` of `WM_KEYDOWN`/`WM_LBUTTONDOWN`/`WM_CHAR` reaches nothing at all, and `keybd_event` types into the console **and** fires gameplay hotkeys through the raw-input path — so typing `print(...)` pulses the scanner (`r`), cycles the target (`t`) and opens the bookmark dialog (`b`) on its way into the input box, quietly corrupting the very state the command is about to report. **The method that works is to pause first**: gameplay keys are inert on the pause menu, the console still runs commands, and the state it reads is the state the game was in. A resume/pause cycle drops the console's focus, which silently voided a whole drive section once before it was noticed.

**Live proof.** A fresh game reads `station 0 'Lyrioa Alpha': identified, 1 km` with **1 of 7 structures found** and every gate hidden. `sol.knowledge()` reads **`1 of 80 systems`** after Lyrioa is fully explored and **`2 of 80`** after flying to Saria — where arrival alone would have charted five before this phase. One pulse from the spawn finds **6 of 7** (Lyrioa Beta at 201,541 km plus four gate beacons) and leaves **Lyrioa Gamma hidden at 458,152 km**; flying to 40 km off Gamma and pulsing again is what finds it. A scan turns `Contact 2` into `Lyrioa Beta`. Arriving in Saria hands over **nothing**: four stations hidden, the gate back to Lyrioa `identified, 1 km`.

**Known gaps.** **The held target scan's range gate is proved by construction, not by drive** — `sol.scan()` is the instant dev lever and has never had a range check, so the 5 Mkm approach that makes identification cost a flight is asserted by reading `updateScan` rather than by flying it; the same shape as 8y's ram and 8w's accidental jump. **The composed map rule has no headless test**: the bits are asserted in `sim.unit` and `markerVisible` in `ui.unit`, but "a Visited system with an undiscovered station shows no station marker" lives in `map_ui.cpp`'s two fills, which sit in `game/src` and have no suite. **Nothing tells the player a system is incompletely swept** — there is no "3 of 7 known" anywhere in the UI, only in the console probe, so a player cannot tell a quiet system from an unswept one. **A hidden station still accepts a docking hail** if you fly within 20 km of it, which is deliberate (you are touching it) but means the fog has a back door that is never explained. **`checkSurveyed` ignores structures**, so a system can be `Surveyed` with two stations never found — defensible, because the ledger is data worth selling and somebody else built the station, but the word is now doing less than it says. **Nothing re-fogs**: knowledge is one-way, so a system cannot be forgotten. **The start system's own gates are found only by pulsing**, which a new player has no prompt to do.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** No passive proximity reveal, and no change to `kPulseCooldownSeconds`, `kTargetScanSeconds`, `kScanConeCosine` or the scanner fits — the ratio this phase relies on is already tuned and re-tuning it inside the same phase would make a bad result impossible to attribute. **No survey-ledger change**: no new `SurveyKind`, no value, no change to `checkSurveyed`, which keeps its exact meaning of *every body scanned and every signal resolved*. That line is defensible rather than lazy — **the ledger is data worth selling, and a station is not survey data; somebody else built it and already knows where it is.** No fog on ships, wrecks, fields, bookmarks or objectives. No change to the mission board, which may still post work at a station the player has never found — the contract is how you learn the place exists, and its objective marker is a dynamic slot that was never fogged. No re-opening of 8q's fog rule for whole *systems*, which is a different ladder and works.

### Phase 9 — The Forge: An Authoring Tool for This Game's Assets 🚧 (spec'd 2026-08-19)

**From playtest session 7's note 5**, the last open item on the board and the one the user deferred at the time — *"a 3D asset authoring tool, specialised to this game's assets and systems, for the user and others to author with"* — picked here over the three roadmap items, and asked for with a wider scope than the note carried: basic shapes as building blocks that can be modified, **manual vertex and edge placement**, **merging objects**, **mesh optimisation**, **LOD generation**, textures, and the def rows that turn a mesh into content.

**Phase 8 is out of letters at 8z, and this is a tools item rather than sandbox depth, so it opens Phase 9.** It is also, by a wide margin, the largest thing this project has scoped: Phase 8x was six stages and ~4,400 lines, and this is bigger. **It is therefore specified as a programme with a checkpoint, not as one phase to be built end to end** — stages A–C are the first slice and are individually shippable; D onward are agreed in shape here and re-confirmed at the checkpoint, because on this project's record a stage that has been flown changes the estimate for the one after it.

**Diagnosis (read from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **Five meshes, three textures, one 780-line PowerShell script.** `tools/scripts/gen_assets.ps1` emits every mesh in the game as base64-embedded glTF and every texture through System.Drawing. **Its vocabulary is already an authoring language** — `Add-Box`, `Add-Beam`, `Add-Torus`, `Add-FlatTriangle`, `Add-Vertex`, with `Write-Gltf` as the emitter — and its header already states the discipline this phase must keep: *"everything here is seeded or analytic: rerunning must leave `git status` clean apart from whatever was deliberately changed."* The tool is not replacing an absent pipeline; it is replacing a write-only one that nobody can see the output of until the game boots.
- **The cooker needs no change to accept new source assets.** `main()` walks the source tree with `platform::listFiles`, which **recurses** (`system_win32.cpp:56-70`), and dispatches on extension: `.png → .stex`, `.gltf`/`.glb` `→ .smesh`, `.wav`/`.ogg` `→ .saud`, `.font → .sfont`, with per-file staleness. **Drop a new `.gltf` under `assets/` and it is cooked on the next build, today, with no code written.**
- **⚑ AND YET THE RUNTIME CANNOT REFERENCE A MESH IT WAS NOT COMPILED TO KNOW ABOUT. This is the wall the whole programme exists to remove.** `ModelId` is a five-member enum (`scene_renderer.hpp:42-52`); `SceneRenderer::initialize` hardcodes five `loadMesh` calls and three `loadTexture` calls into eight named members (`scene_renderer.cpp:78-95`); `SceneRenderer::draw` dispatches through a hardcoded `switch` that also picks the texture and the emissive (`scene_renderer.cpp:266-291`); `modelIdFromName` resolves exactly three strings and warns on anything else (`space_world.cpp:158-170`); and `modelBaseRadius` is a fourth hardcoded switch (`space_world.cpp:107-116`) which **silently omits `Cockpit` and falls through to the ship's 8 m**. `ships.toml` has carried a `model = "ship"` field since Phase 5 and only three names have ever meant anything. **An authoring tool built on today's runtime authors into a wall, so the registry is the prerequisite for every other stage and not a nicety.**
- **⚑ THERE IS NO EDITABLE MESH REPRESENTATION ANYWHERE IN THE REPO, AND FOUR OF THE USER'S ASKS ARE UNBUILDABLE WITHOUT ONE.** `assets::MeshData` is `{vector<MeshVertex>, vector<uint32_t>}` — a GPU upload buffer, not a mesh. The generator's builder is append-only triangle soup: `Add-Box` emits **24 unshared vertices**, `Add-Beam` 24, `Add-FlatTriangle` 3; nothing is welded and no index is reused across parts. Its indices are `uint16`, a **65,535-vertex ceiling that the `.smesh` format itself does not have** (the file stores `uint32`). Merging objects, moving a vertex, optimising and decimating all need **adjacency**, and adjacency exists nowhere. **That is the second load-bearing build of this programme and it is a headless, testable one** — which is the good news, because it is exactly the kind of code this repo verifies well.
- **The metadata worth authoring mostly has no consumer yet, and the two halves must not be confused.** `kCockpitOffset` is a constant in `ship_camera.hpp:102` **shared by hand with a comment in `gen_assets.ps1:496`**; `modelBaseRadius` is a switch; the station's 100 m collision sphere, 130 m avoidance sphere and 200 m berth ring are constants in `space_world.cpp`; and **there are no hardpoints at all** — a muzzle is `transform.position + forward * noseOffset` (`space_world.cpp:5986`), one derived point. So authoring metadata splits into *moving facts that already have consumers out of code and into the asset*, which pays immediately, and *inventing metadata nothing reads*, which **8u's rule forbids** — a lever that reaches a state the sim cannot is a second implementation. **Every metadata field this programme adds must name its consumer in the same stage that adds it.**
- **ImGui is available to a tool, and `DevUi` is not reusable as it stands.** AGENTS §5 permits Dear ImGui for dev tooling and forbids it as shipping game UI, so an editor exe is squarely inside the policy; the vendored Vulkan and win32 backends are already wired in `engine/ui/src/dev_ui.cpp` and `win32/dev_ui_win32.cpp`. But `DevUi::beginFrame(const OverlayStats&)` builds *the game's* overlay and console and offers a caller no way to draw its own windows. **The host must be split from the content**: an ImGui host that owns init / new-frame / record, with `DevUi` becoming its first client and the tool its second.
- **`sol::renderer::MeshRenderer` is reusable; `game/src/scene_renderer.cpp` is not.** `MeshRenderer` is engine — it takes `assets::MeshData` in, has `setSunlight`, and records draws with an emissive push constant, which is the game's own lighting exactly. What is not reusable is the game's frame: the HDR target, sky, particles, tonemap and UI compositing all live in `game/src`, and **AGENTS §4 forbids the engine or a tool from including game code.** The tool therefore writes its own thinner frame — window, context, swapchain, depth, mesh pipeline, ImGui, present — and that bring-up (~300 lines, `main.cpp` and `scene_renderer.cpp` being the worked examples) is real cost that must be budgeted rather than assumed free.
- **There is no mesh LOD path in the renderer, so LODs would today be authored into nothing.** `RenderInstance` names **one** model (`scene_renderer.hpp:56-62`), and `ImpostorRenderer` is not a general LOD system — it is analytic sphere impostors for celestial bodies (*"planets are scenery, rendered scaled-space"*). **Generating an LOD chain and selecting one at distance are two different items**, and the second is a renderer change that has to be built or the first is dead data. Named here so the stage is not half-built.

**Decisions taken with the user before any code.** (1) **A separate in-engine editor executable**, not a mode inside the game — the tool gets the real renderer, real lighting and real scale, and the shipping binary carries no editor. (2) **Full scope is the target**: meshes, mesh metadata, textures and def rows, *plus* real modelling — parametric building blocks that stay editable, manual vertex/edge work, merging, optimisation and LOD generation. (3) **Staged, with a checkpoint after C**, because the honest estimate for D–H depends on what building A–C teaches.

**A. A model registry that is data, not an enum.** `ModelId` and its four hardcoded switches are replaced by a runtime handle resolved by name. A new strict-schema `[[model]]` def — the same shape as 8t's `[[sound]]`, which is the worked example — names the mesh, the texture, the base radius and the emissive, and `SceneRenderer` loads what the manifest names in a loop rather than by hand. `modelIdFromName` becomes a registry lookup; `modelBaseRadius` becomes a field, which incidentally fixes the `Cockpit` fall-through. **Exit: drop a `.gltf` into `assets/meshes/`, add a row to `models.toml`, and a ship def flies it with no C++ change.** This stage ships value on its own and is what finally lets the gate stop being a squashed cube.

**B. An editable mesh core in `sol::assets`, pure and headless.** `MeshData` already lives there and both the cooker and the runtime already link it, so no new module is warranted. New `mesh_build.hpp` (the primitive constructors) and `mesh_edit.hpp` (the editable representation): an indexed mesh with vertex welding by position/normal/uv tolerance, face and vertex adjacency, normal recomputation flat or smooth-by-angle, and the operations the modelling stages need — merge, weld, remove-degenerates, index optimisation, and quadric-error decimation for LODs. The primitive vocabulary is **the generator's own**, ported: box, beam, torus, flat triangle, plus revolve and extrude, which are what the existing meshes are already faking by hand. **⚑ The regression net is the existing five meshes**: the new path must reproduce them, and `gen_assets.ps1`'s own "rerunning leaves `git status` clean" rule is the assertion. **Exit: a new `geometry.unit` suite (the 10th) proves the operations, and the mesh half of the PowerShell script can be retired.**

**C. `tools/forge` — the tool, as a viewer first.** A second executable beside `sol_cooker`, linking the engine up to `sol::renderer` and `sol::ui`: window, orbit camera, the game's own sun and mesh shader, a metric grid, and a **scale reference** (a station at 200 m, a ship at 12 m, the cockpit at arm's length) because *scale* is the thing that cannot be judged from a text file and is exactly what a viewer buys. Opens `.gltf` and `.smesh`; reports vertices, triangles, bounds and the radius the registry will use. Requires the ImGui host split named in the diagnosis. **Exit: a human can look at any mesh in this game, at real scale, under the game's own light, before it is in the game.** A viewer is the cheapest thing that makes every later stage verifiable, and it is the first thing that would have caught "the gate is a provisional squashed cube" three phases ago.

**⚑ Checkpoint after C — held 2026-08-19, and this is what it decided.** Stages A, B and C were on the branch and green; the three questions on the table were what to build next, the parked bake-vs-edit-list rule, and the outstanding playtest.

- **D is picked, alone, with another checkpoint after it** — not D+F, not a jump to E, and not pausing the programme. The argument that won it is one A–C created rather than one the spec predicted: **stage B left two implementations of the same five meshes** (`gen_assets.ps1` and `mesh_build.cpp`), and because B's regression net is *embedded hashes* rather than a comparison against the script's own output, **editing the script and regenerating will not fail the suite** — it will quietly leave the C++ port describing an asset that no longer exists. D's `.forge` importer is what retires the script and closes that, and it is the only actively dangerous item the programme is carrying. D is also the first stage in which the tool *writes* anything at all: C can open and report, and nothing else.
- **The bake-vs-edit-list rule is deliberately NOT decided here, and that is a change from what this spec said.** The spec parked it *for* this checkpoint, but D is parametric-only: with no hand-editing in the stage, **the part tree is the source of truth by construction** and the ambiguity has nothing to bite on. Deciding it now would mean deciding it with strictly less information than the D→E checkpoint will have. **It moves to the D→E checkpoint.** The lean on record, to be argued against rather than assumed: hand-edits *bake*, with the tree retained in the `.forge` file as an explicit "regenerate from parameters, discarding edits" path — an edit list keyed to vertex indices dies the moment a parameter change alters the vertex count, and this game's meshes are 16–750 vertices, not film assets.
- **The playtest of 8x/8y/8z stays outstanding by the user's decision** — raised at this checkpoint with its two measured caveats and declined for now. It is not blocking D. It stays on the handoff.

**⚑ What A–C changed about D–H, recorded so the next checkpoint does not re-derive it.** **F has a constraint now, and it was measured rather than assumed:** a decimated hull *circumscribes* the one it came from — 1.21× bounding radius at 16:1 — so **LOD selection must carry a radius per level and cannot reuse the `[[model]]` row's**, which is a renderer change with a number attached rather than a shape. F also inherits a ready-made target from C: the asteroid costs **2.99** cache misses/triangle against the station's **0.70**. **E is unchanged and still the least certain stage.** And one item that is not a stage and should be folded into whichever one next touches the tool: **the Forge does not read `models.toml`**, which is precisely why C's two radius mismatches (the asteroid's mesh measures 1.1584 m against an authored 1.0, the station's 102.0 against 100.0, so **both collision spheres sit inside their own meshes**) had to be found by eye. Comparing measured against authored turns a one-off discovery into a standing check.

**D. Parametric part-tree authoring, and a source format that survives editing.** A `.forge` source file holds a tree of parts, each a primitive with named parameters and a transform, so an asset stays editable rather than being frozen at its triangles. The tool edits the tree and rebuilds through B live; **the cooker gains a `.forge → .smesh` importer**, which retires the base64-glTF middle-man entirely, with glTF *export* kept as an explicit tool action for interop rather than as a pipeline step. **Exit: the gate is re-authored as an aperture you can see through, and reaches the game through A's registry.**

**D, as refined at the checkpoint.** The shape above is unchanged; these are the decisions taken before writing any of it.

- **`.forge` is TOML, parsed by `sol::core::TomlValue`.** The parser already in the repo handles `[[array-of-table]]`, nested tables, inline tables and arrays, so the format costs no new dependency (AGENTS §5) and the validation idiom is the one `data_defs.cpp` already uses. It is also the only choice that keeps an authored asset **diffable in git**, which a base64-embedded glTF has never been.
- **The tree is a flat `[[part]]` list with `parent` naming another part by id, NOT nested TOML headers.** `[[part.children.children]]` is expressible — the parser's `descend` handles it — and it is the wrong choice twice over: it is unreadable at the 10–17 parts this game's assets actually have, and **re-parenting a part rewrites an indentation block instead of one line**, which is the whole reason for choosing a text format. Validation owes: every parent resolves, and the graph is acyclic.
- **Transforms go into `MeshBuilder`, at `addVertex` and `addTriangle`, because those are the single funnels every primitive already routes through.** Positions by the matrix, normals by the inverse-transpose renormalised, and **winding flipped when the determinant is negative** or a mirrored part turns itself inside out. It stays in double and rounds once at `build()`, which is stage B's own rule and what keeps identity transforms bit-exact — so **B's byte-for-byte shipped-mesh net is untouched by this change**, and that is the assertion, not the hope.
- **The load-bearing test is that a `.forge` transcription of a shipped mesh reproduces `shipped::buildX()` exactly.** Round-trip and validation tests prove the format is well-formed; only this one proves **the format can express what this game already ships**, which is the claim D is actually making.
- **The cooker gains a stem-collision guard in the same stage.** Output paths are keyed on file stem with no cross-extension check today, so `gate.forge` and `gate.gltf` would silently race for `gate.smesh` — a hazard that has existed since Phase 5 and that adding a second mesh source extension is what makes reachable.

**⚑ Checkpoint after D — held 2026-08-19, and it decided the parked rule and picked a debt over a stage.** Stages A–D were on the branch, green, with a clean tree; the three questions were what to build next, the bake-vs-edit-list rule that the C checkpoint deferred to here, and the outstanding playtest.

- **⚑ THE PICK IS D's OWN DEBT, NOT A NEW STAGE — retire the mesh half of `gen_assets.ps1` for real.** D was chosen at the last checkpoint precisely to make that possible, and it made it possible without doing it: five `.gltf` sources still sit in `assets/meshes/` beside the one `gate.forge`, and the script's header still reads *"This half retires for real when the .forge importer lands at stage D."* The importer landed. **So the only actively dangerous item the programme carries — two implementations of the same five meshes, where editing the script and regenerating will not fail the suite because B's net is embedded hashes rather than the file — is still carried.** Closing it is smaller than a stage and it is what D was for. **Folded in with it: the Forge reads `models.toml` and compares measured radius against authored**, which the C checkpoint asked be attached to whichever stage next touched the tool.
- **⚑ Sizing it against the script changed the estimate, in the cheap direction and then not.** Four of the five meshes need **no new engine code at all**: the cube is one `addBox`, the station a torus and nine boxes, the ship sixteen `addFlatTriangle` calls, the cockpit ten beams, seven boxes and six triangles — every one of those a primitive `.forge` already carries, so four of the five are pure transcription. **The asteroid is the one that resists, and what it resists is instructive:** it is a twice-subdivided icosphere displaced by noise **keyed on the PowerShell arithmetic bug stage B measured**, so a primitive that reproduced it would put a bug-compatibility shim into the engine's permanent vocabulary, inherited by every future asset built with it.
- **⚑ THE BAKE RULE IS DECIDED, AND IT IS PER PART — neither of the two options this spec framed.** The lean on record was *bake the document*; the alternative was *an edit list on top of parts*. **Both were rejected for the same reason: they answer at the wrong granularity.** A hand edit converts **only the parts it touches** into a literal `[[part]]` carrying explicit geometry, and every part the hand never reached stays parametric and editable. Baking the whole document means one nudged vertex freezes the gate's ring radius forever, silently. An edit list keyed to vertex indices dies the moment a parameter change alters the vertex count — and this game's meshes are 16–750 vertices, where a literal part is still readable text and still diffs a line per vertex, which a base64 glTF never did.
- **⚑ And the rule gets its first consumer immediately, which is why it is worth deciding here rather than at E: the asteroid is baked.** It is the honest description of that asset — nobody is going to tune `0.13·sin(3.1x + 1.7)` in a TOML file — it keeps the PowerShell hash out of engine code, it is byte-exact by construction, and **it means E arrives with its bake format already built and proved against a shipped asset rather than argued about on paper.** The recipe is not lost: it stays in `engine/test/geometry/shipped_meshes.hpp` as the provenance, pinned to the same embedded hashes the baked file is, which is what makes them one asset with a net rather than two implementations.
- **The playtest of 8x/8y/8z stays outstanding, declined again by the user.** Raised for the second time with the same two measured caveats. It is not blocking. It stays on the handoff.

**⚑ D's DEBT IS CLOSED (`bb97976` the sources, `0288e51` the catalog check). One implementation, and the deletion is what makes it true.** `assets/meshes/` holds six `.forge` files and no `.gltf`; `gen_assets.ps1` is 324 lines of textures and audio with its mesh half gone; `geometry.unit` **reads the committed files** through a `SOL_MESH_SOURCE_DIR` handed in by CMake, which is what makes deleting the glTF safe rather than merely tidy. geometry.unit 51 → **58 tests**. No save change, no sim change.

- **⚑ THE ESTIMATE WAS CHEAP IN THE RIGHT DIRECTION AND THE EXPENSIVE PART WAS SOMEWHERE ELSE.
