Four of the five meshes needed no new engine code at all** — the cube is one `addBox`, the station a torus and nine boxes, the ship sixteen `addFlatTriangle` calls, the cockpit ten beams, seven boxes and six triangles, and `box`/`beam`/`torus`/`flat_triangle` were all primitives `.forge` already had. **All four reproduced the shipped bytes on the first run.** The cost was in the asteroid and in what the *cooked* comparison turned up.
- **⚑ THE SHIP HAS NO PARAMETRIC STRUCTURE TO RECOVER, AND SAYING SO IS THE HONEST TRANSCRIPTION.** The generator wrote a loop over two four-point rings, but the rings are different shapes and the nose is a fan onto a point, so it is a **loft** — and a loft primitive invented to fit one asset would be a general vocabulary shaped around a single caller. Sixteen `flat_triangle` parts is what the ship *is*. **Moving a shared corner therefore means editing four parts, and that is the clearest case for stage E in the repo** — it is written into the file's own header as such.
- **⚑ THE COCKPIT'S EYE OFFSET COULD NOT BE A GROUP TRANSFORM, AND THE REASON IS ONE LINE OF `mesh_build.cpp`.** Authoring the parts eye-relative under a parent at `(0, 0.8, −5)` expresses exactly the same placement and does **not** reproduce the mesh: `addVertex` **renormalises every normal it transforms**, and a beam's oriented normal is not exactly unit in double, so each one shifts in its last bits. Positions would have been exact (a translation is `p + t` through the matrix) and normals would not. **A transform is free for a box and not for anything computed.** So the file carries absolute coordinates with the offsets in its comments.
  - The corollary that made the transcription cheap: **writing the clean literals worked**, because `build()` rounds to float and a double-arithmetic difference of an ulp or two vanishes at 24 bits. `0.8 − 0.46` need not equal `0.34` for the mesh to be identical.
- **⚑⚑ THE FINDING: THE COOKED BYTES ARE NOT IDENTICAL, AND THE REGRESSION NET HAD BEEN PINNED ONE STEP UPSTREAM OF WHAT SHIPS.** Every position, every uv and every index matches to the bit. **Only normals moved, by at most one float ulp** — because `tools/cooker/src/gltf.cpp:213` **renormalises in float** after applying the node transform, while the `.forge` path normalises in double and rounds once. Measured: max `|n|−1` falls from **1.33e−7 to 4.0e−8** on the asteroid, 1.12e−7 to 2.82e−8 on the ship. **The new data is strictly closer to unit**, and the point is not the ulp — it is that stage B's byte-for-byte net compared against the *glTF buffers* and nothing had ever compared the *cooked output* the game actually loads. Both paths existing was what hid it; deleting one closes it by construction.
- **⚑ THE ASTEROID IS BAKED, AND THAT IS THE D CHECKPOINT'S RULE MEETING ITS FIRST REAL CASE.** Its noise is keyed on PowerShell's uint32-multiply-promotes-to-double bug, so a `noise_sphere` primitive would have put a bug-compatibility shim in the engine's permanent vocabulary, inherited by every future asset, to serve one caller. `ForgePrimitive::Mesh` carries literal geometry instead: **one vertex per line, eight numbers, at FLOAT precision** — because a baked part's numbers came out of a `MeshData` and a mesh is float, so a double writer's seventeen digits would be fifteen digits of noise per coordinate in a file whose whole purpose is being readable. 99 KB, 1291 lines, and a moved vertex is one changed line. **`forgeBakePart` is engine API and stage E inherits the bake built and proved rather than argued.** The recipe stays in `shipped_meshes.hpp` as provenance, pinned to the same hashes, which is what makes the two one asset with a net rather than two implementations.
- **⚑ D's STEM-COLLISION GUARD FIRED ON A REAL CASE, WHICH IS THE ONLY WAY TO KNOW A GUARD WORKS.** Adding the four `.forge` files beside their `.gltf` failed the build with *"cockpit.forge and cockpit.gltf both cook to cookpit.smesh — 4 output collision(s); nothing cooked"*. It was written at D against a hazard standing since Phase 5 and had never been triggered by anything but its own unit test.
- **⚑ THE CATALOG CHECK'S TOLERANCE HAD TO BE RELATIVE, AND THE GATE IS WHY.** The Forge now reads `game/data`'s `[[model]]` rows through `sol::assets::DefDatabase` — the game's **data**, never its code, which is the line AGENTS §4 draws — and prints authored radius, avoid radius, texture, emissive and solid beside the measured ones. An absolute tolerance flagged the gate, which authors **106.7** against a measured **106.7005**: somebody rounding a number they read off this very panel. **One tenth of one percent separates that from the four real mismatches (station 2%, ship 12%, cube 13%, asteroid 16%) by three orders of magnitude.** Live: the asteroid and station warn with exactly the numbers stage C found by eye, the gate reads *"matches the mesh"*. **⚑ CORRECTED AT THE E1–E3 CHECKPOINT'S DEBT SLICE: that list of four percentages is UNSIGNED and it is two opposite defects — station `+2.00%` and asteroid `+15.84%` have the collision sphere inside the hull, ship `−12.42%` and cube `−13.40%` have it outside.** See the slice's record below.
  - And the first version of the warning **ran off the panel edge and stopped at "the collision sphere sits"** — the fourth widget in this repo not to fit its box, and here the sentence *is* the finding, so it wraps now.
- **Live proof, both exes.** The Forge opens `station.forge` as **ten named parts** with the torus's own `major_radius 90 / tube_radius 12` editable in the panel — an asset that was a base64 blob an hour earlier. The game boots into the cockpit with the canopy frame, dash and console all drawn from the re-authored source, chase view shows the ship wedge and the station beyond it, `sim.avoidance` and `sim.collision.build` **both still n=11** with `resolve` at 55, and the pause-menu quit returned **exit code 0**. All 10 suites green in ~81 s; `dev-gpu` 2/2.
- **⚑ WHAT IS STILL OPEN AFTER THIS.** **Nothing compares the cooked `.smesh` against anything** — the finding above was made by hand with a scratch script, and `cooker.unit` still does not assert that a `.forge` cooks to the bytes the game draws. **`ModelMatch::radiusAgrees` has no test**: there is no suite for `tools/forge` and the tool is verified by running it (AGENTS §7), but that threshold is a real decision sitting in untested code. **The `.forge` writer still discards comments**, so saving any of these six files from the tool strips the headers that carry every finding above — unchanged from D, and now much more expensive because the headers are where this knowledge lives. **The Forge cannot write a `[[model]]` row**, only read one; that is stage H.

**⚑ Checkpoint before E — held 2026-08-19, and it picked a debt over a stage for the second time running.** Stages A–D and D's debt slice were on the branch, all 10 suites green in 80.7 s, tree clean; the questions were the next slice, E's scope, and the outstanding playtest.

- **⚑ THE PICK IS THE COMMENT-PRESERVING WRITER, THEN E.** `writeForge` regenerates the whole file from the document, so **every save strips every comment** — a gap listed at D and carried since. What makes it the pick now rather than then is that **E is the first stage that saves constantly**: a modeller writes the file on every accepted edit, so the loss stops being a thing you can avoid by not pressing Save. And the thing being lost has grown: the six `.forge` headers are now where this project's knowledge about its own assets actually lives (the cockpit's eye-offset-cannot-be-a-transform finding, the ship's "no parametric structure to recover", the asteroid's provenance and why its hash is wrong on purpose). **Stage H inherits the same fix**, because writing a `[[model]]` row means writing `models.toml`, which is commented too.
- **⚑ E's SCOPE IS AGREED AS THE FULL SPEC** — vertices, edges and faces, plus extrude, split and merge — and A–D moved three of its costs, two down and one up. **Vertex picking is already written**: `sol/ui/pick.hpp:114` `pickNearestPoint` projects candidates to screen and takes the nearest within a grab radius, which is vertex picking exactly, shipped in 8j. **Undo has no precedent anywhere in this project and needs none**: `ForgeDoc` is a plain value (a flat `vector<ForgePart>`) and the five hand-authored files are 0.5–5 KB, so undo is *copy the document*, not a command pattern — only the baked asteroid at 99 KB even registers. **Edge and face picking is the one that costs more than assumed**: there is no ray-triangle test anywhere in the repo, so Möller–Trumbore has to be written. Gizmos are new, and merge is mostly plumbing over `mesh_edit`'s existing weld/append.
- **⚑ G's FORMAT FORK IS NAMED AND DELIBERATELY NOT DECIDED HERE.** The repo has a PNG **decoder** and `sol::core::inflate`, and **no encoder and no deflate** — so G is either a PNG writer (a stored-block deflate is legal and short, but commits ~256 KB where the three current textures are 4–7 KB) or a procedural texture source in the shape D just gave meshes (diffable, tiny, and the precedent is one stage old). Deciding it before E ships would be deciding with strictly less information than the E→F checkpoint will have, **which is the argument the C checkpoint used to defer the bake rule, and that deferral was right.**
- **The playtest of 8x/8y/8z is deferred to a named point rather than declined a third time** — the user chose to play it once this slice ships. That is a change from the C and D checkpoints, where it was declined outright.
- **`feat/the-forge` was pushed.** Ten commits of Phase 9 existed only on this machine; there is a remote copy now. Nothing is merged and `dev` is untouched.

**⚑ What reading the six files decided about the writer slice, before writing any of it.**

- **There is not one end-of-line comment in any of the six `.forge` files.** Every comment is a whole-line block, sitting either at the top of the file or immediately above a `[[part]]`. So preservation is an *attachment* problem, not the span-tracking problem it would be if values and comments shared lines.
- **⚑ AND `cockpit.forge` DECIDES THE SHAPE: a blank line sits between the `# --- canopy frame ---` block and the `[[part]]` it introduces.** So what the document stores is **verbatim leading trivia — comments AND blank lines together — attached to whatever follows**, not "the comment block before a part". The second reproduces the comment and silently loses the blank line, and a writer that quietly reformats whitespace is a writer nobody will let near a committed file.
- **The assertion is byte-exactness against the committed files**: `writeForge(parseForge(f)) == f` for all six. `geometry.unit` can make it because the debt slice already taught it to read them through `SOL_MESH_SOURCE_DIR` — the net that was missing at D is available now for free.
- **Two residuals are named rather than hidden**: a comment *inside* a multi-line array's brackets, and an end-of-line comment after a value, still cannot be placed. Neither exists in this repo. The parser flags a document that carries one so the tool can say so out loud, which is the difference between a known gap and a silent loss.

**⚑ THE WRITER SLICE IS SHIPPED (`8d56988`) — A FILE COMES BACK THE WAY IT WENT IN.** Comments and blank lines are carried on the document as verbatim **leading trivia** attached to whatever stands below them; `writeForge(parseForge(f)) == f` byte for byte for all six committed assets, asserted in `geometry.unit` (58 → **65 tests**). No save change, no sim change, four files.

- **⚑ IT IS TRIVIA, NOT "THE COMMENT BLOCK", AND `cockpit.forge` IS WHY.** It puts a blank line between its `# --- canopy frame ---` divider and the `[[part]]` that divider introduces, so storing only the words would reproduce them and quietly close the gap. The scanner is a second, deliberately dumb pass over the source text, because `sol::core::TomlValue` has discarded every comment by the time a document exists.
- **⚑ THE ATTACHMENT RULE HAD ONE TRAP AND THE ROUND TRIP CAUGHT IT.** A part's trivia belongs to the **part**, even when that part is the first thing in the file. Reading it the other way — *everything above the first element is the header* — makes the writer emit the header **and** its own separator, so **the file grows a blank line on every save**. A tool whose save is not a fixed point is one nobody can leave running.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE COMMENTS WERE NOT THE ONLY THING A SAVE DELETED — THE SECOND LOSS WAS FOUND BY THE TEST, NOT BY THE DIAGNOSIS.** The writer skipped any parameter equal to its schema default, which dropped **four lines a person had typed on purpose**: gate's `segments_u = 32`, station's `segments_v = 12`, ship's `uv2` and cockpit's `width = 0.1`. It writes what was *authored* now — `params` holds exactly the keys the file carried, since the panel reads through `value()` and calls `set()` only on a real edit. **An author who writes the segment count down is naming the knob, and a tool that answers by deleting the line is not one they will trust with the file.** `sameValue` went with it, 45 lines of now-dead comparison.
- **The byte-exact net was reachable only because of D's debt slice** — `geometry.unit` already reads the committed `.forge` files through `SOL_MESH_SOURCE_DIR`, so the assertion is against the real assets rather than a fixture that agrees with the code by construction. Two stages paying for each other, recorded because it is an argument for closing debt when it is cheap.
- **Live, through the tool's own save path rather than through the library.** All six sources opened and saved in the Forge, three seconds apart — `LastWriteTime` moved on every one, byte length unchanged on every one, and **`git status` clean after each**. ⚑ A save that silently never fired would also leave `git status` clean, which is why the timestamps are the assertion and not the diff. The old unconditional *"save rewrites the file: comments are not kept"* line is gone; in its place a warning that fires only for a document carrying a comment this model cannot place, **proved with a positive/negative pair** (a scratch file with a trailing comment shows it in amber, wrapped to three lines inside the panel; the asteroid shows nothing). All 10 suites green in 81.9 s.
- **⚑ WHAT IS STILL OPEN.** Two comment positions remain unplaceable — **after a value on the same line, and inside a multi-line array's brackets** — because either needs a slot per key that the document does not have. Neither exists in this repo's assets, and `ForgeDoc::hasUnplaceableComments` is how the tool says so instead of dropping them in silence. **The writer also fixes element order** (header, `name`, `[build]`, parts), so a file that put `[build]` before `name` would be reordered; none does. **`models.toml` still gets no protection from any of this** — stage H writes def rows and will need the same treatment, one format over.

**⚑⚑ STAGE E — SPEC'D 2026-08-20 ON `feat/move-that-corner`, AND READING THE SIX ASSETS MOVED THE STAGE'S CENTRE BEFORE A LINE WAS WRITTEN.** The E checkpoint framed this stage around the D rule — *a hand edit bakes the parts it touches* — with `ship.forge` as the motivating case. Both are still true, and **the relation between them is the opposite of what it looked like: the motivating case needs no bake at all.**

- **⚑⚑ `ship.forge`'s SHARED CORNER IS A PARAMETER, NOT A GENERATED VERTEX — SO THE STAGE'S HEADLINE CASE IS A PARAMETER EDIT, AND THE BAKE IS THE ESCAPE HATCH BEHIND IT.** A `flat_triangle` part's `p0`/`p1`/`p2` are `Vec3` parameters that `addFlatTriangle` emits **one for one** as vertices. Dragging that corner writes three numbers into a TOML file and the part stays fully parametric, editable and diffable. Reading the D checkpoint's rule as the *mechanism* would have baked sixteen triangles into 48 literal vertices to move one point — converting the most readable asset in the repo into its least readable form, in the name of a rule written for the asteroid. **This is the third phase running where the load-bearing finding was that some other representation already held the answer** (Phase 10's `lookAlong`, Phase 11's autopilot relative velocity), and the second where the estimate that moved was the one written days earlier by this project.
- **⚑ THE HONEST TAXONOMY IS THREE CLASSES, AND IT IS MEASURED ACROSS ALL 61 PARTS IN `assets/meshes/` RATHER THAN GUESSED.** The six files hold **box ×25, flat_triangle ×22, beam ×10, torus ×2, group ×1, mesh ×1**; the group carries no geometry, leaving 60.
  - **(1) The parameter IS the vertex — 23 parts** (`flat_triangle` ×22, `mesh` ×1). The drag writes the parameter directly. Exact, and it is where the motivating case lives.
  - **(2) The vertex solves back to parameters — 35 parts** (`box` ×25 → `center`+`size` with the opposite corner pinned; `beam` ×10 → `from`/`to`). A drag is a resize or a re-aim rather than a move, which is what an author dragging a box corner already expects.
  - **(3) No parametric answer — 2 parts** (`torus` ×2). A ring vertex is a function of two segment indices and there is nothing to write. **These are what the D rule's bake is for, and they are two parts out of sixty.**
- **⚑⚑ THE MECHANISM: SELECTION IS OVER WELDED POSITIONS, AND ONE POSITION MAPS TO A SET OF WRITES.** `toEditMesh` already collapses corners to points over a position graph built for exactly this reason — *"a box has 8 points under 24 corners"* (`mesh_edit.hpp`) — so the granularity a modeller wants exists and is tested. A picked position resolves to every `(part, parameter, slot)` that contributed to it, and the drag applies all of them in one edit. **That is what makes one drag fix a seam instead of opening one.**
- **⚑⚑ AND THE WORKED EXAMPLE IS WORSE THAN THE FILE THINKS IT IS, IN BOTH DIRECTIONS, WHICH IS THE ARGUMENT FOR THE STAGE.** `ship.forge`'s header says moving a front corner means editing *"four of them"*. Measured: **48 corners over 12 distinct positions**, and the corner at `(-2.6, -1.3, -1.0)` is carried by **five parts under three different parameter names** — `nose_0.p2`, `hull_0a.p0`, `hull_0b.p0`, `nose_3.p1`, `hull_3a.p1` — because a front corner is shared across two quadrants. **Six of the twelve positions are five-way shared, three are four-way and three are two-way.**
  - **⚑ The header's ring tables are also incomplete, and that is the sharper half.** It documents *"the two rings the triangles came from"* as a nose and two four-point rings — nine points. The file has **twelve**: `[0.0, 1.7, 1.0]`, `[0.0, 2.0, 5.0]` and `[0.0, 3.8, 4.6]` belong to neither ring and are written down nowhere. **A file that documents its own pain undercounted the sharing by one and omitted a quarter of its own points**, and it is the best-commented asset in the repo. That is what hand-editing scattered literals costs, and it is the case for the tool stated by the asset rather than by the plan.

**⚑ What the code says this costs, read rather than estimated.**

- **`buildForge` can report per-part vertex ranges for almost nothing.** It emits parts in file order into one `MeshBuilder` (an ordering already load-bearing, because vertex numbering is what stage B's hashes pin), and `MeshBuilder::vertexCount()` is public — so the range is a bracket around the existing switch. Attribution needs no second build path.
- **⚑ BUT THE `[build]` POST-PASS DESTROYS THAT ATTRIBUTION, AND THE GUARD IS CHEAP BECAUSE NOTHING USES IT.** When `weld`, `optimize` or `smoothAngleDegrees > 0` is set, `buildForge` runs the soup through `toEditMesh`/`optimizeIndices`/`toMeshData`, which merges and renumbers vertices — after which a built index no longer names a part. **Not one of the six committed assets carries a `[build]` table** (checked; there are zero), which is the same fact that makes them byte-provable. So E offers selection when the post-pass is off and **says why** when it is on, rather than mapping silently and wrongly.
- **`BuildTransform` has no inverse and stage E needs one.** It carries `transformPoint`, `transformDirection`, `determinant` and `isIdentity` — a world-space drag brought into a part's local frame needs the affine inverse, which is ~15 lines and provable headlessly in `geometry.unit`. **Note the neighbouring trap already on record: an adjugate carries the determinant's sign**, which is a normals bug, not a points bug, but it lives in the same fifteen lines.
- **The ray-triangle test is still genuinely absent** — nothing in the repo matches `Trumbore`, `rayTriangle` or `intersectTriangle` outside this document — so face picking still has to write Möller–Trumbore. **Vertex picking does not need it**: `sol::ui::pick.hpp`'s `screenPoint` + `pickNearestPoint` project candidates forward and take the nearest, which is the whole test, and the Forge already has the view matrix and `kCameraVerticalFov`.
- **⚑ `DebugDrawRenderer` SILENTLY DROPS LINES PAST `kMaxVertices = 8192`** — `line()` returns without a word when the buffer is full (`debug_draw_renderer.cpp:91`). The grid, three scale boxes and the bounds box already spend ~270 vertices. **Drawing a three-line cross on every vertex of the baked asteroid would want 7,746 and lose the argument silently**, which is the failure mode this repo keeps meeting from the other end. So markers are budgeted: every point of the selected part while it is under a named cap, and the hovered plus selected points always.
- **Undo is a document copy and the checkpoint was right** — `ForgeDoc` is a plain value and five of the six files are 0.5–5 KB. **The asteroid at 99 KB is the one that argues for a depth cap** rather than for a command pattern.

**⚑ The one question this spec does NOT decide, and the measurement that will.** Baking a part can keep the part's `parent` and placement and store its geometry in the **local** frame, or fold the world transform in and re-hang the part at the root. Local keeps a baked strut attached to the assembly it belongs to; world is **bit-exact by construction**, because a root part with an identity placement short-circuits `setTransform` and the same floats come back out. The gap between them is whether `float → normalize in double → float` survives a non-identity transform, which is an ulp question nobody should answer on paper. **The assertion is written first and it decides the design: baking any part of any committed asset must not change the built mesh.** If local passes it, local wins on authoring grounds; if it does not, world wins and the detach is recorded as its price.

**⚑ The slices, in build order, with the checkpoint named.**

- **E1 — pick a point, move it, undo it.** Position-level picking over the built mesh; the position→writes map; class (1) parameters only; drag in the view plane and on an axis; document-snapshot undo. **This alone closes `ship.forge`.**
- **E2 — box and beam solve back.** Class (2), covering 35 of the 60 geometry parts. Small, and it is what makes the cockpit and the station editable.
- **E3 — bake the rest.** Class (3) on first hand edit, per the D rule, under the bit-exactness assertion above.
- **⚑ CHECKPOINT AFTER E1–E3**, where every vertex in every shipped asset is movable and the tool has been driven. G's PNG-writer-vs-procedural fork is still deliberately open and is *not* decided here either; it is decided when F's shape is known.
- **E4 — edges and faces.** `MeshAdjacency::edges` is already a sorted unique edge list with its faces, so edge picking is a projection over data that exists; face picking is where Möller–Trumbore lands. Moving either is moving its points, which E1 built.
- **E5 — extrude, split, merge.** `mesh_edit`'s `append`, `weld` and `removeDegenerateFaces` are the plumbing; these operate on baked parts, so they follow E3 by construction.

**⚑⚑ E1 IS SHIPPED (`8bf312e` the engine, `179f73d` the tool) — A CORNER IS CLICKED, DRAGGED AND UNDONE, AND FIVE PARTS MOVE TOGETHER.** `buildForge` optionally reports per-part vertex ranges; `forgePoints` turns those into the distinct points of the built mesh, each carrying every authored value standing at it; `forgeMovePoint` writes all of them through a new `BuildTransform::inverse`. `geometry.unit` **65 → 78**. All 10 suites green in 83.2 s, `dev-gpu` 2/2, no save change, no sim change.

- **⚑⚑ THE TESTS FOUND A DEFECT BEFORE THE TOOL EXISTED, AND IT IS THE SAME DISCIPLINE THE WRITER SLICE FOUGHT FOR.** A `ForgePoint`'s position is read out of a `MeshData`, so it is **float**: an authored `-2.6` comes back as `-2.5999999046325684`. A move that took a **destination** would rebuild every authored double from that rounded number — so clicking a vertex and letting go, **a drag of zero distance**, would rewrite five lines in five parts with noise nobody typed, on a tool that saves after every accepted edit. **The move takes a DELTA and adds it to the value already in the document**, and a zero-distance move over every movable point of `ship.forge` and `asteroid.forge` is now asserted to leave both files byte for byte identical. The first three test failures of the stage were all this one fact.
- **⚑ THE LIVE PROOF IS A DIFF, NOT A SCREENSHOT.** Driven through the tool's own save path: clicking one marker on `ship.forge` printed *"at 2.6000 2.0000 5.0000, written by 5 part(s): `hull_1a.p2`, `hull_1b.p1`, `hull_2b.p2`, `rear_lower.p2`, `rear_upper.p1`"*; dragging it read `3.9506 3.1637 3.3070` and **still 12 points written by the same five parts** — a partial write would have split one point into two, and *that* is the assertion rather than the new coordinate. Save then diff: **exactly five lines changed (69, 78, 110, 152, 161), 186 lines before and after, every comment and divider untouched.** Undo then save: **byte-for-byte identical by SHA256, with `LastWriteTime` moved** — the pair a clean `git status` alone cannot prove, per the rule the writer slice wrote down.
- **⚑ THE PANEL AND THE REPORT AGREE ABOUT WHAT A POINT IS, AND THAT WAS A DECISION.** `ship.forge` reads **12 points / 12 movable** beside the Report's own **welded points 12**, because `forgePoints` defaults to `WeldOptions::positionTolerance` rather than inventing a second tolerance. Two numbers meaning "one point" that disagreed would be the same class of bug as two implementations of one mesh.
- **⚑ THE REFUSALS WERE DRIVEN TOO, BECAUSE A GUARD NOBODY HAS SEEN FIRE IS NOT VERIFIED.** `station.forge` reads **552 points / 0 movable** and says why *inside its box* — nine boxes and a torus have no parametric answer to a dragged point. `asteroid.forge` reads **162 / 162**, every baked vertex movable, which is **the D checkpoint's bake rule paying out for the first time**: the asset that was chosen to be baked is the one asset that is fully hand-editable today.
- **⚑ AND ONE GUARD IS WRITTEN BUT UNTRIGGERED, NAMED RATHER THAN CLAIMED.** `DebugDrawRenderer::line()` returns **silently** once its 8192 vertices are spent, so a marker per vertex is a real hazard — but the largest asset here welds to **162 points**, so the cap at 600 has never fired. It is a guard waiting for its case, exactly as D's stem-collision guard was until `cockpit.forge` caught it.
- **⚑ THE LMB ARBITRATION HAD TO BE DECIDED RATHER THAN FALLEN INTO.** One button is both the orbit and the point drag. **The point tool is offered the press first**, and only if it declines does the camera claim; the other order is how a tool ends up needing a modifier key to select anything.
- **⚑ WHAT E1 DOES NOT REACH, SAID IN THE BUTTON RATHER THAN LEFT TO BE DISCOVERED.** Undo covers a point drag and the three part-list buttons, **not the parameter sliders**, which fire continuously while held — an entry per frame would bury the pre-edit state under sixty copies, and the asteroid's 99 KB is what makes that a real cost. The button reads **"undo drag"**. Also still open: **nothing compares the cooked `.smesh` against anything** (carried from D), and `ModelMatch::radiusAgrees` still has no test.

**Load-bearing tests, in `geometry.unit`, all headless.** Per-part vertex ranges partition the built mesh exactly, for all six committed assets. `BuildTransform::inverse()` round-trips a translate/rotate/non-uniform-scale composition — **and not one built from 90°, which passes by luck** (the degrees round-trip bullet in the gotchas is why). Moving `ship.forge`'s front corner writes five parts under three parameter names, and the rebuilt mesh moves that one point and nothing else — asserted against **all twelve** of the file's distinct positions, which is a number the file's own header does not know. Baking any single part of any committed asset leaves `buildForge`'s output unchanged. **And the writer's byte-for-byte round trip stays green throughout**, because a modeller saves on every accepted edit and that is the property the whole stage rides on.

**Out of scope for E, named so it is not half-built.** No gizmo *rendering* beyond debug lines — the tool has no textured 3D UI pass and inventing one is a stage of its own. No multi-select box drag in E1 (one point, then a set). No topology change on a parametric part: adding a vertex to a `box` is a bake, not a box. And **no LOD**, which is stage F and still owes the renderer its selection or it authors dead data.

**⚑⚑ E2 — SPEC'D 2026-08-20, AND READING THE FOUR PRIMITIVES REFUTED THREE THINGS THE SLICE'S ONE-LINE SPEC ASSUMED.** E2 was written down as *"box and beam solve back — class (2), covering 35 of the 60 geometry parts. Small."* The arithmetic is small. What is not small is that **three of E1's own invariants stop holding the moment a corner is not a parameter**, and each one is the kind that ships silently.

- **⚑⚑ E1's `movable()` IS `writes.size() == corners`, AND CLASS (2) BREAKS IT ON THE FIRST BOX.** A flat triangle's three corners are three *different* parameters, so one write per corner was an identity nobody had to think about. A box emits **24 corners over 8 geometric corners — three render vertices per corner, all naming the same `center`+`size` pair** — so the writes must be deduplicated, and a deduplicated set can never equal the corner count. **Left alone this is not a compile error, it is a box that reports itself unmovable**; fixed carelessly it is a delta applied three times and a corner that flies three times as far as the cursor. The point therefore counts **resolved corners** separately from the writes it collected, and `movable()` asks whether every corner found an answer rather than whether every corner produced a line.
- **⚑⚑ `cube.forge` AUTHORS NOTHING, SO E1's FIXED POINT WAS NEVER TESTED WHERE IT COULD FAIL.** The whole asset is `id`, `type` and a name — *every* box parameter is at its schema default, which is the property that file exists to demonstrate. `ForgePart::set` adds a key that is not there, and the writer emits what was authored, so **a click-and-release on the cube with zero drag distance would materialise `center = [0.0, 0.0, 0.0]` and `size = [1.0, 1.0, 1.0]` into a four-line asset** — the same defect E1 spent three test failures on, arriving through the other door. E1's zero-distance assertion covered `ship` and `asteroid`, both of which write every number they use. **The rule gets one more clause: a write whose local delta is exactly zero is not performed at all**, and the assertion widens to all six committed files, cube first.
- **⚑ A BEAM HAS NO AUTHORED CORNERS AT ALL, SO ITS DRAG IS A RE-AIM AND THE FAR END MOVES TOO.** `addBeam` derives `side` and `up` from the axis (`mesh_build.cpp:268`), so the eight corners are a function of `from`, `to`, `width` and `height` and **not one of them is a number in the file**. The only parametric answer to a dragged corner is the end it stands at, which means (a) the other three corners at that end come with it, and (b) **changing the axis re-derives the cross-section, so the four corners at the *pinned* end swing about it** — bounded by the half-diagonal of the section, which is 0.05 m on every beam in `cockpit.forge`. That is a real consequence and it gets said in the panel rather than discovered, because "I moved this end and the other end moved" is indistinguishable from a bug when nobody wrote it down.
- **⚑ TWO GUARDS, BOTH FOR STATES THE PRIMITIVE ANSWERS SILENTLY.** A drag that pushes a box's `size` through zero turns it inside out — the six face normals are authored constants, so a negative half-extent leaves each face lit as though it faced into the solid. And a drag that collapses a beam to zero length makes `addBeam` **return without emitting anything**, so the part vanishes from the mesh with no error anywhere. Both are refused with the part named. A box already flat on an axis (`|size| <= tolerance`) has no opposite corner to pin and is simply not movable, which routes it to the bake message the D rule already wrote.
- **The sign table is pinned by a test rather than trusted.** A box corner is identified by which of `addBox`'s 24 emitted vertices it is, encoded as three sign bits — exact, and immune to the float fuzz that reading the sign off a position would carry. That table is only correct while `addBox`'s face order is, so `geometry.unit` asserts the table against the built geometry instead of against itself.

**E2's numbers, measured before the code.** `station.forge` goes from **0 movable of 552** to **72** — nine boxes at eight corners each, with the habitat ring's 480 ring points still refusing until they are baked, which keeps E1's refusal test alive on the asset that first proved it. `cockpit.forge` becomes **fully movable**: ten beams, seven boxes and six triangles with nothing left over. `cube.forge` becomes eight movable points and is the fixed-point case above.

**⚑⚑ E2 IS SHIPPED (`ee771f1` the engine, `9914b9b` the tool) — A BOX CORNER RESIZES ITS BOX, A BEAM CORNER RE-AIMS ITS END, AND THE COCKPIT IS EDITABLE.** `forgePoints` resolves class (2) through `ForgeWriteKind`, deduplicating the three render vertices standing at every box and beam corner; `forgeMovePoint` writes `center`+`size` or `from`/`to` and refuses the two states the primitives answer in silence. `geometry.unit` **78 → 87**. All 10 suites green in 83.8 s, `dev-gpu` 2/2, no save change, no sim change. **Every count the spec predicted came out exact on the first run**, which is what a spec written from the assets rather than from the plan buys.

- **⚑⚑ THE SPEC'S THREE PREDICTIONS WERE ALL ABOUT E1's INVARIANTS, AND ALL THREE WERE REAL.** `movable()` was `writes.size() == corners` — an identity that was invisible while every corner was its own parameter and is simply **false** for a box, which puts three render vertices at each of eight corners. Read the old way every box in the repo reports itself unmovable; deduplicated without counting, the delta lands three times and the corner flies three times as far as the hand. **The point counts RESOLVED corners separately from the writes it collected**, and that one extra field is the whole difference.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE `cube.forge` TRAP WAS THE ONE WORTH THE READING TIME, BECAUSE IT IS E1's OWN FINDING ARRIVING THROUGH A DOOR E1 HAD NO ASSET FOR.** E1 proved that a value round-tripped through a lossier representation must be written back *relatively*, and asserted it on `ship` and `asteroid` — **both of which write down every number they use, so the only way to fail was arithmetic.** The cube authors **nothing**: `id`, `type` and a name, every box parameter at its schema default, which is the property that asset exists to demonstrate. `ForgePart::set` adds a key that is not there, so `value + 0` would have **materialised `center` and `size` into a four-line file on a click-and-release**. The rule gained a clause — *a write whose local delta is exactly zero is not performed* — and the assertion widened to all six committed files. **The general form is now twice-proved: a fixed point is only fixed on the inputs you tested it on, and the input that breaks it is the one that authors the least.**
- **⚑ A BEAM HAS NO AUTHORED CORNERS AT ALL, AND THAT IS SAID IN THE PANEL RATHER THAN LEFT TO BE MET.** `addBeam` derives `side` and `up` from the axis, so all eight corners are a function of two ends and a section and **not one of them is a number in the file**. The only parametric answer is the end the corner stands at — which means the other three corners at that end come with it, and **the four at the pinned end swing as the cross-section is re-derived**, bounded by the section's half-diagonal (0.05 m on every beam in `cockpit.forge`, 2.83 m in the test that asserts it). A test asserts the dragged corner does **NOT** land under the cursor, so that nobody later "fixes" it by writing a corner that has nowhere to live.
- **⚑ THE SIGN TABLE IS PINNED AGAINST GEOMETRY, NOT AGAINST ITSELF.** A box corner is identified by *which of `addBox`'s 24 emitted vertices it is*, which is exact and free — and correct only while that face order is. Reading the sign off a float coordinate instead would be ambiguous on a thin axis, need the part's inverse transform, and come out backwards on a box authored with a negative size. So `geometry.unit` asserts the eight codes against where the built vertices actually landed, and `mesh_build.cpp` now says in both primitives that its emission order is load-bearing.
- **⚑⚑ THE LIVE PROOF'S BEST WITNESS WAS A PANEL THAT KNOWS NOTHING ABOUT THE SOLVE.** Driven on `cube.forge`: the corner at `0.5 0.5 0.5` reports *"written by 1 part(s): `box.center+size` `+x+y+z`"* — **one write, not the three corners standing there**. After the drag the **Report** section reads `bounds min -0.500 -0.500 -0.500` **unchanged** beside `bounds max 0.757 0.660 0.207`, and `welded points 8` throughout. The pin measured by the mesh's own bounding box is worth more than the same number read back out of the document that produced it. Save: **exactly two lines added — `center` and `size` — header, name and every other line untouched.** Undo, save: **byte-for-byte identical by SHA256 with `LastWriteTime` moved**, which is the pair a clean `git status` alone cannot prove.
- **⚑ THE GUARD WAS MADE TO FIRE, BECAUSE ONE NOBODY HAS SEEN FIRE IS NOT VERIFIED.** One enormous drag straight through the cube prints *"this drag pushes box 'box' through its own opposite corner, which turns it inside out"* in red, wrapped inside its box — and `at 0.5000 0.5000 0.5000` in the same shot shows **the corner did not move at all**. Refused means untouched, live rather than only in a test. (The beam's zero-length guard is asserted headlessly; its silence is worse, since `addBeam` returns without emitting anything and the part would leave the mesh with nothing raised anywhere.)
- **⚑ AND THE PANEL'S REFUSAL MESSAGE GOT SHORTER, WHICH IS THE STAGE STATED IN ONE LINE.** At E1 it read *"a box corner is its center and size"* as an example of something with **no** answer. That is now the commonest thing that **has** one, so what is left is genuinely only the swept surfaces — and a partly answerable file says how many points still need a bake (`station.forge`: *"480 point(s) need a bake first"*) instead of saying nothing.
- **⚑ WHAT E2 DOES NOT REACH.** A box corner drag is a **resize**, so seven of the eight corners move — there is no way to move one corner of a box without baking it, and that is the D rule rather than a gap. `station.forge`'s 480 ring points and `gate.forge`'s 256 still wait on **E3**. Carried unchanged from E1 and D: **nothing compares the cooked `.smesh` against anything**, and `ModelMatch::radiusAgrees` still has no test.

**⚑⚑ E3 — SPEC'D 2026-08-20, AND READING THE FORK FOUND A THIRD OPTION NEITHER BRANCH NAMED.** The stage E spec left one question open and named the measurement that would settle it: *baking a part can keep its `parent` and placement and store geometry in the **local** frame, or fold the world transform in and re-hang the part at the **root**.* Local keeps a baked strut attached to the assembly it belongs to; world is bit-exact by construction. **Both descriptions are of the same axis, and the axis has a middle.**

- **⚑⚑ THE THIRD OPTION: FOLD THE PART'S *OWN* PLACEMENT AND KEEP THE PARENT.** A part's world transform is `P · L` — the parent chain times its own placement. Baking in the **parent's** frame stores `L · p` and leaves the placement at identity, so the rebuild computes `P · (L · p)` and the part still hangs where the author put it. **It is bit-exact exactly when `P` is the identity**, and every group in this repo is: `gate.forge`'s `armature` is a bare `[[part]] type = "group"` with no position, rotation or scale, and it is the only group in `assets/meshes/` at all. So the option that keeps the tree keeps bit-exactness too, on every asset that exists — and where it would not, it degrades to *geometrically correct but re-rounded*, never to *detached*.
- **⚑ WHY LOCAL-IN-THE-PART'S-OWN-FRAME CANNOT BE BIT-EXACT, WHICH IS THE ARGUMENT THE FORK WAS ACTUALLY ABOUT.** The original path is `round(W · p)` — the primitive's corner in double, transformed in double, rounded once at `build()`, which is stage B's rule. Storing local geometry makes it `round(W · round(p))`: an extra rounding **in the middle**, before the transform that would spread it. For an identity `W` the two agree trivially; for a rotation they differ whenever the intermediate rounding tips the result across a float boundary. **`gate.forge` is the only asset with non-identity placements** — the ring at `rotation = [90, 0, 0]` and six arms and housings at 90/180/270 about Z — and 90° is not exact in radians either (`cos` returns 6.12e-17, not 0), so it is precisely the case that would fail.
- **⚑ THE BAKE MUST CARRY THE PART'S COMMENT, AND THAT IS NOT AN AESTHETIC POINT.** `ForgePart::leading` is the verbatim trivia standing above the part, and in this repo those blocks are where the knowledge about the assets lives — *"the four spokes out to the ring… written as four separate boxes rather than one part turned four times because they are AXIS-ALIGNED"* is a `[[part]]` comment. A bake that replaced the part wholesale would delete it, which is the exact loss the comment-preserving writer slice was built to stop, arriving one stage later through a different function. **`id`, `parent` and `leading` survive; the primitive, its parameters and its placement are what the bake consumes.**
- **⚑ IT NEEDS A NEW ENTRY POINT, BECAUSE `forgeBakePart` TAKES A WHOLE `MeshData`.** Today's function is *make a mesh part out of this geometry* and it was built at D for the asteroid, which had no document. E3 wants *bake THIS part of THIS document*, which means emitting one part in isolation — so the primitive switch inside `buildForge` gets factored into an `emitPart` helper that both callers share. **One implementation, or the bake and the build are two answers to what a `box` is** — the trap this programme has already paid for twice.
- **⚑ AND THE PAYOFF IS MEASURABLE BEFORE IT IS BUILT.** `station.forge`'s 480 ring points and `gate.forge`'s 256 are every unmovable point left in the repo after E2. Baking those two toruses takes both files to fully movable, which is the E1–E3 exit condition stated in numbers: **every vertex of every shipped asset is movable.**

**The assertion that decides it, and it is written first.** *Baking any single part of any committed asset must not change `buildForge`'s output* — all six files, every part, byte for byte against the un-baked build. If the parent-frame bake passes it, it wins on authoring grounds as well as arithmetic; if it does not, world-and-detach wins and the detach is recorded as its price.

**⚑⚑ E3 IS SHIPPED (`6e59df1`) — THE ASSERTION PASSED ON THE FIRST RUN, SO THE FRAME QUESTION IS ANSWERED AND THE ANSWER IS THE OPTION NEITHER BRANCH OF THE FORK NAMED.** `forgeBakeDocumentPart` folds a part's own placement into its geometry and leaves the parent alone; `buildForge`'s primitive switch is factored into a shared `emitPart` so the bake and the build are one answer to what a `box` is. `geometry.unit` **87 → 93**. All 10 suites green in 83.1 s, `dev-gpu` 2/2, no save change, no sim change. **Every point of every shipped asset is now movable, which is the E1–E3 exit condition.**

- **⚑⚑ THE STRONGEST ASSERTION AVAILABLE TURNED OUT TO BE THE ONE THE D CHECKPOINT SAID NOBODY HAD EVER MADE.** D's record ends with *"nothing compares the cooked `.smesh` against anything"*, carried as an open gap through the writer slice, E1 and E2. Baking `station.forge`'s ring in the tool, saving, and re-cooking produces a `station.smesh` that is **byte-for-byte identical by SHA256, 36,800 bytes both ways** — so the claim is not merely *the built mesh is unchanged* but *the bytes the game actually loads are unchanged*. It is still a hand-made comparison rather than a `cooker.unit` test, so the gap stays open as a gap; but it has now been closed once, on the case that matters most.
- **⚑ THE LIVE READOUT IS THREE NUMBERS THAT DO NOT MOVE AND ONE THAT DOES.** `station.forge` opened at **552 points / 72 movable** with *"480 point(s) need their part baked first"*. One click on `bake` turned `habitat_ring (torus)` into `habitat_ring (mesh)` — **every other part still `(box)`, which is the D rule visible in a list** — and the panel read **552 / 552** with no bake line at all. The Report was unchanged across the bake: 1068 triangles, 749 render verts, 552 welded points, bounds ±102.000 / ±62.750 / ±102.000, radius 102.0000 m, which is the number this asset's own header documents.
- **⚑ THE PART'S COMMENT CAME WITH IT, AND IT TOOK TWO PARTS TO ASSERT.** *"# Habitat ring. 40 segments around, 12 around the tube…"* now stands above a `type = "mesh"` part carrying 533 literal vertices, and the file's header is untouched. **Not one part in this repo carries both a parent and a comment** — a comment block introduces a *group* of parts and therefore sits above the first of them, which for `gate.forge`'s armature is the group itself — so the test asserts the comment on `ring` and the parent on `arm_east`. ⚑ Note the honest residue: a preserved comment can now describe a shape that is no longer parametric. Keeping it is still right — it is the provenance, which is exactly what `asteroid.forge`'s header is — but a bake does not update prose.
- **⚑⚑ AND ADDING THE BUTTON FOUND A DEFECT THAT HAD STOOD SINCE STAGE D.** `add part` is a combo wide enough that **`delete` was already running off the panel's right edge** with only three widgets on the line — clipped, not scrollable, and nobody had noticed because nobody needs `delete` often. Putting `bake` beside it is what made it visible. **This is the SIXTH time this repo has met "a widget does not fit its box" and the first time it was a CONTROL rather than a sentence** — the previous five were all text, which is why the reflex to check was "is this string long?" rather than "does this row fit?". The buttons have their own line now and `delete` is whole for the first time.
- **⚑ WHY THE PART'S OWN LOCAL FRAME COULD NOT WIN, RESTATED FROM THE OTHER SIDE.** Stage B's rule is *stay in double and round once at `build()`*, so the build path is `round(W · p)`. Storing local geometry makes it `round(W · round(p))` — a rounding in the middle, before the transform that would spread it. The parent-frame bake stores `round(L · p)` and then applies an identity, so there is no second transform to spread anything. **`gate.forge` is the only asset that could tell the options apart** (its ring at 90° about X, six arms and housings at 90/180/270 about Z, none of which is exact in radians), and a test now says so out loud so a future reader does not assume the other five assets were evidence.
- **⚑ A REFUSED BAKE TOUCHES NOTHING, INCLUDING THE UNDO HISTORY.** The tool bakes into a copy and only pushes an undo entry on success. The cheap version — push, try, roll back — would leave a no-op in the history for an author to press through, and `undo()` deliberately marks the document dirty, so a button that did nothing would have claimed unsaved work.

**⚑⚑ CHECKPOINT AFTER E1–E3 — held 2026-08-20, and it picked DEBT OVER A STAGE FOR THE THIRD TIME RUNNING.** E1, E2 and E3 were on `feat/move-that-corner`, twelve commits, all 10 suites green, every point of every shipped asset movable and the tool driven. **`feat/move-that-corner` was merged `--ff-only` to `dev` and deleted** (`dev` == `a6eeb6a`). The pick is the two debts this programme has been carrying since D rather than E4 or E5 — **and the reason it is the right pick is that reading them found a structural cause, not a backlog of good intentions.**

- **⚑⚑ THE COOKED `.smesh` HAS NEVER BEEN COMPARED AGAINST ANYTHING BECAUSE `cooker.unit` PHYSICALLY CANNOT REACH IT.** `sol_cooker_lib` is five files — `bc1`, `font`, `gltf`, `png`, `sound` — and **every `cook*` function and `writeMesh` live in `tools/cooker/src/main.cpp`, which is not in the library the test binary links.** So the gap D recorded is a linkage boundary, and three slices have written "still open" under it without anyone noticing that no amount of diligence inside `cooker.unit` could have closed it.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE SOUND PATH ALREADY SOLVED THIS, WHICH IS WHAT MAKES THE FIX OBVIOUS RATHER THAN INVENTED.** `cooker::encodeSound` is a **pure function in the library returning bytes**, and `main.cpp` does nothing but write them — which is exactly why `soundRoundTripsThroughTheCookedFormat` exists and why there is no mesh equivalent. **The asymmetry is the whole bug.** The mesh path gets the same shape: `encodeMesh` (the format) and `importForgeMesh` (read, parse, build) in the library, `main.cpp` keeping only the plumbing. `cookForge`'s three lines are not copied into a test — that would be a second description of the cook, which is the trap this stage just spent E3 avoiding.
- **⚑⚑ THE ASSERTION WORTH HAVING IS NOT A SECOND SET OF HASHES.** `geometry.unit` already pins `buildForge`'s output against embedded hashes, so re-pinning the same numbers plus sixteen header bytes would be ceremony. **The thing genuinely untested is that the WRITER and the READER agree** — `writeMesh` lives in `tools/cooker` and `loadMesh` lives in `sol::assets`, two descriptions of one format in two modules, which is precisely the pair that bit at D. So the net is: **cook each committed `.forge`, load the file back through the runtime's own `loadMesh`, and require it to equal `buildForge`'s mesh exactly** — *what the game loads is what the author built*, end to end, rather than either half agreeing with itself. Plus the file's exact byte length, and `loadMesh` refusing a bad magic, a bad version and a truncated file.
- **⚑ ONE FRAGILITY THE READING TURNED UP: `writeMesh` NEVER SETS `magic` OR `version`.** It writes `MeshFileHeader header = {}` and relies on the struct's default member initialisers, while `loadMesh` validates both. That is correct today and nothing asserts it, so a future `= {0}` or an explicit constructor would produce files the game rejects with the cooker reporting success. Pinned.
- **⚑ THE SECOND DEBT IS SMALLER AND HAS THE SAME SHAPE: `tools/forge` HAS NO TEST TARGET AT ALL**, which is why `ModelMatch::radiusAgrees` — four lines carrying a `max(1e-4, 0.1%)` threshold chosen to separate the gate's 0.0005% rounding from four real 2–16% mismatches — is a decision sitting in untested code, as the D checkpoint said. **AGENTS §7's rule is about the VIEWPORT** (*"there is no suite for a viewport, and pretending otherwise is how `game/src` ended up with 21,000 untested lines"*), not about pure functions. `mesh_library.cpp` pulls in no ImGui and no device — only `gltf.hpp`, `sol::assets`, `sol::core` and `sol::platform` — so a headless **`forge.unit`** compiles it directly and costs about ten lines of CMake. The viewport stays verified by running it, exactly as the rule says.

**Exit for the slice.** `cooker.unit` proves a committed `.forge` reaches the game as the mesh its author built, through the cooker's own encoder and the runtime's own loader. `forge.unit` exists as the eleventh suite and pins the radius threshold with the four real mismatches and the one rounding that must not be one.

**⚑⚑ THE SLICE IS SHIPPED, AND MEASURING THE FOUR MISMATCHES FOUND THAT THIS PLAN HAS BEEN RECORDING THEM WRONG SINCE STAGE D.** `cooker.unit` **19 → 25**, and a new **`forge.unit`** is the **eleventh suite**. All 11 green in 81.5 s, `dev-gpu` 2/2.

- **⚑⚑ "THE FOUR REAL MISMATCHES (STATION 2%, SHIP 12%, CUBE 13%, ASTEROID 16%)" IS AN UNSIGNED LIST OF TWO OPPOSITE DEFECTS.** Measured: **station +2.00%** (100.0 authored, 102.0 measured) and **asteroid +15.84%** (1.0 vs 1.1584) have the collision sphere **inside** the hull, so ships pass through the picture — which is what the D record describes and what `station.forge`'s own header says. But **ship −12.42%** (8.0 authored, **7.0064** measured) and **cube −13.40%** (1.0 vs **0.8660**) go the **other way**: the sphere is **larger** than the hull, so you stop short of a thing you can still see space around. `ModelMatch::radiusDelta`'s own comment has always said the sign is the finding — *"POSITIVE means the sphere the sim builds is smaller than the hull that is drawn"* — and the plan then wrote the numbers down without it, four times over. **A percentage without a sign is half a measurement.** Both the verdict and the sign are pinned now.
  - Worth noting where that lands elsewhere: 8v's *"with an 8 m hull the ship parks at 78 m"* from a gate is the **collision** radius, and the drawn hull is 7.0 m. The two were never the same number and nothing said so.
- **⚑ THE REFACTOR IS PROVED A NO-OP FOR WHAT SHIPS, BY DELETION RATHER THAN BY TIMESTAMP.** Moving the `.smesh` layout out of `main.cpp` could have changed the bytes the game loads, and the build's own "up to date" would have hidden it. All six `.smesh` files were **deleted and re-cooked from scratch** through the new path: every one byte-for-byte identical by SHA256. That is the same discipline as the writer slice's *"a save that never fired leaves `git status` just as clean as a perfect one"* — force the work to happen, then compare.
- **⚑ AND THE SUITE COUNT IS THE POINT OF THE SLICE.** `tools/forge` is ~2,000 lines that had **no test target at all**, so the `max(1e-4, 0.1%)` threshold was, exactly as the D checkpoint said, a real decision in untested code. It is four lines and it now has a test naming every case that set it — including the gate's 0.0005 m rounding, which an absolute tolerance flagged as a defect and which is what made the rule relative in the first place.

**Load-bearing tests added at E3, in `geometry.unit`, all headless.** Baking any single part of any of the six committed assets leaves `buildForge`'s vertices and indices identical by `memcmp` — the assertion that decided the design. `gate.forge` is the only asset with non-identity placements (seven turned parts) and its one group is at the identity, which is the fact the decision rests on. A bake keeps `id`, `parent` and `leading`, and the document still round-trips through the writer. Baking `station.forge`'s torus takes it from 72 movable points to all 552 **without changing the point count**, and the ring point that refused a move a line earlier now moves. Baking an already-baked part is a no-op rather than a round trip. A group and an out-of-range index are both refused with the document unchanged.

**Load-bearing tests added at E2, in `geometry.unit`, all headless.** The eight box sign codes agree with where `addBox` actually put each vertex, over a box that is off-origin and unequal on every axis — a cube at the origin would let a transposed table pass. The same for which end `addBeam` put each vertex at. A box corner drag writes `center` and `size` to the exact double and leaves the opposite corner where it was. A beam corner drag moves `from` by the whole delta, leaves `to` untouched, and lands the corner somewhere *other* than the cursor. A drag through a box's opposite corner is refused with the document unchanged, and one a metre and a half shorter is accepted. A box flat on an axis has four points of six corners and no writes. A corner shared by two boxes carries **two** writes rather than six and resizes both, with the point count proving no seam opened. And the census over all six committed assets: **cube 8/8, gate 320/64, ship 12/12, station 552/72, cockpit 141/141, asteroid 162/162.**

**⚑⚑ E4 — SPEC'D 2026-08-20 ON `feat/grab-the-whole-edge`, AND READING THE CODE REFUTED THE ONE-LINE ESTIMATE ON BOTH OF ITS HALVES.** E4 was written down at the stage E spec as *"`MeshAdjacency::edges` is already a sorted unique edge list with its faces, so edge picking is a projection over data that exists; face picking is where Möller–Trumbore lands. **Moving either is moving its points, which E1 built.**"* The middle clause is the only one that survives intact. **The first is half true in a way this project has already shipped a bug over, and the last is simply false — and it is false exactly where E2 lives.**

- **⚑⚑ `forgeMovePoint` MOVES ONE POINT AND E4 NEEDS A SET, AND THE TWO DO NOT COMPOSE ON CLASS (2).** For class (1) they do: a `flat_triangle`'s corners are three *different* parameters and a baked vertex list addresses by `element`, so moving two points writes two disjoint values and the edge lands exactly where the hand put it. **A box is the opposite.** `boxSizeAfter` applies `center += step/2` and `size += ±step` **per call**, so calling the move twice for the two ends of an edge applies the resize twice and the edge travels **twice as far as the cursor**. A beam is worse and does not even need an edge to show it: `kBeamCornerEnd` puts corners 0–3 at `from`, which is **four distinct geometric points sharing one write**, so any two of them dragged together move the end twice. **This is E2's own dedup finding — *"one entry per corner would apply the drag three times over"* — arriving one stage later ACROSS points instead of WITHIN one**, which is the third time an E-stage finding has come back through a door the previous stage had no asset for.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE HARDER HALF, WHICH IS GEOMETRY RATHER THAN ARITHMETIC: A BOX EDGE IS NOT EXPRESSIBLE AT ALL.** Moving two of a box's eight corners shears it, and a box has no shear — there is nothing to write. **Only a whole FACE solves back**: the four corners sharing one sign bit collapse to a single `center`+`size` write, which is the write E2 already has. So on class (2) a face drag along its own axis is a resize, and an **edge** drag either silently widens to the face it lies on or is refused. **This is the majority case, not a corner case: 35 of the 61 parts in `assets/meshes/` are class (2)** against 23 of class (1). **E4 is therefore EXACT on class (1) and NEGOTIATED on class (2)**, and saying which in the panel is the E2 precedent — a beam's far end swinging was written down rather than left to be met.
- **⚑⚑ THE EDGE LIST EXISTS AND IT IS INDEXED IN A DIFFERENT NUMBERING FROM THE TOOL'S, WHICH IS THE FINDING THE ONE-LINER HIDES.** `MeshAdjacency::Edge{a, b}` indexes `EditMesh::positions`, which `toEditMesh` builds with `PositionWelder` — a spatial hash taking the first match in a 27-cell scan — and then **`removeUnused` renumbers**, dropping any position no index references. `ForgePoint` indices come from `forgePoints`' own linear first-match-in-push-order scan over **every** `mesh.vertices` entry, referenced or not. **Two independent welds at the same tolerance, two numberings, agreeing only by accident.** This repo has shipped that bug once already — Phase 15's row index and nav slot agreed in a fully-surveyed system and the obvious test passed on the broken code. **So E4 builds its edges over `ForgePoint` indices directly** (`buildAdjacency` is the template and it is ~30 lines), correct by construction, rather than carrying a correspondence map that can fail silently. It is the same argument `forgePoints` used when it borrowed `WeldOptions::positionTolerance` instead of inventing a second tolerance: the tool's idea of *one point* and the engine's have to be one number.
- **⚑ MÖLLER–TRUMBORE IS GENUINELY ABSENT AND GENUINELY NEEDED — the one part of the estimate that stands.** Re-confirmed: nothing in the repo matches `Trumbore`, `rayTriangle` or `intersectTriangle` outside this document. Edge picking does **not** need it (a screen-space point-to-segment distance is the whole test, and `sol::ui::pick.hpp`'s `screenPoint` already projects); face picking does.
- **⚑ AND THE MARKER BUDGET STOPS BEING A GUARD WAITING FOR ITS CASE.** `DebugDrawRenderer::kMaxVertices` is **8192** and `line()` returns **silently** when it is full — the failure this repo keeps meeting from the other end. E1 capped points at 600 and the largest asset welds to 162, so the cap has never fired. **Edges are an order of magnitude more numerous.** `station.forge`'s two independently recorded numbers settle it without a measurement: 552 welded points and 1068 triangles, so for a closed surface **E = 3F/2 = 1602 edges = 3,204 debug vertices**, against a grid-and-reference-box baseline of ~270. Euler agrees exactly — nine boxes at χ=2 and one torus at χ=0 give χ=18, and 552 − 1602 + 1068 = 18. **It fits, with about 4,700 vertices to spare, and it is the first time the cap is within one order of magnitude of firing.** `gate.forge` (320 points) and `asteroid.forge` (162) fit more easily. The cap gets the same named budget E1 gave points, and the number is re-measured at implementation rather than trusted from here.

**⚑⚑ THE ONE QUESTION THIS SPEC LEFT OPEN IS DECIDED, AND THE SPEC'S OWN LEAN WAS WRONG — DECIDED BY THE USER 2026-08-20 ON MEASURED NUMBERS RATHER THAN ON THE DRIVE.** The fork was whether a class-(2) **edge** drag **widens to the face it lies on** or is **refused with the bake offered**. This spec leaned *widen*, on the reasoning that *"a refusal on the commonest primitive in the repo makes edge mode feel broken on the first thing anyone clicks."* **That step is the error: refusing does not kill edge mode, it routes to the bake — which is the D checkpoint's rule verbatim, is one button E3 already built, and which a human has already pressed.**

- **⚑ THE MEASUREMENT THAT SETTLED IT, TAKEN HEADLESSLY BEFORE ANY UI EXISTED.** Widening a cube's `+x +y` edge by 0.2 in x: **grabbed 2, moved 4** — the two corners taken and the two at `y = −0.5` that nobody touched. **That is indistinguishable from a bug**, because nothing the author selected was on the far side of the face. Baked first, the same drag reads **grabbed 2, moved 2** with the rest of the face standing still.
- **⚑ AND THE COST OF THE BAKE IS SMALLER THAN `cube.forge` MAKES IT LOOK.** Baking one box: **`cube.forge` 15 → 55 lines** (576 → 1,863 bytes) but **`cockpit.forge` 271 → 308 lines** (7,435 → 8,727) for one of its seven boxes — **+14% on a real asset.** The cube is the outlier precisely because it authors nothing, which is the property that asset exists to demonstrate.
- **⚑ WHY THE E2 PRECEDENT DOES NOT APPLY, WHICH IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING.** E2 answered a beam's re-aim — *"the four corners at the pinned end swing"* — by **saying it in the panel rather than refusing**, and that was right **because there was no other answer**: a beam's corners are not numbers in the file, so the swing is a consequence of the only expressible move. **A box edge is different: a correct answer does exist, one bake away.** Saying-it-in-the-panel is for a consequence you cannot avoid, not for a wrong result you could have declined.
- **⚑⚑ AND IMPLEMENTING IT FOUND THE SAME SURPRISE ONE LEVEL DOWN: A FACE IS ONLY EXACT ALONG ITS OWN NORMAL.** On an axis a face's corners straddle, the only expressible move is `ds = 0, dc = δ`, which **slides the whole box, all eight corners** — "moved more than you grabbed" wearing different clothes. So an off-normal pull on a face is **discarded** and `dropped` reports it. **Three selections, and only two of them are things a box can do:** one corner (E2's resize, unchanged), a whole face (exact, opposite face pinned), anything else (refused, bake named).

**⚑ What this leaves for the drive, rather than for the paper.** Whether the refusal message reads as help or as an obstacle, and whether an author reaches for `bake` without being told to. **The behaviour is settled; only its wording is not.**

**The assertions, written first, because at E3 that is what decided the design.** Dragging a whole box **face** by δ moves that face by exactly δ and leaves the opposite face where it was — E2's pin, now over a set. A selection whose points resolve to **overlapping writes** is applied once or refused, never twice; the doubling above is asserted as a failure of the naive loop so nobody re-introduces it. And the fixed point survives the stage: **a zero-distance edge or face drag leaves all seven committed `.forge` files byte-identical**, which is E1's rule, E2's cube clause and Phase 14's quantization meeting a set instead of a point.

**The slices, in build order.** **E4a** — `forgeEdges` in `sol::assets`, over `ForgePoint` indices by construction, headless and testable with no viewport. **E4b** — pick, hover and draw an edge; point-to-segment in screen space, under a named marker budget. **E4c** — the write-set union and the two refusals, which is where the class-(2) arithmetic above actually lands. **E4d** — faces: Möller–Trumbore, then the face drag, which is the case that pays out on a box. **⚑ Checkpoint after E4**, with E5 (extrude, split, merge) behind it and G's PNG-writer-vs-procedural fork still deliberately open until F's shape is known.

**Out of scope for E4, named so it is not half-built.** No topology change — adding, splitting or dissolving an edge is **E5**, and on a parametric part it is a bake rather than an edit. No gizmo rendering beyond debug lines, carried unchanged from stage E. No multi-edge or multi-face selection: one element, as E1 took one point before it took a set. And **no LOD**, which is stage F and still owes the renderer its selection or it authors dead data.

**⚑⚑ E4b — SHIPPED 2026-08-21, AND THE LIVE DRIVE FOUND A DEFECT THAT HAD BEEN SITTING IN E2 SINCE E2 SHIPPED.** The tool takes a set where it took a point: `PointTool` gains an element **mode** (`1` points, `2` edges, plus a radio in the panel because a hotkey is not discoverable), `refresh` reads `forgeTopology` so points and edges come out of **one** traversal in **one** numbering, the pick is a screen-space **point-to-segment** distance at an 8 px grab, and the drag routes through `forgeMovePoints` — including for a single point, so E2's path is the *same* code rather than a second arrangement of it.

- **⚑⚑ A REFUSED DRAG HANDED THE REST OF THE PRESS TO THE ORBIT CAMERA AND SWUNG THE MODEL ABOUT THIRTY DEGREES.** `main.cpp` re-decides who owns a press the instant `points.dragging()` goes false, and the refusal path set `m_dragging = false` on the first *moving* frame — so the other fifteen frames of a sixteen-step drag orbited. The author sees a refusal message and a spinning view at once, which is indistinguishable from the tool doing something random. **The tool now keeps the press until the button is released** (`m_refused` says "do not retry", `m_dragging` says "this press is mine"), and the two are separate for exactly that reason. Re-measured after the fix: **15 of 11,875 sampled viewport pixels differ across a refused drag**, and those fifteen are the hover highlight.
- **⚑⚑ AND WHY IT SURVIVED E2: E2's REFUSAL TEST USED A ONE-STEP DRAG.** `Send-Drag … 1` was chosen deliberately, to deliver one enormous delta and trip a guard that only fires on a large move — but a one-frame drag has **no remaining frames to hand over**, so the fall-through could not appear. **A guard verified with a single frame cannot see what happens to the frames after it.** The same shape as this project's other input findings: the thing tested was the *first* frame, and the defect lived in the rest.
- **⚑ THE DRIVE ALSO PROVED THE HEADLESS DECISION FROM THE OTHER SIDE, ON A REAL CUBE.** Point mode is unchanged. Edge mode reads **`edges 18, movable 18`** on `cube.forge` — twelve box edges plus **six face diagonals**, because the wireframe is the triangulation and not the box. Selecting the `+x+y` edge prints `from 0.5 0.5 0.5 / to 0.5 0.5 -0.5 / length 1.0000 m / faces 2`, each end `box.center+size` with its corner code, and the amber warning **before** any drag. Dragging it prints the red refusal naming the bake, and a save afterwards is **byte-identical by SHA256**. **Baked** (`cube.forge` 14 → 54 lines) the same pixel picks the same edge and the same drag moves **exactly the two corners grabbed** by one delta — six vertex lines, three render verts per point, the other eighteen untouched. *Grabbed 2, moved 2, live.*
- **⚑ TWO THINGS THE VIEWPORT NEEDED THAT THE SPEC DID NOT NAME.** An edge line lies **in** the surface it borders, and `DebugDrawRenderer` tests depth without writing it, so a raw wireframe z-fights along its whole length — a point cross straddles the surface and half of it survives, an edge has no half to spare. Each line is pulled toward the camera by `cameraDistance * 0.0015`, a **fraction** rather than a constant because the orbit camera covers four orders of magnitude. And the two budgets do **not** add: edge mode draws no point crosses beyond the ends of the selection, which is what makes `kEdgeBudget = 2400` safe against the 8192-vertex cap rather than merely optimistic.
- **⚑ The header was ALSO fixed here, and it is worth naming: `bb98f3c` changed the refusal in `forge_doc.cpp` and left `forge_doc.hpp` documenting the *widening* it replaced.** The contract said the opposite of the code for one commit. Two descriptions of one thing, in the file whose whole job is to be the description.

**⚑⚑ E4d — FACES — SHIPPED 2026-08-21, AND IT CLOSED THE ONE PART OF E4's ESTIMATE THAT WAS RIGHT.** `forgeTopology` now returns faces from the same traversal; `forgePickFace` is Möller–Trumbore over `ForgePoint` indices; `forgeFaceGroup` widens a picked triangle to its coplanar run; and the tool gains a third mode on `3`. **`geometry.unit` 108 → 118, `ui.unit` 91 → 93.**

- **⚑⚑ THE WIDENING IS THE STAGE, AND THE SPEC'S OWN NOTE WAS THE WARNING: a picked triangle of a box face is THREE of its four corners, and `forgeMovePoints` can express a whole face or nothing.** Without widening, E4d would have been refused on every box in the repo — the commonest primitive there is. It has to happen at **pick** time, because by the time the write set is built, *three corners* and *an author who meant four* are indistinguishable. The rule is a flood across shared **edges** (not shared corners — two quads meeting at a lattice point are not one face) to triangles coplanar with the **seed**.
- **⚑⚑ AGAINST THE SEED, NEVER STEPWISE — AND PROVING THAT NEEDED A TEST THAT HAD TO BE *BUILT* RATHER THAN FOUND.** Compared neighbour-to-neighbour, a slowly curving surface's per-segment angles each look flat against the last and the group creeps right around it. **The first attempt to validate this by breaking the code left all 117 tests green**: no asset in this repo bends slowly enough for the two rules to differ, because the finest curve here is a 32-segment torus at 11.25° per segment and *both* rules reject that in one step. The test that separates them is a **synthetic** strip of twenty quads hinged 0.2° each — under the 0.5° threshold per step, four degrees end to end. Correct: **10 triangles**. Stepwise: **40**. ⚑ *A mutation no asset can see still needs a can-fail test; build the input the assets do not contain.*
- **⚑⚑ AND AN ASSET REFUTED ITS TEST, WHICH IS THE FOURTEENTH TIME SOMETHING WRITTEN DOWN BEFOREHAND WAS MOVED BY THE REAL THING.** The first face-group test asserted that a revolve stops at a bend and pinned `gate_membrane`'s groups under eight. It failed at **32** — because `gate_membrane`'s profile is `[[0.0, 0.0], [70.0, 0.0]]`, two points at the **same height**, so it is a **flat disc** and all 32 of its non-degenerate triangles are one plane. **The flood was right and the test was wrong**: the whole film genuinely *is* one face, and an author dragging any part of it means all of it. It is pinned at 32 now.
- **⚑ MÖLLER–TRUMBORE, AND THE ONE THING WORTH KNOWING ABOUT IT.** It never builds the plane: it solves the ray against the triangle's own barycentric frame, so the determinant that rejects a parallel ray also produces the coordinates that decide *inside*. No plane, no normal, no separate containment test — hence no second expression of "inside" to get wrong. **It does NOT cull by the determinant's sign**, unlike a renderer's: the Forge draws both sides of everything, and culling would make the inside of an open shape unclickable while plainly visible.
- **⚑⚑ THE RAY IS THIS PROGRAMME'S FIRST BACKWARD PROJECTION, AND THE DISTINCTION THAT MAKES IT LEGAL IS WORTH KEEPING.** `pick.hpp`'s whole ruling is *project forwards, never invert*. What that forbids is inverting to recover a **position**, which needs a depth nobody has. A pixel does not name a position but it names a **direction** exactly — and the depth comes back out of the geometry the ray is tested against. `rayDirectionCamera` lives in `pick.hpp` beside `screenPoint`, and **`ui.unit` pins the round trip**: project an off-axis point, ray back through that pixel, and the ray must pass through the point. That is what fixes both signs — including the y flip a screen and a camera disagree about — without trusting either expression alone.
- **⚑ THE ASYMMETRY BETWEEN THE MODES IS A DECISION, NOT AN OVERSIGHT.** The edge pick **skips** an edge with an unmovable end; the face pick **takes** it and names the bake. The rule underneath is *an element you can see is pickable*: edge mode filters unmovable edges out of the **draw**, so picking one would select something invisible, while every face is drawn — the face *is* the mesh. Picking a torus face and being told to bake it beats a click that does nothing, which is the complaint this tool has avoided everywhere else.
- **⚑ LIVE PROOF, so it is not re-derived.** `cube.forge` in face mode reads `faces 12`, and a click well inside a face reads **`triangles 2, corners 4`** with all four `box.center+size` codes carrying `+z` — the widening, visible as a number. A diagonal drag wrote **exactly two lines**: `center = [0.0, 0.0, 0.0983]`, `size = [1.0, 1.0, 1.1966]` — E2's split (half into `center`, all into `size`), so max z moved by 0.1966 while **min z stayed at exactly −0.5**. `x` and `y` were untouched and the amber *"part of that drag had no expressible answer and was dropped"* fired, which is the off-normal rule doing its job in front of a person. **`station.forge`, the worst case, reads `points 552 / edges 1602 / faces 1068` — the 1602 the spec derived from `E = 3F/2`, with Euler agreeing at 552 − 1602 + 1068 = 18 — at 142.4 fps (7.02 ms), i.e. vsync-locked**, so neither the 1068-triangle ray nor the per-frame flood costs anything measurable.
- **⚑ `core::cross(DVec3, DVec3)` WAS MISSING while `dot`, `length` and `normalize` all had a double form.** A private copy inside `forge_doc.cpp` collided with `core::dot` by ADL the instant it was written, because `BuildPoint` **is** a `DVec3` — the compiler caught, in one line, the small version of the finding this project keeps paying for: a second expression of a thing the namespace already had.

**⚑⚑ THE CHECKPOINT AFTER E4 — held 2026-08-21, and the pick is E5.** E4a–E4d shipped, merged `--ff-only` and the branch deleted (`dev` == `42c86c1`), twelve suites green, three element modes driven live. Offered the checkpoint, the user picked **E5**, so the **playtest debt is now six phases deep (12–16 plus the whole of E4) and has been declined twice** — the same shape as the 8x/8y/8z playtest declined at the C and D checkpoints, which then produced the six-note session 8 when it finally happened. It is owed, not discharged.

**⚑⚑ E5 — SPEC'D 2026-08-21 ON `feat/pull-that-face-out`, AND READING THE CODE REFUTED ALL THREE CLAUSES OF THE ONE-LINE ESTIMATE.** E5 was written down at the stage E spec as *"`mesh_edit`'s `append`, `weld` and `removeDegenerateFaces` are the plumbing; these operate on baked parts, so they follow E3 by construction."* Measured: **none of those three functions is the plumbing, "a baked part" is the wrong unit on the very asset stage E exists for, and the three operations do not follow one another in the order the line lists them.**

- **⚑⚑ THE NAMED PLUMBING IS THE WRONG LAYER, AND ONE MEASUREMENT SETTLES IT: `toEditMesh` FOLLOWED BY `toMeshData` CHANGES THE VERTEX COUNT ON THREE OF THE SEVEN COMMITTED ASSETS.** `asteroid` 960 → **954**, `cockpit` 426 → **424**, `ship` 48 → **42**; cube, gate, gate_membrane and station come back unchanged. `mesh_edit` is a **global** representation — it welds every position and merges every identical corner across the whole mesh — while a baked part writes `vertices = [...]` **one vertex per line** precisely so that an edit is a small diff, which is the only reason this format is text. Routing a one-face extrude through `EditMesh` would rewrite all **99,115 bytes** of `asteroid.forge` and shift every `BakedVertex` element index the tool writes through. **So E5's operations edit a baked part's own vertex and index lists in place and call none of `append`, `weld` or `removeDegenerateFaces`.** ⚑ Merge does not want `append` either: two baked parts merge by concatenating one `VertexList` onto another and offsetting the second's indices — the ten lines `append` would have done, minus a re-weld nobody asked for. *(`mesh_edit` is not wasted; it is stage **F**'s layer, where a whole-mesh weld and decimate is exactly the intention.)*
- **⚑⚑ "THESE OPERATE ON BAKED PARTS" ASSUMES AN OPERATION LANDS IN ONE PART, AND THE TOOL'S OWN SELECTION ALREADY SPANS TWO.** E4d's `forgeFaceGroup` floods coplanar triangles across shared edges over `ForgePoint` indices, and a point is shared **across parts** — that is E1's whole mechanism. Measured over all seven assets: **4 of 1,273 face groups span two parts** — `ship.forge`'s `hull_0a`+`hull_0b`, `hull_2a`+`hull_2b` and `rear_lower`+`rear_upper`, and `cockpit.forge`'s `cowl_back_lower`+`cowl_back_upper`. Edges are far worse: **24 of `ship.forge`'s 24 edges have their two faces in different parts**, and 9 of `cockpit.forge`'s 315. **On the asset stage E was specified for, every single edge is cross-part.**
- **⚑⚑ AND THE TWO OPERATIONS DIVIDE ON EXACTLY THAT FACT: A SPLIT COMPOSES ACROSS PARTS AND AN EXTRUDE CANNOT.** A split is **per face** — insert the midpoint, replace each triangle standing on the edge with two — so each part answers for its own triangle and the new point welds positionally to its twin next door. Nothing is created that belongs to nobody. An **extrude** raises side walls around the group's **border**, and a wall straddling the seam between two parts belongs to neither. **So a cross-part extrude is REFUSED with `merge` named** — E4c's rule one level up: *refuse a wrong result you could have declined, and route to the escape hatch*. ⚑ **Which makes merge a PREREQUISITE of extrude rather than a peer, and reverses the order the one-liner lists them in.**
