- **⚑ THE REFUSAL ROUTES SOMEWHERE GOOD, WHICH IS WHAT MAKES IT A RULE RATHER THAN AN OBSTACLE — AND ALL FOUR CASES ARE FREE.** Merging `hull_0a` and `hull_0b` is what an author wants anyway: two triangles that are one quad become one part. **All four cross-part pairs are ADJACENT in file order**, so the merged part emits in the same place and `buildForge`'s output is byte-identical; **the earlier part survives**, and in the one case where either carries a comment — *"# The cowl's open back, so the solid is closed and the weld test passes."* above `cowl_back_lower` — it is the earlier one, so E3's comment rule costs nothing on any of the four. And the bake underneath is nearly free: **baking one `flat_triangle` takes `ship.forge` from 187 to 189 lines.**
- **⚑ MERGE ALSO NEEDS THE TWO PARTS TO SHARE A PARENT, AND THAT IS THE BAKE FRAME TALKING, NOT A CONVENIENCE.** `forgeBakeDocumentPart` stores geometry in the **parent's** frame (E3's decision), so concatenating two parts under different parents would need one side re-expressed through the world and back — bit-exact only while both parents are the identity. **Only `gate.forge` has a parent at all** (eight parts under the bare `armature` group; nothing else in `assets/meshes/` sets one), so requiring a shared parent refuses nothing that exists and keeps the concatenation exact.
- **⚑⚑ THE FILE COST OF A TOPOLOGY CHANGE VARIES BY 750× ACROSS THE PRIMITIVES, WHICH IS WHY THE BAKE IS REFUSED-AND-NAMED RATHER THAN AUTOMATIC.** Measured, one part baked in isolation: a `flat_triangle` **2 lines** (187 → 189), a `beam` **35** (271 → 306), a `box` **40** (15 → 55), a `revolve` **130** (40 → 170), `gate.forge`'s `ring` **807** (109 → 916) and `station.forge`'s `habitat_ring` **1,492** (81 → 1,573). Whole files: ship 187 → 219 (**+17%**), cockpit 271 → 857, gate 109 → 1,206, station 81 → 1,915. **An operation that can multiply a file twentyfold is not one an author should meet by accident**, so E5 refuses on a parametric part and names the part and the button, exactly as E4c does.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE FINDING NOBODY WENT LOOKING FOR: `ForgeEdge::faceCount` COUNTS DEGENERATE FACES, AND ON THE ONE OPEN SURFACE IN THIS REPO NO EDGE READS 2.** `gate_membrane.forge` is a flat disc fanned to a point — 32 real triangles and 32 zero-area ones. `forgeTopology` filters degenerates out of `faces` but **not** out of the edge tally: a face with two corners at one point still pushes its remaining side, twice. So its **32 axis edges read `faceCount` 4** and its **32 rim edges read 1**, and nothing reads two. E4 never noticed because it only ever asked *is there at least one triangle here*; **a split asks WHICH triangles, and would try to split two that have no area.** The count is fixed to skip degenerate faces, under an assertion that the edge **set** is unchanged on all seven assets — the tally is what moves, not the topology.
- **⚑⚑ AN EXTRUDE CANNOT START AT ZERO OFFSET IN THIS TOOL, AND THAT IS THE ONE PLACE THE STANDARD IDIOM DOES NOT TRANSFER.** The modeller's gesture is *extrude, then move*: the new geometry is created flush and the existing drag positions it. **Here a point's identity is POSITIONAL** — `forgePoints` welds at `WeldOptions::positionTolerance` — so a face duplicated in place welds straight back into the face it came from and the drag moves both. The extrude therefore offsets immediately, and it cannot be a fixed number of metres: this tool's orbit camera covers four orders of magnitude, and an offset that reads on the station's 102 m ring is wider than the whole `cockpit.forge` dash. **The offset is a fraction of the group's own longest edge, quantized to Phase 14's 0.1 mm write grid and floored at one step of it** — scale-free, already on the grid the next drag will use, and unable to weld back.
- **⚑ THE POINT COUNT CHANGES, SO THE SELECTION IS DROPPED — AND THAT IS FIXED IN THE SAME SLICE OR THE GESTURE IS TWO STEPS.** `PointTool::refresh` keeps a selection only while the point count is unchanged, which is right for a drag and wrong for the one operation whose entire purpose is to change it. So the extrude reports the points it created and the tool re-selects **that** face, leaving the next drag as E4d's face drag unchanged. Without it an author extrudes, watches the highlight vanish, and has to hunt for the thing they just made.
- **⚑ MERGE NEEDS TWO OBJECTS AND THE TOOL HAS ONE SELECTION — AND THE ANSWER IS THE PANEL, NOT A VIEWPORT MULTI-SELECT.** `PartEditor::m_selected` is a single `int`, and E4 deliberately deferred multi-select to here (*"one element, as E1 took one point before it took a set"*). A viewport multi-select is a selection model, an undo question and a highlight scheme all at once; **a combo naming the other part is the idiom this panel already uses for `parent`**, and it costs one widget. Multi-select therefore stays out of scope and is named as such rather than half-built.

**The assertions, written first, because at E3 and E4 that is what decided the design.** *(1)* Extruding any face of `cube.forge` leaves a **closed manifold with zero border edges, wound outwards** — Phase 16's own invariants aimed at a mesh the tool made rather than at a committed file, which is what catches a side wall wound the wrong way. *(2)* Splitting any edge of `cube.forge` preserves `signedVolume` and leaves it closed: a midpoint lies on the surface, so a split that changes the volume moved something it should not have. *(3)* Merging two **adjacent** parts leaves `buildForge`'s vertices and indices identical by `memcmp` — E3's assertion over a second operation — while merging two distant ones is geometrically identical and renumbered, asserted as such rather than left to be discovered. *(4)* The fixed point survives the stage: **every refused operation leaves the document byte-identical**, and all seven committed files still round-trip through the writer.

**The slices, in build order — which is not the order the one-liner names them in.** **E5a** — the baked-part edit primitives in `sol::assets`, headless and testable with no viewport: `forgeMergeParts`, `forgeSplitEdge`, `forgeExtrudeFaces`, each baking the parts it touches or refusing with the part named, plus the degenerate-face fix to the edge tally. **E5b** — merge in the Parts panel, first among the tool slices because it is the escape hatch the extrude refusal names, and a refusal naming something unbuilt is a lie. **E5c** — split an edge, over E4b's existing edge selection. **E5d** — extrude a face, over E4d's existing face group, with the re-selection above. **⚑ Checkpoint after E5**, where stage E is complete and **F** is next — and F still owes the renderer its LOD selection or it authors dead data, while **G**'s PNG-writer-vs-procedural fork stays open until F's shape is known.

**Out of scope for E5, named so it is not half-built.** No **multi-element selection** in the viewport — merge is a panel operation and one element stays the rule. No **inset, bevel, loop cut or knife**, and no **booleans**. No **un-bake**: a `mesh` part stays a `mesh` part, which is the D rule's one-way door rather than a gap. No **face-interior split** — split means an *edge* split, because a point dropped inside a triangle is a fan whose triangulation nobody has specified. No new primitive, no `[build]` post-pass change, and **no LOD**, which is stage F.

**⚑ What this leaves for the drive rather than for the paper.** Whether the extrude's scale-derived offset reads as *it made something* or as *it jumped*, and whether an author reaches for `merge` when a refusal names it. The behaviour is settled; the two numbers inside it — the fraction of the longest edge, and whether the refusal should quote the line cost of the bake it is demanding — get set at implementation against a real asset.

**⚑⚑ E5 IS SHIPPED AND DRIVEN LIVE 2026-08-21** (`faa3fca` spec, `c7a5294` E5a, `21c007d` E5b–d). `geometry.unit` **118 → 130**. Twelve suites green, `dev-gpu` 2/2, no save change, no renderer change. **The Forge extrudes a face, splits an edge and merges two parts, and every prediction in the spec above came out exact on the first run.**

- **⚑⚑ EVERY NEW TEST WAS THEN MADE TO FAIL, AND THE MUTATIONS SORTED THE ASSERTIONS INTO THE ONES THAT WORK AND THE ONES THAT LOOK LIKE THEY DO.** Reversing a wall's winding leaves the mesh **closed and manifold with zero border edges** and is caught only by the **signed volume** — which is precisely the claim the spec made when it aimed Phase 16's invariants at a mesh the tool had made. Reversing the split's second triangle is caught the same way, on both split tests. Making the **later** part survive a merge is caught by `memcmp` against the un-merged build. Restoring the degenerate faces to the edge tally is caught only by `gate_membrane`. Duplicating where the code should move is caught only by the orphan check. **Five mutations, five different assertions, no overlap** — which is what a test set looks like when each test is load-bearing.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE MUTATION FOUND A REAL DEFECT THE FEATURE ITSELF HAD NOT REACHED.** With the cross-part refusal disabled, two faces of *different* parts both resolved to **triangle 0 of the one part being extruded**, and the second visit re-pointed an already-raised index through `raisedOf`, walking off the end of it — a debug-CRT abort rather than a test failure. **The refusal is what stops that reaching the code, but a bounds guard that depends on a rule one function up is not a guard.** Triangles are deduplicated now and the re-point is bounds-checked, after which the same mutation fails cleanly. ⚑ Note the diagnostic trap: the harness prints `[ RUN ]` to **buffered stdout** and the CRT prints the assertion to **unbuffered stderr**, so the last test named before a crash is *not* the test that crashed.
- **⚑⚑ LIVE ON `cube.forge`, AND THE REPORT IS THE INDEPENDENT WITNESS.** Face mode, click a face, press `extrude`: the panel reads **`points 12 / edges 30 / faces 20`** (Euler 12 − 30 + 20 = 2) and *"extruded 2 triangle(s) by 0.1000 m along the face's own normal - drag it to place it."* The Report — which is computed from the built mesh and knows nothing about the operation — reads **triangles 20, render verts 40, welded points 12, `bounds min -0.500 -0.500 -0.500` UNCHANGED, `bounds max 0.500 0.500 0.600`, size 1.000 × 1.000 × 1.100, volume 1.1 m3**. That is the unit test's number, measured by a different path.
- **⚑⚑ THE RE-SELECTION IS PROVED BY WHAT HAPPENS NEXT, WHICH IS NOTHING EXTRA.** Straight after the press — **with no second click** — a drag moved the raised face to `(-0.6452, -0.2512, 0.6036)` and its three siblings with it, `welded points 12` throughout (no seam), while `bounds min` stayed at −0.500 on every axis. The save wrote `cube.forge` **14 → 78 lines** with the file's header comment intact, the box now a `mesh` part.
- **⚑⚑ THE CROSS-PART REFUSAL, LIVE ON THE ASSET STAGE E EXISTS FOR.** Clicking `ship.forge`'s top deck selects **`triangles 2 / corners 4` spanning `hull_2a` and `hull_2b`**, and `extrude` prints in red: *"this face is made of triangles from part 'hull_2b' and part 'hull_2a' - merge them into one part first, and the extrude will have somewhere to put the walls it raises"*. **A save immediately afterwards is byte-identical by SHA256** — refused means untouched, with its own positive control in the same run.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE PAYOFF IN THE NEXT TWO CLICKS.** `merge with...` → `hull_2b`, and the **earlier** id survives (`hull_2b` is gone from the file, `hull_2a` is a `mesh`). The identical pixel then selects the identical face reading **four corners all `hull_2a.vertices[N]`** — the merge visible as a number — and `extrude` succeeds: *"extruded 2 triangle(s) by **0.6051 m**"*, which is exactly **10% of that deck's longest border edge (6.051 m)**, quantized to the 0.1 mm write grid. **`points 12 → 16, edges 24 → 36, faces 16 → 24`**, χ held at 4 (the ship's two-sided fin means it was never genus zero), and the viewport shows a superstructure standing proud of the hull with walls all round. `ship.forge` 186 → 206 lines.
- **⚑ THE SPLIT, AND ITS BEST NUMBER IS THE ONE THAT DID NOT MOVE.** Edge mode on `cube.forge`, `split edge`: **`points 8 → 9`, `edges 18 → 21`, triangles 12 → 14, render verts 24 → 26** — TWO new render vertices for ONE new point, because the two faces meeting at that hard edge name different vertices at the same place and each gets its own midpoint, which is the crease preserved rather than welded away. The Report reads **bounds ±0.500 unchanged, size 1.000 × 1.000 × 1.000, surface area 6 m2, volume 1 m3, manifold yes, closed yes.** A split is topology and nothing else, measured.
- **⚑ THE PANEL SAYS WHAT IT DID, IN GREEN, AND CLEARS ON THE NEXT CLICK.** The split's line is *"split: a point was added at the middle, and every triangle standing on that edge became two. Pick either half."* beside **`no edge selected`** — the edge really is gone, so nothing is left highlighted, and saying that beats a highlight that silently means the wrong element.
- **⚑ ONE PIECE OF THE SPEC WAS NEVER NEEDED: `forgeExtrudeFaces` TAKES NO `ForgePoint` SPAN.** An extrude is entirely part-local — the geometry, the normal it raises along and the offset are all in the frame the part's own numbers are written in — so unlike every other operation in stage E there is no inverse transform in it anywhere.

**⚑⚑ THE CHECKPOINT AFTER E5 — held 2026-08-21, stage E complete, and the pick is F.** Offered the checkpoint against a playtest, the user picked **F**, so the **playtest debt is now seven phases deep (12–16 plus the whole of E4 and E5) and has been declined four times.** It is owed, not discharged, and stage E's three element modes and three topology operations have still never met a human.

**⚑⚑ F — OPTIMISE AND LOD — SPEC'D 2026-08-21 ON `feat/cheaper-far-away`, AND MEASURING THE COMMITTED ASSETS REFUTED ALL THREE CLAUSES OF THE ONE-LINE ESTIMATE FOR THE THIRD STAGE RUNNING.** F was written down as *"weld, dedupe and decimate exposed in the tool with before/after counts and a side-by-side preview at the distance the LOD is for; this stage must also add LOD selection to the renderer or it authors dead data."* Measured with a scratch probe over all seven committed `.forge` files (built, welded, decimated at 50/25/10%, then deleted): **two of the three named operations must not run at all, the tool half cannot come first because it depends on a number the renderer half defines, and the one clause that survived — the impostor path — is more true than it reads.**

- **⚑⚑ THE ENGINE LAYER F WAS SUPPOSED TO BUILD IS ALREADY BUILT AND ALREADY TESTED, AND EVERY ONE OF ITS TESTS RUNS ON A SYNTHETIC ICOSPHERE.** `mesh_edit.hpp` carries `weld`, `removeDegenerateFaces`, `removeUnused`, `optimizeIndices`, `averageCacheMissRatio` and a quadric-collapse `decimate` with `preserveBorders` and an error ceiling — with monotonicity, border-preservation and error-ceiling tests already green. **So F is not a geometry stage: what it has to build is a *pipeline*, a *policy* and a *readout*.** ⚑ And the icosphere is E4d's trap from the other side. There, a synthetic input was needed because no asset could make the mutation fail; **here the synthetic input PASSES an assertion that the real assets fail.**
- **⚑⚑ "WELD, DEDUPE AND DECIMATE" — MEASURED, ONLY THE THIRD ONE PAYS, AND THE SECOND IS A REGRESSION.** *Dedupe:* welding every committed mesh recovers **14 vertices out of 2,762** across all seven — `ship` 48 → 42, `asteroid` 960 → 954, `cockpit` 426 → 424, and cube/gate/membrane/station unchanged. **0.5%.** That is E5's three-of-seven measurement again, and at this size it is not an optimisation. *Index optimisation:* **`optimizeIndices` makes the cache metric WORSE on the two best-shared meshes in the repo** — `gate` 0.804 → **0.895** (+11%), `station` 0.701 → **0.810** (+16%) — while cube, ship, cockpit and membrane do not move at all and asteroid improves by 0.006. **Its own test asserts `after <= before`; run that assertion over the committed assets instead of the icosphere and it fails on two of seven.** It is not a cache-size mismatch: `kOptimizerCacheSize` is 32 and `averageCacheMissRatio` defaults to the same 32. It is that **`MeshBuilder` emits boxes and beams in a naturally coherent order already near the ~0.6 floor the header names**, and Forsyth's greedy scoring cannot beat an order that is already good. ⚑ **So the pass keeps the better of the two orders, measured** — safe on any imported soup, a no-op on everything here, and one line.
- **⚑⚑ THE DECIMATOR SPLITS THIS REPO CLEANLY IN TWO, AND THE SPLIT IS HAND-BUILT PARTS AGAINST EVERYTHING ELSE.** At half the triangles: **`station` 1068 → 534 costs 0.35% of its volume** and **`asteroid` 320 → 160 costs 0.85%** — but **`cube` 12 → 6 costs 66.67%** and **`ship` 16 → 8 costs 18.34%**, because a box authored as twelve triangles has no redundancy to give up. `gate` 608 → 304 comes back **+2.32%** (a decimation can *grow* a volume), `cockpit` 210 → 104 costs 9.40%, and `gate_membrane` refuses below **32** exactly, where `preserveBorders` holds its 70 m rim and only the 32 degenerate fan triangles are collapsible. **So a chain is generated above a triangle floor and REFUSED below it, and refusing is the correct answer rather than a failure** — on today's content that means `station`, `gate` and `asteroid` get levels and nothing else does.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE ASSERTION FINDING, WHICH IS E5's THIRD SIGHTING AND IS NOW LOAD-BEARING FOR THE WHOLE STAGE: A CUBE DECIMATED TO TWO TRIANGLES IS STILL CLOSED, STILL MANIFOLD, STILL ZERO BORDER EDGES — AND ENCLOSES NOTHING.** Measured at the 25% and 10% levels on `cube` (2 triangles), `ship` (4) and `cockpit` (36): every one reads `closed=1 border=0` with the volume at **−100.00%**. **Phase 16's invariants cannot tell a solid from a flat sliver.** Only the signed volume can — the same thing that caught a reversed extrude wall and a reversed split triangle. **So a level is accepted on a volume band, and a topology check alone would wave through a mesh that has been folded flat.**
- **⚑⚑ THE BOUNDING RADIUS GROWS, WHICH IS THE OPPOSITE OF THE OBVIOUS PREDICTION, AND IT LANDS ON A DEFECT THIS PROJECT HAS ALREADY MEASURED AND RECORDED.** A quadric's optimal position is not constrained to the original hull, so a collapse can push a point **outward**: `station` 102.000 → **102.312 → 102.795 → 103.923** (+0.31%, +0.78%, +1.89%) and `asteroid` **+1.80%** at its 10% level. `models.toml` gives the station `radius = 100.0` against a mesh that measures **102.0** — the **+2.00%** the E1–E3 debt slice recorded as *the collision sphere INSIDE the hull, so ships pass through the picture*. **An LOD widens an already-recorded mismatch to +3.9%.** ⚑ Hence two rules rather than one: **collision and every sim query keep reading the def and level 0 — an LOD is a drawing, not a shape** — and **a level whose bounding radius moves outside a stated bound is refused**, which is the sign finding turned into a guard.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE COST NOBODY BUDGETS FOR: FOLLOWING `mesh_edit.hpp`'s OWN WRITTEN ADVICE MAKES A LEVEL *BIGGER* THAN THE MESH IT REPLACES.** The header says, correctly, that a collapse leaves normals stale and *"call recomputeNormals afterwards if the result is going to be shaded"*. Do exactly that with flat normals and every corner unshares at 3 vertices per triangle: **`station`'s 50% level is 1,518 vertices / 55,000 cooked bytes against a 749-vertex / 36,800-byte source — +49% of the bytes for half the triangles** — and the gate's is 29,776 against 22,960, **+30%**. Carrying the stale normals gives 689 vertices (**−23%**) and re-shading smooth at 40° then re-welding gives 731 (**−19%**); on the asteroid the same choice is **480 flat against 206 smooth**. **A level bigger than its source is not a level**, so levels are re-shaded smooth and re-welded, and *every generated level being smaller in cooked bytes than the one above it* is an assertion rather than an expectation.
- **⚑⚑ WHERE THE LEVELS LIVE IS DECIDED BY A SHARED CONSTANT: ALL FOUR COOKED FORMATS VALIDATE AGAINST ONE `kFormatVersion`.** `formats.hpp` gates `.smesh`, `.stex`, `.sfon` and `.saud` on the same `kFormatVersion = 1`, and `cooker.unit` already pins that a bumped version is rejected — so a multi-level mesh header costs either a **global** bump that tells the font and sound loaders their formats changed when they did not, or a per-format split. **Neither is worth it: the chain goes in sibling files** (`station.smesh`, `station.lod1.smesh`, …), which needs no format change, no version bump, and matches `models.toml`'s own standing rule that a mesh reaching the game is a cooked file plus a row and never a C++ change. `loadModels` builds each catalog entry's level list from whatever siblings exist; **absent means the chain ends, which is today's behaviour exactly.** ⚑ **And level 0 stays byte-for-byte what the cooker writes today** — deliberately *not* re-welded for its 14 vertices — so `cooker.unit`'s end-to-end test and E3's bake → save → re-cook → identical-SHA256 recipe both keep holding through the stage.
- **⚑⚑ THE POLICY IS IN PIXELS RATHER THAN METRES, AND THE CONTENT FORCES IT.** The catalog's radii run from **1 m** (asteroid, sized by instance scale) to **106.7 m** (gate), and a projected radius is `radius / distance × height / (2·tan(fov/2))` — with `kCameraVerticalFov` at 70° that is **514 px at 720p and 771 at 1080p**. Against the game's real distances: **a ship (r 8 m) at 1 km is 4 px tall**, a station (r 100 m) at 5 km is **10 px**, a rock (r 45–240 m) at 2 km is **12–62 px**. A metre threshold would be one hand-tuned number per model *and* wrong at another resolution; **one pixel threshold covers every model at every resolution**, and it puts the cockpit — a `RenderInstance` drawn at zero distance — permanently at level 0 **with no special case to write.** Same instinct as E5's scale-free extrude offset, for the same reason.
- **⚑ THE ONE CLAUSE THAT SURVIVED IS TRUER THAN IT READS: `ImpostorRenderer` DRAWS A RAY-MARCHED SPHERE FROM A CENTRE, A RADIUS AND TWO COLOURS.** It has no mesh, no texture, no orientation and no instance transform. It cannot be the last level of a hull under any amount of plumbing, so **the chain ends at a mesh** and mesh impostors are named out of scope rather than left looking reachable.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE NUMBER TO STATE BEFORE ANY OF IT IS BUILT, BECAUSE THE DRIVE WILL OTHERWISE BE ASKED TO SHOW SOMETHING THAT IS NOT THERE: THERE IS NO FRAME TIME TO WIN HERE.** The entire model catalog is **2,298 triangles** (station 1,068, gate 608, asteroid 320, cockpit 210, membrane 64, ship 16, cube 12) in **116,072 cooked bytes**. The heaviest scene this game can currently build is a fringe system's 3 fields × up to 48 rocks — **144 rocks × 320 triangles ≈ 46,000 triangles at ~150 draw calls** — and Phase 8o already established that what dominates this frame is a **wait**: E4d measured the 1,068-triangle station in the Forge at **142.4 fps / 7.02 ms, vsync-locked**. **So F's exit criterion cannot be fps and must not be reported as one.** What it buys is that an author can see what a mesh costs, and that the mechanism exists before the content that needs it — which is *"or it authors dead data"* read from the other end.

**The assertions, written first, because at E3, E4 and E5 that is what decided the design.** *(1)* **Every generated level is smaller in cooked bytes than the level above it** — the flat-normal trap turned into a test that would have caught the +49% station. *(2)* Every level of a closed source is **closed, manifold, wound outwards, and holds its volume within a stated band** — the band is the load-bearing half, because a two-triangle cube passes the other three. *(3)* **No level's bounding radius moves outside a stated bound over level 0**, pinning the collision-sphere defect the debt slice measured. *(4)* **`optimizeIndices` in the pipeline never raises `averageCacheMissRatio` on any of the seven committed assets** — its icosphere assertion, over the two inputs that break it. *(5)* **A model with no sibling levels draws exactly as it does today, and the seven level-0 files are unchanged by the whole stage**, by SHA256. *(6)* Level selection is a **pure function** of radius, distance and viewport height, pinned at its boundaries — and since no committed asset can demonstrate a wrong pick, **that can-fail input is built rather than found**, per E4d.

**The slices, in build order — and the order is forced, with the tool LAST.** **F1** — chain generation and the acceptance bands in `sol::assets`, headless, carried by `geometry.unit`; nothing writes a file yet. **F2** — the cooker writes the siblings, level 0 untouched, `cooker.unit` gains the round trip. **F3** — the renderer selects: `CatalogEntry` holds a level list, `loadModels` finds the siblings, the draw loop picks by projected pixels, plus a dev-overlay readout and a console probe so a drive can assert the pick **without reading pixels** — the role `sol.contacts` and `sol.pick` already play. **F4** — the Forge shows the chain beside the Report it already computes, with a preview at the distance each level takes over. **⚑ F4 is last because "the distance the LOD is for" is not a number that exists until F3 defines it** — the same prerequisite shape as E5's merge-before-extrude, arriving between the renderer and the tool instead of between two operations.

**Out of scope for F, named so it is not half-built.** **No mesh impostors** — the impostor path is a ray-marched sphere and cannot take a hull. **No GPU instancing or draw-call batching**: LOD cuts triangles, while this renderer's plausible costs are draw calls and a ray-marched sky, so batching is a different stage with a different measurement and pretending they are one is how a stage gets sold on the wrong number. **No frustum or distance culling** — the draw loop has none today, and adding it in the same stage would confound the only measurement F can honestly make. **No hot-reload** of a level chain. **No per-model threshold tuning** and **no change to `ModelDef` or `models.toml`**. **No `.smesh` format change and no version bump.** **No LOD for the cockpit** — the pixel policy already holds it at level 0, and a special case would be a second rule saying the same thing.

**⚑ What this leaves for the drive rather than for the paper.** Whether the switch is **visible** — a pop at the threshold is the one thing no headless test can see — and whether the station's 50% level is honestly indistinguishable at the distance it takes over. The three bands (volume, radius, byte shrink) and the pixel thresholds get their exact numbers at implementation against the real assets, exactly as E5's offset fraction did.

**⚑⚑ F IS SHIPPED AND DRIVEN LIVE 2026-08-21** (`963d6ed` spec, `d0c9921` F1, `1e798c0` F2, `d21ab8a` F3, `725379f` F4). `geometry.unit` **130 → 139**, `ui.unit` **93 → 94**, `cooker.unit` **25 → 29**. Twelve suites green in ~87 s, `dev-gpu` 2/2, no save change. **Every prediction the spec made came out exact, including the three pixel figures it derived before any code existed.**

- **⚑⚑ THE POLICY, MEASURED END TO END, AND THE COOKER SAYS IT IN WORDS.** `station` and `asteroid` take two levels each, `gate` takes one and stops with *"level 2 moved -16.78% of the volume, past the 10.00% band - at 152 triangles this is no longer the same shape"*, and cube, ship, cockpit and gate_membrane are refused by the 256-triangle floor. **Five levels over three models**, which is what the renderer's boot line then reports independently. Generated bytes: `station.lod1` 29,816 and `.lod2` 22,440 against a 36,800-byte source; `asteroid.lod1` 8,528 and `.lod2` 6,288 against 34,384; `gate.lod1` 21,968 against 22,960. **All seven level-0 files unchanged**, which is the claim `cooker.unit`'s end-to-end test and E3's re-cook recipe both rest on.
- **⚑⚑ THE SELECTION WAS PREDICTED FROM ARITHMETIC BEFORE THE RUN AND CAME OUT TO THE DECIMAL.** Station radius 100 m, viewport 720 px, focal 514: **300 m → 171.4 px → lod0, 800 m → 64.3 px → lod1, 3,000 m → 17.1 px → lod2, 20,000 m → 2.6 px → lod2 clamped to what exists.** Predicted 171.4 / 64.3 / 17.1. Clean shutdown, 0 validation messages.
- **⚑⚑ THE MUTATIONS SORTED THE ASSERTIONS AGAIN, AND ONE OF THEM REPRODUCED E4d's LESSON EXACTLY.** Six mutations, six outcomes: flat shading → the shading test; the volume band → the solid test, whose failure message names **the gate at 16.78%**, the probe's own number by a different path; the **radius band → ONLY the built-input band test, with all 138 others green**; the unconditional reorder → the cache test, **while the icosphere test stayed green**; the selection off-by-one → the boundary test; the stray-level deletion → the shorter-chain test. **The radius band is the E4d case verbatim: no committed asset can trip it at the shipping values, so deleting the check outright would have left the whole suite passing.**
- **⚑⚑ AND A MUTATION EXPOSED A WEAKNESS IN A TEST I HAD JUST WRITTEN, WHICH IS THE REUSABLE PART.** `everyGeneratedLevelIsSmallerInCookedBytes` looked like the test for the flat-normal finding and is **near-tautological**: the generator's own byte guard rejects an inflated level, so the test never sees one and passes either way. The finding needed a **direct** test — build the chain twice, smooth and flat, on an asset where both clear the guard, and compare. *A test that only ever sees inputs a guard has already filtered is testing the guard, not the rule.*
- **⚑ THE `optimizeIndices` FIX IS THE SMALLEST CHANGE HERE AND THE ONE MOST LIKELY TO BE UNDONE BY A TIDY-UP: it keeps the BETTER of the two orders.** Unconditional it made the gate 0.804 → 0.895 and the station 0.701 → 0.810, because `MeshBuilder` already emits near the ~0.6 floor. It is not a cache-size mismatch — `kOptimizerCacheSize` and the metric's default are both 32.
- **⚑⚑ TWO TRAPS THE COOK ADDED, BOTH ABOUT AN OUTPUT THAT IS NOW A *SET*.** Stale levels are **deleted**, because a cooked directory is not rebuilt from scratch and an asset edited below the floor would leave a `.lod2.smesh` the runtime loads and draws **at distance, where nobody is looking closely**. And a `.forge` **always cooks**, because how many outputs it has cannot be known without building and decimating it — *a staleness check that cannot count its own outputs is how the stale level survives.*
- **⚑ AN ABSENT OPTIONAL FILE IS NOT AN ERROR.** `loadModels` probes for siblings with a timestamp rather than a failed load, because `assets::loadMesh` logs when it cannot read a file — right when someone named that file, wrong for an optional one nobody promised. Four of seven models would have logged an error on every boot.
- **⚑⚑ THE FIRST VERSION OF THE PROBE MEASURED NOTHING, AND THE SHAPE IS WORTH KEEPING.** `sol.lods` reported the largest thing on screen and read **812.3 px at every range**: the **cockpit** is a `RenderInstance` about five metres from the eye carrying an 8 m radius, so it pins the maximum forever and hides the one number the probe exists to show. **An instrument dominated by the single instance that can never change is an instrument that measures nothing.** It reports the largest thing *with a chain* now, and separates a **cook** failure (no levels loaded) from a **selection** failure (levels loaded, nothing drawn below 0).
- **⚑ THE FORGE'S PREVIEW IS A LOOK AND NOT AN EDIT SURFACE, AND THE DRIVE IS WHAT MADE THAT A RULE.** Previewing put **162 point markers over a hull with 80 triangles that has no such points**. The markers are not drawn during a preview, and the press is withheld from the point tool — the inverse of E4's *an element you can see is pickable*, since picking an invisible marker would edit the document while the screen shows a mesh the document does not contain. The camera still gets the press.
- **⚑ SELECTING A LEVEL IS A PLACEMENT, NOT A FRAMING.** `OrbitCamera::placeAt` joins `frame`, and the camera stands exactly where the level takes over: on `asteroid.forge`, **lod1 from 8 m and lod2 from 23 m**, which are the 96 px and 32 px thresholds at that window's focal length. At 23 m the rock is **60 px across** — the honest picture of what the level is for, and a decimated hull filling the screen would have proved nothing.
- **⚑⚑ THE HONEST NUMBER STANDS AS THE SPEC WROTE IT: F BOUGHT NO FRAME TIME AND WAS NEVER GOING TO.** The catalog is 2,298 triangles and the frame is vsync-bound. What F bought is that an author can see what a mesh costs, that the chain is correct by construction, and that the mechanism exists before the content that needs it. **Reported that way rather than dressed up, which is the point of having said it first.**

**⚑⚑ STAGE G — SPEC'D 2026-08-21 ON `feat/paint-that-panel`, AND MEASURING THE THREE ASSETS CLOSED THE FORK THE C CHECKPOINT DELIBERATELY LEFT OPEN.** The fork was *a PNG writer* versus *a procedural texture source in the shape D gave meshes*, deferred because the E→F checkpoint would know more. It knows enough now, and the deciding fact was not a preference: **these textures are already procedural — the recipe just lives in PowerShell and GDI+.** `tools/scripts/gen_assets.ps1` draws all three with `System.Drawing`: a background clear, a scatter of filled rectangles, some 2 px seam lines and a few accent strips. **The vocabulary needed to describe every pixel this repo ships is five ops.**

- **⚑⚑ THE MEASUREMENT THAT DECIDES IT: ALL THREE COMMITTED PNGs REPLAY BYTE-IDENTICAL FROM THEIR OP LISTS.** Replaying `System.Random`'s draws in the script's own call order, re-issuing the same GDI+ calls and hashing the result gives **the committed SHA256 for `checker`, `hull` and `cockpit`**. So every pixel in this repo comes from a list of at most **60 rectangles, 20 seam lines and 6 strips**, and that list is recoverable *exactly* rather than approximately — which turns the transcription from a thing judged by eye into a thing asserted to the byte.
- **⚑⚑ SO THE SOURCE IS A LIST AND NOT A SEED, AND THE REPO DECIDED THIS ONCE ALREADY.** The tempting file is `seed = 1337, panels = 60`. It cannot be: **`System.Random` is .NET's subtractive generator and nothing in C++ reproduces it**, so a seed re-rolls every panel and changes the cooked bytes. `ForgePrimitive::Mesh`'s own comment already settled the general case — the asteroid is baked because its noise is keyed on *"PowerShell's uint32 multiply promoting to double, and a primitive that reproduced that would put a bug-compatibility shim in the engine's permanent vocabulary for every future asset to inherit."* **A .NET RNG port is that shim, one format over.** ⚑ And the list is what makes the *tool* possible at all: **a seed is a texture you can regenerate but cannot edit** — you cannot nudge one panel of it.
- **⚑ THE VOCABULARY IS MIXED, AND THE SPLIT IS MEASURED RATHER THAN ASSUMED.** `checker.png` is a **true recipe** — a cell size, two colours, one marker square, no RNG anywhere — and stays parametric. `hull` and `cockpit` are **lists**: 60 and 44 panels, 8 and 10 seam pairs, 6 and 5 strips. The format carries both in one file, exactly as `.forge` carries parametric parts beside baked ones, and for the same reason.
- **⚑⚑ THE PIXEL RULES ARE PINNED, BECAUSE C++ WILL NOT BE CALLING GDI+.** `FillRectangle(x, y, w, h)` covers `[x, x+w) × [y, y+h)`; **a width-2 pen at `c` covers `[c-1, c]`**, measured on both axes (cockpit's vertical seam at 222 lights columns **221–222** at a clean row; its horizontal seam at 178 lights rows **177–178** at a clean column) with **no antialiasing**. A seam at 0 clamps to a single row or column — no committed asset needs it, and the op defines it anyway. **Ops apply in file order and rows within an op in row order**, which is what makes a later panel cover an earlier one and the strips sit on top of the seams.
- **⚑⚑ CLAUSE ONE OF G's ONE-LINER IS RIGHT AND THE MODEL IT NAMES WAS RETIRED TWO PHASES AGO.** *"The three procedural textures are the regression set, **as the five meshes are for B**."* The five meshes for B is `the*ForgeSourceIsTheShippedMesh` — **the ratchet Phase 16 deleted**, after it blocked a real human edit. Building its texture twin would re-import a ratchet into the one surface the tool exists to edit. **So the pixel-exact comparison is an acceptance gate for the transcription commit, not a permanent pin.** What survives after it is Phase 16's shape: **invariants** over the committed `.tex` files (parses, builds at its declared size, every op inside the image, no op that draws nothing) plus a builder-level pin that reads no asset.
- **⚑ CLAUSE TWO IS ONE-THIRD VOCABULARY AND TWO-THIRDS CONTENT THE GAME CANNOT USE.** *"Panel grids, wear and faction liveries."* Panel grids are exactly what the three assets are. **Wear and faction liveries would author dead data**: `models.toml` maps one model to one texture stem, **nothing anywhere selects a texture by faction**, and there is no material or variant system to hang one on. That is F's own trap (*"must add LOD selection or it authors dead data"*) seen from the other side — named here so it is not half-built.
- **⚑ CLAUSE THREE IS TRUE AND SMALLER THAN IT READS.** BC1 plus the full box-filtered mip chain is `cookTexture`'s existing path and **G does not touch it**: what changes is where the pixels come from, never what happens to them afterwards. `TextureFormat::R8` does exist and is genuinely reserved for glyph coverage — but a font atlas ships **inside `.sfont`**, so **no `.stex` has ever carried R8**, and G does not make the first one.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE COOKER'S OWN GUARD FORCES A REPLACEMENT RATHER THAN A COEXISTENCE.** Output paths are keyed on the file **stem**, and Phase 9 added a loud failure for two sources cooking to one output. **`hull.tex` beside `hull.png` is precisely that collision**, so G cannot quietly add a format and leave the PNGs in place — it must replace them. D's rule (*"one implementation, and the deletion is what makes it true"*) arrives here through a guard written for an entirely different reason.

**The stages.** **G1 — the document and the cook.** `texture_doc` in `engine/assets` beside `forge_doc`: parse through `core::toml`, a schema table driving parser, writer and panel the way `ForgeParamSpec` already does for primitives, evaluation to RGBA8, and the comment-preserving trivia writer `forge_doc.cpp` proved. `cookTexture` gains a `.tex` branch handing the same `ImageRgba` to the same BC1 path. **G2 — the Forge opens one**: `.tex` joins the asset list, its ops show in a generated panel, a 2D preview shows the image and the mesh in the viewport wears it live, and save writes back with the comments intact. **G3 — the deletion and the live proof**: the three PNGs and `gen_assets.ps1`'s texture half go, `models.toml`'s comment about `hull.png` is corrected, and the script keeps its audio half.

**Tests.** **`cooker.unit`, and the linkage was checked before it was promised** (Phase 15's lesson, which cost a library split): `sol_cooker_tests` links `sol_cooker_lib`, which owns `decodePng` **and** links `sol::assets` publicly, so that suite is the only one that can reach the builder and the decoder at once — `assets.unit` cannot, because the PNG decoder is not engine code. CMake already hands `cooker.unit` a `SOL_MESH_SOURCE_DIR` and gains the texture equivalent the same way. It carries the acceptance gate: **each transcription decodes-and-compares exactly against its committed PNG, and each cooked `.stex` keeps its SHA256.** `assets.unit` carries the document itself: `writeTexture(parseTexture(f)) == f` byte for byte on the committed files, schema rejection of a wrong-arity row, defaults, and the invariants above. **Validated by breaking an asset**, per Phase 16 — a rectangle pushed outside the image must fail something.

**Exit.** Open the Forge, open `hull.tex`, move a panel, watch the station's hull change in the viewport, save, re-cook, and see it in the game. And with nothing edited, **the three `.stex` files the game loads are byte-identical to the ones it ships today** — which is the whole claim about the look: G changes **no pixel the player sees**, by design and by proof.

**⚑ THE HONEST NUMBER, STATED BEFORE THE WORK.** G buys **no visual change whatever** — it is a source-format and tooling change, and the pixel-exactness gate exists to guarantee exactly that. What it buys is that the textures become editable in the tool and diffable in git (three PNGs totalling **17,325 bytes** of opaque binary become an estimated ~5 KB of readable TOML, exact figure at G1), and that texture generation stops depending on **System.Drawing/GDI+**, a Windows-only .NET assembly. **Do not let a later session report it as new art.**

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** **No wear and no faction liveries** — dead data until something selects a texture, see above. **No paint or brush surface**: this is a structured document, not an image editor. **No dragging a panel in the preview** — that is the E1→E2 shape arriving one format over (the generated panel is E1; direct manipulation is its own slice with its own picking), and it is G's own recorded next question rather than a gap. **No PNG writer**, the fork being closed. **No R8, no BC3, no alpha, no atlasing**, and **no noise or gradient ops** beyond what the three assets actually need — the vocabulary grows when an asset needs it, not before.

**⚑⚑ STAGE G IS COMPLETE, VERIFIED LIVE, 2026-08-21 on `feat/paint-that-panel`.** `7c19bf9` spec, `cd6730b` G1a, `a8280fd` G1b, `b2e88f5` G2, `5976673` G2c, `7fb9005` G2d. **`assets.unit` 38 → 51, `cooker.unit` 29 → 30, `forge.unit` 4 → 5**; `geometry.unit` untouched at 144. Twelve suites green in ~87.6 s, `dev-gpu` 2/2. **No save change, no renderer change, and — proved rather than asserted — no change to a single pixel the game draws.**

- **⚑⚑ THE HEADLINE IS THE ONE THE SPEC PROMISED AND IT HELD END TO END: THE THREE COOKED `.stex` FILES ARE BYTE-IDENTICAL TO THE ONES THE GAME SHIPPED BEFORE ANY OF THIS EXISTED.** Baselines were taken before the first line was written and re-measured after the sources were replaced and the cook rewritten: **`checker BA78FF8B`, `hull 3F46A3C0`, `cockpit 0E9F8409`**, all three unchanged through BC1 and the full mip chain. The source went from **17,325 bytes of opaque PNG to 5,911 bytes of readable TOML** and the game cannot tell.
- **⚑⚑ THE FORK THE C CHECKPOINT LEFT OPEN WAS DECIDED ON A MEASUREMENT, NOT A PREFERENCE — AND THE MEASUREMENT WAS THAT THESE TEXTURES WERE ALREADY PROCEDURAL.** `gen_assets.ps1` drew all three with System.Drawing; replaying `System.Random` in its own call order and re-issuing the same GDI+ calls reproduces **every committed PNG byte for byte**. So the transcription could be asserted to the byte rather than judged by eye, and the whole vocabulary needed is **five ops** — `fill`, `checker`, `rects`, `panels`, `lines`.
- **⚑⚑ THE ROW LISTS ARE DATA AND NOT A SEED, AND THE REPO HAD ALREADY DECIDED IT.** `seed = 1337, panels = 60` cannot work: **.NET's subtractive generator has no C++ twin**, so a seed re-rolls every panel and changes what the game draws. `ForgePrimitive::Mesh`'s own comment settled the general case for the asteroid's PowerShell noise — *reproducing another runtime's arithmetic puts a bug-compatibility shim in this engine's permanent vocabulary*. ⚑ And the second reason is the one that matters to the tool: **a seed is a texture you can regenerate but cannot EDIT**, and you cannot nudge one panel of it. `checker` stays a true recipe (no RNG in it at all), `hull` and `cockpit` are lists — **the format carries both, exactly as `.forge` carries parametric parts beside baked ones**.
- **⚑⚑ THE PIXEL RULES WERE MEASURED OFF THE COMMITTED PNGs BECAUSE NOTHING HERE CALLS GDI+.** A rect covers `[x, x+w) × [y, y+h)`; **a width-2 pen at `c` covers `[c-1, c]`**, confirmed on both axes (cockpit's vertical seam at 222 lights columns **221–222**, its horizontal seam at 178 lights rows **177–178**), with no antialiasing anywhere. Getting the pen backwards shifts every seam in the repo by one pixel — *"close enough" is not a property a transcription may have*.
- **⚑⚑ THE DELETION WAS FORCED BY A GUARD WRITTEN FOR A DIFFERENT REASON, AND THAT IS WHY G COULD NOT SHIP AS AN ADDITION.** Output paths are keyed on the file stem, and Phase 9's collision check refuses the whole cook when two sources land on one output. Built with both present, it named **three collisions and cooked nothing**. So D's rule — *one implementation, and the deletion is what makes it true* — arrived through the build rather than through discipline. **The PNG decoder stays**, exactly as the glTF importer did when D deleted the last `.gltf`: it is how art from elsewhere gets in.
- **⚑⚑ THE ACCEPTANCE GATE WAS BUILT TO BE DELETED, AND SAYING SO IN ITS OWN COMMENT IS THE POINT.** A test comparing built pixels against a committed PNG is **Phase 16's ratchet rebuilt in a new format** — it fails the moment somebody edits `hull.tex`, which is the entire purpose of the stage. It ran once, in the commit that performed the transcription, and left with the PNGs. What survives reads no image: the byte-exact round trip, the build invariants, and the pixel rules asserted on documents that reference no asset.
- **⚑⚑ SIX MUTATIONS SORTED THE ASSERTIONS, AND ONE REPRODUCED E4d EXACTLY.** The pen offset, the rect bound and the draw order are each caught by a synthetic test **and** by the gate (13,774 / 2,901 / 45,067 pixels differing). **The channel clamp is caught by the synthetic test ALONE** — no committed panel exceeds 255 (hull peaks at 125+8, cockpit at 43+6), so the gate stayed green on all three and the input had to be built. *A mutation no asset can see still needs a can-fail test.* ⚑ And the invariant was validated Phase 16's way, **by breaking an asset**: an op pushed off the canvas fails with `hull.tex op 3 (rects) draws nothing`, while **a legitimate edit — one panel moved a pixel — passes all 51 permanent tests** and fails only the gate that is leaving.
- **⚑⚑ THE ENCODE HAD TO MOVE INTO THE LIBRARY FIRST, AND IT IS THE THIRD SIGHTING OF ONE STRUCTURAL SHAPE.** The mip loop and header lived in the cooker's `main.cpp`, outside `sol_cooker_lib` — **the same arrangement that kept `.smesh` untestable until the E1–E3 debt slice moved `encodeMesh`**, while `encodeSound` has always been a library function, *which is exactly why sound had a round-trip test and the other two did not*. Textures were the third instance and nobody had looked, because until G nothing but the cooker needed it. `cooker::encodeTexture`/`serializeTexture` are now the one implementation the cooker writes and the Forge uploads, and **`cooker.unit` asserts the `.stex` layout end to end for the first time**.
- **⚑ THE TOOL ENCODES WHAT IT PREVIEWS, WHICH IS A CORRECTNESS PROPERTY RATHER THAN A DETAIL.** `mesh_library` hands the built image through the cooker's encode, so the hull wears the **BC1 the game will load** rather than the RGBA the document built. A tool prettier than the game hides every artefact the compression introduces — the same mistake as previewing a mesh at a distance no player sees it from, which stage F had to learn from the other side.
- **⚑⚑ THE FLAT PREVIEW PAID FOR ITSELF ON THE FIRST TWO FRAMES IT DREW, AND THE ARGUMENT GENERALISES.** Every number in this document is a **pixel coordinate**, so a texture judged only on a lumpy asteroid is a set of numbers whose effect cannot be seen. `checker.tex`'s white orientation marker at `[4, 4, 12, 12]` is obvious flat and **invisible on the mesh**; `hull.tex`'s amber hazard strips are the same story. It is a second *view* of the same `GpuTexture` the mesh samples — ImGui's Vulkan backend hands out its own descriptor set — so there is no second upload and no second encode to disagree with the first.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE DEFECT THE DRIVE FOUND IS A RULE THAT DID NOT TRANSFER BY BEING COPIED: ONE DRAG WAS TWENTY-FOUR UNDO ENTRIES.** Dragging a panel row from **72 to 146** and pressing `undo` gave back **143**. `drawParams` pushed undo from the *write-back* path, and a `DragInt` reports an edit on every frame the mouse moves — so one press stepped back a single frame and the other 23 quietly evicted real history from a 64-deep stack. **`PartEditor` has always known this** (`movePoint` pushes nothing; the caller opens the gesture once), but there the gesture belongs to the viewport, so the rule had no way to arrive here by imitation. The answer is **`IsItemActivated()`** — push on the frame the widget becomes active — and **placement is load-bearing, since it names the LAST SUBMITTED item**. Re-driven: same drag, one click, **72 back exactly**, `git status` clean.
- **⚑ LIVE PROOF, so it is not re-derived.** Boot reads `6 texture(s)` (three sources, three cooked). `checker.tex` opens as `0 checker / 1 rects` with `cell 32` and both colours; dragging `color_a` from **R:232 to R:182** turned the asteroid's squares yellow with the panel still open and marked the file dirty. Save wrote **one line** — `color_a = [232, 122, 42]` → `[182, 122, 42]` — in a 588-byte file, header comments and second op byte-identical. Cooked, `checker.stex` moved **BA78FF8B → 2DA59CC6**; restored, it moved back, **so the positive control moves and the negative is a refusal rather than a miss**. `hull.tex` shows `fill / panels / lines / rects`, `tint 0, 3, 8`, `panels (60)` with rows reading `72 223 26 16 118` — the transcription visible in the tool as the numbers the RNG produced.
- **⚑⚑ THE HONEST NUMBER, STATED IN THE SPEC BEFORE THE WORK AND UNCHANGED AFTER IT: G BOUGHT NO VISUAL CHANGE AND WAS NEVER GOING TO.** The pixel-exactness gate exists precisely to guarantee that. What it bought is that the textures are **editable in the tool and diffable in git**, and that texture generation no longer needs **System.Drawing/GDI+** — `gen_assets.ps1` is audio-only now. **Do not let a later session report it as new art.**
- **⚑ WHAT IS OPEN AFTER G.** **Direct manipulation** — dragging a panel in the flat preview rather than typing its numbers — is the E1→E2 shape one format over and is G's own recorded next question, not a gap. **Wear and faction liveries stay out** until something in the game can select a texture (`models.toml` maps one model to one stem and nothing chooses by faction); building them now would author dead data. **No `.tex` has ever been made by a human**, and the three refusals from E4/E5 are still unmet — see the playtest backlog.

**⚑⚑ STAGE H — SPEC'D 2026-08-21 ON `feat/fly-what-you-made`, AND READING THE GAME FOUND A WALL THE PROGRAMME BELIEVED IT HAD ALREADY TAKEN DOWN.** H's one-liner promises *"a whole ship or station as content — mesh, model row and `ships.toml`/`stations.toml` row — validated against the strict schema that already exists"*. Measured against the code: **the model row's reading half has been built since stage C, the station half is not expressible at all, the validation clause is the first one-liner clause in four stages to survive contact, and the cost is in a place the sentence never mentions.**

**Diagnosis (read from the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **The model row is already READ, and has been since stage C.** `mesh_library.cpp` loads `game/data` into a `DefDatabase` (`loadModelCatalog`) and matches every `[[model]]` row naming the open mesh (`matchModels`); the Report panel prints id, texture, radius, avoid, emissive and solid, and turns orange when the authored radius disagrees with the measured one. **What is missing is only the write.** The tool has been reporting the same four mismatches — 2%, 12%, 13%, 16% — since stage C, and has never been able to fix one; an author reads the warning and goes to a text editor.
- **⚑⚑ "OR STATION" IS NOT EXPRESSIBLE, AND THE REASON IS BIGGER THAN STATIONS: `ModelId` STOPPED BEING AN ENUM AND DID NOT STOP BEING A NAME IN C++.** `StationDef` has no `model` key, and `space_world.cpp:3037` resolves `modelByName("station")` once and hands it to every station in the galaxy. That is the general case, not the exception — **`space_world.cpp:4603` (`shape.model = modelIdFromName(defs, def.model)`) is the ONLY data-driven model resolution in the entire game.** Every other model in the world is fetched by a hardcoded literal: `"asteroid"` (1561), `"cube"` (1598 and 6300), `"ship"` (2221), `"station"` (3037), `"gate"` (3052), `"gate_membrane"` (3057). **Stage A removed the enum wall and left a name wall**, and its exit criterion — *"drop a mesh in, add a row, and a ship def flies it"* — turns out to be exactly, narrowly true: a **ship** def, and nothing else in the world.
- **⚑ THE VALIDATION CLAUSE HOLDS, AND IT IS THE FIRST CLAUSE OF A FORGE ONE-LINER TO DO SO SINCE E4.** `DefDatabase::mergeToml(text, length, sourceName, &error)` is strict by construction — `rejectUnknownKeys` (`data_defs.cpp:284`) fails on any key outside the allowed set, every reader is typed, the range checks are there (`radius > 0`, `alpha` in 0..1, `avoid_radius` 0 or `>= radius`), and **on failure the database is left as it was**. The Forge already links `sol::assets` and already compiles with `SOL_MODEL_DATA_DIR`. So "validate before writing" is a **call**, not a build: hand the candidate document to a scratch database and refuse to write what the game would reject.
- **⚑ BUT THE SCHEMA VALIDATES ONE DOCUMENT IN ISOLATION, AND THE ONE CROSS-REFERENCE H CREATES IS THE ONE IT DOES NOT CHECK.** `parseShip` reads `model` with `optionalString` and never resolves it. A ship naming a model that does not exist parses **clean**, and the failure surfaces at spawn as `SOL_LOG_WARN("unknown model '%s' in ship def; using 'ship'")` (`space_world.cpp:158`) — a warning in a log, behind a fallback that draws something plausible. Since H's whole purpose is to create that reference, it owes the check. **That is not a second schema**: it is the cross-def layer `validateFactions` already occupies, one def type over.
- **⚑⚑ THE COST IS THE WRITER, AND THE `.forge` WRITER'S APPROACH DOES NOT TRANSFER — WHICH IS MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED.** Two comment-preserving writers already exist (`writeForge`, `writeTexture`), each with `header`/`leading`/`trailer` trivia and an unplaceable-comment flag; a def writer would be the **third sighting of that shape**. But both parse values into `double` and re-emit through `appendNumber`, which is safe only because **`.forge` and `.tex` files are TOOL-WRITTEN**. The def files are hand-written in a person's own numeric style, and round-tripping them through a number is not cosmetic:
  - **27 values across the nine def files are TOML INTEGERS, and writing them as floats is a LOAD FAILURE rather than a reformat.** `optionalUint` (`data_defs.cpp:258`) requires `value->isInteger()`, so `slots_engine = 1` re-emitted as `1.0` is rejected by the schema the tool just used to validate it. Fifteen of them are in `ships.toml`, which is the file H writes to.
  - **3 exponent forms would be expanded** — `scan_range = 2.5e8` becomes `250000000.0`, because `appendNumber` deliberately skips exponent forms while a plain one still round-trips (a rule written to protect `90` from becoming `9e+01`, correct there and wrong here).
  - **4 fractional trailing zeros would be dropped** — `alpha = 0.30` becomes `0.3`, `emissive = 0.10` becomes `0.1`.
  - **⚑ So the def document must hold every value as VERBATIM TEXT and re-format only the keys actually edited.** That is a smaller build than a typed document, not a larger one, and it kills the E-checkpoint's own bug by construction: a writer that echoes cannot delete a key an author typed at its default, because it never decides what to emit.
- **⚑ AND THE FILE H WRITES TO MOST IS 53% COMMENTS.** `models.toml` is **57 comment lines out of 107**, the densest in the game, and they are where the project's reasoning about its own assets lives — why the gate is `solid = false`, why the membrane's emissive is insurance against a normal's sign, why the station's avoid radius is deliberately wider than what you can hit. Across all nine def files it is 169 lines of 747. **A writer that regenerates a def file from its values deletes the argument and keeps the number.**

**Decisions taken with the user before any code.**

1. **H opens the station wall.** `StationDef` gains `model`, defaulting to `"station"`, and `space_world.cpp:3037` resolves it per archetype (`StationSpec::archetype` already indexes the def order, so it is a lookup table built once). This is a game-side change inside a tools stage, taken deliberately: **8u's rule is that a metadata field must name its consumer in the same stage that adds it**, and here the consumer already exists and the field is what is missing. Without it, half of H's own one-liner stays impossible. **No visible change until somebody authors a second station mesh** — every archetype defaults to the model it already draws.
2. **The tool owns the ASSET keys of a `[[ship]]` row and no more** — `id`, `name`, `model`, `scale`. Flight tuning, defense, price and slots stay in a text editor, where the strict schema already makes them safe. A generated 33-key panel would answer *"author a whole ship"* literally, and its competitor is a text file that works, with no complaint behind it. **The Forge stays a 3D asset tool rather than becoming a second def editor.**
3. **Values are verbatim text, per the measurement above.**

**H1. The def document — parse and write, headless, in `sol::assets`.** `def_doc.hpp`/`.cpp`: a TOML array-of-table document as *written* — `header` trivia, an ordered list of elements each carrying its `[[type]]`, its `leading` trivia and its ordered `(key, verbatim value text)` pairs, and a `trailer`. Operations are the four H2–H4 need: find a row by id, set a key's value, add a key, append a row. **The regression net is the nine committed `game/data/*.toml` files** — `writeDefs(parseDefs(f)) == f` byte for byte, asserted in `assets.unit` behind a new `SOL_DEF_DATA_DIR`, which is the shape `geometry.unit` already uses for `.forge` and `assets.unit` gained for `.tex` at G. An unplaceable comment (after a value on the same line) is flagged rather than silently dropped, as `ForgeDoc` does.

**H2. The `[[model]]` row, writable.** The Report's read-only block becomes editable, and three actions close the gap stage C opened: **`use measured radius`** (the fix for a warning the tool has printed and been unable to act on for five stages), **`create [[model]] row`** for a mesh that has none — prefilled with id and mesh from the stem, texture from the tool's own texture list, radius from the measurement — and direct editing of texture, avoid, emissive, solid, translucent and alpha. **Every write is validated first**: the candidate document goes through a scratch `DefDatabase::mergeToml`, and a failure is shown as the schema's own message rather than written to disk.

**H3. The `[[ship]]` row.** Pick an existing row or create one, and edit `id`, `name`, `model` and `scale`. `model` is a combo over the model rows, not a text field, which is what makes the reference correct by construction — plus the explicit cross-check for the case where a row already on disk names a model that is gone. **This is the stage's payoff**: it is the step that takes a mesh from "cooked and drawable" to "a thing in the game".

**H4. The station's model.** The def key, its consumer at the spawner, and the same combo in the tool. `stations.toml` gains no row and no visible change; what it gains is the ability to have one.

**Tests.** `assets.unit` carries H1 — the byte-exact round trip over all nine committed def files, plus set/add/append against fixtures, plus the three reformat hazards asserted directly (an integer stays an integer, `2.5e8` stays `2.5e8`, `0.30` stays `0.30`) because those are the failures that would otherwise arrive as a corrupted committed file. It also gains the `[[station]]` `model` key's schema. `forge.unit` carries the validate-before-write path and the model cross-check, headless as ever. **Every new assertion is made to fail before it is trusted**, and the H1 net is validated Phase 16's way — by breaking a def file and confirming the tool refuses it. **The panels are verified by running the tool and saying what was seen**, per AGENTS §7.

**Exit.** **Author a mesh in the Forge, give it a `[[model]]` row and a `[[ship]]` row without leaving the tool, and fly it in the game** — the programme's own closing argument, and the first time the sentence in Phase 9's exit criterion is true end to end. Plus: the four radius mismatches the tool has reported since stage C can be fixed from the panel that reports them, and every def file it writes is byte-identical outside the lines it changed.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** **No flight-tuning editor** — decision 2. **No new def TYPE**, and no editing of weapons, modules, crew, commodities or factions: H writes the two row kinds that name an asset, and a general def editor is a different tool with no complaint behind it. **No deletion of rows** — the tool adds and edits; removing content is a text-editor operation with consequences (a save naming a deleted ship) that H does not own. **No hot-reload into a running game**: the loop stays cook-and-relaunch. **No change to the other six hardcoded model names** (`asteroid`, `cube` ×2, `ship`, `gate`, `gate_membrane`) — the wall is now measured and recorded, and taking it down is a game-side phase with its own exit criteria, not a rider on a tools stage. **And no second schema**, which is the one clause of the one-liner that was right.

**⚑⚑ STAGE H IS COMPLETE, VERIFIED LIVE, 2026-08-21 on `feat/fly-what-you-made`.** `c158634` spec, `efd8e93` H1, `d0929dc` H2, `918a5dc` H3+H4, `2d6e19f` H3b. **`assets.unit` 51 → 70, `forge.unit` 5 → 7**; `geometry.unit`, `ui.unit` and `cooker.unit` untouched. Twelve suites green in 87.8 s, `dev-gpu` 2/2. **No save change and no renderer change.**

- **⚑⚑ THE PROGRAMME's EXIT CRITERION IS MET, END TO END AND IN ONE RUN OF THE TOOL.** `New parts` → `save` (`assets/meshes/untitled.forge`) → `Rescan` → open it → `create [[model]] row` → `create [[ship]] row` → `save defs`. Then a build cooked `untitled.smesh` **with no code change**, and the game booted reading **`[lua] init.lua ready - ships: … sol.freighter, sol.untitled`** and **`models: 8 (13 meshes, 3 textures, 5 level(s) over 3 model(s))`** — 7 models and 12 meshes before. **No `unknown model` warning**, so the reference the tool created resolved. *A mesh authored in the Forge reaches the game without a line of C++ and without a hand-edited text file*, which is the sentence Phase 9 opened with.
- **⚑⚑ H's ONE-LINER WAS WRONG ON ONE CLAUSE, RIGHT ON ONE, AND SILENT ABOUT WHERE THE COST WAS — AND THE WRONG CLAUSE IS THE FINDING. "OR STATION" WAS NOT EXPRESSIBLE**, because `StationDef` had no `model` key and `space_world.cpp:3037` handed `modelByName("station")` to every station in the galaxy. That is the general case: **`space_world.cpp:4603` was the ONLY data-driven model resolution in the entire game**, and the other six are hardcoded literals (`asteroid`, `cube` ×2, `ship`, `gate`, `gate_membrane`). **Stage A removed the enum wall and left a NAME wall**, so its exit criterion — *"drop a mesh in, add a row, and a ship def flies it"* — was exactly, narrowly true: a **ship** def, and nothing else in the world. H4 makes the station's the first of those names to become data.
- **⚑ THE VALIDATION CLAUSE HELD, AND IT IS THE FIRST CLAUSE OF A FORGE ONE-LINER TO SURVIVE SINCE E4.** `DefDatabase::mergeToml` is strict, typed, range-checked and already linked into the tool, so "validate before writing" was a call rather than a build. **Its limit had to be named though**: it validates one document in isolation and `parseShip` reads `model` with `optionalString` **without ever resolving it**, so the one cross-reference this stage exists to create is the one the schema does not check.
- **⚑⚑ THE COST WAS THE WRITER, AND THE `.forge` WRITER'S APPROACH DOES NOT TRANSFER — MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED.** `writeForge` and `writeTexture` parse values to `double` and re-emit, which is safe only because **those files are TOOL-WRITTEN**. Def files are hand-written in a person's own numeric style, and round-tripping them through a number is not cosmetic: **27 committed values are TOML INTEGERS and `optionalUint` REJECTS a float** (`slots_engine = 1` re-emitted as `1.0` is a **load failure**, fifteen of them in `ships.toml`), 3 exponent forms expand (`2.5e8` → `250000000.0`), and 4 fractional zeros drop (`alpha = 0.30` → `0.3`). **So the def document holds every value as VERBATIM TEXT and re-formats only the keys actually edited** — a smaller build than a typed one, and it kills the E-checkpoint's own bug by construction, since a writer that echoes never decides what to emit.
- **⚑ AND THE FILE IT WRITES TO MOST IS 53% COMMENTS.** `models.toml` is **57 comment lines of 107** — where the reasoning about the gate's `solid = false`, the membrane's emissive insurance and the station's deliberately-wide avoid radius lives. Nine committed def files round-trip **byte for byte**, asserted in `assets.unit` against the real files.
- **⚑⚑ ONE MUTATION OF EIGHT SURVIVED, AND IT IS E4d's SHAPE A THIRD TIME.** Trimming the raw line was invisible to all 68 tests, because **no committed def file has trailing whitespace on any line** — measured, all nine, key lines and comment lines alike. The input had to be **built**. Writing it then caught a **real bug beside it**: the `[[table]]` header line was being trimmed, which eats the `\r` of a CRLF file and would rewrite every row header in the document.
- **⚑⚑ AND A TRAP IN THE HARNESS ITSELF, WHICH MANUFACTURED FALSE CONFIDENCE IN BOTH DIRECTIONS: A MUTATION HARNESS THAT RESTORES BY COPYING A BACKUP LEAVES THE BUILD STALE.** `Copy-Item` preserves the backup's timestamp, so the restored source is **older** than the object built from the mutant and ninja skips it — every "clean" run after a mutation was still measuring the mutant. Touch the file after restoring. (A second, smaller one: `[IO.File]` methods use .NET's current directory, which PowerShell's `cd` does not update, so an early harness edited nothing at all and reported seven survivals.)
- **⚑⚑ THE STAGE's POINT IS ONE BUTTON, AND ITS DEFECT WAS FIVE STAGES OLD.** Since stage C the tool has printed *"MESH IS 0.1584 m (15.8%) LARGER: the collision sphere sits inside the hull, so ships pass through the picture"* and an author's only remedy was a text editor. There is now a **`use measured 1.1584 m`** beside it. Live: one click, one save, **`models.toml` differs by EXACTLY ONE LINE of 107** with all 57 comment lines intact, and the witness is a **text A/B with its own positive control** — after the edit `forge: [[model]] asteroid radius 1.1584 m agrees with the mesh`, after `git checkout` the +15.8% warning is back.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE DRIVE FOUND PHASE 14's DEFECT IN A NEW FORMAT.** The first write was **`radius = 1.1583778`** while the button said 1.1584 — unreadable in a file whose neighbours carry one decimal, **and a small lie in the UI besides**. The file now gets exactly what the panel showed (0.1 mm, Phase 14's own grid), and the rounding provably cannot re-open the mismatch it closes: half a unit in the fourth decimal is 0.005% of the smallest radius in this game against a 0.1% agreement band. ⚑ A second drive finding, purely practical: **fixing the mismatch DELETES the three-line warning and its button**, so everything below jumps up 49 px and a `save` click calibrated *before* the edit misses *after* it.
- **⚑ WHAT THE TOOL OWNS OF A SHIP ROW IS FOUR KEYS, BY DECISION** — `id`, `name`, `model`, `scale`. Flight tuning, defence, price and slots stay in a text editor where the strict schema already makes them safe; a generated 33-key panel would answer *"author a whole ship"* literally while duplicating a surface that works, with no complaint behind it. **`model` is a COMBO over the rows that exist**, which is what makes the reference correct by construction.
- **⚑ AND THE RULE MOVED OUT OF THE WIDGET, WHICH IS PHASE 15's LESSON APPLIED BEFORE THE PROMISE RATHER THAN AFTER.** The spec said `forge.unit` would carry the cross-check; `def_editor.cpp` pulls in ImGui and **cannot be in that suite**. `missingModelRefs` lives in `mesh_library.cpp` instead, the panel asks it the same question, and the invariant is asserted over the game's own committed defs — validated Phase 16's way, **by breaking an asset**: renaming `models.toml`'s `ship` id prints `[[ship]] sol.shuttle names model 'ship', which does not exist` for all three ships.
- **⚑ `modelIdFromName`'s CONTEXT AND FALLBACK ARE ARGUMENTS NOW.** Hardcoding *"in ship def"* and a fall-back to `"ship"` was harmless while a ship def was the only caller and is not once a station resolves through it: a bad name would have drawn **a shuttle where a station belongs**, which reads as a spawn bug rather than as a bad name in a file.
- **⚑ WHAT H DELIBERATELY DID NOT DO.** **The other six hardcoded model names stay** (`asteroid`, `cube` ×2, `ship`, `gate`, `gate_membrane`) — the wall is measured and recorded, and taking it down is a game-side phase with its own exit criteria, not a rider on a tools stage. **No deletion of rows**, no editing of weapons/modules/crew/commodities/factions, and no hot-reload into a running game. **⚑ And `stations.toml` gained no row and no visible change** — what it gained is the ability to have one.

**⚑⚑ STAGE I — GRAB THAT PANEL — SPEC'D 2026-08-21 ON `feat/grab-that-panel`, AND IT REOPENS A PROGRAMME THIS PLAN REPORTED AS FINISHED.** H was the last *planned* stage and its exit criterion was the programme's, so *"the Forge programme is finished"* was true of the plan. **It was never true of the tool: stage G recorded its own next question and shipped without answering it** — *"is editing a texture by a list of numbered rows tolerable, or is the first thing anyone will ask for a click-and-drag on the preview?"* Picked by the user over three alternatives. **Reading the code refuted that question's own framing on three counts, and found a fourth thing it never mentioned.**

**Diagnosis (measured against the code as it stands, before any change).**

- **⚑⚑ THE PREREQUISITE IS THE PREVIEW's SIZE, NOT ITS INPUT HANDLING, AND THE NUMBER IS BRUTAL: THE TWO-PIXEL NUDGE DOES NOT EXIST.** `main.cpp:891` draws a 256×256 document into a **200×200** `ImGui::Image`, so one screen pixel is **1.28 texture pixels** and a drag can only produce offsets of `round(n × 1.28)`. Measured over the full range: **56 of 257 offsets (21.8%) cannot be produced at all**, and the first one missing is **2** — a drag goes 0 → 1 → 3. `texture_editor.hpp` opens by declaring that every value in this document is an integer and *"a pixel coordinate and a colour channel are exact"*; **a sub-1:1 surface is precisely what makes that false.** The preview must be integer-scaled before any of this is honest, and at 1:1 it is 256 px inside a 380 px panel, which fits.
- **⚑⚑ THE PICK IS NOT NEW WORK — IT IS A MASK THAT ALREADY EXISTS RECORDING AN ID INSTEAD OF A FLAG. FOURTEENTH CONSECUTIVE ITEM THE REPO HAD ALREADY WRITTEN DOWN.** `drawLayerInto(doc, layer, image, touched)` takes a per-pixel `std::vector<uint8_t>*`, and `plot()` sets `touched[index] = 1` **at exactly the pixel it paints** (`texture_doc.cpp:246` and `:187`); `textureLayerCoverage` already consumes it. So *which row is on top at (x, y)* is answered by the same evaluation that draws the picture. **That matters because a hand-written rect hit-test would be a second implementation of the pixel rules**: it would have to re-derive clipping, file order, the width-2 pen's `[c-1, c]` and the checker's deliberate per-pixel walk, and the first one to drift would put the selection somewhere the picture does not show. One walk, one answer, per D's *one implementation* rule.
- **⚑⚑ THE VIEWPORT's DRAG IDIOM DOES NOT TRANSFER, BECAUSE THIS DOCUMENT IS INTEGER.** `PointTool` drags on `viewport.cursorDelta`, a per-frame float delta, which is correct for a `double` mesh. Here every write is rounded, and **rounding a per-frame delta does not compose to rounding the total**: three frames of 0.4 px are `round(0.4) × 3 = 0` while the hand moved 1.2 — the drag sticks and then jumps. The rule must be **absolute** — the row's value at gesture start plus `round(cursor − cursorStart)` — which is E4c's *a move that works on one element may not compose over a set* arriving in the **time** axis instead of the space one.
- **⚑ DIRECT MANIPULATION IS NOT UNIFORM ACROSS THE VOCABULARY, AND THE QUESTION NEVER MENTIONED IT: ONLY 2 OF THE 5 OPS HAVE A 2-D HANDLE.** `rects` and `panels` carry `x, y, w, h`. **`lines` carries a bare coordinate** — a `vertical` entry is an x and a `horizontal` entry is a y — so a seam drags in **one axis only**, and the other axis must be ignored rather than silently written. **`fill` covers the canvas and `checker` has a cell size**: neither has a position, so a click on bare hull selects the op and has nothing to drag. **A stage that handled only rects would leave two of `hull.tex`'s four ops unreachable by hand**, so the refusals are specified here rather than discovered.
- **⚑ THERE IS NO ARBITRATION PROBLEM, WHICH IS THE INVERSE OF E4b's DEFECT AND IS STATED SO NOBODY GOES LOOKING FOR IT.** E4b's cost was a *refused* viewport drag handing the rest of the press to the orbit camera, because `main.cpp` re-decides ownership the instant `dragging()` goes false. Here the surface is inside an ImGui window, and `PointTool::update` already returns early on `viewport.uiCaptured && !m_dragging` (`point_tool.cpp:420`) — **the 3-D tool never sees this press and there is no second owner to fall through to.** The gesture is ImGui's from beginning to end.
- **⚑ AND G's UNDO RULE TRANSFERS, THROUGH A DIFFERENT WIDGET.** G's live defect was one drag becoming twenty-four undo entries, fixed with `IsItemActivated()` placed *after* the widget. **`ImGui::Image` is not an activatable item**, so the interactive surface must be an `InvisibleButton` over the same rect — which restores `noteActivation()` unchanged rather than inventing a second gesture boundary beside it.

**Decisions.**

1. **The preview goes 1:1 at 256 px and becomes the interactive surface.** Not a zoom control and not a second window: 200 → 256 fits the existing 380 px panel and every committed texture is 256×256, so 1:1 covers the whole repo. A document of another size is drawn at the largest **integer** scale that fits, never a fractional one.
2. **The pick is `textureHitTest` in `sol::assets`, beside `textureLayerCoverage`, returning `{layer, row}`.** It lives in the engine rather than in the tool for Phase 15's reason applied *before* the promise this time, which is H's own lesson: `texture_editor.cpp` pulls in ImGui and cannot be in a suite, and the rule is a property of the **document**, not of the widget.
3. **Click selects, drag moves, and the selection follows the pick across ops** — a click on a panel selects the op that owns it *and* the row inside it, and scrolls that row into view, or the gesture selects something the author cannot see.
4. **The drag is absolute, quantized once, and refuses the axis it cannot express.**
5. **One gesture is one undo entry.**

**The assertions, written first, because that is what has decided every design in this programme since E3.**

*(1)* **The pick agrees with the picture**: for every committed texture and every pixel, the row `textureHitTest` names is the row that wrote that pixel in `buildTexture` — so a divergence in clipping, file order or the pen rule fails here rather than in the tool.
*(2)* **A drag of δ moves its row by exactly δ and changes nothing else** — every other row, every other op and the document's trivia byte-identical. E1's fixed point, one format over.
*(3)* **A zero-distance drag leaves all three committed `.tex` files byte-identical.** Phase 14's rule and E4's, met a third time — and it is also what guards G's proof that the cooked pixels are unchanged.
*(4)* **The absolute rule is asserted against the accumulating one**: a drag whose per-frame deltas each round to zero but whose total does not **must** move the row. **This input has to be BUILT, per E4d** — no committed asset and no real mouse produces it reliably, which is exactly why the mutation would otherwise ship.
*(5)* **A pick on `fill` or `checker` reports "nothing to drag" rather than moving something**, and a `lines` drag writes one axis and leaves the other alone.

**The slices, in build order.** **I1** — `textureHitTest` in `sol::assets` plus the attribution generalisation of `drawLayerInto`'s mask; headless, no UI. **I2** — the preview goes 1:1 and interactive: `InvisibleButton`, click-to-select across ops, scroll-into-view, and the absolute move for `rects` and `panels`. **I3** — the refusals and the one-axis case (`lines`, `fill`, `checker`). **I4** — the record.

**Out of scope, named so it is not half-built.** **No resize handle** — dragging a corner to change `w`/`h` is the obvious next ask, and it needs a *screen-space* handle test that is a different rule from the document-space pick; move first, as E1 took one point before it took a set. **No colour picked off the preview, no marquee, no multi-row selection, no new op.** **No zoom or pan**, because 1:1 covers every texture in this repo and a second viewport is a stage of its own. **And no change to a single committed pixel** — G's byte-identical proof stands, and assertion (3) is what holds it.

**⚑ What this leaves for the drive rather than for the paper.** Whether **topmost** is what an author means when panels overlap — `cockpit.tex` has 60 of them and they overlap heavily, so the row under the cursor is often not the row they were thinking of. And whether 256 px reads as *big enough to work in* or as *still too small*, which is the zoom control this stage is deliberately not building.

**⚑⚑ STAGE I IS COMPLETE, VERIFIED LIVE, 2026-08-21 on `feat/grab-that-panel`.** `b84ae73` spec, `f76ced8` I1, `0a8b2a3` I2. **`assets.unit` 74 → 82, `forge.unit` 7 → 10**; `geometry.unit` 144, `ui.unit` 94, `cooker.unit` 30, `game.unit` 15 all untouched. Twelve suites green in 94.0 s, `dev-gpu` 2/2. **No save change, no renderer change, no game change, and not one committed pixel altered** — G's byte-identical proof is untouched and assertion (3) is what holds it.

- **⚑⚑ THE LIVE PROOF IS TWO DRAGS WITH THEIR NUMBERS PREDICTED BEFORE THE RUN, AND THE SCALE WAS *MEASURED OFF THE SCREENSHOT* RATHER THAN ASSERTED.** `checker.tex` has exactly one movable row — a 12×12 white marker at `[4, 4]` — and the checker's orange appears nowhere else in the panel, so the preview's bounding box is findable in the image: **256 × 256, exactly 1:1**. Then a drag of **+30, +20** screen pixels wrote `[4, 4, 12, 12]` → **`[34, 24, 12, 12]`**, with `git diff --numstat` reading **exactly `1 1`**. And on `hull.tex`, a seam dragged **+15, +40** wrote `vertical[0]` **86 → 101** with the `horizontal` list **untouched** — the one-axis rule, live. ⚑ **A click that only selects left `git status` clean**, which is the negative half and the thing E1 shipped a bug over.
- **⚑⚑ THE PICK WAS NOT NEW WORK — IT WAS A MASK THE BUILDER ALREADY CARRIED, RECORDING AN ORDINAL INSTEAD OF A FLAG.
