Deleting it would remove the only exact check on the primitive generators and buy nothing, since it never blocks an edit. (3) **The invariants are the ones that catch a broken mesh rather than a changed one**: it parses and builds; it welds to a **closed manifold with zero border edges**; its **signed volume is positive** (outward winding, so an inside-out asset fails); welding **collapses** corners onto fewer points; and it contains **no degenerate faces**. (4) **`gate_membrane` is excluded from the closed-solid invariants by name, with the reason in the code**, and keeps its own aperture test — an exclusion list is honest where a silent skip is not. (5) **`forge.unit`'s radius test is left exactly as it is.** It is already file-based and already the right shape; re-opening it here would be scope this checkpoint did not take. **Its softness is now deliberate and recorded**, not an accident. (6) **The property tests that currently read the recipe are repointed at the files** — that is where the value always was. (7) **Session 12's cockpit edit is the phase's live proof**: the suite must go green with that edit in the tree, and the edit is what demonstrates the ratchet is gone.

**A. Repoint the invariants at the committed assets.** `buildFromForgeSource` already exists and is what Family 2 used; it becomes the front door for the whole file. `everyShippedMeshWeldsIntoAClosedManifoldSurface` and `weldingTheCubeFindsEightPointsUnderTwentyFourCorners` build from the files. The five `the*ForgeSourceIsTheShippedMesh` tests become one invariant check per asset, run over **all seven** committed `.forge` files rather than the five the recipe happens to cover — `gate` and `gate_membrane` have never been in this net and both should be.

**B. Retire the exact match.** `checkForgeSourceIsTheShippedMesh` and its `reportFirstDifference` call go. `reportFirstDifference` itself **stays** — `bakingAMeshAndReadingItBackIsTheSameMesh` uses it, and there it compares two meshes built from the same recipe by two routes, which is exactly the case a first-difference report is for. `checkAgainstShipped` stays for Family 1.

**Tests (`geometry.unit`).** Every committed `.forge` parses, builds and produces geometry. Every closed-solid asset is manifold, closed, has zero border edges, positive signed volume and no degenerate faces. The cube still welds 24 corners onto 8 points with 18 edges and unit volume — asserted **from `cube.forge`** now, not from the recipe. `gate_membrane` still fills exactly the 70 m aperture the crossing test uses. **⚑ And the check that keeps this honest: an invariant test must FAIL on a broken asset, so each new assertion is validated by feeding it a deliberately corrupted mesh** — the same move that validated Phase 15's index fix, because a test that cannot fail is worse than no test.

**Exit.** `geometry.unit` green **with session 12's cockpit edit in the working tree** — the edit that motivated the checkpoint is the proof it worked. Then a second, harder proof: **make a fresh edit in `forge.exe`, save, and confirm the suite still passes**, so the claim is "an asset can be edited" and not "one particular edit was blessed". All 12 suites green, `dev-gpu` 2/2, zero warnings. **No save change, no sim change, no renderer change** — tests and one asset only.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** **No change to `forge.unit`'s radius test** — decision 5. **No deletion of `shipped_meshes.hpp` or Family 1** — decision 2. **No re-derivation of the truth table**, which is the option this checkpoint rejected. **No new invariant that pins a SIZE or a POSITION** — "the station is about 100 m" is a ratchet wearing different clothes, and the radius relationship already has a home in `forge.unit`. **Nothing about LOD, stage F, or the Forge's viewport.**

**⚑ PHASE 16 IS COMPLETE** (`f642948` spec, `f099823` the tests, `d507ce8` the cockpit edit). `geometry.unit` **99 → 97** — five ratchet tests removed, three invariant tests and one membrane test added. **No save change, no sim change, no renderer change.**

**⚑ What building it changed about the plan.**

- **⚑⚑ THE EXIT CRITERION EARNED ITS SECOND HALF, AND ONLY THE DRIVE COULD HAVE FOUND IT.** The spec asked for two proofs — the suite green with session 12's edit, *and* green after a **fresh** edit driven through `forge.exe` — on the grounds that the first only shows one edit was blessed. It paid immediately: the blessed edit passed everything, and then a real drag-resize of `cube.forge` **failed `weldingTheCubeFindsEightPointsUnderTwentyFourCorners`**, because that test still asserted `signedVolume ≈ 1.0`. **I had repointed it at the file and left a DIMENSIONAL assertion in it — a size pin, which is precisely the ratchet-in-different-clothes the spec's own out-of-scope list names.** The topology (8 points, 24 corners, 12 triangles, 18 edges) survives an edit; the volume does not. **A test can be repointed at the right object and still assert the wrong kind of thing.**
- **⚑⚑ AND THE INVARIANTS FOUND A REAL FACT ON THEIR FIRST RUN: `gate_membrane` CARRIES 32 DEGENERATE FACES BY CONSTRUCTION.** Its own header predicted it in prose — *"the innermost quad row is degenerate on one edge and rasterises as a triangle fan"* — and nothing had ever measured it. The count is exactly its `segments`, so it is pinned to the shape rather than to a number, in `theMembranesOnlyDegenerateFacesAreTheFanAtItsCentre`. **Tenth consecutive item where the repo had already written the answer down; this time the comment was right and simply unverified.**
- **⚑ THE NET IS VALIDATED BY BREAKING AN ASSET, NOT BY READING IT.** Flattening `cube.forge` to `size = [1, 0, 1]` fails **five** tests: manifold, closed, positive volume and zero degenerates in the new invariant, plus the welding test, plus three pre-existing `forge_doc` round-trip and movability tests. A *changed but valid* asset fails none. **That gap between "broken" and "changed" is the whole phase.**
- **⚑ AND A SECOND RATCHET WAS FOUND AND DELIBERATELY LEFT ALONE.** `forge.unit`'s `theCommittedMeshesAndTheirModelRowsStillDisagreeExactlyWhereTheyDid` already builds the committed files and pins each one's radius **verdict and sign** against `models.toml`. It is softer than a hash and already file-based, so it is the right shape — but it *is* a pin on asset content, and a big enough resize would trip it. **Named here so the next person meets it as a decision rather than a surprise.** `cockpit` is not in its list, which is why session 12's edit never touched it.

**Live proof.** `geometry.unit` **97 tests, 0 failed with session 12's cockpit edit in the tree** — the bake and the apex drag the old suite rejected twice. Then a fresh drive: `forge.exe` → `cube.forge` → drag the `+x+y+z` corner → save wrote **two lines**, `center = [0.0621, 0.0444, -0.0735]` and `size = [1.1242, 1.0888, 0.853]` (Phase 14's quantization holding at four decimals), and after the size-pin fix the suite is **97/0 with that unblessed edit still in place**. **12 suites green in 84.0 s, `dev-gpu` 2/2, zero warnings.**

**Known gaps.** **An asset can now be edited into something ugly without failing anything** — the invariants catch broken, not wrong, and there is deliberately no test that says a station looks like a station. That is the trade this checkpoint chose, and the answer to a wrong-looking asset is a playtest. **`forge.unit`'s radius pin survives** and is the remaining place a large edit can fail. **The recipe in `shipped_meshes.hpp` will drift from the assets from now on** — by design, since it no longer describes them, but its name still says "shipped" and a future reader may expect otherwise; the block comment above Family 1 is the only thing preventing that.

### Phase 17 — A Switch You Cannot See: Setting The LOD Thresholds By Measuring Them 🚧 (spec'd 2026-08-21)

**From playtest session 13, the session that finally cleared an eight-phase debt.** Six surfaces came back confirmed — Phase 15's reverse cycling, Set Target/Autopilot and map clicking, Phase 13's livelier Lyrioa, Phase 12's gate crossing flown rather than parked at — and the session returned **exactly one note, against the newest thing in the game**: *"LOD appear to be working noticed the pop-in"*.

**⚑ That is the question stage F was handed over on, answered in the direction F could not test.** F's own hand-over said the chain was correct by construction and the pick predicted to the decimal, and that **the only failure mode left was a visible one**. It was.

**Diagnosis (read from the code before any change, and it moved the question once).**

- **⚑⚑ `mesh_lod.hpp` DEFENDS THE *UNIT* EXHAUSTIVELY AND NEVER DEFENDS THE *VALUES*.** Fourteen lines (`mesh_lod.hpp:100-113`) argue pixels over metres — radii run 1 m to 106.7 m, the camera covers four orders of magnitude, a pixel threshold is one number for every model at every resolution, and it disposes of the cockpit with no special case. **All of that is sound and none of it is reopened here.** Then: `inline constexpr float kLevelSwitchPixels[] = {96.0f, 32.0f};` — two bare constants with **no derivation anywhere in the repo**. The argument for the axis was never extended to the point on it.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE CALL SITE ALREADY NAMED THIS EXACT FAILURE MODE, ONE DECISION EARLIER.** `scene_renderer.cpp:376-379`, choosing the widest scale axis for a non-uniformly scaled instance: *"Erring large means erring towards detail, which is the safe direction: the cost of being wrong is a few triangles, **not a visible pop**."* **The reasoning existed, was correct, and was applied to the scale axis while the constant that actually decides when the pop happens went undefended.** Eleventh consecutive item where the answer was already written down somewhere in the tree.
- **⚑⚑ THE TRADE IS ONE-SIDED HERE, WHICH IS WHY 96 px IS NOT MERELY HIGH BUT INDEFENSIBLE.** A normal LOD threshold balances popping against frame time: you switch as early as you can tolerate, because triangles cost. **F bought no frame time and its spec said so before the work started** — 2,298 triangles across the whole catalog, vsync-bound. With nothing on the other side of the scale, **every pixel of earliness is pure cost with no purchase**. The correct threshold today is the *latest* one that still switches at all, which is the opposite of how the number was picked.
- **⚑ FOR SCALE: 96 px of RADIUS is 192 px across — 27% of the height of a 720-high viewport.** That is a large object to change the silhouette of, and the level it changes to is a 50% decimation whose quadric collapse pushes points **outward** by ~1.89% on the station, so the whole silhouette steps outward at once. The eye is good at exactly that.
- **⚑⚑ A SECOND DEFECT IS CERTAIN BY CONSTRUCTION AND IS *NOT* WHAT THE PLAYER SAW: THERE IS NO HYSTERESIS.** `selectMeshLevel` is a pure function of the current frame's screen radius (`mesh_lod.cpp:146-162`) and both call sites (`scene_renderer.cpp:383` and `:472`) recompute it per instance per frame from a stateless loop — **the renderer holds no per-instance state at all**. Anything holding near a boundary flips level every time the radius crosses it. **That is a shimmer while manoeuvring, not a pop while approaching**, so it is a different defect with a different fix, and it is named here rather than folded in — see *Out of scope*.
- **⚑ AND THERE IS NO INSTRUMENT TO MEASURE THE POP WITH.** `sol.lods()` reports which level *was* drawn; nothing can force a level, so an A/B of level 0 against level 1 at a fixed camera today needs an edit to a `constexpr` and a rebuild. **Stage F's own lesson applies to its own follow-up**: the first version of `sol.lods` measured nothing because it was dominated by the cockpit, and it took a second version to become an instrument. This phase needs one more.

**Decisions taken with the user before any code.**

1. **The numbers get MEASURED, not re-guessed.** Replacing 96 with a smaller number chosen by taste would repeat the original defect with a nicer result. The threshold is derived from **pixel A/B of level 0 against level 1 at a ladder of screen radii**, which is Phase 12's method and the precedent this repo already trusts for "measure a rendering change rather than look at it".
2. **The measurement gets a dev lever: `sol.lod_pin(n)`.** It forces every chained model to level `n`, or `-1` for automatic. **It reaches only states the renderer already reaches** — it moves the same selection the threshold moves — so 8u's rule (*a dev lever that reaches a state the sim cannot is a second implementation*) is satisfied rather than dodged. It is permanent, because it is the instrument for judging **any** future LOD change, not scaffolding for this one.
3. **Hysteresis is explicitly OUT of this phase**, named and left. It is a different symptom, and it needs a stable per-instance handle the renderer does not have — a real structural change misrepresented as a follow-on tweak.
4. **The unit and the shape of the policy are NOT reopened.** One global descending pair, in pixels, in `kLevelSwitchPixels`. The header's argument for that is sound and this phase strengthens it rather than replacing it.
5. **The derivation goes into the header beside the constant**, so the next person to touch these numbers inherits the measurement instead of re-running it — which is the whole complaint this phase is fixing.
6. **⚑ A PRE-REGISTERED FAILURE CONDITION, recorded now so the answer cannot be rationalised later: if the measured curve shows the swap is visible at *every* size a level is ever drawn at, the honest conclusion is that selection should be turned off** and the chain kept as insurance for future content (the fourth option the user was offered). **This phase is allowed to conclude that.** Reporting a threshold that merely moves the pop somewhere less convenient would be the failure.

**A. The instrument.** `sol.lod_pin(n)` in `content.cpp` beside `sol.lods()`, and whatever `scene_renderer` needs to honour it — a single override consulted at both call sites, so the opaque and translucent paths cannot drift into two rules (the same care `:464-467` already takes). Exit: `sol.lod_pin(1)` visibly draws the decimated station at a range where level 0 would be selected, and `sol.lods()` reports it.

**B. The measurement.** Park at a ladder of ranges around the current threshold, capture the viewport at each with the level pinned to 0 and then to 1, and count differing pixels. **⚑ The HUD is drawn after tonemapping and is identical in both frames by construction, so it must be cropped out or it inflates every statistic** — the trap Phase 12 recorded. The drive is bit-deterministic, so any surviving difference is real signal, not noise. Output is a curve of *differing pixels vs screen radius*, and the threshold is read off where it falls into the few-pixel silhouette floor. Exit: a table committed in this record, so the number is defensible from data rather than from taste.

**C. The new constants.** `kLevelSwitchPixels` takes the measured values, the header comment carries the derivation and a corrected worked example, and the tests confirm the shape rather than the magnitude. Exit: the suites are green and the recorded switch distances in this document are updated where they are load-bearing rather than historical.

**Tests.** **⚑ `mesh_lod_tests.cpp:344-363` already references `kLevelSwitchPixels` symbolically rather than by literal**, so the selection tests survive a value change by construction — that was written correctly and is not touched. What is added is a check on the **shape** the walk depends on: the thresholds are strictly descending and all positive, which is the invariant `selectMeshLevel`'s loop actually relies on and which nothing asserts today. `ui.unit`'s `screenRadiusPixels` round trip is unchanged. **No test asserts a specific threshold value** — pinning 40 px would be the same undefended constant with a test wrapped round it.

**Exit (phase).** The user flies at a station and **cannot catch the switch**. Everything F verified still holds: the boot line still reports its levels, `sol.lods()` still separates a cook failure from a selection failure, and level 0 is still byte-identical.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** **No hysteresis** — decision 3, and it is a real remaining gap the moment anything hovers at a boundary. **No crossfade, dither or geomorph**: `mesh.frag`'s push block is already exactly 128 bytes with no free lanes and translucency has no depth sorting, so a blended transition is a renderer programme, and it earns its keep only once content exists that makes LOD pay for itself. **No change to the chain, the cooker, the acceptance bands or the `.lodN.smesh` files** — the generation half of F is not what was reported. **No per-model thresholds**: one pair for every model is the property that makes the policy comprehensible, and the fix for a model that pops is a better level, not a private constant.

**⚑ What shipped, and the measurement that set it.** `991c8ef`. **`kLevelSwitchPixels` is `{20.0f, 10.0f}`, down from `{96.0f, 32.0f}`**, with the derivation committed beside it. `geometry.unit` **139 → 140**, 12 suites green in 86.9 s. No save change, no cooker change, no asset change.

- **⚑⚑ THE MEASUREMENT'S OWN FIRST ANSWER WAS AN ARTEFACT, AND CATCHING THAT IS THE METHOD LESSON.** The first sweep read a dramatic cliff — signal falling 94 → 4 changed pixels between r=24 and r=12 — which would have put the threshold at a comfortable 16 px on what looked like a natural knee. **It was the HUD.** The docking marker is drawn at a *fixed* screen size on the nearest dockable station, so below about r=32 it covers the whole object and the diff collapses to the unoccluded fringe. **A measurement whose subject shrinks below a fixed-size overlay stops measuring the subject**, and the tell was that the "cliff" sat exactly where the object's width crossed the marker's. Re-run in the clean band (r ≥ 44, where the station dwarfs the marker) there is **no knee at all**.
- **⚑⚑ BOTH SWITCHES ARE A CLEAN SQUARE LAW IN THE PROJECTED RADIUS**, measured with the ship at 0 m/s, the station boresighted, and each sample carrying its own pin-0-twice control (noise **zero** at every r ≤ 96):

  | r px | range m | 0→1 px | 1→2 px | noise | k(0→1) | k(1→2) |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | 224 | 229 | 24,858 | 31,673 | 228 | 0.49 | 0.63 |
  | 192 | 268 | 15,633 | 20,582 | 226 | 0.42 | 0.56 |
  | 160 | 321 | 10,195 | 13,282 | 88 | 0.40 | 0.52 |
  | 128 | 402 | 5,963 | 7,761 | 108 | 0.36 | 0.47 |
  | 96 | 535 | 3,138 | 3,980 | 0 | 0.34 | 0.43 |
  | 72 | 714 | 1,609 | 2,047 | 0 | 0.31 | 0.40 |
  | 56 | 918 | 891 | 1,196 | 0 | 0.28 | 0.38 |
  | 44 | 1,168 | 492 | 695 | 0 | 0.25 | 0.36 |

- **⚑⚑ SO THERE IS NOTHING TO READ OFF THE CURVE, AND THAT IS THE FINDING RATHER THAN A FAILURE OF IT: the decimation error is scale-invariant in PROPORTIONAL terms** — about 8% of the object's own footprint changes at *every* size — **and only ever becomes invisible in ABSOLUTE ones.** The threshold is therefore a judgement about how small is small enough, and it needs an anchor from outside the curve. The one used: **ordinary ambient traffic moves 88–228 pixels between two captures of the same scene** and nobody has ever called that a pop, so a switch smaller than what the world is already doing on its own is not a switch anybody can catch. A ~100 px ceiling gives **20 px and 10 px**.
- **⚑ THE SECOND SWITCH IS 1.4× AS DISRUPTIVE AS THE FIRST at the same screen size** (k 0.37 vs 0.26), which is why the new pair is **2:1 where the old was 3:1** — matching the criterion rather than the spacing. The consequence is honest and worth stating: **the lod1 band is narrow**, 20 px down to 10 px, because two levels of a three-level chain simply cannot both be invisible and widely spaced.
- **⚑ WHAT THE CHANGE IS WORTH, IN THE UNIT THE COMPLAINT WAS IN.** At the old thresholds the two switch events changed **~3,138 and ~379 pixels**; at the new ones they change **~104 and ~37** — roughly **30× and 10× smaller**. The old first switch is visible in a static side-by-side without magnification: the station's ring goes from smooth to lumpy and its panel markings smear.
- **⚑ THE PRE-REGISTERED FAILURE CONDITION DID NOT FIRE.** It was recorded that if the swap were visible at every size a level is drawn at, the honest answer would be to stop selecting. The square law has no floor, so the swap is never *zero* — but it falls below the scene's own motion well before a level stops being useful, so selection stays on. **It was close enough to be worth having written down first.**
- **⚑ `sol.lod_pin(n)` IS PERMANENT AND IS THE REASON ANY OF THIS IS RE-DERIVABLE.** Forces every chained model to a level, `-1` for automatic, clamped to the levels a model has. Both draw paths go through one `chooseLevel` so the opaque and translucent passes cannot drift into two rules. **If a future content load makes triangles matter, re-measure with it rather than reasoning about it.**
- **⚑ THE TESTS SURVIVED THE VALUE CHANGE UNTOUCHED**, because `mesh_lod_tests.cpp` referenced the constants symbolically rather than by literal — written correctly at stage F and worth not "tidying". What was added is the property the walk depends on and nothing asserted: the thresholds **descend**, are positive, and every level is reachable. **Validated by breaking it** — ascending values fail 2 tests, 140 ran.
- **⚑ LIVE PROOF, predicted before the run and exact to a decimal.** Station r=100 m, focal 514 px at 720p: **2,000 m → 25.8 px lod0; 2,400 m → 21.5 px lod0; 2,700 m → 19.1 px lod1; 4,000 m → 12.9 px lod1; 5,500 m → 9.4 px lod2; 8,000 m → 6.4 px lod2.** Boot line unchanged at `models: 7 (12 meshes, 3 textures, 5 level(s) over 3 model(s))`.

**⚑⚑ SIGNED OFF BY THE PLAYER, 2026-08-21: *"no more pop-in."*** The phase's exit criterion was always a human rather than a number, because the ~100 px ceiling is an anchored judgement and nothing headless can confirm a perceptual claim. **Session 13 reported the defect and the same session confirmed the fix.** ⚑ Note what that does and does not settle: it settles that the *approach* pop is gone at 20/10 px. It does **not** exercise the boundary shimmer, which needs a ship holding station near a threshold rather than flying past one — hysteresis stays an open gap on evidence, not merely on decision.

**Known gaps.** **Hysteresis is still absent** and is now the only known LOD defect left: selection is stateless, so anything holding near 20 px or 10 px flips level every frame the radius crosses it — a shimmer while manoeuvring rather than a pop while approaching. **The Forge's level preview now stands ~5× further back**, because `placeAt` computes the range from these same thresholds; a level that was a 60 px speck is now a ~12 px one. That is arithmetically correct and is arguably a better demonstration of the point, but it makes the preview harder to use, and wheeling in is the answer. **The threshold is calibrated against one asset** — the station is the only subject with three levels that can be parked at an exact range, since gates are not warp-addressable and Lyrioa has no rock. **And the ~100 px ceiling is an anchored judgement, not a perceptual measurement**: the only test that settles it is a human flying at a station, which is the exit criterion.

### Phase 18 — Hold That Level: The Boundary Shimmer Phase 17 Named And Did Not Fix 🚧 (spec'd 2026-08-21)

**Phase 17's own out-of-scope list, picked by the user the moment 17 merged.** 17 fixed the *approach* pop and was signed off on it; it named the other half in the same breath and declined to fold it in, on the grounds that it is a different symptom needing state the renderer does not have. **This phase builds that state.**

**Diagnosis (read from the code before any change, and the estimate moved once).**

- **⚑ SELECTION HAS NO MEMORY, BY CONSTRUCTION.** `selectMeshLevel` (`mesh_lod.cpp:146`) is a pure function of the current frame's projected radius, called per instance per frame from a stateless loop at `scene_renderer.cpp:406` and `:495`. Nothing anywhere remembers what an object drew last frame, so a radius sitting on a threshold re-decides from scratch every frame and can answer differently each time.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE HANDLE THIS NEEDS IS THROWN AWAY ONE LINE UPSTREAM — TWELFTH CONSECUTIVE ITEM THE REPO HAD ALREADY WRITTEN DOWN.** Phase 17 recorded that hysteresis "needs a stable per-instance handle the renderer does not have", which is true of `RenderInstance` — it is `{position, rotation, scale, model}` and nothing else. But `buildRenderInstances` (`space_world.cpp:6708`) walks `shapes.entityIndices()` and **drops the index on the floor** while building each instance. The identity exists; it simply never crosses the boundary.
- **⚑⚑ AND IT MUST BE THE FULL HANDLE, NOT THE BARE INDEX.** `sol::ecs` slots are **recycled** — `Registry::destroy` bumps the slot's generation precisely so stale handles compare dead (`entity.hpp:8-14`, `registry.hpp:53-57`). Keying LOD state on the index alone means **a despawned freighter's level is inherited by whatever spawns into its slot**, which is a wrong draw with no error, exactly the class of bug Phase 15's row-vs-nav-slot was. `entityFromIndex` (`registry.hpp:66`) rebuilds `{index, generation}` and is what a pool-iterating caller is already expected to use.
- **⚑ ONE DRAWABLE HAS NO ENTITY AT ALL.** The cockpit is pushed by the game layer directly (`main.cpp:857`), not produced from the ECS, so identity has to be **optional**. It needs none: it has no chain (210 triangles, under the 256 floor), so `levelCount == 1` and the answer is always level 0 — the same property that already disposes of it in the pixel policy with no special case.
- **⚑⚑ HONEST SCOPE, STATED BEFORE THE WORK SO NOBODY OVERSELLS IT AFTERWARDS: THIS DEFECT IS NARROWER THAN IT SOUNDS.** The radius falls off as `51400/d`, so `dR/dd` at the 20 px threshold is **−0.0078 px per metre**: a ship drifting several metres moves the projected radius by about **0.02 px**. Flicker therefore needs the radius parked within a few hundredths of a pixel of a threshold — real, reachable by hovering at the switch range, and **not something a player will meet often**. This is insurance against a visibly broken frame, not a frequent defect, and it buys no frame time either. **Same discipline as F's "bought no frame time": say the size of the win before measuring it, not after.**

**Decisions taken before any code.**

1. **The policy stays PURE; the state lives in the renderer.** `selectMeshLevel` gains a defaulted `previousLevel` parameter, so the hysteresis rule is still a function of its arguments and still assertable in `geometry.unit` with no device. That is what `mesh_lod.hpp` exists to be, and routing the rule through a stateful renderer object would put the one interesting decision somewhere no test can reach.
2. **⚑ THE MEASURED THRESHOLDS KEEP THEIR EXACT MEANING: they stay the DOWN-switch, where detail decreases.** Phase 17 calibrated *that* boundary against a pixel budget, so the margin is added to the **up**-switch only — a level is handed back when the radius rises past the threshold *by the margin*. The sentence "level `i` takes over when the radius falls below `kLevelSwitchPixels[i-1]`" stays literally true and the numbers keep the measurement behind them.
3. **The margin is a RATIO, not a pixel count** — scale-free, matching the reason the policy is in pixels at all. **10%.** The cost is stated rather than hidden: the up-switch then happens at 22 px where the event is **~126 changed pixels instead of ~104**, still far below the 88–228 px of ambient motion Phase 17 anchored on.
4. **The key is `{index, generation}` packed into one `std::uint64_t`, with a sentinel for "no identity".** Packing rather than including `sol/ecs/entity.hpp` in `scene_renderer.hpp` keeps the ECS out of the renderer's public header, and makes the cockpit's "no entity" case a default rather than a branch.
5. **Eviction is automatic and needs no sweep**: two maps, swapped at the same per-frame point the LOD report is already reset (`scene_renderer.cpp:370`). An instance not drawn this frame simply does not carry over and re-derives from the stateless rule when it returns. Both the opaque and translucent passes run inside one `draw()`, so they share one swap by construction — the same care `:487-490` already takes about not becoming a second rule.
6. **A pin bypasses hysteresis and records itself**, so `sol.lod_pin(-1)` resumes coherently instead of from a level nobody chose.

**A. The rule, pure and headless.** `selectMeshLevel(radius, levelCount, previousLevel = kNoPreviousLevel)`; with no history it is bit-identical to today. **Exit: `geometry.unit` asserts the defining property — a radius swept down and back up switches at different points — with no renderer in the test.**

**B. The identity.** `RenderInstance::instanceKey`, filled by `buildRenderInstances` from `entityFromIndex`, left at the sentinel by the cockpit push. **Exit: the key is stable across frames for a live entity and differs after a slot is recycled.**

**C. The state.** Prev/next maps on `SceneRenderer`, swapped once per frame, consulted by `chooseLevel`. **Exit: a ship parked at a switch range holds one level across a drift that crosses the nominal threshold.**

**Tests.** `geometry.unit`: the down-sweep and up-sweep switch at different radii (the property that *is* hysteresis); a radius oscillating inside the band does not change level; no history reproduces the stateless answer exactly; clamping to existing levels still holds through the new path; and a previous level that is out of range is ignored rather than trusted. **Validated by breaking it**: with the margin at zero the asymmetry test must fail, which is the can-fail demonstration this project requires.

**Exit (phase).** Park at the switch range and drift across it: the level holds instead of flipping. Driven with a slow warp ladder straddling 2,570 m and `sol.lods()` read at each step.

**Out of scope.** **No crossfade, dither or geomorph** — unchanged from 17, and still a renderer programme. **No change to `kLevelSwitchPixels`** — measured, signed off by the player, and not reopened. **No per-model margin**: one ratio for every model, for the same reason there is one threshold pair. **No hysteresis on anything but the mesh level** — the impostor path and the target pick are untouched.

**⚑ What shipped.** `e98ab10`. **`kLevelSwitchHysteresis = 0.10f`**, the rule still pure, the memory in the renderer. `geometry.unit` **140 → 144**, 12 suites green. No save change, no cooker change, no asset change, no threshold change.

- **⚑⚑ THE ESTIMATE MOVED IN THE CHEAP DIRECTION AND IT IS THE TWELFTH CONSECUTIVE ITEM THE REPO HAD ALREADY WRITTEN DOWN.** Phase 17 recorded that this "needs a stable per-instance handle the renderer does not have" and budgeted a structural change. **The handle was being computed and discarded one line before the instance was built**: `buildRenderInstances` iterates `shapes.entityIndices()` and never carried the index across. Adding a field and one `entityFromIndex` call is the whole of stage B.
- **⚑⚑ BUT THE BARE INDEX WOULD HAVE BEEN A REAL BUG, AND IT IS ONE THIS PROJECT HAS SHIPPED BEFORE.** `sol::ecs` recycles slots and `Registry::destroy` bumps the generation *precisely* so stale handles compare dead. Keyed on the index alone, **a despawned freighter's level is inherited by whatever spawns into its slot** — a wrong draw with no error, which is Phase 15's row-index-vs-nav-slot in new clothes. The key packs `{generation, index}`, and `+1` on the index keeps entity 0 generation 0 (the player's ship since Phase 4) out of the sentinel.
- **⚑ THE MARGIN IS SPENT ON ONE SIDE ONLY, WHICH IS WHAT PROTECTS PHASE 17's WORK.** Detail is still dropped at exactly the measured threshold, so `kLevelSwitchPixels` keeps the pixel budget behind it and the header's sentence stays literally true; only the return trip pays. The asymmetry's cost is stated rather than buried: the up-switch lands at **22 px**, where the event is **~126 changed pixels instead of ~104** — still far under the 88–228 px of ambient motion 17 anchored on.
- **⚑ THE RULE STAYED PURE, WHICH IS WHY IT HAS FOUR REAL TESTS.** `selectMeshLevel` takes `previousLevel` rather than keeping it, so hysteresis is assertable in `geometry.unit` with no device — the property that `mesh_lod.hpp` exists to have. The renderer owns only the memory, because only the renderer knows which instance is which. **Validated by breaking it**: margin to zero fails exactly the two tests that assert the asymmetry and the anti-jitter behaviour, and leaves the other 142 green.
- **⚑ EVICTION IS FREE AND DELIBERATE.** Two maps swapped at the same per-frame point the LOD report already resets; an instance not drawn this frame does not carry over. That matters because **a system jump retires every entity in the bubble at once**, and a single accumulating map would keep their levels forever.
- **⚑⚑ LIVE PROOF, predicted before the run and exact to a decimal — and the assertion is a PAIR, not a reading.** Coming **in**: 2,600 m 19.8 px lod1; **2,500 m 20.6 px lod1 (held)**; **2,400 m 21.5 px lod1 (held)**; 2,300 m 22.4 px lod0 (released). Going **out**: 2,000 m 25.8 px lod0; **2,400 m 21.5 px lod0**; **2,500 m 20.6 px lod0**; 2,600 m 19.8 px lod1. **The same range answers differently depending on which side it was approached from, which is the definition of the feature and is visible in a text probe.**

**Known gaps.** **The win is small and was said to be small before it was built** — `dR/dd` is −0.0078 px/m at the threshold, so this needs a ship parked within hundredths of a pixel of a switch. **It buys no frame time either.** Do not let a later session report it as more than insurance against a visibly broken frame. **An instance that leaves the frame and returns forgets its level** and re-derives statelessly; that is correct (it cannot shimmer if it was not drawn) but it means the memory is only as long as continuous visibility. **The margin is one global ratio** with no measurement behind the specific value of 0.10 — unlike the thresholds, it is bounded below by jitter (~0.02 px) and above by the pixel budget, and anywhere in that very wide range works, so it was not worth measuring. **And the shimmer itself was never observed in play** — it was derived from the code, so this phase fixes a defect nobody has reported seeing.

### Phase 19 — The Rest Of The World: Taking Down The Name Wall Stage H Measured ✅ (completed 2026-08-21)

**Stage H's headline finding, picked by the user the moment the Forge programme closed.** Stage A removed the *enum* wall and left a *name* wall: `space_world.cpp:4629` was the only data-driven model resolution in the entire game, and everything else in the world is fetched by a literal compiled into C++. H made a station's model data as a rider on a tools stage and recorded the rest as owed. **This phase takes the wall down.**

**Diagnosis (read from the code before any change — the recorded list was wrong, and the obvious design is unparseable).**

- **⚑⚑ THE RECORDED LIST OF SIX IS SEVEN.** `main.cpp:303` resolves `"cockpit"` by literal too. It went unrecorded because it calls `content.defs().modelIndex("cockpit")` **directly** rather than through `modelByName`, so the grep that produced H's list could not see it. **⚑ And it is the one site outside the library**: `main.cpp` is the executable and `sol_game_lib` holds everything else, so the cockpit's resolution is the only one of the seven that **no test can reach where it stands** — the linkage boundary for a fourth time, found before the promise rather than after.
- **⚑⚑ ONE OF THE SEVEN IS NOT "MAKE IT DATA", IT IS "STOP DISCARDING WHAT YOU ALREADY HAVE" — THIRTEENTH CONSECUTIVE ITEM THE REPO HAD ALREADY WRITTEN DOWN.** `WreckRecord::defId` is commented *"the victim's ship def (name and hull size)"* and the wreck spawn (`space_world.cpp:2227`) ignores it to draw `modelByName("ship")` at 1.4×, under a comment admitting the placeholder. The victim's own `ShipDef` is one `findShip` away and needs **no new def key at all**.
- **⚑⚑ THIS PHASE LOOKS COSMETIC AND IS NOT: A MODEL NAME IS A COLLISION RADIUS.** `modelBaseRadius(shape.model) * scale` is what the collision set, the avoidance set and the mining/salvage beam sweep all read (`space_world.cpp:6216`). Re-pointing a name moves physics, not pixels. Two concrete traps: `asteroid` is authored at **radius 1.0 specifically so a rock's real size is its instance scale**, so a commodity naming a model of any other radius silently resizes every rock of that type *and* its mining hit sphere; and the wreck change below moves a salvage target's radius by 4×.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE PARSER CANNOT EXPRESS A SINGLETON TABLE, WHICH KILLED THE FIRST DESIGN.** `DefDatabase::mergeToml` (`data_defs.cpp:809`) rejects any top-level key that is not an array of tables — `"'x' must be an array of tables ([[x]])"` — and rejects unknown kinds outright one branch above. A `[world]` table naming role → model is **a load failure, not a schema**, and adding a singleton branch would also mean **inventing merge rules that have no precedent here** (what does a mod's `[world]` do to the base game's?). An `id`-keyed array reuses `mergeDef` and inherits mod override for free.
- **⚑ THE BOLT IS CHEAPER THAN IT LOOKS.** `ShipWeapon` is flattened to eight floats and keeps **no def id**, so nothing at the spawn site knows which weapon fired — but `applyLoadout` (`space_world.cpp:4657`) already holds the `WeaponDef` in hand. One component field, one line, no lookup path.
- **⚑ NO SAVE CHANGE, AND THAT IS WORTH SAYING BECAUSE THE PRECEDENT CUTS THE OTHER WAY.** Nothing serializes a model id; `RenderShape` is rebuilt from specs on load. Unlike Phase 13 this changes no world generation, so no save describes a world that no longer exists.
- **⚑ ALL THREE SHIP DEFS NAME `model = "ship"` AND DIFFER ONLY IN SCALE** (1.0 / 0.8 / 4.0), which is exactly what makes the wreck the one visible change in the phase — and exactly why nothing else in it is visible.

**Decisions taken before any code.**

1. **The role table is `[[role]]`, an array of tables keyed on `id`** — not the `[world]` singleton the scoping conversation started from, because that shape does not parse and its merge semantics would have to be invented. `RoleDef {id, model, source}` is the same shape every other def kind has, so it reuses the dispatch, `FieldReader`, `rejectUnknownKeys`, `mergeDef` and the `source` diagnostic. **A mod re-points a role exactly the way it re-points a ship.**
2. **⚑ A ROLE NAME IS NOT AN ASSET NAME, AND THAT DISTINCTION IS THE WHOLE POINT.** The list of roles the engine asks for (`gate`, `gate_membrane`, `ore_chunk`, `rock`, `bolt`, `cockpit`) stays in C++, because it is the set of slots the engine *has*. What leaves C++ is every answer. The exit criterion is therefore literal and checkable: **no model name appears in `game/src`.**
3. **A missing required role, or a role naming a model that does not exist, is a HARD LOAD FAILURE** — not `modelIdFromName`'s warn-and-fall-back. That treatment is right for a ship def, where one bad name breaks one ship and a real hull is still there to fall back to. A role has no fallback once the literal is gone, and a silently invisible gate in every system is worse than a refusal naming the file. Same treatment `loadModels` already gives a missing mesh.
4. **Every per-thing override is OPTIONAL and falls back to its role.** An empty `model`/`cockpit` key means "use the role". So `ships.toml`, `weapons.toml` and `commodities.toml` gain **the ability** to differ and are not required to, and the shipped data changes nothing.
5. **The radius contract gets written down and pinned.** `rock`, `ore_chunk` and `bolt` are drawn at an instance scale that carries meaning (a rock's radius in metres, a 6 m chunk, a 0.3 × 0.3 × 4 m bolt), so the models filling those roles **must be authored at radius 1.0**. Stated in the row's comment and asserted in `assets.unit`, because finding 3 says the failure is a physics change with no visual tell.
6. **The cockpit's resolution moves out of `main.cpp` and into `SpaceWorld`.** It has to move anyway to follow the active ship through a fleet switch, and moving it puts the one untestable site of the seven inside `sol_game_lib`. `main.cpp` reads `world.cockpitModel()`; the resolve happens where the loadout is already applied, so there is no per-frame string lookup.
7. **⚑⚑ STATED BEFORE THE WORK, AS F AND G BOTH WERE: THIS PHASE BUYS NO NEW ART AND NO VISUAL CHANGE BY DEFAULT.** Every role row points at exactly the literal it replaces and every override ships unset. What it buys is that the *rest* of the world becomes authorable — the same honesty G needed about byte-identical textures. **The one exception is the wreck**, below, and it should be reported as the one exception rather than as the phase's payoff.
8. **⚑ THE WRECK IS THE PHASE'S ONE BEHAVIOUR CHANGE AND IS CALLED OUT SEPARATELY SO IT CAN BE VETOED.** Drawing the victim's own def means a freighter's wreck goes from `ship` at 1.4 to `ship` at **4.0 × 1.4 = 5.6** — a ~45 m derelict where there was a ~11 m one, and a salvage hit sphere 4× wider. That is almost certainly *correct* (a freighter should leave a freighter-sized wreck) but it is a real change to how easy a wreck is to hit, and it is the only thing here a player could notice without authoring anything.

**A. The role table, in `sol::assets`, with nothing depending on it yet.** `RoleDef`, the `[[role]]` dispatch branch, `DefDatabase::roleModel(id)`, the required-role check at load, and six rows in `models.toml` pointing at today's literals. **Exit: the game builds and runs bit-identically because nothing reads the table yet, and `assets.unit` proves the table before anything depends on it.**

**B. The six sites read the table.** `gate`, `gate_membrane`, `ore_chunk`, `rock`, `bolt`, `cockpit` resolve through `roleModel`. **Exit: re-pointing one `[[role]]` row changes what the game draws with no C++ change — the phase's mechanism, proved once in isolation.**

**C. The per-weapon and per-commodity overrides.** `WeaponDef::model` → a `ShipWeapon` field set in `applyLoadout`; `CommodityDef::model` for the rock and `chunk_model` for the chunk, both read where the commodity index is already in hand. **Exit: two weapons fire visibly different bolts and two ores leave visibly different rock, from data alone.**

**D. The per-ship cockpit.** `ShipDef::cockpit`, resolution moved into `SpaceWorld` and following the active ship. **Exit: switching ships in the fleet changes the cockpit, and the resolution is reachable from `game.unit` for the first time.**

**E. The wreck reads its victim.** `findShip(wreck->defId)` → that def's `model` and `scale × 1.4`. **Exit: a freighter's wreck is freighter-sized, and the radius contract test covers the hit-sphere consequence.**

**F. Live drive and the record.**

**Tests.** `assets.unit`: a `[[role]]` row parses; a later file overrides a role by `id` (the `mergeDef` path); an unknown role `id` fails at load naming the file; a role naming a missing model fails at load; a missing required role fails at load; the radius contract holds for every role whose scale carries meaning; and `models.toml` still round-trips **byte for byte** through `def_doc`, which is the guard stage H built and this phase must not break. `game.unit`: the cockpit follows the active ship, and an override falls back to its role when unset. **Validated by breaking it** — each new load-failure test must be shown to fail when its check is removed, and the radius contract must be shown to catch a role re-pointed at a model of the wrong radius, because finding 3 says nothing else would.

**Exit (phase).** **No model name literal remains in `game/src`.** Proved live: re-point a `[[role]]` row and a per-thing override in data, rebuild without touching C++, and see both in the game. Plus the wreck, flown.

**⚑ What shipped.** `2f46af4` A, `492defc` B, `a600d93` C, `879d6e3` D, `2b79f3a` E. **`game/src` contains no model name.** `assets.unit` **70 → 74**, `game.unit` **7 → 15**; `geometry.unit`, `ui.unit`, `cooker.unit`, `forge.unit` untouched. 12 suites green in 87.6 s, `dev-gpu` 2/2. **No save change, no world-generation change, no cooker change, no new asset.**

- **⚑⚑ THE RECORDED TALLY OF SIX WAS TEN, AND STAGE H HAD CREATED TWO OF THE FOUR IT MISSED.** `main.cpp:303` resolved `"cockpit"` through `defs().modelIndex` rather than `modelByName`, so the grep behind the record could not see it; `space_world.cpp:3060` kept a `"station"` default for an out-of-range archetype; and the two `modelIdFromName` fallbacks were literals **because H turned a hardcoded fallback into an argument**. Moving a literal to the call site reads like a generalisation and removes nothing. **A count produced by one grep pattern measures the pattern.**
- **⚑⚑ THE DESIGN THE SCOPING CHOSE DOES NOT PARSE, AND THE REPLACEMENT IS BETTER FOR A REASON NOBODY RAISED.** `mergeToml` (`data_defs.cpp:809`) refuses any top-level key that is not an array of tables, so a `[world]` singleton is a load failure. The `id`-keyed `[[role]]` array that replaced it goes through `mergeDef` — **so a mod re-points a role exactly the way it re-points a ship**, while the singleton would have needed merge semantics invented for it with no precedent in the codebase. The unparseable option was the one that also had the design flaw.
- **⚑⚑ A MODEL NAME IS A COLLISION RADIUS, WHICH IS WHY THIS PHASE IS NOT COSMETIC.** `modelBaseRadius() * scale` is read by the collision set, the avoidance set and the mining/salvage sweep. Three roles (`rock`, `ore_chunk`, `bolt`) are drawn at scales that mean metres, so their models must be authored at radius 1 — **pinned by a test, because the failure has no visual tell**. Pointing `[[role]] rock` at the station model leaves the existence check **green** and is caught only by the radius pin.
- **⚑⚑ THE MUTATION THAT SURVIVED IS THE STAGE-F LESSON IN A NEW SHAPE.** Deleting `modelOverrideOr`'s empty-name branch left **all twelve tests green**, because `modelIdFromName("")` also finds nothing and also falls back to the same role — the two paths converge by construction, so the obvious test **could not fail**. The branch's real job is the **log**: without it every unset override warns once per commodity per system load. Rewritten against a log sink, the same mutation fails. *A test that cannot distinguish the rule from its fallback is testing the fallback.*
- **⚑⚑ AND WRITING A TEST FOUND A DEFECT THE FEATURE HAD NOT REACHED.** `applyDefs` has **two** paths that put a def on the player's entity — the fleet one and a pre-universe branch for an empty fleet — and only the first resolved the cockpit. **On a fresh boot the pre-universe branch is the one that runs**, so a hull's `cockpit` key would have been silently ignored until the next refit. The rule was right; the second caller was the thing nobody looked at.
- **⚑ `modelByName` IS DELETED RATHER THAN RENAMED**, which turns the exit criterion into a compile error instead of a habit: game code can no longer ask for a model by name at all. A drawable either fills a declared role or is named by a def an author wrote. **The role ids stay in C++** (`model_roles.hpp`) because the set of slots is a property of the engine; only the answers moved out.
- **⚑ ROLES REFUSE WHERE A DEF WARNS.** `validateRoles` fails the load — naming the file — for an unfilled role, an unknown role or a role naming a missing model, while a ship or commodity naming a bad model still warns and falls back. The asymmetry is whose mistake it is: one broken def is one broken ship, an unfilled role is the game having no answer.
- **⚑⚑ LIVE PROOF IS TWO A/Bs, BOTH ON THE SAME BINARY WITH NO REBUILD, BOTH PREDICTED BEFORE THE RUN.** *Role:* `[[role]] gate` → `station` takes `sim.avoidance` and `sim.collision.build` from **15 to 19** while the entity count holds at 19 — four gates, unchanged in number, joining both sets because their **model** changed, which proves the table reaches the **sim** and not just the renderer. *Override:* `sol.shuttle` gains `cockpit = "station"` and `sol.lods()` reads **10153.5 px** against a prediction of 812.3 × 100/8 = **10,153.75** — exact to a quarter pixel — with the screenshot showing the habitat ring sweeping across the seat.
- **⚑ `sol.roles()` IS NEW AND PERMANENT**, the instrument for re-deriving any of this: the phase claims an answer lives in a file, and this reads it back out of a running game without a screenshot.

**⚑ FIRST CONTENT THROUGH THE MECHANISM — `assets/meshes/freighter_cockpit.forge`, the game's second interior.** 28 parts (12 beams, 16 boxes), 336 triangles, authored against `ShipDef::cockpit` and flown from the seat by buying a freighter in-game (`sol.dock` → `sol.add_credits` → `sol.buy_ship` → `sol.select_ship(2)` → `sol.undock`, log line `now flying 'sol.freighter'`). Boot reads `models: 8 (13 meshes)`, up from 7 and 12.

- **⚑⚑ THE EYE IS A CONSTANT, SO A FREIGHTER'S DECK IS NOT A BIGGER CABIN — IT IS A DIFFERENT ONE THE SAME SIZE.** `kCockpitOffset` does not scale with the hull and the cockpit instance is pushed at scale 1, so a freighter drawn at scale 4 puts its pilot's head in exactly the same place as a shuttle's. What separates them is styling: a letterbox windscreen (header 1.28 against 1.38) that is wider (±1.05 against ±0.80), heavier 0.10 structure, an overhead panel, a second crew station, and a blunt stepped nose instead of a taper.
- **⚑⚑ THE FORGE WROTE THE `[[model]]` ROW AND MEASURED THE RADIUS (7.4173 m, *"matches the mesh"*), AND THE THREE VALUES IT COULD NOT INFER ARE THE ONES THAT MAKE IT AN INTERIOR.** `texture` defaulted to `checker` because a combo must default to something, `emissive` needs 0.10 or a room lit by 1.2% vacuum ambient is pitch black, and `solid` must be off. **Geometry cannot tell you any of that** — worth knowing before stage H's def rows are called complete.
- **⚑⚑ AND THE DEFECT WAS ONLY VISIBLE FROM THE SEAT: THE FIRST DRAFT PUT THE SPLIT-SCREEN POST AT x = 0, WHICH IS EXACTLY THE BORESIGHT.** The Forge's Report said manifold, closed, zero border edges and was right about all of it; the tool's camera orbits the model from outside and has no idea where the head is. Moved to x = 0.43, between the two crew stations. **A tool that validates a mesh cannot validate a viewpoint.**
- **⚑ ADDING ONE ASSET BROKE TWO TESTS THAT WERE ABOUT THE WRITER, NOT THE COUNT** — `doc.count("model") == 7` and `defs.models().size() == 8`, both content pins on a file the tool exists to let people edit. **Phase 16's ratchet in a third format.** Both now derive their number, so they assert *the document sees every row the schema does* and *appending adds one*.

**Known gaps.** **This phase bought no new art and no visual change by default** — every role points at the literal it replaced and every override ships unset, so nothing looks different until somebody authors something. Do not let a later session report it as new content. **The one exception is the wreck**: a freighter's derelict goes from 1.4 to 5.6 scale, which is a **4× wider salvage target** — flown only in a headless test, never in play. **Gates have no variety**: one `gate` role galaxy-wide, and per-gate models would need `sim::GateSpec` to carry an identity, i.e. a world-generation change and a save bump. **The Forge cannot write a `[[role]]` row** — it writes `[[model]]` and `[[ship]]`, so a role edit is still a text-editor job. **A re-pointed role needs a relaunch**; defs hot-reload but instantiated entities keep the model they spawned with. **And the unit-radius contract is only pinned for ROLES** — an override in somebody's mod can only be warned at, because no test of ours will ever see their file.

**Out of scope (named so they are not half-built).** **No new meshes or textures** — this phase is the plumbing and the art is a separate decision; do not let it be reported as new art. **No `[[gate]]` def kind** and no per-gate variety: `sim::GateSpec` would have to carry which gate it is, and that is a world-generation change, which by this project's precedent forces a save bump for a feature nobody has asked for. **No Forge support for writing `[[role]]` rows** — H writes `[[model]]` and `[[ship]]`, and extending the tool is a tools item with no complaint behind it. **No hot-reload of a re-pointed role** into a running game; the loop stays cook-and-relaunch. **No change to `modelIdFromName`'s warn-and-fall-back for ship and station defs** — decision 3 is about roles only. **And no per-faction or per-region furniture**: one role, one model, galaxy-wide.

## 5. Standing Design Questions

Tracked here so they're decided consciously (record outcomes in `docs/decisions/`):

1. ECS storage model — ✅ decided: sparse-set (`docs/decisions/001-ecs-storage-model.md`).
2. Fixed-point vs. double for sim positions — default double; revisit only with evidence.
3. Render graph — introduce when pass management hurts (est. Phase 4–6), not speculatively.
4. Travel model baseline — ✅ decided: gates + cruise, drive as late-game unlock; no time compression (`docs/decisions/004-travel-model.md`, `docs/decisions/005-time-compression.md`).
5. Audio decoder dependency (ogg) — ✅ decided: approved and vendored, **cooker-only** (`docs/decisions/009-audio-decoder.md`).
6. GPU particles, atmosphere shaders, planet detail — post-Phase 7 polish tier.
